SPINOZA’S THEOLOGICO-POLITICAL TREATISE
This book presents the first accessible analysis of Spinoza’s Tractatus Theologico-
politicus, situating the work in the context of Spinoza’s general philosophy and its
17th-century historical background.
According to Spinoza it is impossible for a being to be infinitely perfect and to have
a legislative will. This idea, demonstrated in the Ethics, is presupposed and further
elaborated in the Tractatus Theologico-politicus. It implies not only that on the level
of truth all revealed religion is false, but also that all authority is of human origin and
that all obedience is rooted in a political structure. The consequences for ‘authority’
as it is used in a religious context are explored: the authority of Scripture, the
authority of particular interpretations of Scripture, and the authority of the Church.
Verbeek also explores the work of two other philosophers of the period – Hobbes
and Descartes – to highlight certain peculiarities of Spinoza’s position, and to show
the contrasts between their theories.


Spinoza’s Theologico-political
Treatise
Exploring ‘the Will of God’

Foreword
For all the too many years that I have been working on this book there were various
people who in sharing with me their thoughts on Spinoza and commenting on earlier
drafts have greatly contributed to its final form. In alphabetical order they are: Erik-
Jan Bos, Wiep van Bunge, Sarah Hutton, Jonathan Israel, Cees Leijenhorst, Pierre-
François Moreau, Han van Ruler, Karl Schuhmann, Leen Spruit and Piet
Steenbakkers. If it did not look ungrateful to them I would add that they bear no
responsibility whatsoever for the result. What I mean to say is that I might have
avoided the errors left in it if I had listened to them more carefully. I also recall with
great pleasure the various occasions that provided me with an opportunity to present
my argument and quite often to considerably modify it: a colloquium at Marsh’s
Library (Dublin) in the summer of 1994; a seminar at the Istituto Italiano per gli
Studi Filosofici (Naples) and a colloquium on toleration organized by the University
of Chicago, both in the spring of 1996; the annual meeting of the Vereniging ‘Het
Spinozahuis’ in Rijnsburg in May 1998; a colloquium organized in November 1998
in Milan (CRS) by Luisa Simonutti, on determinism and freedom in English and
Dutch thought of the 17th century (‘Dal necessario al possibile’); a conference of the
British Society of the History of Philosophy, organized at Keele University in March
1999 on philosophical reactions to Christianity in modern thought; and a colloquium
on Spinoza’s Theologico-political treatise, organized by the cultural representatives
of France, Germany, Great Britain, Italy and Spain, at the Maison Descartes
(Amsterdam) in November 1999 to celebrate in a truly European spirit the new
critical edition of that work by Fokke Akkerman and Pierre-François Moreau (Paris:
PUF, 1999). Guest lectures on the subject were given at the Leiden-Utrecht Zeno
Research Institute in Philosophy, the Erasmus University (Rotterdam) and the École
Normale Supérieure (Fontenay). I take great pleasure in thanking all who were
somehow engaged in organizing these venues and am profoundly grateful for their
help and encouragement. I thank my publisher, Sarah Lloyd, and various other
people working for Ashgate for the efficient and intelligent way they dealt with my
problems.
I dedicate this book to the memory of a great scholar and a generous friend Onofrio
Nicastro (1939–1994). Despite his fatal illness I could have some talks with him
when I started work on this project but feel frustrated and sad that I have not been
able to profit more from his unfailing hospitality, his profound knowledge and his
perfect wisdom.
Utrecht, February 2002


Author’s Note
In quotations from Spinoza the first reference is to the critical edition by Carl
Gebhardt (G, followed by volume and page number), which can also be found in
some translations. Since quotations from the Political treatise, which consists of
short paragraphs, can be easily identified without reference to any critical edition,
the only reference I give there is by chapter and paragraph number. In the case of the
Short treatise this is followed by a reference to the critical edition by Filippo
Mignini (M, followed by page number). The critical edition of the Theologico-
political treatise I used is that by Akkerman and Moreau.
These references are generally followed by one to the English translation by
Curley (C, followed by volume and page number) and, in the case of the Theologico-
political treatise, which as yet is not translated by Curley, to that by Shirley.

translations of Curley or Shirley are generally not noted. Spinoza’s works are cited

emendatione)

Introduction
Every reader of Spinoza’s Theologico-political treatise (1670) will know that it is a
difficult book but will also realize that its difficulties are not like those of, say, the
Critique of Pure Reason or the Phenomenology of the Mind. Its vocabulary is not
technical at all; nor is its reasoning complicated or its logic extraordinary. If it is
difficult it is not because of particular phrases, paragraphs, concepts, but because
one fails to see how things combine; how particular arguments fit into a
comprehensive argument; how a single chapter or couple of chapters relate to the
book as a whole and how the book relates to Spinoza’s other work; indeed, it is not
clear most of the time what it is all about even if every now and then one stumbles
across something familiar and recognizable.
The secondary literature is not very helpful. There is little unanimity as to whether
Spinoza explains the origin of civil society by a covenant, whether he endorses
democratic government, whether the book is against all religion or only against
specific aspects of a specific religion, how we should read the chapters on prophecy
(chaps 1 and 2), on the interpretation of Scripture (chap. 7), on theocracy (chaps 17
and 18). Even if many people would probably agree that ‘toleration’ is one of the
issues, little is done to match this with chap. 19, which seems to aim at subjecting
all theological and pastoral activity to the sovereign; and even though few people
would find it difficult to understand the point of the chapter on miracles (chap. 6),
there is little inclination to connect it with, say, that on prophecy (chap. 2) or the
interpretation of Scripture (chap. 7). The safest course generally is to select one
particular topic and be satisfied with some very broad hints about the rest.
Still, a close reading of even a single paragraph may end in considerable
confusion. For example, Spinoza starts his book with a definition: ‘Prophecy, that is
[sive], revelation, is the certain knowledge of some matter revealed by God to man.’1
So revelation would be prophecy; prophecy would be revelation; revelation as well
as prophecy would be ‘certain knowledge’ (certa cognitio). But usually ‘revelation’
is either a process (God revealing himself or his will) or the result of a process
(revealed knowledge about God and his will). The two are mediated by a prophet,
although that is by no means necessary – after all there is an important tradition
according to which God reveals himself (also) in nature.2 Leaving that point for what
it is, it is not evident that revelation and prophecy are strictly identical. Hobbes, for
example, makes a useful distinction between ‘revelation’, as the process of directly
receiving a message from God (in a dream or vision or through a voice), and
‘prophecy’, as the process by which the recipient (a ‘prophet’) communicates
whatever he believes was ‘revealed’ to him by God to others – a distinction which,
as we shall see, Spinoza also relies on in a different context.3 Most Christians, on the
other hand, although they would concede that prophecy is revelation and that
revelation yields knowledge (albeit knowledge of a particular kind, not to be
confused with ordinary, natural, knowledge), would not be prepared to say that
revelation is always prophecy given their belief that God reveals himself, not only
1


in the Old Testament (the ‘Prophetic Books’), but also in the New Testament (the
‘Apostolic Books’) – and an apostle is not a prophet, as Spinoza himself will insist
later on.4
And what does it mean that prophecy (revelation) is ‘knowledge’? That it is
‘certain knowledge’? Or would it be significant that Spinoza does not use ‘science’
(scientia) or ‘wisdom’ (sapientia) but ‘cognition’ (cognitio), that is, anything from
‘awareness’ up to ‘knowledge by acquaintance’?5 And does ‘certain’ (certa) mean
‘known to be true’ or is it no more than ‘some kind of’? A Spinozist at any rate, who
learned from the Ethics that truth and certainty are reserved to adequate ideas, would
not agree that awareness is ‘certain’, that is, known to be true. In any case, if
prophecy is ‘known to be true’ the Spinozist would have to admit, not only that it is
philosophy, but also that it consists of adequate ideas.6 And that indeed is what
Spinoza seems to be aiming at: ‘Given this definition natural knowledge could also
be called prophecy, for whatever we know by natural light also depends on
knowledge of God and his eternal decrees.’7 But on almost the same page the
identity of philosophy and prophecy is denied or at least qualified:
The only difference with what is generally called divine knowledge is that the latter goes
beyond the limits of the former [philosophy] and cannot be explained by the laws of
human nature in themselves. Yet in respect of the certainty involved in natural knowledge
and the source from which it derives, that is, God, it is in no way inferior to prophetic
knowledge, unless one would be willing to understand, or rather to dream, that prophets
have human bodies but non-human minds and that their sensations and consciousness are
of an entirely different order from our own.8
Despite the fact that philosophy is not inferior to revelation either in truth or in
certainty, revelation goes beyond it. But apart from the fact that that is clearly a
contradiction in itself – for how can revelation go beyond philosophy without
philosophy losing its certainty? – it is also against Spinoza’s general philosophy,
according to which the real coincides with the conceivable. The idea therefore that
there could be something real and knowable (to a prophet) but inconceivable (for a
philosopher) is absurd.9 Besides, the only conceivable reason why there could be
knowledge going ‘beyond the limits of ordinary knowledge’ is that prophets have
faculties other people have not, but that is impossible because they had a human
body and therefore a human mind – indeed, the idea that it could be otherwise is
dismissed as ‘a dream’.
At the end of the day the alleged identity of ‘prophecy’, ‘revelation’ and ‘certain
knowledge’ turns out to rest on an equivocation on the word ‘divine’. The reason
why natural knowledge (Spinoza’s philosophy) is called ‘divine’ is that it is based
on a concept of God which, being rational, is accessible to everyone, whereas the
reason why prophecy is called ‘divine’ is that it goes beyond what people can
‘naturally’ know, that is, that it is not ‘divine’ in the first sense – indeed, the ‘God’
of Spinoza is not the ‘God’ of prophecy, the first being ‘nature’ and the second a
supreme legislator of a kind which, according to philosophy, is inconceivable and
therefore impossible. It also rests on an equivocation on ‘certain cognition’, which
is taken sometimes as subjectively certain awareness (or belief) and sometimes as
objectively certain knowledge (science). As a result, the point of Spinoza’s
definition of ‘prophecy’ is not to explain the meaning of the word ‘prophecy’ but to
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
2


lay down a dialectical principle such that he can establish an identity of some sort
between his own philosophy and traditional religion.10
This becomes even more evident if one looks at Spinoza’s definition of ‘prophet’,
which is clearly contrary to that of ‘prophecy’. According to Spinoza a ‘prophet’ is
someone who ‘interprets God’s revelations for those who cannot have certain
knowledge of the things revealed by God and can embrace them only by faith’.11
What the definitions of ‘prophet’ and ‘prophecy’ have in common is their concern
with ‘things revealed by God’. But whereas according to the definition of ‘prophecy’
the result is ‘certain knowledge’ (even to the point that it is identical with
philosophy), the definition of ‘prophet’ suggests that there is a real distinction
between knowledge and belief and a corresponding difference between philosophy
and prophecy. Some people ‘cannot have certain knowledge’ of the things revealed
by God and embrace them ‘by faith’. So faith is an alternative to knowledge, made
possible by prophecy. Accordingly, there is a real distinction between ‘reason and
knowledge’, which in an objective way are certain, and ‘faith and belief’, which are
at best believed to be certain. Philosophy and prophecy may both be ‘divine’ but
they cater for different types of people and deliver with different degrees of
certainty.
This also reflects on the way those two types of ‘cognition’ are communicated. By
following the arguments of a philosopher we eventually know and understand the
things he knows and understands in the way he himself knows and understands them
and become as certain of their truth as he himself is; but if we listen to a prophet we
at best accept his words ‘on authority’ and embrace whatever he says by ‘faith’. So
there is a difference, not only in the result (knowledge by understanding versus
belief on authority), but also in the process leading to that result. Whereas in the first
case we speak of ‘learning’ and ‘teaching’, Spinoza calls the second process
‘interpretation’. A prophet ‘interprets’ God’s decrees as they are ‘revealed’ to him.
Others come to ‘know about’ God through a prophet (provided they trust and believe
him) but can justify their beliefs and be certain of them only by alleging his
authority. The philosopher, however, knows God on the basis of a concept and
everyone having the same concept will know God in the way he does:
An interpreter of God is someone who has a revelation of God’s decrees, which he
interprets to others who did not have this revelation and accept it solely in reliance on the
prophet’s authority. Now if those who listen to prophets were themselves to become
prophets just as those who listen to philosophers become philosophers the prophet would
not be an interpreter of divine decrees; for his hearers would not rely on the testimony and
authority of the prophet but on the divine revelation itself and on their own inward
testimony, just as the prophet does. Similarly, sovereign powers are the interpreters of their
own sovereign right, since the laws they enact are upheld only by their own sovereign
authority and are supported only by their own testimony.12
‘Having a revelation’ is a private experience (an ‘inward testimony’) which cannot
be shared with others but only ‘interpreted’ for others. This ‘interpretation’ is what
we call prophecy, that is, the words spoken by a prophet – which shows,
incidentally, that, like Hobbes, Spinoza makes a distinction between ‘revelation’ and
‘prophecy’. Moreover, an ‘interpreter’ does not bring other persons into a state of
mind even remotely comparable with his own; indeed, however much we listen to a
Introduction
3


prophet we will never be a prophet, that is, someone to whom God directly
communicates his will. We believe an interpreter because he has ‘authority’: it is his
‘authority’ that provides certainty to a belief which would otherwise be doubtful.
Similarly, we obey a sovereign, not because we understand his decisions, but
because we have given him the ‘authority’ to decide what is right. Indeed, if we were
willing to obey him only to the extent that we believe he is right, he would have no
‘authority’ at all. So in spite of the fact that Spinoza aims at establishing an identity
of some sort between prophecy (revelation) and philosophy he is clearly not
prepared to concede that there is no difference – indeed, the differences indicated
imply that prophecy is not knowledge, that in general it is not true and that, if it
happens to be true, it is not by any means certain but on the contrary that whatever
certainty it has derives from the authority we have given to a prophet.
This suggests that if Spinoza defines something he makes use of essentially
dialectical or perhaps rhetorical techniques.13 The definitions of ‘revelation’,
‘prophecy’ and ‘prophet’ are formulated and used so as to allow ‘philosophy’, that
is, presumably, Spinoza’s own philosophy, to be described as ‘divine’, as being in
some way on one line with prophecy and revelation. Accordingly, philosophy would
deserve the same treatment as revealed religion. Indeed, the definitions of
‘prophecy’ and ‘prophet’ would amount to an implicit refutation of the claim that
Spinoza’s philosophy is ‘impious’ or ‘atheist’.
Now the charge of atheism is extremely complex. In the 17th century an atheist is
someone who denies, not necessarily the existence, but in any case the will of God
– if the atheist denies God’s existence at all it is in order to be able to deny God’s
will and do as he pleases.14 The most famous ‘atheist’ of the early 17th century,
Giulio Cesare Vanini (1584–1619), speaks of God on almost every page of his work
but was seen as an atheist because of the properties he attributed to God.15 Descartes,
whom nowadays few, if any, would call an atheist, was called an ‘implicit’ or
‘indirect’ atheist by Reformed theologians of his day only because he rejected the
traditional proofs of the existence of God.16 By denying therefore that his philosophy
is an atheist philosophy and by claiming that his philosophy is ‘divine’, Spinoza may
simply mean that his philosophy does not weaken morality – despite the fact that, as
we shall see, he does deny the existence of God’s legislative will.
This makes the use of the expression ‘God’s decrees’ rather significant. In
connection with prophecy the expression ‘God’s decrees’ evidently means ‘God’s
commands’ (for example, the Ten Commandments); in connection with Spinoza’s
own philosophy, however, it would rather mean ‘laws of nature’, that is, explanatory
laws (even if these laws have a specific behavioural effect if they are known).17
Spinoza’s point therefore might simply be that ‘God’s decrees’ as they are
understood in his philosophy have the same relevance for the values people adopt as
‘God’s decrees’ as they are understood in revealed religion. Spinoza’s message
could be in other words that, by adopting his philosophy (by knowing the truth), one
becomes as morally perfect as one would be by heeding a prophet. Or even more
paradoxically, that by replacing the idea of a God–Lawgiver with that of
God–Nature – that is, precisely, by denying God’s legislative will – one achieves a
more perfect morality. Accordingly, the idea of Spinoza as the virtuous atheist,
which became so popular with Pierre Bayle (1647–1707), would already be
developed by Spinoza himself to show, not like Bayle that ‘men do not live in
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
4


accordance with their principles’, but that ‘atheism’, in the strictly theoretical
sense of ‘denying the existence of a Divine command’, entails ‘piety’ – in the
strictly practical sense of doing what is usually called ‘good’.18 In any case
Spinoza’s philosophy and revealed religion would be morally equivalent ceteris
paribus.
Confirmation of this can be found on the title page. The motto there is taken from
the First Epistle of John: ‘Hereby know we that we dwell in God and he in us,
because he hath given us of his Spirit’ (1 John 4:13). It is not the only time Spinoza
refers to this text. In chap. 14 he explains it, not as one would expect in a pantheistic
sense, but in the context of an analysis of ‘faith’ (fides).19 ‘Faith’ is ‘to have such
opinions [sentire] about God that, if they are ignored, there cannot be obedience to
God and that if this obedience is posited those beliefs are necessarily posited as
well’.20 So ‘faith’ is a necessary condition for moral behaviour (‘obedience to God’)
but, inversely, for a belief or sentiment (the word used is sentire, not cognoscere or
scire) to qualify as ‘faith’ it is sufficient that it produces ‘obedience to God’. Three
things are said to follow. First, ‘Faith does not bring salvation through itself, but
only by reason of obedience; or as James says (2:17), faith in itself without works is
dead.’21 So it is not belief as such (nor presumably the alleged truth of that belief)
that brings ‘salvation’ but only the extent to which it produces ‘good works’, that is,
moral behaviour. Second, ‘He who is truly obedient necessarily possesses faith; for,
as we have said, obedience being posited, faith is necessarily posited as well.’22 So
we can judge someone’s ‘faith’ simply by judging his behaviour. Third, ‘Faith
requires not so much true dogmas as pious dogmas, that is, such as move the heart
to obedience; and this is so even if many of those beliefs contain not a shadow of
truth, provided that he who adheres to them knows not that they are false.’23 So the
truth of faith is irrelevant provided the moral agent firmly believes in its truth. In fact
the same belief could be ‘faith’ as well as ‘unbelief’ (‘atheism’ ‘impiety’): ‘If
someone’s works are good, he is a believer [fidelis], however much his opinions
[dogmata] are different from those of other believers, whereas if his works are evil
he is an unbeliever.’24
Again, despite Spinoza’s remark that ‘it is so clear […] that it requires no
explanation’, this definition of ‘faith’ seems to be of the same order as those of
‘prophet’ and ‘prophecy’.25 Its aim one would say is to qualify a belief as faith only
to the precise extent that it produces moral behaviour. So there would be no socially
or politically relevant distinction between an ‘atheist’ like Spinoza and a traditional
Christian as long as the ‘atheist’ manages to live virtuously – indeed, given their
behaviour, many Christians would presumably have less ‘faith’ than the ‘atheist’.
Now Spinoza attributes this notion to St John, who in the passage quoted earlier
(1 John 4:7–8) by ‘spirit’ would mean love: ‘“Hereby,” [John] says, “we know that
we dwell in him and he in us, because he hath given us his spirit,” that is, love,
whence he concludes […] that he who has love truly has the spirit of God.’26 So ‘the
spirit of God’ would be with all those who love their neighbours, that is, all those
who do good works, regardless of their beliefs. For to have the ‘spirit of God’ it is
not necessary to know God: ‘Since nobody has seen God […] it is only through love
of one’s neighbour that one can perceive, or pay attention to, [sentire vel
animadvertere] God.’27 Whenever we love our neighbours we are aware of ‘God’
and heed his will even if neighbourly love neither requires nor provides knowledge
Introduction
5


(let alone true and certain knowledge) of ‘God’. Clearly, philosophy does not
coincide with faith even if on a practical level it is equivalent with faith.
Apparently, Spinoza wants us to read his book in the light of two claims: (a) when
judging what is traditionally called ‘faith’ what one should consider is not its
conformity with a given standard of truth (a philosophy or a given confession) but
the behaviour produced by it; (b) the behaviour produced by Spinoza’s own
philosophy is eminently moral and therefore either classifies as ‘faith’ or is
practically equivalent to faith. These are important claims, which, if it can be proved
that Spinoza makes them, would have far reaching consequences for the
interpretation of the Theologico-political treatise and perhaps of Spinoza’s
philosophy as a whole. They would mean, not only that one should be ‘tolerant’ of
any religious denomination only for as long as it entails moral behaviour, but also
that, given the fact that it does entail moral behaviour, Spinoza’s own philosophy
should be ‘tolerated’ on the same terms. The full title of Spinoza’s Treatise suggests
that, apart from those two claims, there may be a third one, even more challenging:
A theologico-political treatise, containing several dissertations wherein it is shown that the
freedom to philosophise can not only be granted without injury to piety and the peace of
the commonwealth, but that the peace of the commonwealth and even piety are
endangered by the suppression of that freedom.
First of all, the main theme of the ‘several dissertations’ – an expression worth
underlining28 – that compose this ‘theologico-political treatise’ is ‘the freedom to
philosophise’ (libertas philosophandi). Now most commentators interpret libertas
philosophandi as the general freedom to judge, to speak and to worship. But that
interpretation is implicitly rejected in the preface, where the freedom to speak, to
judge and to worship is taken for granted:
Since we have the rare good fortune to live in a commonwealth where freedom of
judgement is fully granted to the individual citizen and he may worship God as he pleases,
and where nothing is esteemed dearer and more precious than freedom, I think I am
undertaking no ungrateful or unprofitable task in demonstrating that not only can this
freedom be granted without endangering piety and the peace of the commonwealth but
also that the peace of the commonwealth and piety depend on this freedom.29
So the point might be that, given the fact that there is freedom to judge, to speak and
to worship, one should not make an exception for philosophy – even if it is ‘atheist’
or if some people think it is ‘atheist’.
In any case philosophari is ‘to do philosophy’, that is, a formal discipline
concerned with the search for truth. That at any rate is how Spinoza himself
generally uses those terms.30 The ordo philosophandi is the order in which
philosophical propositions are demonstrated.31 The ratio philosophandi is the
method of natural philosophy, and so on.32 More importantly, perhaps, if a
philosopher speaks of philosophy it is generally his own philosophy that he means.
So if Spinoza proclaims the necessity of libertas philosophandi, what he actually
means is that he and his followers should be free to know, and perhaps publish, the
truth as they understand it. Why? Because Spinoza’s philosophy is compatible with
peace and piety and because without it peace and piety are impossible. So the very
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
6


notion that there is no God–Lawgiver, which is the ground for accusing Spinoza of
atheism, would be not only compatible with peace and piety but indispensable for
peace and piety. Does that make any sense? It does with respect to peace. A follower
of Spinoza will understand that peace is necessary and therefore do nothing to
compromise it.33 So philosophy and peace are indeed compatible and one can even
see in what way philosophy could be indispensable: after all, if there were a
God–Lawgiver the sovereign should not follow his own advice but listen to
theologians (which would mean civil war and not peace).
It is less easy to make sense of Spinoza’s claims with respect to ‘piety’ (pietas).
Indeed, many contemporaries would allege that Spinoza’s very impiety is enough
reason to find him guilty of threatening the peace. Actually, we should take ‘piety’
(pietas) in a practical sense. For a 17th-century reader the practical connotations of
the word pietas – generally translated in the period as ‘holiness’ ‘godliness’ or, in
Dutch, ‘godzaligheid’ – would be evident anyway. Pietas is the Calvinist version of
‘good works’, except that the works are done, not to pacify God, but out of gratitude:
we obey the will of God because we are grateful for faith.34 In so far as pietas is
made possible by faith it is also the only visible sign of faith.35 And that is also the
main connotation in a Jewish context, except that there it is allied with ceremonies
and rituals.36 According to the Ethics, in turn, ‘pietas’ is ‘the desire to do good
produced by the fact that we live in accordance with reason’.37 So ‘piety’ would be
moral behaviour which becomes habitual for no other reason than that it reinforces
itself – that virtue is its own reward.38 A similar definition is given of religion:
‘whatever we do or desire by ourselves [cujus causa sumus] in so far as we have the
idea of God, that is, in so far as we know God counts as religion’ (ad religionem
refero).39 Religion is behaviour: it is things we do and desire. It could also be called
‘true life’ (vera vita),40 or indeed ‘Godsdienst’ – literally ‘service to God’, that is,
obedience to God, or a life in accordance with the laws of nature.41 So Spinoza can
make his point if it is shown, not only that on a moral level there is no distinction
between a Spinozist philosopher and someone living in accordance with the alleged
will of God, but also that the only way to acquire a truly habitual moral life is by
knowing the truth as he does.
All this would mean that the Theologico-political treatise is an oratio pro domo,
a special plea on behalf of Spinoza’s own philosophy, aimed at showing that, since
it produces nothing but the most impeccably moral behaviour, it does not undermine
the public order. In any case there would be as little reason to prohibit the
publication of Spinoza’s philosophy as to prevent an orthodox minister from
preaching the gospel, unless it could be shown that Spinoza’s philosophy entails
‘impiety’. If that is right, we can discard two alternative interpretations. First of all,
in the Theologico-political treatise Spinoza would not be working with a different
concept of truth so as to accommodate religion.42 Indeed, it is precisely by separating
religion from truth and turning it into moral behaviour that Spinoza manages to
present his own conception of truth and true knowledge as being eminently ‘pious’.
But we can also qualify a second interpretation, namely, that Spinoza’s ‘theological’
argument conceals an ‘esoteric’ argument which should not be divulged.43 Indeed, if
knowledge of truth is essential to establish peace and piety he would have no interest
in reserving it to those who are initiated already. Still, it cannot be denied that,
although much of the Treatise would be quite unintelligible without Spinoza’s own
Introduction
7


philosophy, Spinoza does very little to explain it even if it is undeniably and
unambiguously there. Thus, for example, Spinoza is very outspoken in his rejection
of Divine Law – which would be enough for most people to qualify his philosophy
as ‘atheist’ – but does very little to prove that God cannot have a legislative will.
In any case this interpretation raises a difficult problem, namely, why Spinoza
should be concerned with theology at all. That it is meant to conceal a different
argument is hardly likely; in any case the trick did not work.44 If we stick to the
interpretation outlined so far, one can think of two possible answers: (a) the reason
why Spinoza uses ‘theological’ arguments (that is, arguments borrowed from
Scripture) is that he wants to convince all those who believe in the authority of
Scripture; (b) the so-called ‘theological’ argument is in fact a ‘demonstration’ of the
freedom to philosophize, that is, the freedom to apply whatever we know to be true
in philosophy to the interpretation of Scripture. Something could be said for both
options, as we shall see in due course, but the question is almost certainly more
complicated. Indeed, the title of the Treatise announces a ‘theologico-political’
argument, and what does it mean for an argument to be ‘theologico-political’?
Presumably, a ‘theologico-political’ argument is a theological argument on politics
or, alternatively, a political argument based on Scripture. Many 16th- and
17th-century treatises on politics are ‘theologico-political’ in that sense. The last
chapter of Calvin’s Christian Institution, for example, is a ‘theologico-political’
treatise.45 But an example closer to home might be Hobbes’s Leviathan (1651), the
second half of which looks like a prototype of Spinoza’s ‘theologico-political
treatise’.46
In Leviathan, Hobbes is concerned with two different arguments on the relation
between the Church and the State, one deduced from the principles of nature (reason
or the natural word of God) and the other from an interpretation of Scripture (the
revealed word of God).47 The first argument is philosophical; the second theological,
even if probably no theologian ever came to share Hobbes’s conclusions.48 Hobbes’s
question is whether there can be a ‘Christian commonwealth’, that is, a
commonwealth based on ‘the word of God delivered by Prophets’.49 And that
question is raised, not because Hobbes would be attached to Christian theocracy, but
on the contrary because he is concerned with refuting it. However, to the extent that
no valid arguments for a Christian theocracy can be drawn from Scripture, there is
not only a theological argument but also an argument on theology, that is, on the way
Scripture should be interpreted. So Hobbes deals – as Spinoza would do after him –
with the question of ‘how God speaketh to men’ and ‘by what marks prophets are
known’ (chap. 32); with ‘the number, antiquity, scope, authority and interpreters of
the books of Holy Scripture’ (chap. 33) – naturally because no argument could be
based on Scripture if Scripture had no authority and because ‘authority’ is based on
that type of considerations; he makes a detailed inquiry into the meaning of terms
like ‘spirit’, ‘angel’, ‘inspiration’ (chap. 34), and ‘kingdom of God’, ‘holy’, ‘sacred’,
‘sacrament’ (chap. 35); he examines the concepts of ‘Word of God’ and ‘Prophet’
(chap. 36) and treats ‘of miracles and their use’ (chap. 37); he asks for the meaning
of the words ‘eternall life’, ‘hell’, ‘salvation’, ‘the world to come’, ‘redemption’
(chap. 38); he analyses the idea of ‘church’ (chap. 39), and so on. His argument
culminates, however, with two claims: that the only kingdom of God properly
speaking – that is, the only authentic theocracy – was that of the Hebrews (so there
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
8


can be no Christian commonwealth properly speaking); and that all ecclesiastical
power derives from the power of the sovereign (the Christian sovereign is no more
bound by a higher law than a heathen king). By the ‘kingdom of darkness’, on the
other hand, Hobbes means any argument used to give independent authority to
priests, ministers and theologians. And that includes, not only the papacy (chap. 44),
but also ‘vain philosophy and fabulous traditions’ or ‘Schoole-Divinity.’ So in his
own way – and it remains to be seen whether that could also be Spinoza’s – Hobbes
aims at separating philosophy and theology, relying for his claim, in a paradoxical
way, on a theological argument.
There can be no doubt that Spinoza knew this argument.50 In 1667 his friend
Abraham van Berckel (1639–86) had published a Dutch translation of Leviathan.51
The preface shows that Van Berckel’s main reason for publishing it was Hobbes’s
argument on theocracy, which he believed was highly relevant to the Dutch
situation. Ever since 1650, when William II mounted a coup d’état, which failed
only because of his sudden death,52 efforts to break the power of the States were
initiated, not only by the entourage of William II’s posthumous son William, but
also by the Church, which hoped that an Orange king would be more vigilant against
‘heresy’ (Socinianism) and ‘atheism’ (Cartesianism). Van Berckel uses Leviathan to
show that the Church as well as the army should be subject to the absolute power of
the sovereign – that is, to the States of Holland.
Hobbesian and ‘theologico-political’ arguments were also used (more or less with
the same aim) by another friend of Spinoza, Adriaen Koerbagh (1632/3–69).53
According to Koerbagh, ‘a sovereign government has the supreme authority, power,
and jurisdiction over religion, under God and after his holy word’.54 That is
confirmed not only by constitutional documents but also by Scripture: ‘Was it not
by means of Moses that God instituted religion, in spite of the fact that he was not a
priest?’55 And after Moses’ death it was Joshua, the captain of the army, who became
the leader of the nation instead of Eleazar, the priest.56 Like Van Berckel, Koerbagh
concludes that the Church should be subordinate to the only legitimate sovereign,
that is, to the States of Holland.57
Against this background it seems rather significant that the only specific
government analysed in the Theologico-political treatise is the theocracy of Israel.
It is certainly very paradoxical given Spinoza’s denial of there being a
God–Lawgiver. It suggests that his underlying problem may be the same as
Hobbes’s, namely, whether God’s revealed will can have any specific ‘authority’ at
all, independently of the sovereign’s. And as in Hobbes that problem can be
approached in two different ways: either by an analysis of the idea of sovereignty (as
conducted in Leviathan II and in chap. 16 of the Theologico-political treatise) or by
an examination of Scripture (as in Leviathan III and in chaps 17 and 18 of the
Theologico-political treatise).
Does it make sense to interpret Spinoza’s Theologico-political treatise as a
commentary on Hobbes or to see Part III of Leviathan as a prototype of Spinoza’s
Theologico-political treatise?58 There certainly is more than one analogy. An
important subject matter for both is theocracy, a subject on which Hobbes and
Spinoza tend to think in ambiguous terms. For if, at the end of the day, both reject
it, the reason is not that they find it intrinsically impossible or undesirable – after all,
both agree that Israel was truly governed – but that it is impossible now. And that
Introduction
9


means that, if a Christian theocracy is impossible, it is not because it is theocratic but
because it is Christian.59 Moreover, both argue for this position, not only on rational
grounds, but also on the basis of an analysis of Scripture, no doubt because those
who argue for the opposite position also make use of Scriptural arguments. Finally,
both are defenders of absolute government, although not without serious
qualifications if only because, ultimately, Hobbes as well as Spinoza believe that,
irrespective of his power, a sovereign would inevitably lose all the authority he
enjoys if he ignored the thoughts and feelings of his subjects.
But the relation between Spinoza and Hobbes may be more indirect in the sense
that Spinoza reacts to the importance attached to Leviathan by friends like Van
Berckel and Koerbagh. Indeed, their example reminds us that, despite all the
differences between England and the United Provinces, Leviathan was extremely
pertinent to the Dutch situation, especially as it was seen from the Province of
Holland. The Union of Utrecht (1579), followed by the Act of Abjuration (1589),
had called into being an ill-defined federation (or perhaps – nobody knew – a
confederation) of seven nominally autonomous republics, which however were
actually dominated by Holland: indeed, it seems correct to think of the other
provinces as satellites of Holland rather than partners, if only because 60 per cent of
the military budget came from that province. The Prince of Orange on the other
hand, who was ‘stadholder’ (viceroy) in each of the provinces separately, was also
captain of the federal army and in that role could easily use his power against
Holland, as happened in 1619 and in 1650, and again in 1672. The Church finally,
as a supraprovincial institution, could easily channel the resentment of the other
provinces and lend support to the dynastic aspirations of the Orange family in
exchange for support against what she saw as heresy and atheism, as happened in
1619, when the intervention of Maurice of Nassau helped the Counter-Remonstrants
to their victory. In fact, something similar threatened to happen in the 1660s as a
consequence of the debate on the public prayer – and did actually happen in 1672
(known as ‘year of disaster’) when, with the help of the Church and the people,
William III seized power.60
Again Hobbes’s Leviathan contains many a lesson relevant to that situation even
if its message could be interpreted in different ways: should there be an absolute
sovereign (someone like William III) or was there in fact a sovereign (the
‘Provincial States’), whose only problem was that they had no grip either on the
Church or on the army? In any case, Hobbes had introduced a certain number of
concepts which allowed people to discuss those problems even if his solutions could
not be fully applied in the Dutch situation.
In sum, the Theologico-political treatise seems to reflect a variety of themes and
motives. It may count as a vindication of Spinoza’s philosophy or any philosophy
like Spinoza’s – Koerbagh’s, for example, who less than a year before died in the
Amsterdam Rasphuis. It is probably also a commentary on Hobbes’s Leviathan, or,
rather, a critical reaction to whatever use was made of it in Spinoza’s environment.
And it also contains a discussion of the political situation in the Netherlands. As a
result the programme developed in the Theologico-political treatise is highly
complex. For, in so far as it is a vindication of Spinoza’s own philosophy, it is
concerned with defending truth without providing the opportunity to explain it. In so
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
10


far as it deploys a political theory, it is, as we shall see in more detail, a defence of
strong authority, which is mitigated only by the consideration that a sovereign can
keep his authority only if he does not hurt and frustrate his subjects in their deepest
feelings – and those feelings were not in favour of Spinoza’s position. In so far as
Spinoza acknowledges the double role of revelation as an instrument to forge social
cohesion and as a factor of social and political antagonism, his conclusions can
hardly be anything but ambiguous. And finally, in so far as the Treatise reflects
Spinoza’s predicament as a Dutch citizen with a deep concern for freedom but also
with a great and overwhelming fear of social and political turmoil, it is not surprising
that his conclusions are hesitant. Still, many problems can be solved and many
ambiguities explained by concentrating on two conclusions the author of the Ethics
is led to draw with respect to God, one negative and the other positive: God cannot
be a lawgiver; and the very essence of God is power – a power unmitigated by
goodness, wisdom, providence and so on. The first claim implies, positively, that all
authority in the proper sense of the word is of human origin and, negatively, that all
non-political ‘authority’ (of the Church, of Scripture, of theology, and so on) should
be reinterpreted. The second claim, on the other hand, entails a definition of right as
power – a power that is mitigated only by the fact that those who enjoy it are
essentially finite.

boldly’ (Karl Schuhmann, Hobbes: Une chronique, Paris: Vrin, 1998, p. 206).

sed cujus nomen adhuc premendum est silentio’. See H.W. Blom and J.M. Kerkhoven,
