Spinozana, 1 (1985), 371–8.
59
See below, pp. 140–41.
60
In March 1663 the States of Holland decreed that, in their public prayer, ministers should
first pray for the States of their own province, then for those of the other provinces and
finally for the States General and the State Council (but not for the Prince of Orange,
who, as far as the States were concerned, was no more than a private individual); cf.
Jonathan Israel, The Dutch Republic, pp. 760–63. The link with the events of 1619 was
explicitly made in several pamphlets, especially Gulden Legende van den Nieuwen St
Jan, dat is: Kort Verhael vande Edeldom […] van Meester Jan van Barnevelt, Gedruckt
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
14


anno 1663 (Knuttel, 8975–6); Den verresenen Barnevelt betabbert met alle syne
Politycke Maximen, Zierickzee: Voor Pieter de Schryver, In ’t Jaer onses Heeren, 1663
(Knuttel, 8797–8), a pamphlet attributed to Voetius. The suggestion that all these
pamphlets come from Utrecht is made in Hollands Nieuw Jaar gezonden aan den Heere
Officier van Utrecht, Gedruckt in het vrije Holland voor de vrije Patriotten, 1664
(Knuttel, 8916). On the events of 1672, see Israel, Dutch Republic, pp. 796–806.
Introduction
15


Chapter 1
Religion and Truth
‘If you ask me what truth is apart from a true idea, you might as well ask what
whiteness is apart from a white body.’1 Truth is the property of an idea true and
known to be true. A defence of ‘truth’ or of ‘knowledge of truth’ is a defence of
specific claims and ideas true and known to be true. The first question to be asked
therefore is what known truths Spinoza has in mind when he reassures his readers
on the freedom to philosophize. Many come to mind but, as was indicated earlier,
Spinoza’s claims make sense only if we concentrate on the notion of a
God–Lawgiver. For the falsity of that notion entails (a) that all revelation (prophecy)
is false, (b) that all authority is of human origin, and (c) that the use of ‘authority’
outside a strictly political context (as in ‘the authority of Scripture’) is meaningless.
In fact, the non-existence of a God–Lawgiver is a basic presupposition of the
Theologico-political treatise and possibly the main point of Spinoza’s philosophy as
a whole. Since on the other hand the very essence of ‘atheism’ and ‘impiety’ consists
in the rejection of God’s legislative will, Spinoza’s ultimate claim would be that we
can behave ‘morally’ only if we know that there is no God–Lawgiver. We shall first
examine the idea of a divine legislative will, showing that, according to Spinoza, it
is radically false; then discuss two notions that seem to defy this conclusion, namely,
‘superstition’ and ‘universal faith’, and finally show why, although Spinoza finds it
necessary to demonstrate that there is no ‘Divine legislative will’, he also somehow
respects that notion.
‘The Will of God’
More than any other thing the distinction between an architectonic will of God (God
as creator) and a legislative or providential will (God as King or as Father), and
correspondingly that between natural theology and revealed theology, is
fundamental to the Judaeo-Christian tradition. The Dutch Confession formulates it
as follows:
We know him [God] by two means: First, by the creation, preservation, and government
of the universe, since that universe is before our eyes like a beautiful book in which all
creatures, great and small, are as letters to make us ponder the invisible things of God: his
eternal power and his divinity, as the apostle Paul says in Romans 1:20. All these things
are enough to convict men and to leave them without excuse. Second, he makes himself
known to us more openly by his holy and divine Word, as much as we need in this life, for
his glory and for the salvation of his own.2
The idea of a twofold revelation is of course commonplace: God reveals himself in
the Book of nature as well as in the Book of books.3 The Book of nature can be read
17


and understood by anybody and is the source of a ‘natural’ theology; Scripture can
be understood by those who benefit from God’s particular grace and is the source of
‘revealed’ theology. Although both qualify as ‘revelation’ in the broad sense that
they give us knowledge of God, they do not carry the same contents, nor are they
absolutely equivalent. What can be known of God through nature is, as Paul says,
his power and godhead (Rom. 1:20), that is, God as the omnipotent cause of all finite
reality; what is revealed in Scripture on the other hand is, not only an eschatological
perspective, but also the precise contents of God’s legislative will. Someone who
knows nature has an awareness of the moral law and therefore cannot be absolutely
guiltless, but someone who knows and understands Scripture knows God’s will to
perfection and if he has the gift of faith will do everything he can to perform it: the
heathens ‘are without excuse’ (Rom. 1:20; 2:1) but it is only ‘the just [that] shall live
by faith’ (Rom. 1:17). Let us see what Spinoza makes of that.
According to Spinoza the will belongs to natura naturata, not to natura naturans;
to God as effect, not to God as cause.4 The will is not an attribute but a modus, so,
like all modi, ‘can exist and be determined to work only by a cause, which in
turn presupposes another cause, and so on’.5 In classically nominalist fashion,
Spinoza rejects the existence of faculties to stop this infinite regress: ‘The intellect
and the will relate to this or that concept (idea) or to this or that volition as
“Stone” to this or that stone or “Man” to Peter and Paul.’6 The will is at best an ens
rationis, just as the distinction between the will and the intellect is at best a distinctio
rationis. But an ens rationis cannot be a cause, let alone a free cause.7 The idea
therefore that at the origin of a ‘free’ act we find an equally free act of the will is
absurd.
Since this is true of any will, finite or infinite, it also affects the concept of God,
to whom traditionally an infinite intellect (‘God is omniscient’) and an infinite will
(‘God is omnipotent’) are attributed. According to Spinoza, however, God does not
act from the freedom of his will but is free only in the sense that he ‘exists in virtue
of his own nature and is moved to act only by himself’.8 In other words God is ‘free’
only in the sense that nothing outside him could ever cause him either to be or to act:
‘Whatever is is in God and can neither be nor be conceived without God.’9 God’s
‘actions’ – that is the whole of the natural world – are determined only by God’s own
nature, not ‘freely’ (in the traditional sense of that word), but necessarily.10 As a
result, things ‘could be produced neither in a different way nor in a different order’.11
They are not freely ‘produced’ or ‘created’ by God but ‘follow’ (sequi) from the
necessity of a divine nature.12 The relation between God and things created is that of
a thing and its properties, of the antecedens and the consequens of an argument, of
the axioms and definitions and the theorems of Euclid, and so on.13 For all their
variety natural things are, like nature as a whole, ‘determined to be and to act in a
determinate way’.14 In sum, possibility and contingency do not characterize things
or events in themselves but are signs of ‘a defective intellect’.15
According to Christian theology God created the world ‘from nothing’, that is, he
freely called into being something different from himself, without losing any reality
of his own or making use of any sort of pre-existing reality (‘matter’). Traditionally,
that entails a difference in perfection between God and the world: the very fact that
the world is created would make it less perfect than its creator, the uncreated first
cause which alone is causa sui.16 According to Spinoza, however, being ‘caused’
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
18


does not make a thing necessarily less perfect than its cause, especially if the cause
is itself perfect. For ‘if something is limited by its cause, then the reason must be that
this [the cause] was either unable or unwilling to yield more.’17 One of Spinoza’s
axioms – although as far as I know he never explicitly formulates it – is that a cause
necessarily yields its maximum effect unless it is prevented from doing so either by
its own finite constitution or by something else.18 Given the perfection of ‘God’, the
one is as radically impossible as the other: ‘That he would be unable is contrary to
his omnipotence and that he would be able but unwilling smacks of envy, which is
not in any way in God, who is all goodness and fullness.’19 In sum, the idea of a
world both ‘caused’ and ‘infinitely perfect’ is not contradictory at all as long as the
cause of the world is itself infinitely perfect.
That seems to rule out the idea of ‘creation’. According to Spinoza, however, it
does make sense to distinguish between ordinary causation and creation. Whereas
ordinary causation can be seen as the process by which ‘existence’ is given to an
‘essence’ – that is, to something possible – ‘creation’ is the ‘production of a thing
both in its essence and its existence’ (quo ad essentiam et existentiam simul).20 The
emphasis is on ‘both’ or ‘simultaneously’ (simul): in the case of ‘creation’ essence,
the possibility of a thing, and existence, the actuality of a thing, are produced ‘at the
same time’. ‘Creation’ is not a process by which pre-existing essences are
instantiated or pre-existing possibilities realized but a simultaneous production of
essence and existence. That excludes all finality: God does not choose (as he does
according to Leibniz, for example) the best out of all the worlds he knows to be
possible but the world as it is (‘God’ as effect) exists merely because it can exist,
that is, because its possibility follows from there being an infinite ‘perfection’
(‘God’ as cause). Being is power to exist, infinite and inexhaustible in the case of
the infinitely perfect being called ‘God’, finite and easily spent in the case of finite
things.21 In sum the world is whatever it is simply because it is possible.
This absence of finality has radical consequences for all notions involving norms
and values. ‘Good’ and ‘evil’, ‘beautiful’ and ‘ugly’, ‘order’ and ‘disorder’ are entia,
not even rationis, but imaginationis: they are words and notions that may be useful
in a context of rhetorical persuasion but not in that of reason and truth.22
Nonetheless, Spinoza claims that things are produced ‘with the greatest
perfection’.23 This seems to be inconsistent with his rejection of finality and, indeed,
in so far as ‘perfection’ is used as a normative concept, Spinoza explicitly rejects it.24
His official definition of ‘perfection’, given only in Pt II of the Ethics but
extensively used in Pt I, is ‘reality’ (realitas).25 And since reality admits of different
degrees it does make sense to speak of things as being more, or less, ‘perfect’ than
others without committing oneself to a moral or aesthetic norm.26 For a thing to be
more, or less, perfect than another thing means that it has more, or less, reality than
that other thing; that is, that it has more, or less, power to exist. Accordingly, to say
of the world (or a thing) that it is ‘perfect’ in itself is to affirm that it has all ‘reality’
it needs; that, being the necessary product of an absolutely ‘perfect’ cause, it could
not be different from what it is; that there is nothing it should or could be without
being it. Consequently, the very ‘perfection’ of the world and of its cause entails the
absence, not only of finality and choice, but also of ‘values’ and ‘norms’, that is, of
rules and principles to which things – and for ‘things’ read ‘humans’ – should or
could conform:
Religion and Truth
19


But, it will be objected, in things themselves there is no perfection or imperfection; what
makes them perfect or imperfect and the reason why they are called ‘good’ or ‘evil’ has
only to do with God’s will. If God had willed, he could have made that what is now
perfection is imperfection and the other way round. But what else can that mean than
that God, who necessarily understands whatever he wills, can by his will manage to
understand things in a different way from how he actually understands them – which is
absurd.27
The argument is that perfection is mediated by God’s will or in other words that one
becomes more perfect by obeying God’s will, which in turn, being absolutely free,
could be different from what it actually is. Perfection would ultimately depend on an
arbitrary decision of God, who freely fixes the norms, men being more perfect
according as they freely decide to live in accordance with them. Spinoza’s counter-
argument is that God cannot ‘will to conceive’ something in a way different from
what he does. Indeed, in a being absolutely perfect there must be identity of
‘conceiving’ and ‘willing’. So to say of God that he wills a mathematical theorem to
be true is the same as to say of God that he conceives it to be true; to say of God that
he conceives something to be the case is the same as to say of him that he wills it to
be the case.28 However, the claim that God has a legislative will implies that it is
possible for God to will and conceive a thing in two different ways: as it actually is
(as God actually wills it and causes it to be) and as it should be (as God willed and
conceived it without causing it – as it ‘should’ be without being it). And that is
absurd because for God there is only one way to will and conceive a thing, namely,
as it actually is and exists (that is, as it must be), and accordingly only one way for
a thing to act, namely, as it actually does – all of which is as necessarily willed by
God as it is conceived. As a result, the notion of a God–Lawgiver, which implies that
God can conceive something without causing it, is contradictory. If ‘God’ is the
name of an infinitely perfect being, a God–Lawgiver cannot be conceived and
therefore cannot exist: ‘Nobody will perceive rightly the things I maintain unless he
takes great care not to confuse God’s power with human power or with the right of
kings.’29
Such is the theory of the Ethics. There is no ground for assuming that the view
adopted in the Theologico-political treatise is different. On the contrary, the same
theory is upheld with much the same arguments: ‘Whatever occurs does so through
God’s will and eternal decree […] all that happens does so in accordance with laws
and rules involving eternal necessity and truth.’30 Expressions like ‘divine law’ and
‘natural law’ are at best metaphors.31 Whatever is attributed to God’s legislative and
providential will – that is, whatever is attributed to God by revealed theology –
should be interpreted as manifestations of the uniformity of nature:
By God’s guidance [directio] I mean the fixed and immutable order of nature or the chain
of natural events […] Since the power [potentia] of nature is nothing other than the power
of God through which alone all things happen and are determined it follows that whatever
man – who is part of nature – acquires for himself to help preserve his own being or
whatever nature provides for him without any effort on his part all this is provided for him
solely by God’s power, acting either through human nature or externally to human nature.
Therefore whatever human nature can effect solely by its own power to preserve its own
being can rightly be called God’s internal help and whatever falls to man’s advantage from
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
20


the power of external causes can rightly be called God’s external help. And from this, too,
can readily be deduced what must be meant by God’s election [electio], for since no one
acts except by the predetermined order of nature – that is, from God’s eternal government
and decree – it follows that no one chooses a way of life for himself or accomplishes
anything except by special vocation of God, who has chosen one man before others for a
particular work or a particular way of life.32
By reducing notions like ‘government’, ‘vocation’ and ‘election’ – all expressions
of God’s allegedly providential and legislative will – to manifestations of a uniform
and impersonal natural law, Spinoza makes it clear that, if those terms have any
meaning at all, they must refer to nature. That is also true of the idea of God as a
Lawgiver. In the Ethics Spinoza’s argument was that in an infinitely perfect being
the will and the intellect are identical. The argument in the Theologico-political
treatise is exactly the same: ‘In respect of God our affirmation is one and the same,
whether we say that God has eternally willed and decreed that the three angles of a
triangle should be equal to two right ones or that God has understood it.’33
As if to make his conclusion even more obvious, Spinoza illustrates it with an
analysis of the Fall: if it were God’s will that Adam should not eat from the tree ‘it
would have been a contradiction if Adam had been able to do so’.34 It is impossible
for God to will Adam not to eat from the tree without preventing him from eating;
inversely, if in fact Adam did eat, that was precisely what God willed. The only way
to save God from contradiction is to say that God’s legislature existed only in
Adam’s mind. What Adam held to be a command was in fact a warning (presumably
like the injunction of a family doctor that we ‘must not’ or ‘should not’ eat or smoke
or drink); that the only reason why Adam saw that as a command is that he did not
know the natural law on which it was based:
Since Scripture tells us that God did so command Adam and that Adam nevertheless ate
of the tree, it must be accepted that God revealed to Adam only the punishment he must
incur if he should eat of the tree; but the necessary entailment of that punishment was not
revealed. Consequently, Adam perceived this revelation not as an eternal and necessary
truth but as a law, that is to say, as an enactment from which good or ill consequence
would ensue not from the intrinsic nature of the deed performed but only from the will and
absolute power of some ruler. Therefore that solely in relation to Adam and solely because
of the limitations of his knowledge revelation was a law and God a kind of lawgiver or ruler
[emphasis added].35
What made Adam ‘sin’ (eat of the tree) was a uniform causal and intelligible law,
not a ‘free’ decision to ignore a command. What made Adam believe that there was
a command was his lack of knowledge: if he had known the truth he would have
acted differently. Finally, the law that made Adam eat also made it necessary for him
to be ‘punished’ (presumably in the way that we could say that overeating is
‘punished’ by indigestion). The words of Scripture should therefore be reinterpreted.
Scripture presents God’s word as a command: ‘The Lord God commanded the
man …’ (Gen. 2:6).36 But, according to Spinoza, that cannot be the true meaning
given the fact that it cannot be true. God’s words must actually mean something like:
‘Eating of that tree will cause death – and therefore I advise you not to eat.’ But
because Adam did not know the natural law on which that advice was based the only
Religion and Truth
21


way for him to make sense of what ultimately happened was to see it as the effect of
God’s free decision (institutum), that is, as the ‘punishment’ of his ‘crime’.
However, as Spinoza concludes many chapters later, in actual fact ‘God cannot be
conceived as a king or a lawgiver who prescribes laws to man.’37
A similar analysis applies to the Decalogue. To the Israelites the Ten Command-
ments were laws properly speaking (positive laws) ‘but if God had spoken to them
directly, employing no physical means, they would have perceived this not as a law
but as an eternal truth’.38 So if the Israelites had used their reason (‘if God had
spoken to them directly’) instead of relying on their miraculous interpretation of
natural phenomena (‘physical means’) they would have understood the Law as an
eternal truth, which can be seen as a command and therefore obeyed only to the
precise extent that it is not understood. So God was not really the King of Israel but
the Israelites could see him as their king because, being ignorant about nature, they
could not account for their law in a different way: ‘It is in concession to the
understanding of the multitude and the defectiveness of their thought that God is
described as a lawgiver or ruler and that he is called just, merciful, and so on.’39
To believe in a God–Lawgiver is an effect of the imagination, which as the
Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect explains in great detail is livelier when
the mind has less knowledge: ‘Less people know about nature, the more easily they
fantasise about speaking trees, men changing into stones or fountains, spectres
appearing in mirrors, nothing becoming something, gods changing into animals
or into men, etc.’40 The ability to imagine a God–Lawgiver – and as a result to
imagine a ‘divine law’ – disappears under the action of intellectual knowledge,
which teaches us that the only ‘divine’ law is the ‘law’ of nature, that is, a causal
law. Even so Spinoza believes there are things Adam ‘should’ have done, not in
the sense that he was obliged to do them, but in the sense that he would be better
advised if he did them, meaning that they would have made him ‘more perfect’ or
given him ‘more reality’. Nobody is obliged to give up smoking or drinking but
someone who does is better advised, supposedly, than someone who does not.
Indeed, it gives one ‘more power to exist’, that is, it improves one’s chances for
survival, and someone who understands that connection does not need to be obliged
to act accordingly.41
Similarly, if we live in the state of nature nobody can oblige us to live in peace
with our neighbours but if we believe that there is a good chance of making peace
we will do whatever we can to achieve it, seeing that peace would make us ‘more
perfect’. But a lifestyle could also be imposed, by a doctor, by an educator, by a
sovereign, and, supposedly, must be imposed on persons who do not understand the
connection between a particular lifestyle and a greater ‘perfection’. So if we assume
that the point of a ‘moral code’ is to make us ‘more perfect’ there is no contradiction
in believing that ‘moral behaviour’ can be produced in two different ways: by an
explanatory law which, if known, produces ‘moral’ behaviour; and by a real or
imagined command which, if the person (or God) issuing it is respected or loved,
produces the same behaviour. Although therefore a ‘moral code’ can be effective in
various ways – either as a command (in which case it is not understood) or as an
eternal truth (in which case it cannot be obeyed) – it can be imposed only by human
authority, given the fact that the existence of a God–Lawgiver is imaginary. As a
result the pragmatic equivalence of revelation (prophecy) and philosophy (true
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
22


knowledge), which I commented on in the Introduction, is mediated by a positive
law issued by an earthly sovereign.
We can lay down a twofold principle: (a) a view of the world based on the idea of
a God–Lawgiver (a religious view of the world) and true knowledge of reality (a
philosophical view of the world) are incompatible on a speculative level (because we
can see God as a Lawgiver only in so far as we do not truly understand reality); (b)
against the background of a positive law a religious view of the world and true
philosophy are perfectly equivalent on a practical level. In any case, in so far as
revealed religion presupposes the idea of a God–Lawgiver, it is radically and
irremediably false; in so far as obedience is due it is to a positive law.
How relevant would all that be? After all, Spinoza finds the truth or falsehood of
‘faith’ irrelevant as long as it produces ‘moral behaviour’.42 And that would mean
that, from a practical point of view, it does not really matter that revealed religion
and faith are false. That is true. Still, it should be underscored that, even at a
behavioural level, philosophy and faith are not absolutely equivalent. Revealed
beliefs are based on the imagination, which is intrinsically uncertain, whereas true
knowledge is absolutely certain, so in theory at least the behaviour produced by
philosophy would be more reliable and more firm than that based on revealed belief.
Moreover, the source of our moral values is not a matter of indifference. If there
actually is a God–Lawgiver no human has the right to impose a law without God’s
consent and no law should be contrary to God’s law as it is ‘known’ through
prophecy – so the authority of any sovereign could be contested in the name of God
and revelation. Inversely, if there is no God–Lawgiver we should ‘tolerate’ revealed
religion only to the extent that its moral commands agree with those of the highest
authority, that is, the sovereign, and we should ignore any allegedly ‘divine’
command unless it is endorsed by the sovereign. Or in other words, if there were a
God–Lawgiver, ‘moral obedience’ would be obedience to God as his will is known
through prophecy; but if there is no God–Lawgiver, ‘moral obedience’ coincides
with civil obedience. Accordingly, we must pursue the question of the truth of
religious beliefs a little further, so as to eliminate every doubt.
An objection based on Spinoza’s work could be that, even if we know that a belief
of the imagination is false, it is still possible for us somehow to adhere to it. The
example given by Spinoza himself is the perception of the sun: even if we exactly
and certainly know the distance that separates the earth from the sun we continue to
see (‘imagine’) it at a distance of 200ft.43 According to Spinoza, however, the reason
why we see the sun at 200ft is not that we ignore the truth but that ‘an affection of
our body involves the being of the sun in so far as that body is affected by it’.44 So
what causes us to see the sun at 200ft is our body, which is not to any extent changed
by what we truly and certainly know about the sun. Regardless of what we know or
ignore about the sun, this heavenly body makes a specific impression on our nervous
system, which necessarily and inevitably causes us to have a particular sensation.
The same is true of other sensations, all of which reflect a condition of the body as
it is changed and affected by its environment. However much we know about that
environment, neither its effect on our body nor our perception of our body will be
affected by that knowledge. Could a similar argument be used in defence of a
God–Lawgiver? Would the image of a God–Lawgiver be an inevitable illusion? It
seems not. God is not given to the imagination (like a material body) but produced
Religion and Truth
23


by the imagination (like the hero of a novel) – indeed, God does not make an
impression on the body (the nervous system). Whatever we know or believe about
him is the result of demonstration and argument. Therefore as soon as we know the
true nature of ‘God’, that is, as soon as we have a true and intellectual idea of God,
it becomes impossible for us to imagine God, let alone to imagine him as a lawgiver.
As a result the only way in which the image of a God–Lawgiver can coexist with the
true idea of God is that we remember how we imagined God before we knew him.45
But there are more fundamental objections.
True and False Belief
It might also be argued that according to Spinoza it is impossible to have any
radically false ideas (if only because all ideas are ideas in the divine mind), and
therefore to have any radically false beliefs (given the fact that a belief is nothing
but an idea or set of ideas). In the Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect,
Spinoza makes a distinction between four types of knowledge, based on,
respectively:
I. The perception we have from report or from some conventional sign. II. The perception
we have from random experience. III. The perception we have when the essence of a thing
is inferred from another thing but not adequately. IV. The perception we have when a thing
is perceived through its essence alone or through knowledge of its proximate cause.46
Similarly, in the Ethics Spinoza speaks of knowledge of the first (opinion or
imagination), second (reason) and third kind (intuitive science). This suggests that
one and the same truth can be known (or at least believed to be true) in three different
ways: by hearsay, by experience and reason, and by ‘intuition’, which in turn would
make it possible that something known in philosophy (by means of the second kind
of knowledge) could also be known by means of prophecy (through the first kind of
knowledge).47 Other texts suggest that all ideas (including those of the human mind)
are God’s; so given, supposedly, that God’s ideas are true, human ideas must also be
true and in any case could not be radically false.48 There must be some truth in any
idea, including, one would say, the idea of a God–Lawgiver.49
According to the Ethics all ideas are true ‘in so far as they are referred to God’
(quatenus ad Deum referuntur).50 But what does ‘to be referred to God’ mean?
Indeed, would it be possible for an idea not ‘to be referred to God’ if all ideas are in
God’s mind?51 Spinoza’s demonstration relies on an earlier proved corollary: ‘God’s
power to think is equal to his actual power to act. In other words, whatever formally
follows from God’s nature also follows objectively in God from God’s idea, in the
same order and the same connection.’52 In the first phrase we find a reaffirmation of
Spinoza’s principle of the identity of the real and the conceivable or, in other words,
of the impossibility to distinguish between God’s will and his intellect. ‘Whatever
formally follows from God’s nature’ is ‘the totality of natural things and events’
taken in themselves (formaliter), which however exist also ‘objectively’ (objective):
that is, as objects of thought. So if a thing follows from God’s being its idea must
follow from God’s idea – the (causal) order displayed by natural things and events
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
24


is reflected by the (logical and deductive) order of their ideas, both being ultimately
grounded in God or in the idea of God.53 So if Spinoza claims that all ideas are true
‘in so far as they are referred to God’, and demonstrates it by alleging this corollary,
what he means may be no more than that in so far as an idea conceives a thing as
part of a causal chain (which in turn is explained by God) it must be true; and,
inversely, that if an idea fails to do so it is false. Accordingly, all it means would
be that the truth and certainty of an idea ultimately derive from its being integrated
in a network of ideas, which is ultimately explained by ‘the idea of God’, just
as things are whatever they are for being part of a total reality called ‘nature’ or
‘God’.
The same idea seems to be present, albeit in a slightly different way, in the
immediately following proposition, which claims that ‘ideas contain nothing
positive that could make them false’.54 So if an idea is false (note that an idea can be
false) it would not be for whatever it is ‘positively’. The demonstration takes the
form of a reductio ad absurdum: let us try to conceive, ‘if we can’, a ‘manner of
thinking’ (modus cogitationis) that would constitute a ‘form’ (forma) of error or
falsehood. So we must try to actually form a false idea that is intrinsically false, that
is, the idea of something that cannot exist. But the reason why something cannot
exist is that it cannot be conceived. So to form a false idea would be to conceive
something that cannot be conceived, which is plainly absurd: the idea of something
inconceivable is itself inconceivable. Accordingly, a false idea cannot be false
because of what it actually is – that is, because of whatever is actually conceived in
it – but only because of what it is not: that is, because of whatever is not actually
conceived in it.55
This interpretation is by no means the only one possible, given the uncertain
meaning of the word ‘positive’ even in Spinoza himself.56 Here is, for example, the
first proposition of Pt IV of the Ethics: ‘Nothing positive which a false idea has is
removed by the presence of something true in so far as it is true.’57 The claim is an
essential part of Spinoza’s (Cartesian) theory of the passions, according to which a
passion can be counteracted only by a different passion: if I feel depressed the fact
that I know that it is wrong to feel depressed will not bring me in a different state
because my feeling is nothing but the perception of a bodily state on which my
thought has no influence. So the general meaning of the proposition – and the
general basis on which Pt IV of the Ethics is constructed – is that to the extent that
my ideas are nothing but perceptions of my body, my intellect has little or no
influence upon them. This suggests that ‘positive’ could mean ‘causally necessary’,
‘naturally determined’, ‘independent from the will’, and so on. So Spinoza’s claim
here may be analogous to that on the perception of the sun. Just as there is nothing
erroneous in having a sensation of the sun as if it were at 200ft as long as one does
not judge that it actually is at 200ft we might say of any idea that in so far as it is no
more than the perception of a bodily state, it cannot be false, or inversely that an idea
can be false only in so far as it is not only a reflection of the body but also a concept,
or a judgment. If I hallucinate a red object my sensation of something red can be said
to be ‘positive’ to the precise extent that, given the laws of nature, I cannot avoid
having the sensation of a red object. To that extent, too, my hallucination could even
be called ‘true’, if only because a physician could take it as evidence about the
condition of my body. It is not true of course in so far as I judge or think that there
Religion and Truth
25


actually is a red object in front of me. Indeed, to that extent it is a thought which,
besides, is false. In sum, Spinoza’s point could simply be that the body is always
‘right’, or indeed that ‘it is minds that are said to be wrong and to make mistakes,
not bodies’.58
In the end we have two possible meanings of ‘positive’ and therefore two possible
interpretations of the claim that ‘ideas contain nothing that could make them false’.
An idea could be called ‘positive’ to the extent either that something is actually
conceived in it (without being imagined) or that it actually reflects the state of the
body (without there being anything conceived or judged). So if we are dealing with
the idea of God the question would be whether the idea of a God–Lawgiver is
‘positive’ in either of these two senses. Again, it seems not. To be positive or to
contain something positive in the first sense, the idea of a God–Lawgiver should be
the result of an act of conceiving, whereas Spinoza’s point is precisely that a
God–Lawgiver cannot actually be conceived. To be positive in the second sense it
should reflect the state of the body in the way a sensation does, whereas Spinoza’s
point is precisely that the idea of a God–Lawgiver is produced by, instead of given
to, the imagination.
In fact Spinoza believes that the idea of a God–Lawgiver is a cosmic projection
of our own supposed freedom.59 As a result it would be as ‘positive’ as the idea of
freedom, which, however, is nothing but the empty notion of an action without a
cause.60 There is no idea of freedom: if people say ‘that human actions depend on
the will, that is nothing but words of which they have no idea’.61 Similarly, there is
no ‘idea’ of a God–Lawgiver: it is not just a false idea, it is no idea at all. I can say
(utter the phrase) that there is a God–Lawgiver (as I can say that it is possible to
square a circle) but since what I say can be neither conceived nor seen my words are
completely meaningless.62
I conclude that according to Spinoza the notion of a God–Lawgiver is either
totally and radically false or completely meaningless, and by implication that to the
extent that revelation rests on that idea it is also either totally and radically false, or
devoid of meaning. These conclusions seem inescapable. Still, there are two grounds
for challenging them: (a) Spinoza shows himself concerned with superstition – and
the very fact that something is called ‘superstition’ seems to imply that there is also
‘true religion’; (b) in chap. 14, Spinoza claims truth for what he calls ‘universal
faith’.
Religion and Superstition
On the basis of Spinoza’s general principles, ‘religion’, that is, ‘moral’ behaviour
can have three different causes: a positive law, true philosophy and ‘the will of God’
– the belief that there is a God–Lawgiver. From the outside the behaviour of the
faithful, that of the loyal citizen and that of the true philosopher are more or less
identical. This means that knowledge of truth is sufficient but not necessary to
produce moral behaviour, that knowledge of truth may be necessary to determine
what is ‘moral behaviour’, and that moral behaviour can be motivated by a false
belief, namely, that there is a God who orders us to live in accordance with the rules
he revealed through a prophet.
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
26


In any case, the only requirement of faith is piety, that is, the capacity to produce
moral behaviour: ‘A man’s beliefs should be regarded as pious or impious in so far
as he is thereby induced to obey the moral law.’63 It is not contradictory for a true
belief to be impious, any more than it is for a false idea to be pious: ‘If anyone by
believing what is true becomes self-willed, he has a faith which in reality is impious;
and if by believing what is false he becomes obedient to a moral law, he has a faith
which is pious.’64 In sum, the question of the objective truth of faith is immaterial
‘provided that he who adheres to it knows not that it is false’.65 How can we
distinguish ‘true’ faith from superstition if both are false? And is there any point in
making that distinction if it does not matter that faith is false?
A solution could be that ‘true religion’ or ‘true faith’ is somewhere halfway
between superstition and philosophy: ‘Its goal [of ‘true religion’] is taken from
reason but its means are taken from superstition.’66 In a certain sense that is true, of
course. If religion is moral behaviour and if moral behaviour is rational behaviour,
one might say that all ‘religion’ is ‘rational’ by definition – not in the sense that it is
based on rationally justified beliefs (which most of the time it evidently is not) but
in the sense that it agrees with rational criteria. But, apart from the fact that it is not
inconceivable that superstition also produces ‘moral’ behaviour, all religion,
superstitious or not, rests on the assumption that God is a lawgiver, which, as we
have seen, is false. And if it does not make sense to say that 2+2=5 is more false
than 2+2=7, it seems to make no sense either to say that superstitious beliefs are
more false then all the other false beliefs usually associated with revealed religion.
But let us first see how Spinoza describes superstition.
According to the preface of the Theologico-political treatise, superstition arises
from hope and fear, which in turn are caused by feelings of insecurity. People would
not be superstitious ‘if they were able to exercise complete control over all their
circumstances or if continuous good fortune were always their lot’. Hope and fear
cause credulity ‘for if, while possessed of fear, people see something happen that
calls to mind something good or bad in the past they believe that this portends a
happy or unhappy issue and therefore call it a lucky or unlucky omen’.67 This is
further explained in the Ethics. Hope is an ‘unsteady joy produced by the idea of
future or past things, the outcome of which is to a certain extent doubtful’, fear being
an ‘unsteady sadness produced by the idea of future or past things, the outcome of
which is to a certain extent doubtful’.68 The cognitive basis of both hope and fear is
ignorance, their common nature uncertainty.69 Hope and fear are more or less
interchangeable: any object of hope can become an object of fear and the other way
round.70 Like confidence and despair, gladness and remorse, hope and fear are
proper to weak and irrational minds. The more we live under the guidance of reason,
the less we depend on them.71 Accordingly, the best way to fight them (and
accordingly to fight superstition) is by acquiring true and certain knowledge: the
more we know, the less we are subject to hope and fear and the less chance there is
for superstition.
Being forms of mental pain, hope and fear are good only as means to an end.72 If
that end is peace there is nothing against the use of either, although Spinoza has a
preference for hope.73 All sovereigns use hope and fear to achieve peace and
supposedly all sovereigns would find some benefit at least in superstition. This use
has its limits, though, because superstition is strictly uncontrollable: ‘As the
Religion and Truth
27


multitude ever remains at the same level of wretchedness, so it is never long
contented and is best pleased only with what is new and has not yet proved
delusory.’74 In fact, superstition is specifically associated with despotic political
systems, whose secret ‘is to keep men in a state of deception, and with the specious
title of religion to cloak the fear by which they must be held in check’.75 Again, how
can Spinoza make a distinction between superstition, which he rejects, and true
religion?
Hope and fear are not the only feelings relevant in this context. Spinoza also
mentions, albeit more rarely, devotion. According to him, we must select from
sacred histories those ‘that are most effective in instilling obedience and devotion’.76
They ‘teach and enlighten men, and especially the vulgar, as far as suffices to
impress on their minds obedience and devotion’.77 In fact, the meaning of the word
‘sacred’ is invariably associated with devotion. Words are ‘sacred’ if they are
‘arranged so that readers are moved to devotion’.78 Scripture is ‘sacred’ because it
‘is most effective in moving men to devotion’.79 Moses on the other hand introduced
religion ‘so that his people would do their duty not by fear but from devotion’.80 This
last quotation in particular turns the distinction between devotion and fear (and
supposedly hope) into something politically significant. Apparently, obedience
based on devotion is different from obedience based on fear. And so it is according
to the psychology of the Ethics. Devotion does not belong to the same class of
feelings as hope and fear. It is not a passion but a type of love based on admiration:
‘Devotion is love of one whom we admire.’81 If we admire something superior to
ourselves we speak of veneration; but if our admiration concerns something we also
love, veneration turns into devotion.82 Admiration and wonder (which arise from our
inability to connect events with other events and to understand them as part of a
chain of causes and effects) are feelings based on imagination (rei singularis
imaginatio) and ignorance.83 That is probably what religion and superstition have in
common. The difference, however, is that in one case ignorance produces fear and
in the other love. That might also be the difference between superstition and ‘true’
religion, as the quotation on Moses seems to suggest. This is borne out by Spinoza’s
philosophy of love. Love strengthens the soul whereas hope and fear make it weak
and dependent. To do something out of love is more ‘free’ than to do the same thing
out of fear.84 Accordingly, a distinction between a religion based on hope and fear
and a religion based on devotion makes sense and could be the distinction we are
looking for. Fear and superstition make people unpredictable but devotion turns
subjects into citizens. As a result, it is possible to take into account the distinction
between superstition and ‘true’ religion without making any concession on the level
of truth. It remains to be seen whether that is also possible with respect to universal
faith.
Universal or Catholic Faith
In chap. 14 of the Theologico-political treatise, Spinoza presents what he calls
‘universal faith’. After explaining what he means by ‘faith’ – which turns out to be
any set of ideas or beliefs that incite people to behave morally85 – he goes on to
explain that, on the basis of that definition, it is possible for people to have radically
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
28


different beliefs, all of which would qualify as faith, or in other words that, as far as
faith is concerned, truth is not a relevant category:
Each man’s faith then is to be regarded as pious or impious not in respect of its truth or
falsity but as it is conducive to obedience or obstinacy. Now nobody questions that there
is to be found among men a wide variety of wit [ingenium], that all men are not equally in
agreement in all matters and are influenced by their beliefs in different ways, so that what
moves one man to devotion will move another to ridicule and contempt.86
First of all it must be underlined that we are in the realm not of superstition and fear
nor of true knowledge but in that of ‘devotion’. Indeed, we are in the sphere of
ingenium (imagination) and temperament (passion), which are sources of disagree-
ment, whereas the use of reason brings unanimity and peace.87 Nonetheless, a
‘universal’ or ‘catholic’ faith is possible although it ‘must not contain dogmas about
which there is controversy among honest people for such dogmas are pious to one
and impious to another, whereas actually they should be judged only by the works’.88
As a result, a catholic faith should contain only what is absolutely necessary for
obedience:
A catholic faith should therefore contain only those dogmas which obedience to God
absolutely demands and without which such obedience is absolutely impossible. As for
other dogmas every man should embrace those that he, being the best judge of himself,
feels will do most to strengthen him in love of justice. Acceptance of this principle would,
I suggest, leave no occasion for controversy in the Church.89
So, first of all, Spinoza is concerned with faith and obedience, not with truth. Ideally,
the universal faith should contain just those beliefs which obedience requires,
regardless of their truth or falsehood. Furthermore, it is left to every individual to
adopt other dogmas as well, even if they are controversial; indeed, this is inevitable
given the fact that judgments based on the imagination are idiosyncratic. Finally,
universal faith should be the basis of the Church, that is, function as a common
creed, adoption of which is both necessary and sufficient to be admitted as a
member. What does this mean?
Some commentators have interpreted Spinoza’s universal faith as a minimal
creed in the sense that it is a set of true beliefs on which all, including philosophers,
could agree.90 So the creed would consist of true beliefs – believed to be true by
most people but known to be true by philosophers. Accordingly, the creed would
be a skeleton of the Ethics. This is not impossible in itself. If it is possible to
believe something on authority it is also possible to believe something true on
authority. However, from the perspective of Spinoza’s own philosophy, that is
not very satisfactory and in any case raises questions that are difficult to answer.
For, first of all, the God of Universal Faith must be obeyed, whereas the ‘God’
known by philosophy by definition cannot be obeyed. Faith on the other hand
does not require truth but piety: ‘Faith requires not so much true dogmas as pious
dogmas, that is, such as move the heart to obedience.’91 Indeed, for a belief to
count as faith it is neither necessary nor sufficient to be true: ‘Faith does not
bring salvation through itself but only by reason of obedience; or as James
says (2:7) faith in itself without the works is dead.’92 There seems to be no
Religion and Truth
29


point therefore in establishing a list of propositions that are true as well as
pious.
The most worrying thing, however, is what Spinoza says about the necessity of
‘adapting’ (accommodare) universal faith to one’s other beliefs. This is necessary
because otherwise universal faith cannot be morally effective: ‘Just as Scripture was
once adapted to the understanding of the people of that time, in the same way anyone
may now adapt it to his own beliefs if he feels that this will enable him to obey God
with heartier will in those matters that pertain to justice and charity.’93 This is a
strange injunction, especially if we suppose that the articles of universal faith are
literally true in themselves and the beliefs to which they are adapted most of the time
false. Suppose we are asked to adapt, say, a mathematical theorem to the ideas and
beliefs of someone who has no mathematical education. What are we supposed to
do? Should we demonstrate it? I suppose not, for in that case the result would be
knowledge rather than true belief. The question is even more difficult if we suppose
that the person in question, say A, has false opinions on mathematics. Presumably,
we would have to induce A to give up some at least of those opinions – those at any
rate that are contrary to the ‘adapted’ theorem. According to Spinoza, however,
universal faith should be adapted to other beliefs, even if these are false. To illustrate
this procedure Spinoza provides some examples of a successful ‘adaptation’ of the
first article of the faith, claiming that ‘God, that is, a Supreme Being exists,
supremely just and merciful, an exemplar of true life’.94 This can be ‘adapted’ in the
following way:
But as to the question of what God, exemplar of true life, really is, whether he is a fire, a
spirit, a thought or something else is irrelevant to faith. And so likewise is the question as
to why he is an exemplar of true life, whether this is because he has a true and merciful
disposition, or because all things exist and act through him and consequently we, too,
understand through him, and through him we see what is true, just and good. On these
questions it matters not what beliefs a man holds.95
So the proposition ‘God exists’ can be ‘adapted’ by imagining God as a fire, a spirit,
a thought, and so on – all of which is demonstrably false. As a result we can believe
anything, true or false, about God without undermining the truth of the claim that
God exists. But that does not seem to make any sense at all if we suppose that the
proposition ‘God exists’ is literally true and can be known to be true. For ‘God
exists’ can be known to be true only if ‘God’ is defined as ‘substance’.96 If ‘God’ is
defined as a lawgiver the proposition ‘God exists’ is demonstrably false. So on the
basis of some definitions of ‘God’ the statement ‘God exists’ is actually false.
Furthermore, that God is not a fire, a spirit, a thought and so on is not contingently
false but necessarily false – indeed, whatever we claim about God is either
necessarily true or necessarily false. Finally and most importantly, if the word ‘God’
can refer to different types of beings (fire, thought, spirit and so on) we should
probably say that the word ‘God’ has no fixed meaning and therefore that the
statement ‘God exists’ is meaningless. That would mean that adaptation is actually
interpretation (of the word ‘God’) but it would also mean that ‘God exists’ is not true
in itself.
We could of course accept that consequence and drop the requirement that the
statement ‘God exists’ is literally true. That would mean that the aim of ‘adaptation’
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
30


is to specify the meaning of the word ‘God’ so as to make sentences like ‘God exists’
either true or false. The statement ‘God exists’ would be true and known to be true
only if we suppose that ‘God’ refers to an infinitely perfect being; it would be false
(but not necessarily morally ineffective) if it refers to a fire, a thought, a person, a
spirit, and so on, none of which qualify as infinitely perfect beings. Indeed, we
cannot say of X that it exists and say something true if we are completely free to
imagine what kind of being X is. There must be some constraints on the use of X for
the statement ‘X exists’ to become meaningful and as a result either true or false.
Accordingly, if ‘God’ can mean anything – if we are completely free to imagine a
being we call ‘God’ – the statement ‘God exists’ is meaningless. The implication
would be, however, that as soon as the statement ‘God exists’ can be interpreted –
that is, as soon as we have given to the word ‘God’ a specific meaning – that
statement can become false; and that means that in itself the statement ‘God exists’
is not literally true.
This may be an attractive solution given the fact that Spinoza seems to lay down
at least one constraint on the use of the word ‘God’. ‘God’ must be what he calls an
‘exemplar of true life’, this being presumably a moral ideal, something that, if
contemplated, provides us with a ‘moral code’.97 Accordingly, ‘God’ would be a
name given to whatever can function as ‘exemplar of true life’, which in turn could
be different for everybody. So if we say ‘God exists’, ‘exists’ should be taken, not
in the ordinary sense, but as an indication that there is always a way to imagine ‘an
exemplar of true life’. Just as when we say that there is a ‘highest good’ we do not
mean that there exists a being we call ‘Highest Good’ but that everyone must be able
to provide an example of what he or she considers to be ‘the highest good’.
Accordingly, ‘universal faith’ would be a linguistic convention to use the word
‘God’ only for things, real or imagined, that can function as ‘an exemplar of true
life’, that is, for any being that can serve as a source of moral inspiration.
This may solve some of the problems mentioned earlier as well as provide an
interesting clue as to the interpretation of Spinoza’s overall position. First of all, this
interpretation would make it clear why an ‘adaptation’ or ‘interpretation’ of the
creed is necessary. Indeed, if the creed is nothing but a linguistic convention, any
statement on God is meaningless as a description of reality as long as there is no
interpretation of the word ‘God’. Secondly, it explains why Spinoza feels entitled to
give his own ‘substance’ (nature) the name of ‘God’: indeed, just as other people are
led to behave morally by the contemplation of the image of a God–Lawgiver, he is
led to behave morally by studying the idea of substance. Let us see whether that
proposal can be worked out into a coherent interpretation of ‘universal faith’ as a
whole.
The Details of Faith
‘Universal Faith’ consists of seven ‘dogmas’, all following from the fundamental
principle that ‘there is a supreme being who loves justice and charity, whom all must
obey in order to be saved, and must worship by practising justice and charity to their
neighbour’.98 Presumably, this principle is the same as the ‘principle of theology’,
which he formulates at the end of chap. 15 of the Theologico-political treatise:
Religion and Truth
31


I wish to emphasise in express terms – though I have said it before – the importance and
necessity of the role I assign to Scripture and Revelation. For since we cannot perceive by
the natural light that simple obedience is a way to salvation and since only revelation
teaches us that this comes about by God’s singular grace which we cannot attain by reason,
it follows that Scripture has brought very great comfort to mankind. For all men without
exception are capable of obedience, while there are only a few in proportion to the whole
of humanity who acquire a virtuous disposition under the guidance of reason alone. Thus
did we not have the testimony of Scripture, the salvation of nearly all men would be in
doubt.99
Again the wording is carefully complex and potentially misleading. Everybody can
obey but few can understand, and those who do understand do not obey but have
what Spinoza calls habitual virtue (virtutis habitum). But if everybody can obey,
everybody can also be ‘saved’ provided one obeys the right orders. And since those
who must obey do not understand how and why an order is right, they may congratu-
late themselves on having the testimony of Scripture. So we could formulate the
principle of theology as the requirement to find a form of discourse which, without
being true, suffices to induce people to behave ‘morally’, that is, in conformity with
certain specific rules, without losing their freedom.100 Such obedience cannot be
mediated by reason, which actually denies the existence of a God–Lawgiver:
‘nobody knows by nature that he has any duty to obey God.’101 Indeed, that is the
reason why the will of God must be ‘revealed’. If we obey God it is not because we
know something to be the case (on the contrary, obedience is possible only in so far
as we do not know the true nature of reality) but because we are motivated by a
product of our imagination such that it excites our passions. Accordingly, the articles
of universal faith would reflect the laws of motivation rather than the laws of logic.
Now there is one single but universal law of human behaviour:
It is a universal law of human nature that nobody rejects what he judges to be good except
through hope of a greater good or fear of a greater loss, and that no one endures any evil
except to avoid a greater evil or to gain a greater good. That is to say, everyone will choose
of two goods that which he judges the greater and of two evils that which seems to him
the lesser. […] This law is so deeply inscribed in human nature that it should be counted
among the eternal truths universally known.102
Since this is a universal law of human behaviour, it must be presupposed in the
articles of the faith. To ‘obey God’s will’ one gives up immediate gratification in the
hope of something better. In any case, obedience to ‘God’ must produce some sort
of pleasure (laetitia): the pleasure of attaining a greater perfection.103 Moreover, the
object called ‘God’ must be able to arouse feelings of devotion, that is, it must be
something both unique and admirable.104 And that means that the object called ‘God’
must be unusual and exalted.105
If we examine the seven articles in this light most of them do indeed respect those
requirements. Apart from the first and the fifth articles they are easily divided into
two groups. The first (arts 2–4) are a consequence of the idea that the object called
‘God’ must be an object of strong emotions:
2. God is one alone. No one can doubt that this belief is essential for complete devotion,
reverence and love towards God; for devotion, reverence and love spring only from the
pre-eminence of one above all others.
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
32


3. God is omnipresent and all things are open to him. If it were believed that things could
be concealed from God or if it were not realised that he sees everything one might doubt
or be unaware of the uniformity of the justice wherewith he directs everything.
4. God has supreme right and dominion over all things. He is under no compulsion but acts
by his absolute decree and singular grace. All are required to obey him absolutely, while
he obeys none.106
Each of these articles seems to be designed to reinforce the feelings of devotion and
fear. The second article almost explicitly relies on devotion; the third and fourth
articles seem to rely rather on fear.107 The second group (arts 6 and 7), however,
make use of the universal law of human behaviour:
6. All who obey God by following this way of life and only those are saved; others, who
live at pleasure’s behest, are lost. If men did not firmly believe this, there is no reason why
they should obey God rather than their desires.
7. God forgives repentant sinners. There is no one who does not sin, so that without this
belief all would despair of salvation and there would be no reason to believe that God is
merciful. He who firmly believes that God forgives men’s sins from the mercy and grace
whereby he directs all things and whose heart is thereby the more inspired by love of God,
that man verily knows Christ according to the spirit and Christ is in him.108
The seventh article is a corollary of the sixth: repentance (penitentia) – which
according to the Ethics is an effect of education109 – is positively appreciated. The
seventh article is also the only one in which Christ is explicitly mentioned. It
suggests that ‘universal faith’ addresses itself to a Christian world and is meant to
bring together various Christian denominations rather than the three monotheistic
religions. Finally, it is to avert some of the dangers inherent to the Christian religion,
with its strong emphasis on knowledge of God (‘theology’), that in the fifth article
‘religion’ is reduced to behaviour:
5. Worship of God and obedience to him consist solely in justice and charity, or love
towards one’s neighbour.110
Theology is not part of our duties towards God: ‘God has asked no other knowledge
from men but knowledge of his divine justice and charity, this knowledge being
necessary not for philosophical understanding but for obedience to the moral law.’111
Again, it is difficult to see how any of these articles could be literally true. Indeed,
it could be argued that most are literally false. Thus it is by no means clear what the
philosophical equivalent would be of the seventh article. Nor is it clear what
demonstrable truths are concealed in the third and the fourth articles. For even if it
is true that God – in the sense of ‘nature’ – is omnipresent, this does not mean that
‘he sees everything’ or that his ‘justice is uniform’ (unless this means that the causal
law applies to everything that exists). The fourth article, which claims that God is
sovereign, is even patently false. And that love of one’s neighbour is rewarded by
salvation (arts 6 and 7) is true only in a civil society, not in the state of nature.
Finally, that God is one alone may be one of the main theses of Spinoza’s own
philosophy (which claims that there is no more than one substance) but if taken
literally it implies that there is nothing else and that means that it cannot be an object
of devotion – wholly apart from the fact that an object of devotion is by definition
Religion and Truth
33


imagined and not known.112 In sum, I see no reason to withdraw the claim that
according to Spinoza revealed religion is radically and irremediably false and that
the only thing it has in common with true philosophy, other things being equal, is
the behaviour produced by it. Even so, the identity of that behaviour is, as we shall
see in the next chapter, mediated by the will of a sovereign, which from the point of
view of universal faith is not obvious. As a result, religion can become ‘true religion’
only if its interpretation is redirected either by the sovereign or by philosophy.
Why Revealed Religion?
The question raised by all this is why we should not start on a programme of radical
enlightenment and anti-religious propaganda. The answer is that religion and
revelation are useful. Even if the emotions involved in traditional religion (hope and
fear) are in themselves bad according to reason, they can be used as means to an end
and are good if the end is good. Given the fact that the majority of people do not or
cannot live on the basis of reason, they are even indispensable:
Since men live rarely on the basis of reason (ex dictamine rationis), these two emotions
(affectus), namely, humility and contrition and also hope and fear are more useful than
harmful; indeed, if there must be sin it is better to sin in this respect. For if the mentally
weak were all equally arrogant; if they would not be ashamed of anything or would not
fear anything, how could they ever be strongly united? Indeed, how could they ever be
constrained? The people are terrifying without fear. It is no surprise that the prophets,
whose aim was not the interest of the few but that of the whole community, laid so much
stress on humility, contrition and respect.113
Humility and contrition, hope and fear are passive emotions (passiones) and as such
harmful. Their only rational use therefore is that they allow those who will never
acquire insight to live ‘in accordance with reason’.114 This is especially important
because people who are subject to ‘passions’ are not naturally inclined to live in
harmony.115 Although, therefore, it would be best if all people were reasonable, for
rational people are naturally harmonious,116 it is more realistic to rely on the
‘passions’. Accordingly, the state, which uses the passions in the interest of peace,
will always be necessary and it will always need the assistance of religion, if only
because it allows people to keep the illusion that they are free.117
That human beings are not naturally ‘reasonable’ becomes clear on almost every
page of Spinoza’s work: ‘It is rare that people live under the guidance of reason;
indeed, most of the time they envy each other and try to harm each other.’118 It is this
fact that accounts for most of the social evil discussed in the Theologico-political
treatise: ‘The light of reason is not only despised but condemned as a source of
impiety [and] merely human suppositions are regarded as divine doctrine.’119 In a
sense it even explains why the sovereign should pay little attention to what people
say and think; for it is only because they do not and cannot know the truth that ‘their
ways of thinking vary considerably and that different beliefs are better suited to
different men’.120
Spinoza explains these mechanisms in Pt II of the Ethics. The human mind being
nothing but the idea of an actually existing human body, it primarily perceives the
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
34


body to which it is attached – exclusively.121 If therefore we perceive a physical
object what we actually perceive is not that object but the effect it has on our body.
As a result, our first ideas of the world are a reflection of our own body rather than
the external world.122 And that finally explains not only why we falsely believe
ourselves to be free but also why we project that freedom on a cosmic level, falsely
believing that there is a free agent, who created the world with a specific purpose
and who in return wishes to be adored and revered.123 It is very difficult to reverse
that process. Since our mind is almost completely occupied with ‘passions’ many
active emotions would be needed to resist their pressure. Accordingly, it is only in
so far as we manage to produce active emotions that we can hope to overcome the
passions. Indeed, their final defeat depends on the ‘intellectual love of God’, which
nothing in nature can resist or destroy.124
Since the only way to survive, even physically, is by respecting others, and since
on the other hand few people understand why it is necessary to respect others,
religion is for most people the only road to salvation: ‘All men without exception are
capable of obedience, while there are only a few – in proportion to the whole of
humanity – who acquire a virtuous disposition [virtutis habitum] under the guidance
of reason alone [ex solo ductu rationis].’125 Without religion and revelation it would
be impossible for most people to acquire happiness: ‘If we did not have the
testimony of Scripture, the salvation of nearly all men would be in doubt.’126
Conclusion
We are now in a position to interpret more fully the programme announced at the
beginning of the Theologico-political treatise. In fact, Spinoza’s second claim has
now become evident: given the fact that true knowledge produces moral behaviour,
philosophy can be said to produce piety and is therefore practically equivalent with
faith.127 Accordingly, the freedom to philosophize does not harm piety, especially
now that we know that its findings can be expressed in a religious language. The
claim that without the freedom to philosophize true piety becomes impossible
logically completes the first claim, not only because, being true and certain,
philosophy produces the most reliable form of piety (morality), but also because
without philosophy no criteria for pious behaviour can be fixed. Accordingly,
although this is the subject of the next chapter, we can already conclude that the truth
about ‘God’, which is different from the correct rules for using the word ‘God’, is of
great moral and political importance.
Again, Spinoza would have no reason to place his investigation under the sign of
libertas philosophandi if the question of truth did not play any role. Indeed, all
depends on the concept of truth. Although Spinoza is not absolutely consistent in his
use of terms like verum (‘true’) and veritas (‘truth’), using the former also for acts
and behaviour, as in vera vita (‘true life’), vera virtus (‘true virtue’), vera religio
(‘true religion’), and so on, it seems fair to assume that his notion of truth is basically
Cartesian in the sense that, if we know something true, we know that we know
something true and are therefore certain of knowing the truth.128 A short survey
confirms this.
Truth is neither a transcendental term nor a generic property of being.129 Indeed,
Religion and Truth
35


truth is nothing in itself: ‘If you ask me what truth is apart from a true idea, you
might as well ask what whiteness is apart from a white body.’130 Truth is embodied
in true ideas, that is, true concepts. This seems to be anti-Cartesian, given the fact
that according to Descartes it is not ideas that are true but judgments on ideas.
However, according to Spinoza ideas contain a judgment: ‘To affirm or deny is
nothing besides ideas.’131 As a result, we can safely conclude that Spinoza agrees
with Descartes that all truth is propositional, albeit that for him the propositional act
or attitude is part of the idea in so far as it is an idea.132 That being said, it should be
admitted that Spinoza is not absolutely consistent. Thus he also finds it meaningful
to say of a sensation that it is ‘true’ – and that precisely in so far as it is not a
judgment.133 But that inconsistency is caused, probably, by his rejection of two other
Cartesian ideas: that the senses are intrinsically doubtful, and that judgments are free
(that, given any idea, we are free to affirm or deny it).
Although these are important deviations from the Cartesian example it is
important to underscore the very Cartesian point that truth has nothing provisional:
that if we know the truth we should not be afraid that at some point we will discover
that what we believed to be true is actually false. According to Spinoza, certainty
belongs to ideas (concepts), not to things: ‘Those who look for certainty in things
themselves are as mistaken as those who look to things for truth.’134 Indeed, if an
idea is true it is also certain: ‘Someone who has a true idea knows that he has a true
idea and is unable to doubt the truth of the matter.’135 As a result, if we have a true
idea we are certain not only of its truth but also of the falsehood of its denial:
‘Something true is a sign of its own and of what is false’ (verum index sui et falsi).136
So, if we know, truly and certainly, that God’s will and intellect are identical, we
also know, as truly and certainly, that the notion of a God–Lawgiver is false.
The only alternative would be to attribute to Spinoza a different concept of truth.
Several candidates come to mind: pragmatist, dialectical, idealist. According to a
pragmatist concept of truth something is true ‘if it works’, that is, if it makes an
observable difference. Accordingly, given the fact that philosophy and faith produce
the same behaviour, they would both be equally true. But, apart from the fact that
there is no indication whatsoever that Spinoza adopts this criterion of truth, there is
practical equivalence of philosophy and faith only from the point of view of the
sovereign; they are not equivalent from the point of view of the subject: a
philosopher is always happier than the faithful because his happiness is based on true
and certain ideas, whereas the salvation of the faithful is at best an object of
(uncertain) hope.
A dialectical conception of truth would allow for a history of truth, in which
philosophy emerges as the ‘truth’ of religion; or in which religion, although it is
dialectically ‘aufgehoben’ (elevated as well as abrogated) on a higher level, remains
‘true’ as part of the definitive truth of philosophy (the idea or concept – Begriff).
Something could be said for this interpretation. Especially in Pts II–IV of the Ethics,
Spinoza allows for a ‘history of reason’ on a practical as well as a theoretical level,
and in any case in the Ethics, more than in the Treatise on the Improvement of the
Intellect, Spinoza seems to be concerned with showing the continuity between the
imagination and the intellect, that is, between opinion and truth.137 But, again, apart
from the fact that there is no certainty at all that Spinoza adopted a dialectical
scheme, it would also only partially apply. The earlier stages of this evolution are
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
36


not ‘aufgehoben’ to a higher stage but on the contrary unmasked as being radically
false; indeed, the very sense of the expression verum index sui et falsi is that by
discovering the truth about some matter we dispel whatever false opinions we may
have on the same matter. In any case there is no indication at all that Spinoza thinks
of truth (as distinct from reason) as being subject to a historical evolution. For even
if it is true that reason is a historical phenomenon, based on the history of the
individuals as well as the species, an independent standard of truth is given with
God’s infinite understanding – for whatever that may be (a real understanding which
is accompanied by some sort of awareness or the symbolic expression of the
intelligibility of nature, as I would be inclined to believe) the expression would be
pointless, if at the end of the day, there were more than one way of understanding a
thing.138
An idealist interpretation, finally, would allow for some sort of parallel expression
of truth on different levels. Thus, as in Hegel’s final synthesis, we could envisage an
expression of truth on the levels of art, religion and philosophy, corresponding to,
respectively, sensation, imagination (Vorstellung) and concept (Begriff).139 On an
unconscious level I think this interpretation does play a role in many Spinoza
interpretations, mainly because we tend to think of religion, not in terms of
knowledge, but in terms of specific emotions: what makes someone a religious
person is not that he or she believes specific propositions to be true but that he or she
has ‘religious’ feelings, regardless of the specific images that are connected with
those feelings. And from that perspective, it is thought, Spinoza is the most religious
person one could imagine – truly a ‘Gotttrunkener Mensch’, as he was called by
Novalis.140 And, indeed, from the viewpoint of modern liberal Protestantism,
Spinoza’s idea of a religious language (as I interpret his attempt to formulate a
universal faith) is very attractive. Still, from a strictly historical point of view, it
must be underlined that the unity Spinoza aims at is not a unity of feeling but a unity
of action. What is ideally achieved is a moral community in the strong sense of the
word, that is, a community of people acting in accordance with the same rules,
which can either be understood as eternal truths which do not involve the existence
of a God–Lawgiver (on the contrary) or be imagined as ‘Divine’ commands.
However, these rules are the same only in a context of positive law, that is, if there
is someone who interprets ‘the Will of God’ as a positive law.

Eth. V, prop. 41, G. II, 306/C. I, 615.
128

[convenientiam] between an idea and what is represented by the idea and the other way
round’ (CM, I, vi, G. I, 246/C. I, 312). This is probably enough to refute the claim that
