Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
42


Chapter 2
‘The Will of God’
Spinoza’s rejection of ‘Divine Law’ (‘law’ being a prescriptive and normative law)
implies that all authority is of human origin and ultimately that all obedience is
rooted in a political structure. This means that other forms of ‘authority’ (like the
authority of Scripture, of the Church, and so on), deriving allegedly from the
authority of God or of Christ, either are not authority proper, but belief and
judgment, or should be subject to the authority of a sovereign, whom Spinoza calls
‘the interpreter of God’s will’. This brings Spinoza rather close to Hobbes, except
that he may be more aware of the intrinsic limitations of authority. In any case,
Spinoza completes his theory with a theory of freedom, which locates freedom in
true knowledge: someone who truly and certainly knows the nature of reality does
not obey but is free. I first study Spinoza’s ideas on authority as such – especially in
relation to Hobbes – and then examine how this relates to authority in a religious
context, to start with the notion of ‘Church’. I reserve a discussion of the authority
of Scripture to the next chapter and of the authority of theology to the one after that.
The Nature of the State: Hobbes
According to Hobbes man’s natural condition is a ruthless war of all against all,
without right or wrong, justice or injustice:
Where there is no common power, there is no law; where no law, no injustice. Force and
fraud are in warre the two cardinal virtues. Justice and injustice are none of the faculties,
neither of the body nor of the mind. If they were they might be in a man that were alone
in the world as well as his senses and passions. They are qualities that relate to men in
society, not in solitude.1
Man is not altruistic by nature; indeed, man is not even social. Still, since he can
survive only if there is peace, the creation of a civil society is an aim he is bound to
pursue whenever he can have hope to attain it. If not, he has the right to go on
fighting his neighbours because that is the only way in which he can defend himself
and assure his survival. Hence the first law of nature: ‘That every man ought to
endeavour Peace, as farre as he has hope of attaining it; and when he cannot obtain
it, that he may seek, and use, all helps and advantages of warre.’2 Man is not under
an obligation to seek peace but will seek peace if he thinks it is likely he can obtain
it, that is, if others can be persuaded of the necessity of peace. Hence the second law
of nature: ‘That a man be willing when others are so, too, as farre-forth as for peace
and defence of himselfe he shall think it necessary, to lay down this right to all things
and be contented with so much liberty against other men as he would allow other
43


men against himselfe.’3 The decision to seek peace is a free decision. It is taken by
people who believe that peace can be achieved and that it is the best way to survive.
People, on the other hand, who do not have those expectations have no valid reason
for giving up war. Finally, if we do seek peace we give up a right, transferring it to
others, which can be done in a legal manner only by means of a contract:
For where no covenant hath preceded there hath no right been transferred and every man
has right to every thing; and consequently no action can be unjust. But when a covenant is
made then to break it is unjust. And the definition of injustice is no other than the not
performance of covenant. And whatsoever is not unjust is just.4
So if in the end there is justice this is the result, not of a natural evolution, but of a
convention made in the interest of peace and justified by the possibility of peace.
Hobbes expresses this by saying that there must be a ‘covenant’: a free and mutual
agreement on the desirability of peace. That is not enough, however, for if it were
left to every individual to decide by what means peace is to be achieved, chaos and
anarchy would be the inevitable result. Agreement about the necessity of peace does
not entail agreement about the means to achieve it. And that is the reason why the
contractants decide ‘to conferre all their power and strength upon one man or
assembly of men, that may reduce all their wills, by plurality of voices unto one will:
which is as much as to say, to appoint one man or assembly of men to beare their
person; and every one to owne and acknowledge himselfe to be author of whatsoever
he that so beareth their person, shall act or cause to be acted in those things which
concerne the common peace and safetie; and therein to submit their wills, every one
to his will, and their judgements to his judgement.’5 So if the basis of the distinction
of good and evil – ‘good’ being whatever is conducive to peace and ‘evil’ whatever
threatens the peace – is a covenant, the basis of obligation is the institution of a
sovereign, to whom it is left to decide in what precise way peace should be achieved.
If there were not a sovereign we would fall back into the state of nature. The result
in any case is a Civitas or Great Leviathan, the mortal god ‘to which wee owe under
the immortal god our peace and defence’.6 Here alone is true unity: ‘This is more
than consent or concord; it is a reall unitie of them all, in one and the same person.’7
In sum, a commonwealth is not an arbitrary collection of people but ‘one person, of
whose acts a great multitude, by mutuall covenants one with another, have made
themselves every one the author, to the end he may use the strength and means of
them all, as he shall think expedient, for their peace and common defence’.8
Hobbes’s account of the state is puzzling and complex, probably because he tries
to reconcile two points of view which others would probably find contradictory: one
voluntaristic, according to which a civil society and a state are the result of a free
decision, based on rational motives; the other naturalistic, according to which the
state is the result of the working of natural laws. So, first of all, there is the vexing
problem of the status of the laws of nature: are they in themselves a source of
obligation or are they prudential laws?9 They would seem to be obligatory (their
verbal formula does contain an ‘ought’) but of course they could not possibly be so
in the state of nature, which is characterized by the absence of obligation. All one
can make of it is that, if people realize the advantages of peace, they will seize every
opportunity to obtain it. So if at a certain point people do strive for peace the reason
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
44


is not that they obey a command but that they know a truth (but then why call it a
‘law’? Certainly if we take ‘law’ in a more neutral sense there are others which are
more fundamental). Furthermore, Hobbes makes an implicit distinction between
state and civil society. Whereas a civil society is a community of people who by
covenant agreed to strive for peace, the state is necessary because, as Hobbes says
elsewhere, ‘covenants without the sword are but words’.10 Naturally, this distinction
is purely analytical, for no civil society could actually subsist without a sovereign.
All Hobbes achieves by it is that he fixes a limit to the sovereign’s authority, as if to
remind him that he is sovereign only in virtue of a free decision of his subjects in so
far as they want peace; but to admit that explicitly would of course be incompatible
with the absoluteness and unconditionality of sovereign power. Hobbes also makes
a curious distinction between natural and artificial.11 A civil society is an artefact:
‘The agreement of [bees and ants] is naturall; that of men is by covenant only, which
is artificial; and therefore it is no wonder if there be somewhat else required to make
their agreement constant and lasting.’12 Still, this artefact is the result of a natural
law. In itself, of course, that could be seen as typical of mechanical philosophy,
which uses artefacts as models to explain the natural world and uses natural laws to
explain and produce artefacts; the astonishing thing, however, is that in spite of
being the result of a natural law this particular artefact requires extra protection
because it is an artefact – as if a civil society would be against nature instead of being
in accordance with it. Finally, although the state rests on a covenant, there is no legal
difference between sovereignty by acquisition and sovereignty by institution:
This kind of dominion or soveraignty [by acquisition] differeth from soveraignty by
institution onely in this, that men who choose their soveraign do it for fear of one another
and not of him whom they institute: But in this case they subject themselves to him they
are afraid of. In both cases they do it for fear.13
So the true and only justification is fear, no matter of whom or for what. The
covenant seems to be a legal fiction meant on the one hand to legally justify the
transference of rights that forms the basis of a civil society and on the other to
provide symbolic expression to the fact that a civil society is possible only if people
agree about its end and meaning.
The underlying problem can be formulated as one concerning the limits of
naturalism. To what extent are ‘laws of nature’ explanatory laws, which are followed
rather than obeyed, and to what extent do they contain a moral precept, which is only
obeyed? That is the point where Spinoza steps in.
Spinoza: Right, Power and Authority
Spinoza shares with Hobbes the idea of a state of nature in which man is ‘no more
in duty bound to live according to the laws of a sound mind than a cat to live
according to the laws of a lion’s nature’.14 Man is a natural being and is governed by
the same laws as other things. That means, not only that there is nothing that a man
should do or could do apart from what he actually does, but also that from the point
of view of nature the question of the legitimacy of someone’s acts does not even
‘The Will of God’
45


arise: ‘Whatever every thing does by the laws of its own nature, it does with
sovereign right, inasmuch as it acts as it is determined by nature.’15 So ‘whatever
every man […] believes to be to his advantage, whether under the guidance of sound
reason or under the sway of passion, he may by sovereign natural right seek and get
for himself by any means, by force, deceit, entreaty, or in any other way he best can,
and he may consequently regard as his enemy anyone who tries to hinder him from
getting what he wants.’16 Without correction the result obviously is a condition of
extreme misery and war, in which the possibility for developing specifically human
faculties is severely limited: ‘The life of men without mutual assistance must be
most wretched and lack the cultivation of reason.’17 Although life in the state of
nature is as it ‘should’ be it is not what is ‘best’ for human beings because it
absolutely prevents the development of reason: ‘Since our intellect forms the better
part of us, it is evident that we should endeavour above all to perfect it as far as we
can, for in its perfection must consist our supreme good.’18
But even on a purely biological level civil society enhances our chance of
survival: ‘There cannot be any doubt as to how much more it is to men’s advantage
to live in accordance with the laws and sure dictates of reason, which […] aim only
at the true good of man.’19 However, given the fact that people are not reasonable by
nature, a civil society (civitas) – indeed any society (societas) – is possible only if
there is authority (imperium) and obedience: ‘No society can subsist without
government and coercion and consequently without laws to control and restrain
man’s lusts and unbridled urges.’20 Without laws, ‘nothing good can survive,
everything is endangered and anger and wickedness reign supreme amidst universal
fear’.21 Without government, people ‘are carried away by their emotions, which take
no account of the future or of other considerations’.22 The use of reason makes it
possible to sacrifice short-term interests to long-term interests but emotions demand
immediate gratification: ‘A desire produced by the knowledge of good and evil is
easily conjured or suppressed by a desire for things that promise an immediate
gratification.’23
So for many people government and authority replace reason. On the other hand
neither the state nor the law would be necessary if men were absolutely rational: ‘If
men were so constituted as to desire nothing but what is prescribed by true reason
society would stand in no need of any laws.’24 A perfectly rational man can live in
perfect agreement with the dictates of reason without any coercion. Finally, since
complete rationality is never fully attained by anybody, one must presume that the
state will always be necessary as a condition for peace.25
Like Hobbes, therefore, Spinoza considers a natural state of misery and fear as the
under-limit of human behaviour; like Hobbes he believes that civil society improves
the quality of human life and enhances the chance of survival; like Hobbes he
believes that a civil society can be realized only if there is a law and if there is a
government to enforce it. On the whole, however, it seems to be Spinoza’s intention
to adhere more thoroughly to the naturalistic principle than Hobbes. Thus he thinks
of the transition from the natural state to a civil society, not as an act of obedience
to a law of nature (in the sense of a normative law), but as something explicable by
laws of nature (in the sense of a causal or explanatory law). Unlike Hobbes, Spinoza
is helped by a metaphysics which teaches (a) that God (nature) is nothing but infinite
power, (b) that God is the only thing that exists, and (c) that finite natural things
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
46


(including humans) are so to speak fragments of ‘God’.26 God is under no obligation
to act in a specific way because his power is unlimited. There are no internal limits
to the exercise of God’s power, except that whatever is produced by it must be
conceivable for an infinite intellect.27 Nor are there any external limits, given the fact
that ‘apart from God there can neither be nor be conceived any substance’.28 As a
result, he has an unlimited, that is, a ‘sovereign’, right to whatever he can do which
on the level of the finite modi manifests itself as the natural right, that is, the natural
power, of each individual thing:
For nature’s power is the very power of God, who has sovereign right over all things. But
since the universal power of nature as a whole is nothing but the power of all individual
things taken together, it follows that each individual thing has the sovereign right to do all
it can do, or in other words the right of the individual is co-extensive with its determinate
power (determinata potentia).29
According to the ordinary view God’s power is, perhaps, not limited (after all God
is ‘omnipotent’), but in any case tempered by his wisdom, goodness, and so on,
which prevent him from using all his power; indeed traditionally the idea that God
can do many things without actually doing them has nothing contradictory.30
According to Spinoza, however, ‘the power of God is his very being’ (Dei potentia
est ipsa ipsius essentia).31 If finite things are modi of the only existing substance
called ‘God’ their intrinsic power should not be limited either, except of course by
the fact that they are finite.32 Accordingly, the only factor that could legitimately
limit God’s power as it manifests itself in a particular finite thing A is either A’s own
constitution (in virtue of which there are certain things it can and other things it
cannot do) or the power of a different thing B, which, since it is also a part of
God/nature and therefore also embodies a determinate power, is as much entitled to
do whatever it can do as A even if that is to the detriment of A: ‘The natural right of
nature as a whole and therefore of every individual thing extends as far as its
power.’33 The only thing contrary to a thing’s natural right – the only thing a thing
‘should’ not do – is what it cannot possibly do or want: ‘Nature’s right and her
established order […] forbids only things that no one desires and no one can do.’34
So Spinoza’s discussion of ‘natural right’ does not serve to introduce any other
modal categories than the ones already known, namely, necessity and possibility:
there is not anything like ‘natural right’ or ‘natural duty’ as distinguished from
(logical and physical) necessity and possibility.
Having no soul in virtue of which they would belong to a different realm, humans
are as fully part of nature as other things: ‘I do not acknowledge any distinction
between men and other individuals of nature.’35 So the type of laws by which human
beings are ‘naturally’ governed cannot be different from those governing the rest of
nature even if there may be specific as well as individual differences. Indeed, in so
far as there is a difference in power between different species and between different
individuals of the same species, there are also differences in right: ‘It is by sovereign
natural right that fish inhabit water and that the big ones eat the smaller.’36 So there
could be a specifically human right to the extent that there is a specifically human
power. That is the power of thinking, which makes it possible to foresee events, to
improve our health and to produce technical innovations.37 Even so, it is not certain
‘The Will of God’
47


that that produces a universal human right, given the fact that many people do not
(or indeed cannot) use their reason: ‘On the contrary, all men are born in a state of
complete ignorance and before they can learn the true way of life and acquire a
virtuous disposition […] a great part of their life has gone by.’38 Hence individual
differences, which however do not affect the fundamental equality in rights (in a
normative sense) between divers human individuals: ‘A man, whether guided by
reason or mere desire, does nothing save in accordance with the laws and rules of
nature, that is, by natural right.’39 Given the fact that a fool or a criminal are as much
‘natural beings’ as a philosopher, their acts are in themselves as much in accordance
with natural law and can therefore not be against natural right.40 Indeed, the only
difference could be that at the end of the day the philosopher enjoys more ‘power’
or more ‘perfection’, that is, more ability to exist, but there is nothing in nature on
the ground of which all beings should enjoy an equal power to exist.41 In any case,
whatever people of any kind or description do is invariably explained (and
completely justified in terms of ‘natural right’) by one single and fundamental
law:
It is a universal law of human nature that nobody rejects what he judges to be good except
through hope of a greater good or fear of a greater loss, and that no one endures any evil
except to avoid a greater evil or to gain a greater good. That is to say, everyone will choose
of two goods that which he judges the greater, and of two evils that which seems to him
the lesser. I say expressly, ‘that which in his belief is the greater or lesser.’ I do not say
that the facts necessarily correspond with his judgement. This law is so deeply inscribed
in human nature that it should be counted among the eternal truths everybody should
know.42
Human beings have the power (that is, the ‘natural right’) to do whatever their
environment and their physical and mental constitution allow them to do, but of all
the things they could do they will actually do only those which they know or believe
to be ‘best’. The fact that we see different people behave differently, and some
obviously against their ‘best’, is explained by the fact that motives are subjective
perceptions – which may or may not be well informed:
If human nature had been so constituted that men should live according to the mere dictate
of reason and attempt nothing inconsistent therewith, in that case natural right considered
as special to mankind would be determined by the power of reason only. But men are more
led by blind desire than by reason: and therefore the natural power or right of human
beings is limited not by reason but by every appetite whereby they are determined to action
or seek their own preservation.43
But a second thing is explained by this law, namely, that people never give up their
rights unconditionally. Indeed, they are prepared to do so only if they believe (truly
or falsely) that in that way they actually serve their interests better. Accordingly, a
civil society, which in terms of the immediate gratification of desires may be a
sacrifice, can be realized and preserved only to the extent that people actually
believe that it is in their interest to live under a law, the expected benefit being peace
and prosperity.
In any case the reason why people leave the state of nature and give up their
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
48


freedom is not that they are obliged by a moral law. ‘Obedience’ and ‘sin’ do not
belong to the state of nature:
Just as a wise man has the sovereign right to do all that reason dictates, i.e., to live
according to the laws of reason, so, too, a man who is ignorant and weak-willed has the
sovereign right to do all that is urged on him by appetite, i.e., to live according to the laws
of appetite. This is the same doctrine as Paul’s who declares that prior to the law – that is,
as long as men are considered as living under Nature’s rule – there can be no sin.44
Without a positive law there is neither obedience nor sin because there is no
obligation: ‘Sin is nothing but disobedience, which therefore is punishable only
under a civil law.’45 The notions of ‘good’ and ‘evil’ make sense only with reference
to the will of a lawgiver who, given the fact that it cannot be God, is a man or group
of men who, since they manage to impose their will, in fact create or preserve a civil
society. So ‘good’ and ‘evil’ have nothing absolute; they are conceivable only in a
civil society and completely meaningless in the state of nature.
This is shown most clearly and most explicitly in the Political treatise, where
Spinoza discusses two objections to his theory. Sin, one could say, is disobedience,
not only to a positive law, but also to reason, or to God.46 And since, supposedly, the
commands of reason as well as the will of God are eternal (and therefore universally
valid) the implication would be that there is a more fundamental good and a more
fundamental evil than that defined by a positive law. Spinoza’s reply is
straightforward. As far as reason is concerned he does not fundamentally object to
the use of words like ‘the commands of reason’ – no more, one supposes, than he
objects to the use of words like ‘good’, ‘perfection’, ‘order’ and so on. He rejects
them only to the extent that they create the false impression that, if men do not live
in accordance with reason, they are ‘free’, whereas the main lesson of the Ethics is
that the opposite is the case – that the more one lives in accordance with reason the
more one is free. Actually reason is not like government or law. It has no authority
(imperium). It is as improper to speak of the authority of reason as it is to demand
obedience to reason: ‘Obedience has regard to the will of him who commands, not
to necessity and truth.’47 If we understand truth we act in accordance with it and in
so doing acquire our freedom.48 Since on the other hand the multitude can be ‘guided
by one mind’ only if the laws they are guided by are ‘in accordance with reason’, we
should not blame too much those who define ‘evil’ as ‘disobedience to reason’.49 So
apparently we should interpret expressions such as ‘dictates of reason’, ‘authority of
reason’, ‘obedience and disobedience to reason’ (some of which are actually used by
Spinoza), not literally, but in the same way as we do ‘laws of nature’ – as ‘human
ways of speaking’.
With respect to the will of God, Spinoza arrives at similar results. Parallel to what
we saw in the case of reason one could say ‘that a man is more free and more
obedient to himself according as he loves God more and worships him with all his
heart’. However:
If we look, not at the order of nature, which we do not know, but only at the dictates of
reason in so far as they concern religion; and if at the same time we reflect that these are
revealed to us by God, speaking as it were in ourselves, or indeed that they were revealed
in the form of laws to the prophets, then we must say, speaking in a human way, that a man
‘The Will of God’
49


who loves God with all his heart is obedient to God and by contrast that a man who is led
by blind desire sins.50
To say of ‘someone who loves God with all his heart that he is obedient to God’ is
a ‘human way of speaking’, that is, if taken literally it is false. This way of speaking
is made possible, I presume, by the fact that ‘we’ – and ‘we’ apparently means, not
the philosopher, but his readers – do not know ‘the order of nature’. If we did we
would not easily speak of ‘obedience to God’, given the fact that then we would
know that God is not a lawgiver. However, if we concentrate on whatever follows
for human behaviour (‘religion’) and are determined, not by true knowledge, but
either by God ‘speaking in ourselves’ – presumably the moral conscience which
orthodox theology attributes even to the Gentiles51 – or by God as he speaks through
the prophets, then it could make sense to speak of obedience to God. Accordingly,
the notion of obedience to God does not make sense absolutely but only against the
background of ignorance about the ‘laws of nature’ and in the context of revealed
religion.
Even so, Spinoza goes on, we should remember that we are in God’s power as
clay in the hand of the potter, who ‘of the same lump makes one vessel unto honour
and another unto dishonour’.52 So even a believer, who does not know or care about
man in so far as he is part of nature, should realize (on the very basis of Scripture)
that nobody has the power either to obey or to disobey and that all our acts, whether
good or evil, are causally determined. On the whole therefore we must say, again,
that ‘sin and obedience in the strict sense, as well as justice and injustice are possible
only under a government’.53
The same point is made – perhaps even more forcefully – in the Theologico-
political treatise:
Nobody knows by nature that he has any duty to obey God. Indeed, this knowledge cannot
be attained by any process of reasoning and one can gain it only by revelation confirmed
by signs. Therefore prior to revelation nobody can be bound by a divine law of which he
cannot be aware. So a state of nature must not be confused with a state of religion; we must
conceive it as being without religion and without law and consequently without sin and
without wrong […] For if men were by nature bound by a divine law or if the divine law
were a law by nature there would have been no need for God to enter into a contract with
men and bind them by covenant and oath. Therefore we must concede without
qualification that the divine law began from the time when men by express covenant
promised to obey God in all things, thereby surrendering as it were, their natural freedom
and transferring their right to God in the manner we described in speaking of the civil
state.54
Spinoza’s first point, that ‘nobody knows by nature that he has any duty to obey
God’, is a bit surprising, for the knowledge we have about how we ‘should’ behave
is after all ‘knowledge by nature’. But perhaps we should stress the word ‘obey’. If
we know nature – and have a correct notion of ‘God’ – we know that God cannot be
a lawgiver and as a result that we cannot obey him. In other words, we can ‘obey’
God (in the strict sense) only to the extent that we do not know God; that is, that we
do not know nature. Spinoza’s second and more interesting point is of a legal nature:
a law can bind those only to whom it is made known. So if there is a divine law (in
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
50


the sense of a command) it must be published before obedience can be claimed. But
since it cannot be known by natural means (see the first point) all awareness of it
must be mediated by prophecy. Accordingly, if there is a divine law at all it can be
binding only for those who benefit from prophecy. Third, and perhaps most
importantly, no law, not even a divine law apparently, is binding in itself: it is so
only in so far as people agree to obey it; that is, in so far as they acknowledge the
sovereignty of God, which they are ready to do only if they have the prospect of
becoming better by it. So God is not king in an absolute sense but only to the extent
that his sovereignty is acknowledged in a covenant.55 Finally, the decision to
institute God as a sovereign (that is, to acknowledge his authority) can be made only
collectively – the individual is bound by God’s will only if others are also bound by
it. Accordingly, God can have authority only in a civil society, which alone creates
the conditions for his will to be known and obeyed.
Much of this can also be found in Hobbes, most of the time with the same
reservations. Referring to Ps. 96:1 and 98:1, Hobbes admits that ‘God is King’ but
like Spinoza he interprets this in the sense that everything (including humans) is part
of nature: ‘Whether men will or not, they must be subject alwayes to the Divine
Power.’56 That this ‘power of God’ is actually the power of nature is clear by the fact
that it ‘extendeth it selfe not onely to man but also to beasts and plants and bodies
inanimate’. To speak of God as a king in this sense is for Hobbes nothing but ‘a
metaphoricall use of the word’.57 Subjects of God in the proper sense of the word are
those only who recognize God as their king, that is, basically, those who by covenant
instituted God as their sovereign.
According to Hobbes, God rules over those to whom his law was revealed in one
of three possible ways: ‘God declareth his laws three wayes; by the dictates of
naturall reason, by revelation and by the voice of some man to whom by the
operation of miracles he procureth credit with the rest.’58 So the word of God can be
rational, sensible or prophetic and in all these cases, apparently, God’s word can be
of a prescriptive nature. Given the fact that direct revelation is strictly individual and
therefore binds no one but the individual who benefits from it, reason and prophecy
are the only ways that are politically relevant. Hobbes implicitly eliminates
prophecy from the state of nature because, of the two criteria he wants a prophet to
fulfil, one ultimately presupposes sovereignty: ‘One is the doing of miracles; the
other is the not teaching any other religion than that which is already established.’59
Since a religion can be established only by a sovereign, this means that prophecy
presupposes the existence of a civil society.
Still, Hobbes does speak of the laws known by reason or what he elsewhere calls
the laws of nature.60 These are laws but not in the sense in which the laws of falling
bodies are laws. The first law of nature, for example, according to which ‘every man
ought to endeavour peace’, is a law only for those who understand that peace is
‘better’ than war – it is not a law in the sense that everybody in fact does endeavour
peace.61 And that prepares the ground for the working of the second law, ‘that a man
be willing when others are so, too, as farre-forth as for peace and defence of himselfe
he shall think it necessary, to lay down this right to all things and be contented with
so much liberty against other men as he would allow other men against himselfe’.62
Both laws are ‘binding’ only in the sense that knowledge of those laws will
invariably produce a particular type of behaviour: if people know about the
‘The Will of God’
51


advantages of peace, they will unavoidably strive for peace. The working of these
laws therefore is contingent upon the fact that people can compare a state of war,
which they actually experience, with peace, which at best they anticipate.
If we compare that with what we found in Spinoza it becomes clear that one of
Spinoza’s points against Hobbes may be that he refuses to see the transition to a civil
society as the result of an act of obedience. First, the transition to a civil society
cannot be an act of obedience because in the state of nature no obedience (to reason,
to God, to nature) is due. Second, the transition to a civil society is a matter of
rational decision making, which to that extent is not obedience. Third, the law of
reason can become binding only if it is given the form of a positive law: ‘in order
that the precepts of true reason – that is […] the very precepts of God – have the
absolute force of law, every man must surrender his natural right and they must all
transfer that right to the whole community or to a number of men or to one man’.63
So even if the ‘Law of Nature’ actually were a ‘Law of God’, as Hobbes implies, it
could still not be binding because it does not have the form of a law. Accordingly,
nothing is gained by seeing the act by which a civil society is founded as an act of
obedience.
In sum, even if certain types of behaviour are preferable to others there is no
natural obligation to adopt them. But if there is no natural obligation there is no
natural law. Indeed, Spinoza’s insisting on the fact that in the state of nature we can
obey neither reason nor God is explicable only if we assume that there is an
important subtext, directed either against Hobbes or against the natural law tradition.
Without a positive law nobody can be obliged to live in accordance with reason.
Accordingly, the source of all obligation is always human – if we are obedient at all
it is to someone who has the power to make us obey.
Power and Authority
If one assumes that a civil society is not the result of an act of obedience (to a divine,
or rational, or natural law) but a natural phenomenon (explicable by a causal and
intelligible law), the laws governing the state of nature are not abrogated in a civil
society. This has two immediate consequences: (a) if in the state of nature people are
led by the subjective perception of their interest, it must be the same perception that
leads them in a civil society; (b) if right is power, sovereign right must be some form
of superior power. It is that which Spinoza himself sees as his main difference with
Hobbes: ‘The difference between me and Hobbes is that I stick to natural law and
claim that the Supreme Magistrate in any town has as much right over his subjects
as he surpasses them in power, whereas in the state of nature this is true of
everybody.’64 In the state of nature everybody has as much power as everybody else
and therefore enjoys the same rights (or power) as everybody else – and that means
that nobody has any specific individual power (or right) at all: ‘As long as the natural
right of a man is determined by the power of every individual and belongs to
everybody it is nothing and exists in opinion rather than fact because it cannot be
used.’65 Equality which is not tempered by any form of positive law is destructive of
individual right but, paradoxically, the redistribution of power inherent to a civil
society enhances everybody’s power because only a sovereign has ‘the power to
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
52


impose a common rule of behaviour, to issue laws and to enforce them, not by
reason, which is unable to constrain the emotions, but by threats’.66 By imposing his
will a sovereign harmonizes the naturally antagonistic acts and minds of people that
are fundamentally ruled by the passions and the imagination.67 Sovereign rule
therefore has the same effect as reason would have on an individual level.68
From Hobbes’s point of view, Spinoza makes the basis of political and moral
obligation unacceptably small, presumably. For if prior to the institution of
sovereignty there can be no obedience, the obedience claimed by the sovereign
cannot be justified and explained with respect to a higher principle (Nature, Reason,
God). The sovereign’s ‘right’ would perfectly coincide with his ‘might’, that is, with
his power to constrain people. Spinoza agrees that the absence of higher authority
causes a serious limitation of sovereign right and a corresponding increase in
popular power. This is entailed by his very definition of sovereignty: ‘The right of
the sovereign is nothing but simple natural right, limited by the power, not of every
individual, but of the multitude, which is guided as it were by one mind.’69 The very
fact of imposing one’s will on a multitude of people creates a power greater than that
of any particular individual. This power is not tempered by the social contract, for
even if one has promised obedience one is bound to keep one’s promises only to the
extent that one believes it is in one’s interest to do so: ‘nobody is going to keep any
promises whatsoever except through fear of a greater evil or hope of a greater
good’.70 Nobody could be expected to do otherwise, given the universal law of
human behaviour:
Since by natural right I am bound to choose the lesser of two evils, I have the sovereign
right to break faith and go back on my pledged word. Now this, I say, is fully justified by
natural right, whether it was true and infallible reasoning or whether it was mistaken belief
that made me realise I was wrong to have made that promise.71
Without credible sanctions a promise does not create an obligation: ‘It is folly to
demand from another that he should keep his word for ever, if at the same time one
does not try to ensure that if he breaks his word he will meet with more harm than
good.’72 So there is neither a moral nor a legal obstacle to prevent a revolt if the
people believe that it is in their interest to overthrow the sovereign. Indeed, if they
believe (rightly or wrongly) that it is in their interest to do so it is impossible for
them not to act accordingly. For a sovereign to remain ‘in power’ it is therefore
imperative that he gives his subjects the belief that it is in their interest to obey him,
either because they know that all disobedience will be severely punished or because
they believe or know that obedience brings them peace and prosperity. Ultimately,
therefore, the authority of any sovereign depends on the subjective feelings and
thoughts of those over whom he governs.
Both Hobbes and Spinoza are committed to naturalism. But, by making the state
(sovereignty) and the civil society rest on pacts and promises and by explaining
these in terms of obedience to a law of nature, Hobbes seems at least to reduce the
obedience due to the sovereign to an obedience due to a higher principle. People
should keep their promises because that is part of their natural duties, whereas
according to Spinoza people are committed to keeping their promises only as long
as they believe (rightly or wrongly) that it is in their interest to do so. Again, this
‘The Will of God’
53


may be no more than a question of conceptual economy, for actually Hobbes admits
that a word is not enough; indeed, that is the very reason why he believes that no
civil society can subsist without a sovereign:
The force of words, being […] too weak to hold men to the performance of their
covenants; there are in man’s nature but two imaginable helps to strengthen it. And those
are either a feare of the consequence of breaking their word; or a glory or pride in
appearing not to need to breake it. This later is a generosity too rarely found to be
presumed on, especially in the pursuers of wealth, command or sensuall pleasure, which
are the greatest part of mankind. The passion to be reckoned upon is fear.73
Covenants based on trust only are by definition void, except ‘if there be a common
power […] with right and force sufficient to compel performance’; that is, if there is
a sovereign who protects good faith against bad faith.74 And, of course, what is
trusted in such a case is not the other party but the sovereign. In the state of nature,
however, the only valid ‘covenants’ are those based on fear: ‘Covenants entered into
by fear, in the condition of meere nature, are obligatory.’75 Ultimately, therefore, a
covenant is valid and lasting only if all parties fear the consequences of breaking it.
So is right really nothing but might? According to Spinoza it is, but he admits
that, given the very nature of sovereign authority, the formula is strictly
meaningless: ‘The picture […] of the overriding right of sovereigns […] must
remain in many respects no more than theory.’76 In fact, ‘no government can be so
mighty that those in command have unlimited power’.77 ‘Authority’ is a dynamic
concept, which depends, not only on power, but also on the way power is perceived
and appreciated. If citizens judge, rightly or wrongly, that higher values are at stake
(their freedom, their honour, their faith) they may choose to ignore it and then the
sovereign has no authority, no matter the physical power (army, police, and so on)
he can mobilize.
Given the fact, on the other hand, that ultimately all authority depends on the
subjective perception of the subjects, there are other means than power to acquire
and preserve authority: Moses did not have an army at his disposal, but was
successful in obtaining sovereignty in virtue of an alleged divine power ‘which he
convinced the people that he possessed’.78 Generally speaking, the ways in which
authority can be established or increased are almost infinite:
He has another under his authority who holds him bound or has taken from him arms and
means of defence or escape or inspired him with fear or so attached him to himself by past
favour that the man obliged would rather please his benefactor than himself and live after
his own. He that has another under authority in the first or the second of these ways holds
but his body, not his mind. But in the third or fourth way he has made him dependent on
himself the mind as well as the body of the other; yet only as long as the fear or hope lasts
for upon the removal of the feeling the other is left independent.79
The best way to control people’s acts is to control their minds. Even according to the
Theologico-political treatise, which is generally – but wrongly – believed to be less
obsessed with strong government than the Political treatise, the commonwealth has
the duty to control minds because obedience and respect are ‘not so much a matter
of outward act as an internal act of the mind’.80 If the sovereign does not want to rely
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
54


exclusively on his power he must control the feelings and opinions of his subjects
because only ‘he who wholeheartedly resolves to obey another in all his commands
is fully under another’s dominion and consequently he who reigns over his subjects’
minds has the most powerful dominion’.81 This is not beyond the bounds of the
possible: ‘There is no absurdity in conceiving men whose beliefs, love, hatred,
contempt and every single emotion is under the control of a sovereign power’, even
if ‘command cannot be exercised over minds in the same way as over tongues’.82
In the end the question is not whether a sovereign should influence and control
minds – indeed, by issuing laws, that is, by giving his subjects things to fear or to
expect, he continually and inevitably does – but to what extent he can. The answer
entirely depends on the circumstances. Spinoza never questions Moses’ right to
impose a law by invoking a special revelation, but praises him for his political
wisdom.83 But he also praises the Macedonians for being ‘too sensible’ to adore
Alexander the Great as a god.84 From Spinoza’s point of view the religious notions
involved are not specifically different, the God of Israel being as imaginary as
Alexander’s Divinity. The only reason in fact why Moses enjoyed a right denied to
Alexander is that he dealt with a different people, over whom he had a different kind
of power. Moses, whom Spinoza sees as an ordinary king,85 had other means to
assert his authority because he was believed to be a prophet and because his subjects
were more credulous.
So a sovereign is not free in choosing the means to preserve his authority. Those
depend, not on his own force, but on the traditions, the intellectual level, the history
of his people, in brief on what they believe to be true and valuable: ‘Reason and
experience tell us quite clearly that the preservation of the state (imperii) depends
mainly on the subjects’ loyalty and virtue and their steadfastness in carrying out
orders.’86 More specifically, the Political treatise identifies three factors that limit
any sovereign’s rights. First, ‘that commonwealth will be most powerful and most
independent which is founded and guided by reason’.87 Any unreasonable act of the
ruler punishes itself by a weakening of the commonwealth and therefore – since the
weakening of the commonwealth will be attributed to him – by a weakening of his
authority. Second, ‘such things as no one can be induced to do by rewards or threats
do not fall within the rights of the commonwealth’.88 Any attempt to make the
citizens think or do things they cannot possibly think or do (because they are
contrary to what they believe to be true or sacred) undermines the ruler’s authority.
Third, the sovereign must not take decisions that cause indignation in the majority,
because ‘it is certain that the power and right of the commonwealth are so far
diminished as it gives occasion for many to conspire together’.89 Since no ruler,
howsoever powerful, can resist the will of the majority, he must not give his people
reasons for forming a majority against him.
All this works both ways. A sovereign who can think rationally will find the
means to assert his authority and keep it. But people who can think for themselves
cannot be duped by their sovereign: ‘A judgement can be dependent on another only
in so far as that other can deceive the mind.’90 The more people can think, the less
possibility there is to control their minds: ‘In as much as human power is to be
reckoned less by physical vigour than by mental strength, it follows that those men
are most independent whose reason is strongest and who are most guided by it.’91
That still does not mean that critical citizens are a threat to authority:
‘The Will of God’
55


Reason altogether teaches to seek peace and peace cannot be maintained unless the
commonwealth’s general laws be kept unbroken. And so, the more a man is guided by
reason, that is, the more he is free, the more constantly he will keep the laws of the
commonwealth and execute the decrees of the supreme authority whose subject he is.
Furthermore, the civil state is naturally ordained to remove general fear and prevent
general suffering and therefore pursues above everything the very end after which
everyone who is led by reason strives but in the natural state strives vainly. Wherefore, if
a man who is led by reason has sometimes to do by the commonwealth’s order what he
knows to be repugnant to reason, that harm is far compensated by the good he derives from
the existence of a civil state. For it is reason’s own law to choose the less of two evils; and
accordingly we may conclude that no one is acting against the dictate of his own reason if
he does what by the law of the commonwealth is to be done.92
No true philosopher limits the right of any sovereign, given the fact that he
understands the sovereign’s indispensability as a guardian of peace. As a result, no
sovereign should be afraid of granting philosophers the freedom to philosophize.
Indeed, by giving his people a chance to know the truth he turns them into loyal
subjects – an important argument of course against the background of the general
plea developed in the Theologico-political treatise.
All being said, the best way for a government to retain its right is ‘by granting to
the individual citizen the right to have his own opinions and to say what he thinks’,
– if only because in a modern state it is virtually impossible to control the thoughts
of all.93 This creates a delicate problem with respect to religion. For, whereas
philosophy is by definition universal because it is based on reason, prophetic beliefs
are by definition idiosyncratic because they are based on the imagination.94
Moreover, since the peculiar nature of those beliefs prevents them from being
corrected by reason and experience there is little one can do to change them into true
beliefs. Finally, given the imaginative nature of religious beliefs, those who hold
them will be firmly convinced that their beliefs are true and as firmly convinced that
the beliefs of others are false. This creates what one may call the paradox of revealed
religion: in so far as it produces a particular type of behaviour it is a contribution to
peace; but in so far as it rests on a particular type of beliefs it is divisive and
undermines the unity of the commonwealth. Revealed religion is always a political
problem.
Religion and the State
The will of God – the ‘moral code’ – can become a source of obligations only if it
is mediated (‘interpreted’) by a positive law. Indeed, the sovereign is ‘the only
interpreter of the will of God’. He is an ‘interpreter’ in the sense in which a prophet
is an interpreter: he does not explain his decisions (in any case his decisions do
not derive their validity from such an explanation) any more than a prophet explains
his teachings: their certainty derives from his authority. The attitude of subjects
should be one of obedience, as the attitude towards the prophets is one of trust
and faith. However, if the sovereign is the interpreter of God’s will one cannot
without undermining the position of the sovereign allow other interpretations of
God’s will to have the same, let alone more, authority. The extraordinary position
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
56


of Moses was due to the fact that ‘God’s decree was revealed to [Moses]
alone’ whereas ‘ordinarily a monarchy is ruled in accordance with a decree which
is hidden even from the monarch’.95 This made it possible for him to invoke divine
authority for his law without making it possible for others to test his ‘interpretation’
of the will of God against their own version of it. God’s command could never
be used against Moses because he consulted God ‘alone in his tent’ (in suo
tentorio).96
Religion cannot be free, whether we define it as behaviour or as the thoughts and
ideas that motivate it (‘faith’). Indeed, if the point of sovereignty is to give an earthly
lawgiver control over other people’s behaviour, the thoughts that motivate that
behaviour cannot leave him indifferent. On the other hand, the instruments he can
use to influence those thoughts are limited for at least two reasons: a belief is not an
act of obedience but a judgment, and in a pluralist society it is impossible to impose
a belief without losing one’s authority over a part of the population. As a result we
seem to be facing another paradox: the sovereign should attempt to influence the
thoughts and feelings of his people (more particularly their ‘faith’) but he cannot do
so without losing his authority. Again, to form a clearer picture we may turn to
Hobbes.
According to Hobbes the aim people had in mind when instituting a sovereign was
‘their own preservation and […] a more contented life thereby, that is to say, […]
getting themselves out from that miserable condition of warre which is necessarily
consequent […] to the naturall passions of men, when there is no visible power to
keep them in awe and tye them by feare of punishment to the performance of their
covenants and observation of [the] laws of nature’.97 Peace is possible only if there
is a sovereign. The sovereign’s rights, however, which form ‘the essence of
soveraignty’, are indivisible.98 To renounce a single one of them is to renounce
sovereignty as a whole, for ‘a kingdome divided in it selfe cannot stand’.99 Now
among those rights is, according to Hobbes, the right ‘to be judge of what opinions
and doctrines are averse and what conducing to peace; and, consequently, on what
occasions, how farre, and what, men are to be trusted withall in speaking to
multitudes of people; and who shall examine the doctrines of all bookes before they
be published’.100 Hobbes concedes that the only criterion for accepting a doctrine is
its truth: ‘Nothing ought to be regarded but the truth.’101 However, ‘a doctrine
repugnant to peace can no more be true than peace and concord can be against the
law of nature’.102 Religious beliefs demand special care:
The first founders and legislators of commonwealths amongst the Gentiles […] have in all
places taken care, first, to imprint in [the people’s] minds a beliefe that those precepts
which they gave concerning religion might not be thought to proceed from their own
device but from the dictates of some God or some other Spirit […] Secondly, they have
had a care to make it believed that the same things were displeasing to the gods which were
forbidden by the lawes. Thirdly, to prescribe ceremonies, supplications, sacrifices and
festivalls, by which they were to believe the anger of the Gods might be appeased […] And
by these and such other institutions, they obtained in order to their end (which was the
peace of the commonwealth) that the common people in their misfortunes, laying the fault
on neglect or errour in their ceremonies or on their own disobedience to the lawes, were
the lesse apt to mutiny against their governors. […] And thus you see how the religion of
the Gentiles was a part of their policy.103
‘The Will of God’
57


Hobbes is prudent enough to restrict his argument to the Gentiles; indeed, he admits
that in a true theocracy things are different.104 But since he also believes – as we shall
see in chap. 5 – that a theocracy is not possible, the obvious implication is that the
rights of a Christian king are not fundamentally different from those of a Gentile
king. So if the heathen king has ‘the right to teach’, that is, to impose, in the
interest of peace, a specific belief, a Christian king has that right too: ‘This right [the
right to teach] of heathen kings cannot bee thought taken from them by their
conversion to the faith of Christ, who never ordained that kings for beleeving in him
should be deposed, that is, subjected to any but himself or (which is all one) be
deprived of the power necessary for the conservation of peace amongst their subjects
and for their defence against foraign enemies.’105 Hobbes admits that a belief as such
cannot be an act of obedience and, inversely, that obedience is not ‘a submission of
the intellectual faculty to the opinion of some other man but of the will’.106
Accordingly, what a sovereign can actually ask from his subjects is not conviction
but behaviour: ‘We captivate our understanding and reason when we forbear
contradiction; when we so speak as (by lawfull authority) we are commanded; and
when we live accordingly; which in sum is trust, and faith reposed in him that
speaketh, though the mind be incapable of any notion at all from the words
spoken.’107 If we are convinced that p implies q, nobody can make us believe or
judge that p does not imply q, but we can legitimately be forced to act and to speak
as if p does not imply q. Accordingly, the sovereign has the right to teach to the
precise extent that he can force his subjects to act and to speak as if a belief which
he believes to be in the interest of the peace and therefore teaches to be true is
actually true.
Spinoza does not look at the question in absolutely different terms; indeed,
whatever differences there are must probably be explained by the political and
historical differences between England and Holland. According to Spinoza it is
better not to try to impose a belief at all because there will always be people who
disagree – so the very act of imposing a belief would cause disobedience. Moreover,
people who think for themselves cannot be made to believe that something actually
false is true. Finally, as we have seen, if the sovereign should try to impose a belief,
all people would resent that as an intrusion on their liberty, which in turn would
ultimately undermine the sovereign’s authority.
Unlike Hobbes, who solves the problem by placing the Christian king at the head
of a national Church, Spinoza does not believe that the problem can be solved by
political means; all one can do is either show (by an interpretation of Scripture) that
‘religion’ actually entails obedience to the sovereign or propose institutional
arrangements such that the negative consequences are minimized. Both strategies
are adopted in the Theologico-political treatise, which, although it generally tackles
the problem on the level of rhetoric and persuasion, in chap. 19 considers an
institutional solution.
Again, according to Spinoza, God ‘has no special kingdom over men save through
those who hold sovereignty’.108 Accordingly, it is logically impossible that someone
who is not sovereign could claim any authority in the name of God; indeed, all the
authority a person or an institution could have must in some form or another derive
from that of the sovereign. As a result, those who claim independent authority for
the Church ‘make a division of sovereignty, paving the way to their own
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
58


supremacy’.109 In fact, sovereigns are interpreters and guardians of all law, whether
of reason, of nature or of God: ‘justice and in sum all the precepts of true reason,
including charity towards one’s neighbour, acquire the force of law and command
only from the right of the state, that is […] from the decree of those who possess the
right to command’.110 Patriotism (pietas patriae) is ‘the highest form of piety that
can be shown; for if the state is destroyed, nothing good can survive, everything is
endangered and anger and wickedness reign supreme amidst universal fear’.111 To
replace patriotism with something else is not in the interest of the commonwealth
and therefore not in the interest of the people: ‘The welfare of the people is the
highest law, to which all other laws, both human and divine must be adapted.’112
However, since ‘it is the duty of the sovereign alone to decide what is necessary for
the welfare of the entire people and the security of the state, and to command what
he judges to be necessary, it follows that it is also the duty of the sovereign alone to
decide what form piety towards one’s neighbour should take, that is, in what way
every man is required to obey God’.113
The solution is basically the same as Hobbes’s. All ecclesiastical authority derives
from the authority of a sovereign: ‘No one [but the sovereign] has the right and
power to exercise control over [religion], to choose its ministers, to determine and
establish the foundations of the church and its doctrine, to pass judgement on
morality and acts of piety, to excommunicate or to accept into the church, and to
provide for the poor, except by the authority and permission of the sovereign.’114
Ministers of God’s word are those only ‘who are authorised by their sovereign to
teach piety in the form that by decree of the sovereign is adapted to the public
good’.115 This is essential ‘both to religion and to the preservation of the state for
everyone knows how much importance the people attach to the right and authority
over religion and how they all revere every single word of him that possesses that
authority, so that one might even go so far as to say that he to whom that authority
belongs has the most effective control over the minds’.116 The sovereign will retain
this right, ‘provided [he does] not allow religious dogma to be multiplied or
confused with science’.117 Faith is not knowledge; indeed, it is not even true belief.
By confusing faith with truth a sovereign undermines his authority.
The question is how that works in practice. Although Spinoza does not speak of
this in the Theologico-political treatise (except in a context to which I revert in chap.
5), the Political treatise contains two more or less precise accounts of the way it
works in an aristocracy and in a monarchy. According to Spinoza an aristocracy has
the best chance of survival if patricians embrace the universal faith outlined in chap.
14 of the Theologico-political treatise, to which he explicitly refers. They should not
belong to different sects and ‘show favour, some to this and others to that, and
thence become mastered by superstition and try to deprive the subjects of the liberty
of speaking out their opinions’. Religious denominations must have freedom of
worship; they may have their temples but ‘these must be small and limited to a
certain standard of size and built on sites at a little distance from one another’. The
temples of the national (patria) religion, however, should be large and costly and
‘only patricians and senators should be allowed to administer its principal rites’. In
this way patricians become recognizable ‘as the guardians [vindices] and interpreters
of the religion of the country’. Preaching and the management of church affairs on
the other hand should be left to commoners: what we would call civil servants.118
‘The Will of God’
59


The fact that Spinoza refers to the patricians as ‘guardians’ and ‘interpreters’ of
religion (this being ‘universal faith’) shows that he has not changed his view and
therefore that the position outlined in the Theologico-political treatise is not
incompatible with religious pluralism. After all, Spinoza acknowledges the
existence of various denominations, which do have freedom of worship as long as
they respect the law. However, over and above these particular denominations there
is some sort of public Church based on ‘universal faith’. According to Spinoza, its
rites mainly concern events such as marriage, birth and so on. What he has in mind
therefore is some sort of ‘état civil’, whose language and rhetoric would be
‘religious’. Other activities of the Church – preaching, teaching, charity – are left to
commoners, probably to prevent patricians from becoming involved in religious and
theological controversies. As a result, we may presumably interpret Spinoza’s
proposal as an attempt to realize as much of Hobbes’s idea of a public Church as the
Dutch situation of religious pluralism allows.
The surprising thing is that Spinoza does not find this arrangement suitable for a
monarchy. According to the Political treatise, there should be freedom of worship
under a monarch (although temples should be paid for by the faithful themselves),
but ‘the king may have in his palace a chapel of his own to practise the religion to
which he belongs’.119 So under a king, apparently, religion is entirely a private affair,
which requires no government involvement at all. Whence this difference? Since
Spinoza does not even raise the question, any answer must be hypothetical. A
possible answer may be that the chapters on monarchic and aristocratic government
date from different periods. Given the fact that the work was never published – or
even achieved – by Spinoza himself, that is a distinct possibility, but it is not a very
interesting option. Another possibility is that it is inspired by the circumstances. In
1672, William III assumed for himself the powers of a monarch and it could be the
case that Spinoza simply did not trust either him or any other members of the Orange
family. Another possible answer is that a king, although himself a Calvinist,
Lutheran, Catholic or whatever, would rule over a mixed population. He would not
be able to renounce his faith; indeed, that would probably weaken his authority. Nor
would he be able to impose his own faith, for that would also weaken his authority.
Accordingly, the best way for him to retain his authority would be to keep his own
religion and worship in the privacy of his chapel, while giving unlimited freedom to
other denominations.
Conclusion
Like Hobbes and other 17th-century theorists of sovereignty, Spinoza seems to
believe that a strong central power is necessary. The reason for this is generally that
the Reformation deeply divided the population – a division which in many cases (the
Habsburg Empire, France, the Low Countries, England) had important political
consequences. At the same time this was seen to imply that the sovereign should
take a more or less neutral stance: that he should either be above all religious
diversity or place himself at the head of a national and public Church, provided this
had a limited dogmatic basis. Spinoza takes something from each of these solutions
but at the same time seems to be acutely aware of their limitations. If I am right,
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
60


these are caused mainly by two factors, which Spinoza may have seen at work in his
native town of Amsterdam: (a) a strongly divided population – so much so that no
particular denomination has a clear majority; (b) the intellectual level of the
population. Both would make it simply impossible for the sovereign to exercise ‘the
right to teach’. Moreover, for someone so fundamentally conservative as Spinoza,
the fact that the Netherlands hardly ever had any form of central government must
have been a decisive reason not to endorse the Hobbesian model, despite the fact that
he is tempted by it, as were his friends.120 And, indeed, in the Political treatise at any
rate, Spinoza does endorse the Hobbesian model for an aristocratic context, which,
given the aristocratic nature of Dutch government, may be significant enough. But
the underlying reason may be more personal, namely that, being a defence of his
own philosophy and of the freedom to publish it, the Theologico-political treatise
can hardly be expected to mount a defence of the sovereign’s ‘right to teach’. Still,
thoughts and words are not unimportant: the sovereign should at least mind the
words and thoughts of his citizens even if his power to influence them is limited.
A thing that could be worrying is the seemingly complete identification of moral
values with the values incorporated in a positive law – indeed, any positive law.
Although most people do not hesitate to attribute that position to Hobbes (or for that
matter to many 18th-century philosophers, and as La Mettrie or D’Holbach) there
seems to be much reluctance to do the same for Spinoza, whose main work after all
is called Ethica. But apart from the fact that it would be wrong to take ‘ethics’
completely in our own post-Kantian sense as a doctrine on duty or on ‘what should
be done’, we should, as far as Spinoza is concerned, probably make a distinction
between three different levels of ‘morality’. The first and most general is that on
which one can say that peace is necessary, not because we ‘should’ strive for peace
or are ‘obliged’ to do whatever we can to realize peace, but because, even if
sometimes we do prefer war because we do not see the advantages of peace, peace
is the only way to survive. It is on that level that Hobbes speaks of a ‘Law of Nature’
and that Spinoza speaks of the ‘Will of God’. It is on that level too that there is some
room for criticizing the sovereign – a sovereign who does not manage or does not
want to achieve peace simply continues the state of nature or allows the
commonwealth to fall back into the state of nature – and on that level too that one
can make a broad distinction between atheism and piety. Spinoza remains cautious,
though, about granting citizens an explicit right to revolt, which in any event seems
to him the worst solution:
Now perhaps at this point I shall be asked: ‘Then if those who hold sovereignty choose to
be impious, who will be the rightful champion of piety? Are the rulers still to be regarded
as the interpreters of religion?’ […] It is indeed true that if those who hold sovereignty
choose to go what way they will, then, with or without control over religion, all things,
religious and secular, will go to ruin: but this will come about far more quickly if private
citizens seditiously seek to be champions of religious law.121
On a second level, this broad injunction to strive for peace must be ‘interpreted’, that
is, translated into specific rules or laws. This is done by prophets and rulers but, even
if prophets did once play a historical role within a specific context, the right to do so
should now be reserved to the second because they alone are powerful enough to
create a credible system of sanctions by which people can be obliged to behave in
‘The Will of God’
61


accordance with those rules. This implies of course that either all other would-be
interpreters of God’s will – prophets and theologians – should be subject to the
authority of the sovereign, or their words should be reinterpreted in such a way that
they become harmless.122 Finally, the true philosopher can seek and find lasting
happiness under any circumstances by trying to understand things sub specie
aeternitatis.123 That viewpoint is further developed in the Ethics.
Two or three other circumstances are essential for understanding Spinoza’s
position. First of all, in the 17th century the means to exercise control were much
more limited than they are now. The state had little power and whatever social
control there was was exercised locally, most of the time, one must presume, by the
Church. Second, whatever democratic sentiments were around were generally
inspired by religious motives and incited by ‘prophetic’ leaders, whether these were
right-wing orthodox Calvinists, who asked for a complete reformation of society, or
Anabaptists, who rejected all forms of authority. Indeed, as we shall see in more
detail when discussing Spinoza’s idea of the theocratic variants of government, what
he distrusts most in them is precisely the power they give to the people. In fact, what
we find in the background of Spinoza’s political philosophy is the threatening
presence of what he usually calls vulgus or multitudo, which frightens unless it is
checked by fear: terret vulgus nisi metuat.124 It is impervious to rational argument,
being governed, not by reason, but by the passions.125 As a result, it can be made to
live in accordance with reason only by means of a common passion, which may be
hope, or fear, or a mixture of both.126
According to Spinoza, reason is not, as it is in Descartes, accessible to anybody at
any time. It is a specific style of thinking, which it is difficult to acquire at all given
the fact that man is born as a passionate rather than a rational creature: ‘All men are
born in a state of complete ignorance and before they can learn the true way of life
[…] a great part of their life has gone by.’127 Accordingly, there are only two ways
of making the multitude live in accordance with reason: political authority and
religion – in that order, for one of Spinoza’s points is that religion is helpful only if
it is radically subordinated to political authority. Again, the question is whether that
is possible. A full discussion of it requires a clear view of the nature of revelation
(prophecy) and its interpretation, which are the subject of the two following
chapters.
