Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
66


Chapter 3
‘The Word of God’
As we have seen in chap. 1, Spinoza sees the expression ‘will of God’ as a metaphor
for a moral code, whose highest ‘norm’ is peace. Its precise content is, as I showed
in chap. 2, always mediated by a sovereign, who for that reason is called ‘an
interpreter of the will of God’. Objectively, therefore, the only way to know what
God ‘wills’ is to know the will of an earthly sovereign, that is, a positive law.
Traditionally, however, there is an alternative way of becoming aware of ‘God’s
will’, namely, by means of ‘theology’, or the interpretation of a text (Scripture) to
which ‘authority’ is attributed. Moreover, as we have also seen, ‘the will of God’ can
also be understood as an eternal truth by philosophy and reason. Now, there can be
no tension between the will of God as it is understood by reason and as it is
interpreted by a sovereign, given the fact that someone who knows the truth about
nature will understand that the sovereign is the only legitimate guardian of peace;
but there may be a tension between the will of God as it is interpreted by a sovereign
and as it is interpreted by theology. This raises complex problems which can be
solved only at the cost of theology. In this chapter I deal with the authority attributed
to Scripture; in the next with the status of the interpretation of Scripture; in the
chapter thereafter with theocracy as government by means of a law revealed in
Scripture.
A Natural History of Prophecy
The ideal basis of ‘religion’ and ‘piety’, defined as ‘moral behaviour’ and ‘habitual
moral behaviour’ respectively, is true philosophy, given the fact that it is not only
true but also certain.1 ‘True religion’, based on devotion, let alone ‘superstition’,
based on hope and fear, are inferior not only because they are not based on certain
knowledge but also because of the emotions involved. Devotion wears off according
as the object is better known.2 And hope and fear are variable in the extreme and
therefore unreliable as motives.3 The certainty of true philosophy on the other hand
provides an unshaken foundation for ‘religion’ and ‘piety’. But, again, in so far as
there can be a pragmatic equivalence of philosophy and faith (or revelation), it is
always mediated by a sovereign and that means that it is only in so far as faith and
revelation stimulate obedience to the sovereign that they can be equivalent to
philosophy at all. But how can prophets and theologians be led to recommend
obedience to a sovereign if they attribute to God an authority higher than to any
mortal person? Unlike Hobbes, Spinoza does not believe that there is a political
solution to that problem, as we saw in chap. 2. Accordingly, Spinoza must resort to
persuasion, that is, he must critically examine the ‘authority’ of Scripture and
theology and show that what is actually involved is not ‘authority’ but judgment. So
67


to attribute authority to Scripture would mean to believe that Scripture contains the
truth and nothing but the truth and, like any other belief, that belief must be justified.
But before that can be done prophecy must be described as a psychological and to a
certain extent sociological phenomenon. It is that ‘natural history’ that is recorded in
chaps 1 and 2 of the Theologico-political treatise.
According to Spinoza, prophecy is produced by the imagination without any help
from the intellect: ‘Prophets perceived God’s revelations with the aid of the
imaginative faculty alone, that is, through the medium of words and images, either
real or imaginary.’4 In the first chapter of the Theologico-political treatise this is
used to explain three facts about prophecy. First, it explains why prophets perceived
much that is beyond the reach of the intellect for ‘many more ideas can be
constructed from words and images than from the principles and axioms that form
the basis of all our natural knowledge’.5 Second, it explains the use of allegories and
parables, literary forms that have nothing to do with logical reasoning: ‘Now we see
why the perceptions and teachings of the prophets were nearly all in the form of
parables and riddles and why they referred to spiritual matters as if they were
bodies.’6 And third, it explains why prophecy is sporadic: ‘The imaginative faculty
being fleeting and inconstant, the gift of prophecy did not remain with the prophets
for long, nor did it often occur.’7 In chap. 2 Spinoza adds three other things. Fourth,
it explains why men without learning and even women could be prophets:
‘Countrymen without learning – indeed, even women of humble station, like Hagar,
the handmaid of Abraham – were endowed with the gift of prophecy.’8 Fifth, it
explains why prophecy is not intrinsically certain because ‘imagination by itself,
unlike clear and distinct ideas, does not of its own nature carry certainty with it’.9
And sixth, it explains why prophecy reflects the personality, temperament and
beliefs of the prophet: ‘Revelation also varied […] in the case of each prophet
according to his temperament, the nature of his imagination and the beliefs he had
previously held.’10 In a fundamental way all these explanations presuppose
Spinoza’s epistemology and more particularly his distinction between the
imagination and the intellect, which must be elucidated first.11
According to the Ethics, the mind is the idea of an actually existing human body
– ‘and of nothing else.’12 This means that primarily the mind perceives nothing but
its body or in other words that the mind ‘imagines’ before it can ‘think’. It knows
the external world only in so far as it has an effect on the body.13 And it knows itself
only in so far as it perceives the ideas of those affections.14 The epistemological
relevance of the distinction between the imagination and the intellect is that the ideas
of the intellect are adequate (and therefore true and known to be true), whereas those
of the imagination are not.15 Still, in the Ethics this is presented as a difference of
degree rather than kind, possibly because in the end Spinoza rejects the existence of
faculties: ‘Falsehood consists in the lack of knowledge involved in inadequate, that
is, mutilated and confused ideas.’16 Accordingly, it would seem that a gradual
clarification of ideas is enough to overcome the distinction between imagination and
reason.17
Inadequate ideas do have a ‘logic’ of their own, though, which is not only
different from that of clear and distinct ideas but also as compelling or perhaps even
more: ‘Inadequate and confused ideas follow each other [consequuntur] with the
same necessity as adequate and clear and distinct ideas.’18 Ideas of the imagination
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
68


are connected and formed by the laws of association, which are ultimately physical
laws and therefore as necessary as the laws of collision.19 It means, however, that the
imagination reflects the personal history of the individual, whereas the results of
intellectual thinking are universally valid, being based on principles shared by all.
In the Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect on the other hand the intellect
is presented as a means to constrain and control the imaginative faculty by the use
of principles like causality, uniformity, intelligibility, and so on. So the more we
know, the less we are free to imagine and the less we know, the more the
imagination is ‘free’, that is, the more it follows the particular idiosyncrasy of the
imagining subject: ‘Less people know about nature, the more easily they fantasise
about speaking trees, men changing into stones or fountains, spectres appearing in
mirrors, nothing becoming something, gods changing into animals or into men,
etc.’20 In so far as its workings are based on the laws of the body, the imagination is
basically passive, whereas intellectual knowledge is a product of the activity of the
intellect. So there is a very real and profound distinction between the imagination
and the intellect.
The theory of the Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect is also the one
presupposed in the discussion of prophecy: ‘Those with a more powerful
imagination are less fit for purely intellectual activity, while those who do more by
their intellect and devote themselves to its cultivation keep their imagination under
greater control and restraint and they hold it in rein, as it were, so that it should not
invade the province of the intellect’21 – this clearly being a province of truth and
knowledge. So if prophets are characterized by a powerful imagination, that implies
that they are bad thinkers. In fact, being based ‘on imagination alone’, prophecy is
under no intellectual control whatsoever, and that would mean that it is exactly like
dreaming or madness.22 Unlike ordinary experience, which is also a form of
‘imagination’, prophecy itself does not contribute anything to that control either:
‘The gift of prophecy did not make prophets more learned than they were but left
them with the beliefs they previously held.’23 The reason is, presumably, that the
prophetic ‘experience’ (the fact of hearing voices and having visions) is essentially
private whereas most other beliefs are based on shared experiences and common
principles. Whereas in ordinary circumstances people submit their beliefs to the test
of the experiences and the judgments of other people, a prophet is in a way a prisoner
of his own subjectivity. Moreover, given the fact that the vulgar are always ‘eager
for what is strange and foreign to their own nature’, prophets are encouraged in that
attitude by their audience.24 In any case, it would be folly to expect from them
‘knowledge of science and spiritual matters’.25
It is widely believed, though, that their words are God’s words, that prophecy is
‘inspired’ by the Holy Ghost. Spinoza refutes that claim by means of an
interpretation of Scripture, aimed at showing that the use of the expression ‘Spirit of
God’ – as in ‘the spirit of the Lord God is upon me’ (Is. 61:1) – does not mean that
a prophet is literally inspired by a higher agency. Indeed, all meanings of ‘spirit’
derive from the basic meaning, ‘wind’.26 Expressions like ‘divine’, on the other
hand, are often used in the sense of ‘extraordinary’, ‘above average’ and so on.27 So
‘Spirit of God’, or ‘Divine Spirit’, sometimes means a ‘very fierce, dry, deadly
wind’ (as in Is. 40:7; Gen. 1:2 and so on), sometimes ‘high courage’ (as in Ex. 31:3;
Is. 11:2 and so on) or ‘a man’s mind’ (as in Job 27:3; Ez. 37:14 and so on),
‘The Word of God’
69


sometimes ‘God’s mind’ (as in Is. 40:13, 63:11; Deut. 4:6 and so on), ‘breath of
God’ (Ps. 33:6; Job 33:4 and so on), ‘emotions of God’ (Mich. 2:7; Zach. 4:6) and
so on and so forth.28 All it means in contexts like ‘the Spirit of the Lord is upon a
prophet’ is that prophets were men of uncommon virtue, who ‘devoted themselves
to piety with particular constancy’.29 Or also that they ‘perceived the mind and
thought of God’30 – which of course is the same thing. Or finally that other people –
a prophet’s audience – considered his performance a miracle.31 So on the whole
Spinoza acknowledges the extraordinary status of prophets (expressed by terms like
‘Spirit of God’) but he ascribes it either to their virtue or to the way other people
looked at them.
Despite all this, Spinoza seems to acknowledge the point that it is not the prophets
themselves that speak: ‘The Hebrew word for prophet is nabi, that is, speaker
[orator] and interpreter [interpres] but in Scripture it is always used in the sense of
interpreter of God.’32 The prophet is not an ‘orator’ (that is, a rhetorician) but an
‘interpreter’. Of course, one should understand the meaning of ‘interpretation’. The
fact that someone (a prophet, a sovereign) is an ‘interpreter’ means that he has a
peculiar way of speaking, which relies, not on persuasion (rhetoric) or logic
(philosophy), but on trust  and ‘authority’.33 Whatever he says is held to be true not
because his arguments are convincing but because he has authority: ‘An interpreter
of God is one who has a revelation of God’s decrees, which he interprets to others
who have not had this revelation and who accept it solely in reliance on the prophet’s
authority and the confidence he enjoys […]. In the same way are sovereigns the
interpreters of their own sovereign right and are the laws they enact upheld only by
their own sovereign authority and supported only by their own testimony.’34
‘Interpretation’ is translation only in the sense in which a family doctor can be said
to ‘translate’ medical knowledge into a particular advice or therapy. His advice can
be followed up without the reason behind it being understood by the patient. But just
as a student of medicine can criticize the doctor’s advice, a philosopher can criticize
a prophet:
The mind of God and his eternal thoughts are inscribed in our minds and therefore we also
perceive ‘the mind of God’ – to use the language of Scripture. But since natural knowledge
is common to all men, it is not so highly prized […] and particularly in the case of the
Hebrews, who vaunted themselves above all men; indeed, who despised all men and
consequently the sort of knowledge common to all men.35
A philosopher also ‘knows the mind of God and his eternal thoughts’, although if he
were allowed to use a language different from Scripture he would probably say
‘nature and its laws’. So he does not need the ‘inward testimony’ of a prophet, that
is, what is traditionally called ‘revelation’.
Interpretation requires us to give up the will to understand or criticize. If a prophet
has ‘authority’ that means that we trust him unconditionally. If we were to subject
his words to the test of reason or accept his words only in so far as they agree with
reason he would no longer have ‘authority’, just as a sovereign would have no
authority at all if we obeyed him only in so far as we agreed with his laws and
decisions. Indeed, whatever a prophet, or a sovereign, says is held to be true not
because we would be convinced by valid arguments but because it is said by him.
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
70


Still, we may ask an authority, and therefore a prophet, to show us his ‘credentials’,
not to test the truth of whatever he says, but to examine the source of his authority.
Spinoza calls these ‘signs’. It is to them that we must turn now.
Signs and Reasons
Apart from being traditional, the idea of ‘sign’ fits the idea of the prophet as an
‘interpreter’. We trust an interpreter (in the ordinary sense) because he studied at
university or is member of a professional organization, not because we can check his
translation. We could call the reasons for attributing ‘authority’ to an interpreter (a
prophet, a sovereign, anyone we trust) ‘signs’ to distinguish them from
demonstrative arguments. ‘Signs’ are reasons for attributing to someone a position
of ‘authority’ and trust.
In the present context, however, the notion of ‘sign’ creates more problems than
it solves. Spinoza admits that signs are not always specified: ‘Scripture does not
always relate in full every detail and circumstance, as many scholars have remarked,
but tends rather to take such things for granted.’36 This would mean that the authority
of a particular prophet rests on the authority of Scripture as a whole; that the fact that
a prophecy belongs to Scripture is enough to warrant its truth. Circularity would
probably be the result (unless we have ‘signs’ on the basis of which we can attribute
authority to Scripture as a whole).37 Of even more significance is that Spinoza makes
no difference between the ‘signs’ required from a prophet and the signs required by
a prophet: between the signs by which we judge that one’s claim to be a prophet is
legitimate and the signs by which a prophet knows that he had a valid revelation.
According to Hobbes, this makes an enormous difference: ‘When God speaketh to
man, it must be either immediately; or by mediation of another man, to whom he had
formerly spoken by himself immediately.’38 The first is ‘revelation’ and the second
is ‘prophecy’. Whereas according to Hobbes there are no universally valid signs for
the first, ‘how God speaketh to a man immediately, may be understood by those well
enough, to whom he hath so spoken’,39 he requires very specific signs for the second.
By making no difference between the two – requiring, as we shall see, the same
signs in both cases – Spinoza creates a confusion that was carefully avoided by
Hobbes. This confusion occurs on almost all levels, to start with what Spinoza says
about the grounds of prophetic certainty:
Prophetic certainty [certitudo prophetica] was based entirely on these three things: 1. That
the things revealed were most vividly imagined, just as we are wont to be affected by
objects in our waking hours; 2. the occurrence of a sign; 3. lastly and most important, that
the minds of the prophets were directed exclusively towards what is right and good.40
This is reformulated in a slightly different way in chap. 15 and in a context
dominated by the notion of authority:
The authority of the Bible is dependent on the authority of the prophets, and can thus have
no stronger arguments to support it than those by which the prophets of old were wont to
convince the people of their authority. Indeed, our own certainty as to this authority can
have no other foundation than that on which the prophets based their certainty and
‘The Word of God’
71


authority. Now, we have shown that the certainty of the prophets rested entirely on these
three factors – first a distinct and vivid imagination; second, a sign; third, and most
important, a heart turned to what is right and good.41
First of all, the credentials of each prophet should be examined individually: for a
particular prophecy to be a true prophecy it is not enough to be part of Scripture.
More importantly, Spinoza makes no difference between the ‘authority’ of a prophet
(our ‘certain’ belief that he is a prophet and that we may trust him) and the subjective
certainty of a prophet (his own ‘certain’ belief in the truth of what he says). Again,
here is what Hobbes has to say of it:
When a mans Discourse beginneth not at Definitions, it beginneth either at some other
contemplation of his own, and then it is still called Opinion; Or it beginneth at some saying
of another, of whose ability to know the truth, and of whose honesty in not deceiving, he
doubteth not; and then the Discourse is not so much concerning the Thing as the Person;
And the Resolution is called BELEEFE, and FAITH: Faith, in the man; Beleefe, both of the
man, and of the truth of what he sayes. So that in Beleefe are two opinions; one of the
saying of the man; the other of his vertue. To have faith in, or trust to, or beleeve a man,
signifie the same thing; namely, an opinion of the veracity of the man: But to beleeve what
is said, signifieth onely an opinion of the truth of the saying.42
The reason why a prophet believes his opinions to be true is that he had a vivid
experience. The reason why we believe them to be true is that we believe that he is
in fact a prophet; inversely, if we do believe that this or that particular man is a
prophet we no longer judge and evaluate the truth of what he says. Accordingly, the
difference between revelation and prophecy yields a corresponding difference
between the ‘signs’ used in judging the one and the other.
In fact, if the grounds for attributing authority to a prophet were the same as his
grounds for being subjectively certain of revelation that would be disastrous for
‘prophetic certainty’ in general even on Spinoza’s own terms. According to Spinoza
(citing Gen. 15:8, Judges 6:17 and Deut. 18:22) prophets often asked for a sign,
this turning out to be, if one refers to the places cited, the ability to predict
future events.43 But apart from the fact that Spinoza does not list this as a sign,
Scripture, as we saw, does not always disclose the sign actually given. Moreover,
Spinoza dismisses (like Hobbes for that matter) what he gives as the first sign: ‘The
first of these, their vivid imagination, was a personal quality confined to the
prophets…’44 The reason why a prophet believes he had a revelation is not
universally valid, dreams and hallucinations being as vivid presumably as prophetic
experiences,45 and in any case, given the fact that the prophetic experience is
strictly private, no quality of that experience can be alleged as a valid sign for us.
Moreover (but that is an argument from the Treatise on the Improvement of the
Intellect) the clearness and distinctness of ideas of the imagination is irrelevant
to their truth: ‘If we do not make a distinction between the imagination and the
intellect we believe that whatever we imagine easily is more clear and therefore
better understood, whereas all we actually do is to have an image of something.’46
Accordingly, even if the first ‘sign’ explains why prophets were subjectively certain
it is of no importance whatsoever to us. The authority of prophets, and the authority
of Scripture, comes to rest therefore on the two remaining ‘signs’, in spite of the fact,
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
72


presumably, that they do not contribute anything to the subjective certainty of the
prophet.
The second group of signs are miracles. The question is whether they are good
reasons for attributing ‘authority’ to a prophet. As it happens they are not. Miracles
can be spurious because the prophet that performs them is not a true prophet (like
the prophets of Pharaoh) or because ‘the Lord works signs and miracles to try his
people’.47 So a miracle is a miracle only if the prophet who works them is a true
prophet – and that means that without committing circularity we cannot use miracles
to evaluate the authenticity of a prophet. At best a true prophet ‘can be distinguished
from a false prophet by doctrine and miracles taken together’(emphasis added)48 and
that would mean that the ultimate sign is doctrine, not miracle. Finally, on a more
general level, there can be no miracles, as Spinoza extensively demonstrates in chap.
6. What generally passes as a miracle is nothing but ‘wonderful work’.49 Miracles
are unusual but nonetheless natural phenomena, called ‘works of God’ by the
ignorant only.50 People speak of miracles because ‘it is only by abolishing natural
causes and imagining supernatural events that they are able to worship God and refer
all things to God’s governance and God’s will’.51 In fact, however, miracles
undermine the belief in the uniformity of nature and therefore in the constancy of
God. Accordingly, a text reporting miraculous events is either an ‘impious’ text or a
text that has been tampered with:
If anything be found in Scripture which can be conclusively proved to contravene the laws
of nature or which could not possibly follow from them, we have to believe that this was
inserted by sacrilegious men. For whatever is contrary to nature is contrary to reason and
whatever is contrary to reason is absurd, and should therefore be rejected.52
If miracles were the only signs available, prophecy and Scripture would in fact be
extremely doubtful. Indeed, Moses ‘gives warning that if any prophet should seek to
introduce new gods, even if he should confirm his teaching by signs and wonders,
he must nevertheless be condemned to death’.53
What remains therefore is the third ‘sign’, that ‘the minds of the prophets were
directed exclusively towards what is right and good’. Prophets were eminently
virtuous men: ‘Since we see that the prophets commend above all else justice and
charity and have no other objective we may conclude that it was no evil intent but
sincere conviction that prompted them to teach that men may achieve blessedness by
obedience and faith.’54 So their virtue convinces us that they were sincere in
professing what is actually not evident at all, namely, that one can achieve
blessedness by obedience and faith. This would be a very weak reason if it were truth
we were looking for – indeed, the fact that someone is sincere is at best a guarantee
that he believes he speaks the truth, not that he in fact speaks the truth. Spinoza’s
official argument, however, is that ‘God never deceives the good and his chosen
ones’,55 meaning presumably that the words of a virtuous person must also be
virtuous. But that saves the authority of a prophet only as far as moral matters are
concerned. And, indeed, according to Spinoza the prophets were ‘ignorant of matters
that have no bearing on charity and moral conduct, so knowledge of science and of
matters spiritual must in no way be expected from them […] we must believe the
prophet only with regard to the purpose and substance of revelation; in all else one
‘The Word of God’
73


is free to believe as one will’.56 The ‘purpose and substance’ of revelation being
moral obedience, the rest is simply irrelevant; indeed, it is ‘adaptation’ to the false
beliefs and opinions either of the prophets themselves or of their audience. In any
case no ‘authority’ can be claimed for it, given the fact that the only ‘authority’ for
truth is reason. However, apart from the fact that, as Spinoza admits, this third sign
is a sign only for us, not for the prophet himself (which on Spinoza’s own criteria
would be enough to discredit it as a sign), on a more fundamental level its use
probably involves circularity.57 For if we can recognize a prophet only by the fact
that he teaches a true moral doctrine we must be able to judge and appreciate the
morality of that doctrine independently of the teaching of that prophet. We could test
his words against the teaching of another prophet but since his words too would have
to be tested that means, ultimately, that prophecy itself cannot be the source of our
knowledge of good and evil.
Spinoza admits that there is a problem but, according to him, there are other ways
of becoming aware of moral values. The first is reason: ‘All their [the prophets’]
moral teaching is in full agreement with reason, for it is no accident that the Word
of God proclaimed by the prophets agrees in all respects with the Word of God that
speaks in our hearts.’58 We can always compare the morality of a prophet (his
behaviour as well as his teaching) with the practical teachings of reason. Of course,
by using reason we leave the domain of ‘signs’ and ‘authority’ and make the
transition from interpretation to knowledge. Moreover, the use of reason is
unsatisfactory in the case of people who have no access to rational knowledge,
which is typically that of the audience of a prophet. For a philosopher, however,
it makes it possible to discriminate between the ‘Word of God’, that is, the
specifically moral doctrine proclaimed by a prophet (in so far as it is a contribution
to peace), and all other aspects of his discourse, which are covered by the term
‘adaptation’ and which from an objective point of view are either false or simply
irrelevant.
The second way of becoming aware of moral principles is a positive law: ‘We can
allow that when their prophecy revealed nothing beyond what was contained in the
Law of Moses the prophets stood in no need of a sign for the Law was their
assurance.’59 An extra authority is needed only in so far as a prophet deviates from
a given moral code. One wonders, though, what sign or authority that could be. It
cannot be reason, given the fact that reason would be satisfied with any given law in
so far as it contributes to peace. In any case, the ordinary authority of a prophet
would derive from a law and, inversely, the certainty we have about a particular
prophecy should derive from our respect of the law.
In sum, prophecy can be acknowledged as the ‘Word of God’ only in a context in
which moral values are already appreciated and generally respected. That means,
first, that the prophet’s authority is not absolute (a prophet would have no authority
in the state of nature), and second, that the prophet cannot teach anything new, even
in the limited area of morality (for whatever values he teaches are those endorsed by
a positive law). So what prophets teach is at best obedience to an existing positive
law and it is only to that extent that they can legitimately claim authority. But one
could also see the problem as one of truth and judgment, even if that means that we
leave the domain of authority and interpretation. It is with that in mind that Spinoza
introduces the notion of ‘moral certainty’.
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
74


‘Authority’ and Moral Certainty
The confusion about the ‘authority’ of prophecy and Scripture is caused by the
ambiguity of the notion of ‘authority’, an ambiguity which, although it is certainly
increased by Spinoza, is perfectly traditional. Reduced to its essence, it is the
confusion between ‘power’ and ‘truth’.60 If we attribute authority to someone – to a
prophet, to Scripture, to the Holy Ghost, but also to, say, a scientist or any other non-
political person or body – we declare our belief that whatever he says is true and in
so doing give him, as it were, power over our judgment. From a Cartesian point of
view that is not impossible at all, given the fact that one of the components of
judgment is a ‘volition’. Spinoza, however, rejects the Cartesian analysis, possibly
for that very reason.61 Accordingly, no belief can be an act of obedience but must be
judged on the basis of ordinary criteria of truth and falsehood. It is to make that
possible, presumably, that Spinoza introduces the notion of ‘moral certainty’.
Spinoza starts his discussion of prophecy by saying that the type of certainty one
should expect is ‘of a moral nature’.62 So the certainty attached to ‘prophetic’ beliefs
would be no more than moral certainty and in any case definitely inferior to the
certainty produced by logical or mathematical argument. And in fact, since prophecy
is a product of the imagination, that would only be natural. But some commentators
go beyond that simple assessment, claiming that ‘moral certainty’ has an objective
status, that it can be a quality of true beliefs for which no demonstration is given or
even for beliefs of which no demonstration is possible. A case in point would be the
principle of theology according to which we can be saved by obedience.63 This
interpretation is very unlikely if only because it would be contrary to Spinoza’s
general principle of the identity of the real and the conceivable.64 But let us take the
question from the beginning.
According to many 17th-century philosophers ‘moral certainty’ is a type of
certainty which, even if it can be doubted in theory, is by and large sufficient in
practice. A ‘morally certain’ belief is simply a ‘probable’ belief, a belief we can rely
on even if there is no opportunity to prove it.65 According to Descartes, for example,
people who have never been to Rome can be morally certain of the existence of that
city ‘even though it could be the case that everyone who has told them this has been
deceiving them’.66 We cannot prove that Rome exists (nor can we prove that it is
impossible that all people deceive us) in the same way as we can prove that the sum
of the angles of a triangle is equal to that of two right angles, but the idea that
everyone would try to deceive us is so unlikely that we can be pretty certain that
Rome exists. Similarly, if we eat something that tastes good and never does us any
harm we do not certainly know that it is harmless in the way we certainly know the
laws of collision or the propositions of Euclid. Indeed, if we took the time to find out
in a scientific way whether whatever we are willing to eat is in fact eatable we would
probably die from starvation, but given the fact that God does not deceive us we can
be morally certain that our food is safe most of the time as long as we allow
ourselves to be guided by taste, instinct and experience.67 So in the context of daily
life moral certainty is useful and indeed indispensable. In any other context we
should not be content with anything less than mathematical certainty, that is,
anything less than the clear and distinct ideas of science, whose certainty is
guaranteed by God.68
‘The Word of God’
75


Spinoza also reserves certainty to adequate ideas, which, having the intrinsic
properties (denominationes) of truth, are true and known to be true.69 Ideas of the
imagination, however, which are not adequate, may be true but, if they are, cannot
be known to be true. Indeed, there is no intrinsic difference between a false idea and
a true but inadequate idea – that is also the reason why people can have false ideas
and believe they are true.70 By contrast we cannot have a true and adequate idea
without knowing that it is true. The only way, therefore, to become certain of an idea
of the imagination is to try to confirm it by reason, that is, to actually replace it with
an adequate intellectual concept; and the only way to become certain that an idea of
the imagination is false is to try to conceive whatever is imagined in it: if that does
not work it is a false idea or, rather, no idea at all.
In any case there seems to be no room for moral certainty at all, which, for that
matter, is not discussed or even mentioned, either in the Ethics, or in the Treatise on
the Improvement of the Intellect. Spinoza’s only attempt to explain it is indirect and
occurs in a note:
By perceptible things [perceptibilis] I mean not only those that can be logically proved but
also those which we are wont to accept with moral certainty and to hear without surprise,
although they can by no means be proved. Anyone perceives [percipit] Euclid’s pro-
positions before they are proved. Similarly, I call perceptible those narratives of future or
past events that do not exceed human belief, and likewise laws, institutions and customs,
although they cannot be proved with mathematical certainty. But mysterious symbols and
narratives that exceed all human belief, I call imperceptible [imperceptibilis].71
It must be underscored that the subject is not moral certainty but what Spinoza calls
‘perceptibility’, Spinoza’s point being that the things in Scripture that really matter
(moral principles and doctrines) can be readily understood because they are
‘perceptible’.72 So what we learn basically about a morally certain belief is that it is
‘perceptible’, not that whatever is ‘perceptible’ is by definition also morally certain.
However, percipere and its derivatives (perceptio, perceptibilis, and so on) are
among the vaguest terms of Spinoza’s vocabulary.73 Thus a demonstration is more
perceptibilis than another if it is more easily understood.74 Sentire, imaginari and
pure intelligere (sensation, imagination and pure understanding) are all modi
percipiendi, that is, forms of consciousness, faculties of the mind, and so on.75
Spinoza comes closest to a more precise characterization in his definition of idea at
the beginning of Pt II of the Ethics, where he explicitly avoids perceptio and
replaces it with conceptus because, as he explains, perceptio ‘seems to indicate that
the mind is passive with respect to its object’.76 So perceptio does not necessarily
involve any activity on behalf of the mind – it is just awareness. This suggests that
‘to find something perceptible’ or ‘to perceive something’ does not mean anything
precise except that ‘mysterious symbols and narratives that exceed all human
belief’, which to be understood do require a mental effort, cannot be its object:
indeed, they are ‘imperceptible’. If, on the other hand, we concentrate on the context
for which the term is coined, we find the following:
The certainty acquired by prophets from signs was not mathematical certainty – that is, the
certainty that necessarily derives from the apprehension of what is apprehended or seen –
but only of a moral kind, and the signs were vouchsafed only to convince the prophet. It
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
76


therefore follows that the signs vouchsafed were suited to the beliefs and capacity of the
prophet. A sign that would validate his prophecy for one prophet might fail to convince
another who held different beliefs, and so the signs varied in the case of each prophet.
Similarly, revelation also varied […] according to his temperament, the nature of his
imagination and the beliefs he previously held.77
A mathematically certain idea is intrinsically certain (‘its certainty necessarily
derives from the apprehension of what is apprehended’). Either it is self-evident or
it can be demonstrated to be true; that is, it can be shown to be contained in a self-
evident idea.78 A morally certain idea or belief, however, would be held to be true
on the basis of ‘signs’. Given the fact that ‘signs’ are contrasted with demonstrative
arguments, I suggest that they stand for something like ‘evidence’, that is, facts and
reasons which, without entailing the truth of the belief in question, are a motive for
finding it likely. From a general point of view there is nothing wrong with that. We
believe a newspaper report not because whatever is reported in it is demonstrably
true (let alone that we can demonstrate it to be true) but because it was written by a
competent journalist; we believe that Caesar was murdered in 44 BC, not because we
can demonstrate it (in the way we would demonstrate a mathematical theorem) but
because it is warranted by reliable documents. Moral certainty of this kind is not
arbitrary. There are rules and criteria which, even if they vary from case to case,
have a basis either in common sense and common experience or in an informal
agreement between competent professionals: historians, journalists and physicians
are examples of professionals each having different criteria for finding something
likely or probable. Spinoza, however, claims that ‘signs were vouchsafed only to
convince a prophet’, meaning, obviously, an individual prophet. Indeed, ‘a sign that
would validate his prophecy for one prophet might fail to convince another holding
different beliefs’. So if moral certainty is certainty produced by ‘signs’, prophets as
a professional class do not even agree on what type of evidence would be needed to
validate their own utterances. In fact what makes prophecy ‘morally certain’ is not
the nature of the evidence (the ‘signs’) but its relation to the prejudices of a
particular prophet. As a result, ‘moral certainty’ is a purely subjective category: the
moral certainty of a prophet is of no significance to other prophets, whose prejudices
require evidence of a different sort.
Spinoza’s account of ‘moral certainty’ deviates from ordinary usage. If I am
morally certain that Caesar was murdered in 44 BC the reason is not primarily that
that agrees with the beliefs I already have – although if it did not agree with my other
beliefs I would probably require stronger evidence – but that on the basis of the
evidence available so far this is the only conclusion I can honestly draw and that in
the event of there being found new and contrary evidence I would be ready to revise
my conclusion. Indeed, the only reason why we speak of moral certainty is to
express that readiness. However, to say of a prophet that he is entitled to ‘moral
certainty’ with respect to whatever was revealed to him (or of ourselves that we can
be morally certain of a given prophecy, if it is in line with his, or our own, other
beliefs) means that anybody may be morally certain of anything as long as it agrees
with his prejudices. Moreover, ‘moral certainty’ normally applies to historical and
contingent facts, of which, since they cannot be ascertained by mathematical proof,
we can be certain only on the basis of empirical evidence, which is never absolutely
conclusive. But truths about ‘God’ are not historical or contingent truths; indeed,
‘The Word of God’
77


according to Spinoza the only way to know something at all about God is by proof
and demonstration:
If someone says that it is not necessary to understand the attributes of God but that it is
enough to believe, without demonstration, that would clearly be nonsense. For in the case
of things invisible, which are objects of the mind, proofs are the only eyes by which they
are seen; those who do not have such proofs can see nothing of those things. So when
people merely repeat what they have heard this is no more relevant to or indicative of their
mind than the mindless – and meaningless [sine mente et sensu] words of a parrot or a
machine.79
With respect to God, and generally things invisible, the class of ‘true beliefs’ is
empty. A belief about ‘God’ that is not supported by arguments is meaningless. The
only way to know about God is to know God, that is, to have true demonstrative
knowledge of God of the kind proposed in Pt I of the Ethics. Accordingly, the
category of ‘moral certainty’ does not apply.
Again the comparison with Hobbes may be helpful because it suggests that
Spinoza’s real point has nothing to do with truth and probability but has to do with
authority. Hobbes starts by calling into question the very notion of revealed
knowledge:
When God speaketh to a man, it must be either immediately; or by mediation of another
man to whom he had formerly spoken by himself immediately. How God speaketh to a
man immediately may be understood by those well enough to whom he hath so spoken;
but how the same should be understood by another is hard if not impossible to know. For
if a man pretend to me that God hath spoken to him supernaturally and immediately and I
make doubt of it I cannot easily perceive what argument he can produce to oblige me to
beleeve it.80
The reasons a prophet has for believing that he had a revelation cannot be shared
with others, or in any case cannot be convincing enough to oblige anyone but the
prophet involved. Accordingly, Hobbes completely dismisses the signs Spinoza
locates in the first group:
To say [God] hath spoken to [a prophet] in a dream is no more then to say that he dreamed
that God spake to him […] To say he hath seen a Vision or heard a Voice is to say that he
hath dreamed between sleeping and waking […] To say he speaks by supernaturall
inspiration is to say he finds an ardent desire to speak or some strong opinion of himself
for which hee can alledge no naturall and sufficient reason. So that though God Almighty
can speak to a man by Dreams, Visions, Voice and Inspiration; yet he obliges no man to
beleeve he hath so done to him that pretends it; who (being a man) may erre, and (which
is more) may lie.81
It is not absolutely impossible for God to communicate by dreams but if he does it
is of no consequence to anyone but the dreamer. The question, therefore, of where
the authority of prophecy comes from cannot be answered by pointing to the fact
that the dreamer has reasons to believe that he had a revelation. So Hobbes resolutely
rejects Spinoza’s idea that the signs we use to judge prophecy must be the same as
those used by the prophet; indeed, that would be sensible only if ‘revelation’ and
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
78


‘prophecy’ were the same, which according to Hobbes they are not. So how do we
judge a prophet’s authority if it is not by the quality of his revelation? Hobbes’s first
solution is based on Scripture:
I answer out of Holy Scripture, that there be two marks by which together, not asunder, a
true prophet is to be known. One is the doing of miracles; the other is the not teaching any
other religion than that which is already established. Asunder (I say) neither of these is
sufficient.82
Being based on Scripture, the answer is valid only for those who already accept the
authority of Scripture whereas, presumably, the authority of Scripture should derive
from the authority of the prophets. Hobbes’s second answer addresses that problem
by reformulating it in terms of political authority:
It is a question much disputed between the divers sects of Christian Religion, From
whence the Scriptures derive their Authority; which question is also propounded
sometimes in other terms, as How wee know them to be the Word of God or Why we
beleeve them to be so: And the difficulty of resolving it, ariseth chiefly from the
impropernesse of the words wherein the question it self is couched. For it is beleeved on
all hands that the first and originall Author of them is God; and consequently the question
disputed is not that.83 Again, it is manifest that none can know they are Gods Word (though
all true Christians beleeve it) but those to whom God himself hath revealed it
supernaturally; and therefore the question is not rightly moved of our Knowledge of it.
Lastly, when the question is propounded of our Beleefe; because some are moved to
beleeve for one and others for other reasons, there can be rendered no one generall answer
for them all. The question truly stated is, By what authority they are made Law.84
The knowledge that Scripture is God’s word is reserved to the prophets and cannot
be shared. The belief that Scripture is true can be based on a variety of reasons, none
of them universally compelling. The question therefore is not how we know or
believe that Scripture is true but by what authority Scripture is turned into a law. And
then the answer is obvious:
As far as they differ not from the Laws of Nature there is no doubt but they are the Law
of God and carry their Authority with them legible to all men that have the use of naturall
reason: but this is no other Authority than that of all other Morall Doctrine consonant to
Reason; the Dictates whereof are Laws, not made but Eternall. If they be made Law by
God himselfe, they are of the nature of written Law, which are Laws to them only to whom
God hath so sufficiently published them, as no man can excuse himself by saying he knew
not they were his. He therefore to whom God hath not supernaturally revealed that they
are his nor that those that published them were sent by him is not obliged to obey them by
any Authority but his whose Commands have already the force of Laws; that is to say, by
any other Authority than that of the Commonwealth residing in the Sovereign, who only
has Legislative power.85
So if we think of ‘authority’ as the counterpart of obedience, Hobbes reduces the
authority of Scripture to its being a positive law, which it can be only in virtue of the
authority of a sovereign (or, but that really amounts to the same, of that of the laws
of nature). But if we think of ‘authority’ as true belief, Hobbes finds the notion of
the ‘authority’ of Scripture irrelevant and unattractive.
‘The Word of God’
79


If we now go back to Spinoza it appears, first of all, that he is generally less
careful than Hobbes in making a distinction between ‘revelation’ and ‘prophecy’.
This suggests that he thinks of the problem mainly in terms of belief, for it is only
in that case that it makes sense to say the reasons we have for believing a prophet
are the same as he has for claiming his beliefs to be true. That also means that
Spinoza cannot make the same distinction as Hobbes between the truth of a revealed
belief (which Hobbes finds irrelevant) and the authority by which a belief is turned
into a law for, again, if prophecy is nothing but belief, ‘authority’ does not apply –
all one has to do is examine it critically in the light of the beliefs one already has.
Since on the other hand all beliefs that are relevant in this respect are based on the
imagination, the certainty achieved in that process can be no more than ‘moral
certainty’. So the aim of Spinoza’s use of ‘moral certainty’ in this context seems to
be to replace the notion of ‘authority’.
The point is made more clearly in chap. 15, which explicitly deals with the notion
of ‘authority’ in conjunction with Scripture. Spinoza organizes his discussion
around an examination of the views of Jehudah Alpakhar, an opponent of
Maimonides. Indeed, the discussion in chap. 15 is a sequel to the discussion in chap.
7, which concentrates on Maimonides.86 Spinoza ascribes to Alpakhar two distinct
claims, both of which he believes are untenable: ‘we must accept as true or reject as
false whatever Scripture affirms or denies’, and ‘Scripture never expressly affirms
or denies anything that contradicts what it elsewhere affirms or denies.’87 The first
claim implies that Scripture is the only criterion of truth (that the fact that a claim is
made in Scripture is enough to make us certain of its truth); the second is based on
the idea that there is only one author of Scripture (although perhaps different
scribes), namely, the Holy Ghost (which being a spirit of truth will never contradict
itself).88 This suggests that Alpakhar represents the orthodox Calvinist view. In any
case, Spinoza characterizes both principles as ‘rash’ (temere dicta): we can know
that Scripture contains the truth only after examining it by reason and we can know
that it has one author only be examining its ‘history’. I reserve a discussion of
Alpakhar’s second claim (and Spinoza’s criticism of it) for the next chapter, limiting
myself here to the first, which is that Scripture should be interpreted ‘by itself’ for
the very reason that it has ‘authority’.
Spinoza begins by pointing out that Alpakhar’s principle implies that we should
ignore the teachings of reason, which we normally use to assess the truth: the simple
fact that a statement is made in Scripture would be enough to be certain of its truth.89
According to Spinoza, however, it simply is not the case that Scripture contains the
truth for in fact ‘there are numerous statements […] which reason and philosophy
tell us are false and which nevertheless are all to be taken as true in our author’s
view, there being no appeal to reason’.90 Naturally, this is an ignoratio elenchi given
the fact that the only reason why ‘Alpakhar’ believes in ‘the authority of Scripture’
is precisely that he does not believe in the authority of reason. In any case it shows
that Spinoza interprets the problem exclusively in terms of natural beliefs, that is,
beliefs which to be held as true should be examined by reason – and then of course
the ‘authority’ of Scripture (that is, the claim that the totality of beliefs expressed in
Scripture are true) does not stand the test of reason. Naturally one could focus the
discussion on the reasons for ascribing ‘authority’ to Scripture. If we rationally
justify the belief that Scripture contains the truth and nothing but the truth
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
80


Alpakhar’s position could still be saved. But according to Spinoza that is
impossible:
If reason, in spite of her protests, is nevertheless to be made completely subservient to
Scripture, must this submission be effected with reason’s concurrence, or without it,
blindly? If the latter, then surely we are behaving like fools, without judgment. If the
former, then it is only at reason’s behest that we accept Scripture, which we should
therefore not accept if it were repugnant to reason. And again, I ask, who can give mental
acceptance to something against which his reason rebels? For what else is mental denial
but reason’s rebellion? I am utterly astonished that men can bring themselves to make
reason, the greatest of all gifts and a light divine, subservient to letters that are dead and
may have been corrupted by human malice; that it should be considered no crime to
denigrate the mind, the true handwriting of God’s word, declaring it to be corrupt, blind
and lost, whereas it is considered to be a heinous crime to entertain such thoughts of the
letter, a mere shadow of God’s word.91
Spinoza’s argument rests on the idea that ‘authority’ is indivisible: either we admit
rational arguments, and then we accept them always and everywhere, or we believe
in the authority of Scripture, and then we totally reject the authority of reason. So if
on rational grounds we accept the authority of Scripture what we actually accept is
the authority of reason and that would mean that we should not ‘accept Scripture if
it is repugnant to reason’. If on the other hand we believe in the authority of
Scripture we should give up the authority of reason completely and that would mean
that we would cease to be human. Consequently, Alpakhar’s view is either
incoherent or absurd. But that also means that the question of the authority of
Scripture cannot be solved in terms of ‘authority’, that the only way to solve it is to
see it as a matter of truth and belief, that is, of moral certainty. But on this very point
there may be a distinction between prophecy and Christian teaching: between the
Old Testament and the New Testament.
Christian Teachers
Although in the course of his discussion Spinoza indiscriminately speaks of all
revelation as prophecy, he admits that there is a distinction between the Old
Testament (the ‘Prophetic Books’) and the New Testament (the ‘Apostolic Books’).
Still, he is reluctant to discuss the New Testament for the somewhat spurious reason
that he does not know enough Greek.92 Indeed, that argument is completely
neutralized by Spinoza’s claim that the divinity of Scripture has nothing to do with
the language in which it was written.93 In any case both the Old and the New
Testament are supposedly ‘sacred’ or ‘divine’ in so far as their ‘purpose is to foster
piety and religion’.94 Accordingly, we should see their difference in terms of a
different type of ‘divinity’, that is, of a different type of piety or morality: ‘Before
the coming of Christ the prophets used to proclaim religion as the law of their
own country in virtue of the covenant made in the time of Moses, whereas after
the coming of Christ the Apostles preached religion to all men as a universal
law solely in virtue of Christ’s passion.’95 In the Old Testament, God’s will
(the moral code) coincides with the Law of Israel, whereas the rules taught in
‘The Word of God’
81


the New Testament are universal. Moreover, the ‘authority’ of a prophet is based on
a covenant (prophecy is part of the political structure of Israel) whereas apostolic
teaching is based on a personal experience, and that means that it has no ‘authority’
by itself.96
In any case to the extent that both Testaments are ‘divine’ the New Testament was
not really new: ‘The books of the New Testament were new, not because they
contained a different doctrine, or because they were written as documents of a
covenant, or because a universal religion [religio catholica] – which is entirely in
accord with Nature – was new, except that it looked new for those who knew it
not.’97 The ‘novelty’ of the New Testament is that the teaching of the Apostles was
on the level of a general conception of God’s will, before it is ‘interpreted’ and
turned into a positive law:
Christ was sent, not to preserve a state and to institute laws, but only to teach the universal
law. Hence we can readily understand that Christ by no means abrogated the laws of
Moses, for it was not Christ’s purpose to introduce new laws in the commonwealth. His
chief concern was to teach moral doctrines, keeping them distinct from the laws of a
commonwealth.98
Christian Law is ‘universal’, not in the sense that it should be obeyed by all under
all circumstances – indeed, it neither should nor could be obeyed in the state of
nature – but in the sense that it is not a law at all: ‘If Christ proclaimed these things
as a law he did so because of the people’s ignorance and obstinacy.’99 Indeed,
‘although he appears to have laid down laws in God’s name, we must maintain that
he perceived things truly and adequately’.100 The teaching of Christ consists in a
general precept of peace comparable to Hobbes’s First Law of Nature. To become a
positive law it should be turned into a set of precise behavioural rules, which,
however, Christ left to the earthly lawgiver.
A distinction must be made between Christ and the Apostles and a corresponding
distinction between the Gospel and the Epistles. The Apostles were primarily
teachers: ‘The Apostles wrote not as prophets but as teachers […] and they chose
such methods of teaching as they thought best adapted to those whom they wished
to instruct at the time.’101 Accordingly, an Apostle would not be an ‘interpreter’ but
an ‘orator’ or perhaps even a ‘philosopher’. Again, Spinoza’s evidence is Scriptural.
Paul speaks of two kinds of preaching: one from revelation and the other from
knowledge.102 Paul also frequently speaks according to his own opinion and never
uses typically prophetic expressions like ‘Thus saith the Lord’ and so on.103 The
Epistles ‘originated not from revelation or from God’s command but from the
Apostles’ own natural faculty of judgement’.104 Moreover, the Apostles did not write
‘from revelation and express mandate’ but ‘as private individuals’.105 Finally, the
Apostles patently disagree, not on religion (morality) as such, but on what Spinoza
calls the foundations of religion – that is, not on what constitutes moral behaviour
but on the reasons why we should behave morally.106 Spinoza explains this by the
fact that the Apostles addressed different audiences: ‘To avoid offending men’s ears
by the novelty  of [the Gospel’s] doctrine they adapted it, as far as possible, to
the character of their contemporaries (see 1 Cor. 9:19–20, etc.), building on
foundations that were most familiar and accepted at that time.’107 Paul preached to
the Gentiles, so used philosophy; other Apostles preached to the Jews, who despised
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
82


philosophy – no doubt a bitter allusion to Spinoza’s own struggle with the
Synagogue.108
However, the apparent doctrinal diversity among the Apostles is also the cause of
numerous schisms and disputes in the Church.109 And that undoubtedly is the true
reason behind Spinoza’s argument. Apostles are theologians, that is, people who
interpret a given message for different audiences and have as little authority as
ordinary theologians have. In fact, the best thing is to go back to Christ’s simple
message of love and to separate religion from philosophical speculation.110 Even if
there is no properly political dimension to Christianity as, according to Spinoza, it
should be understood, it can be misunderstood in a politically undesirable way if we
fail to reflect on its proper historical origins and evolution:
It now remains for me to indicate the reason why this right [that is, the sovereign’s right
over religion] has always been the subject of disputes in Christian states […]. It was not
kings who were the first teachers of the Christian religion but men of private station who,
despite the will of those who held sovereignty and were their rulers, were long accustomed
to address private religious assemblies, to institute and perform sacred rites, to make all
arrangements and decisions on their own responsibility without any regard to the state.
Many years later, when their religion began to be adopted by the state, the churchmen were
obliged to teach it to the emperors themselves in the form they had given it, from which it
was an easy step for them to gain recognition as its teachers and interpreters, and
furthermore as the pastors of the church and virtually God’s own representatives. And to
prevent Christian Kings from later seizing authority for themselves, the churchmen
devised the very effective precaution of forbidding marriage to the chief ministers of their
church and to the supreme interpreter of religion. In addition, they multiplied religious
dogma to such an extent and confused them with so much philosophy that the supreme
interpreter had to be a consummate philosopher and theologian….111
Christianity is a popular, almost a plebeian, religion, not a political instrument as
religion had been in the hands of Moses. Although this explains the absence of a
properly political dimension it also makes it possible for theologians to claim
authority for themselves in a way which is obviously improper and which turns
Christianity into a political liability.
That this is the main ratio behind Spinoza’s theory of Christianity is confirmed by
the confused and unclear nature of what he says on it. For, first of all, if religion is
moral behaviour one cannot possibly say that the only differences between the
Apostles concern the foundations of religion. Indeed, the very example cited by
Spinoza – Paul emphasizing the importance of faith against James proclaiming the
emptiness of faith without works – shows that for Paul faith (belief) was more
important than works, whereas for James, reasoning ‘within a small compass’, the
works (moral behaviour) were more important.112 Moreover, if we look more closely
at Spinoza’s text his account of apostolic teaching is contradictory. For whereas on
the one hand he stresses the fact that they were not prophets but ‘orators’ or even
‘teachers’, he also admits that they were ‘interpreters’ and ‘prophets’: ‘Nobody who
reads the New Testament can doubt that the Apostles were prophets.’113 Indeed, in
so far as they used signs they were undoubtedly prophets: ‘The Apostles were
inspired by special revelation only in what they orally preached when confirmed by
signs, while that which they taught in writing or orally without the attestation of
‘The Word of God’
83


signs was spoken or written from knowledge, that is, natural knowledge (I Cor.
14:6).’114 And in so far as they were actually prophets they stood in need of a specific
authorization: ‘The Apostles had received the power [virtutem acceperant] to preach
the story of Christ as prophets – that is, confirming it with signs – but also the
authority to teach and exhort in whatever way each should think best.’115 Indeed,
they preached religion ‘by right of the power they had received from Christ against
unclean spirits (Mat. 10:1)’.116 At the end of the day therefore the Apostles were not
only teachers but also prophets, albeit in a limited area: ‘Nowhere do we read that
the Apostles were commanded to write but only to preach wherever they went and
to confirm their words by signs.’117 At best they were teachers as well as prophets:
‘By virtue of their apostolate they were not only prophets but teachers.’118 In any
case their position was not such that anybody now can claim to have inherited
authority from them.119
Finally, despite the fact that the Apostles were teachers rather than prophets and
used their natural faculty of judgment rather than ‘revelation’, they taught many
things that do not fall within the scope of reason. Spinoza addresses this problem in
a complex passage in chap. 11:
If we attend to what we said in chap. 7 of this treatise on interpretation of Scripture the
difficulty will disappear. For although the contents of the Bible for the most part surpass
our understanding, they may safely be the subject of discourse provided that we admit no
principles of interpretation other than those that Scripture presents. In the same way the
Apostles, on the basis of what they had seen and heard and finally had acquired by
revelation, were able to reach many conclusions and make many inferences and to teach
these to men at their own discretion. Furthermore, although religion as preached by the
Apostles – who simply related the story of Christ – does not come within the scope of
reason, yet its substance, which consists essentially in moral teachings as does the whole
of Christ’s doctrine, can be readily grasped by anyone with the natural light of reason.
Finally, the Apostles needed no supernatural light to adapt a religion, which they had
previously confirmed with signs, to the common understanding of mankind so as to be
readily and sincerely accepted by everyone; nor yet did they need a supernatural light in
their task of exhortation.120
Let us start with the third part of the quotation, which is, in a way, easiest to
understand. The religion the Apostles ‘adapted’ was a religion already confirmed by
signs, so what they did was conceptually elaborate a given content. In any case, they
relied on prophecy, adapting it to new circumstances. Their preaching ‘did not come
within the scope of reason’: they did not attempt to prove the doctrines they were
teaching but in any case a proof was not really needed because in so far as it is
relevant its contents ‘can readily be grasped by anyone with the natural light of
reason’. The first part of Spinoza’s answer, however, amounts to an explanation per
obscurius if only because it is not easy to identify the exact reference to chap. 7.121
The meaning is probably that the Apostles ‘interpreted’ something given (‘what they
had seen and heard and finally had acquired by revelation’), and that would mean
that, in so far as they were not inspired by the life of Christ, they were prophets after
all.
The only clear message seems to be that, whatever the Apostles did, whether
teaching or prophesying, it was without any specific authority at all. It was on their
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
84


own initiative and their own responsibility that they went about teaching. What they
taught never was or became a specific law. And whatever law or morality
(‘religion’) they taught was adapted to their audiences. Accordingly, no one is under
any obligation to heed their words. The question is whether the same applies to
Christ.
The Meaning of Christ
Of all the things Spinoza says about Christ three only are more or less
straightforward: Christ was not literally the son of God; Christ did not literally rise
from the dead; Christ led a life that was morally exemplary. With respect to the first
point Spinoza claims that ‘son of God’ or ‘child of God’ is nothing but a way of
speaking. In Genesis ‘men of extraordinary strength and great stature’ are called
sons of God, whereas actually they were ‘impious robbers and whoremongers’.122 So
all it means to say of Christ that he was son of God is that he had an extraordinary
stature. Something similar can be said of the second point. According to a letter to
Oldenburg, Christ’s resurrection from the dead was spiritual: ‘Nothing more was
revealed to the faithful according to their understanding than that Christ was given
eternity and that he stood up (surrexit) among the dead, “dead” understood in the
sense in which Christ said “let the dead bury their dead” – while giving by his life
and death an example of uncommon holiness, and that in that sense he also woke
from the dead his disciples to the extent that they followed the example given by his
life and death.’123 Christ did not rise from the dead (resurrexit); he stood up
(surrexit) among the dead, ‘dead’ being those who, having no moral rule, lead a
life of anxiety and war. Christ’s role therefore was to give a moral example, that
is, to instantiate a rule of behaviour. Christ’s ‘eternity’ is nothing but the eternity
of that rule and therefore nothing but a sign of the universal validity of his
example.
Christ was not a prophet (or an Apostle) interpreting (or teaching) the will of God
(a moral code) but the perfect medium through which others could ‘perceive’ the
will of God. As a result we should see Christ as the visible manifestation of a moral
code:
I do not believe that anyone has attained such a degree of perfection surpassing all others,
except Christ. To him God’s ordinances leading men to salvation were revealed not by
words or visions but directly, so that God manifested himself to the Apostles through the
mind of Christ as he once did to Moses through an audible voice. The Voice of Christ can
thus be called the Voice of God in the same way as that which Moses heard. In that sense
it can also be said that the wisdom of God – that is, wisdom that is more than human –
took on human nature in Christ and that Christ was the way to salvation.124
The relation of Christ to ‘God’, that is, to a moral rule, was immediate in the sense
that Christ did not need any ‘sign’. In him the ‘wisdom of God’ was incarnated –
‘took on human nature’. So in a way his life could be a revelation to others and as
such an ‘interpretation’ of the moral code comparable in force with Moses
interpreting the will of God by means of a positive law. And that is also the reason
why his example will be eternally valid: ‘If we say that the Father has begotten the
‘The Word of God’
85


son from eternity, all we mean to say is that the Father has communicated his
eternity to the son always.’125
The parallel between Moses and Christ is interesting for more than one reason.
Moses communicated with God ‘face to face’ (de facie ad faciem) but Christ ‘mind
to mind’ (de mente ad mentem).126 Moses as well as Christ had an immediate relation
to ‘God’, a relation which is not, as for other humans, mediated by a prophet or a
law. But there is an important difference in style, for ‘it was through the mind of
Christ that God made revelations to mankind as he had done earlier through angels,
that is, through created voices, visions, etc’.127 The phantasms of the imagination are
replaced by something else: ‘God’s eternal wisdom [sapientia] has manifested itself
in all things, especially in the human mind and above all in Jesus Christ.’128 As a
result, those who do have access to reason can reflect on that example and formulate
their conclusions, not as a law, but as an eternal truth:
Doubtless, to those to whom it was granted to know the mysteries of Heaven, his teaching
took the form of eternal truths, not of prescribed laws. In this way he freed them from
bondage to the law, while nevertheless giving further strength and stability to the law,
inscribing it deep in their hearts.129
As a result, a teaching based on Christ’s life is consistent with any law for no other
reason than that it instantiates a rule whose validity is independent of any law. Christ
is a demonstration of the moral law, not in the sense in which a philosopher
demonstrates the moral law as an eternal truth but in the sense that his behaviour
perfectly instantiates the moral law. Accordingly, his ‘mind’ (that is, the thoughts
that form the motives of his behaviour) could be said to communicate directly with
‘God’s mind’.
Conclusion
The only way to make sense of Spinoza’s treatment of prophecy and apostolic
teaching is to keep in mind that his true subject is what is usually called the
‘authority of Scripture’. Again, the notion of ‘authority’ can be interpreted in two
ways: either as the counterpart of obedience or as the unconditional belief that a
given text contains the truth and nothing but the truth. In the context of prophecy the
first interpretation is not entirely impossible. In the Old Testament in particular,
prophecy does function in the context of civil obedience, not only because the law
itself was revealed through prophecy (Moses) but also because the role of the
prophets was to remind the people and the kings of their duty. Still, ‘authority’ can
be claimed only for the properly ‘moral’ part of the prophets’ teaching, all the rest
being ‘adaptation’. For the New Testament, on the other hand, that interpretation is
no longer useful, unless we resort to the Hobbesian solution of placing the sovereign
at the head of a national Christian Church. But Spinoza cannot adopt that solution,
not only because he denies the ‘right to teach’, but presumably for three other
reasons as well: (1) the commands of the Christian God as they are mediated by
Christ do not have the form of a law (to become a law they require a sovereign who
‘interprets’ them, that is, turns them into precise behavioural rules); (2) the Christian
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
86


religion is universal, so the notion of a national Christian Church is contradictory;
(3) the Christian tradition has an obsessive concern with truth (it is not just a law but
also and mainly a theology claimed to be true). This last aspect in particular makes
it imperative, not only to reduce the role of the founders of the Christian religion to
that of moralists, but also to keep the sovereign as far away from it as can possibly
be done, for nothing is as detrimental to sovereign power as to become engaged  in
a controversy about truth.
The basic problem, which cannot be solved, is of course that we are dealing with
the imagination, which, if it is unaided by reason, provides a false certainty about its
beliefs. These can be rectified by ‘common notions’ (causality, uniformity,
universality) and common experience – in sum by philosophy – but, as we have seen
earlier, it simply is not realistic to expect many people to have access to philosophy.
All one can do, accordingly, is to encourage people to be less dogmatic about their
beliefs and to make them less inclined to draw political consequences from them.
This is done by Spinoza in three different ways: by making a distinction between the
‘true’ doctrine of the prophets and whatever was added to that doctrine by way of
adaptation, by reducing Christian teaching either to private morality or to a vague
precept of peace, and by reserving for religious beliefs no more than ‘moral
certainty’.

‘The Word of God’
91


Chapter 4
The Impossibility of Theology
Rulers, prophets and theologians all do more or less the same thing: they interpret
‘God’s will’, that is, make others aware of a ‘moral’ code without explaining it as an
eternal truth. Their various ‘interpretations’, however, must be coordinated with –
and, given the terms involved, that means subordinated to – the will of the sovereign,
given the fact that he is the only legitimate ‘interpreter’ of God’s will. Since on the
other hand a modern sovereign does not enjoy the ‘right to teach’ we cannot,
according to Spinoza, give him the right to interpret Scripture as long as the result
of that interpretation is supposed to be a particular form of true belief (called
‘theology’ or ‘faith’) and as long as that belief is not simply about what should be
done, which is the legitimate domain of the sovereign, but also about the existence
and nature of God, the destiny of man, and so on. Indeed, theology claims to be
knowledge, albeit knowledge of a peculiar kind. This raises problems, not only
about the type of knowledge involved (can we know something at all by reading a
text?) but also about the nature and the necessity of interpretation (is reading enough
or should there be some rule to guide us in our interpretation and to provide authority
to a particular interpretation?). To unravel these problems is allegedly the aim of the
famous chap. 7 of the Theologico-political treatise although in practice Spinoza
seems to be more intent on obscuring the issues. Since there is an important subtext
I shall have to analyse the text in some, I am afraid rather tedious, detail. First,
however, I present some other ideas on theology, not only of Voetians and
Cartesians, but also of Lodewijk (Louis) Meyer, with whom Spinoza is often
associated.1
Preliminaries
Revealed knowledge – theology – is mediated by the interpretation of a ‘text’
(written or spoken), which is believed to contain the truth. According to most
Christian denominations (in the 17th century, at any rate, the only exception was the
Socinians) the belief that Scripture contains the truth and nothing but the truth
should be unconditional. One knows that Scripture contains the truth and nothing but
the truth, not because one has tested its contents against an independent criterion of
truth, but because Scripture was written by the Holy Ghost, who is a spirit of truth.
This fact about Scripture is called its ‘authority’ or ‘divinity’. By submitting
ourselves to the authority of Scripture we give up our ordinary ways of ascertaining
the truth. But before this can lead to true belief one must go through a process of
interpretation, that is, of reading and understanding, which can be easy and
straightforward or difficult and arduous according as the text is more or less clear.
So theology comprises two firmly interlocked stages: theology-as-interpretation, the
93


Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
process of identifying the meaning of a ‘divine’ text, and theology-as-knowledge,
the articulated result of theology-as-interpretation. But at the basis of both lies the
belief that Scripture contains the truth and nothing but the truth. This produces
various problems. First of all we should be certain that there actually was a
revelation. With that aspect of the problem Spinoza deals in chaps 1 and 2 of the
Theologico-political treatise. I discussed it in the last chapter but, inevitably, it will
turn up here. Second, if there is a separation in time between the actual moment of
revelation and our reading we should be certain that there is a reliable ‘tradition’ of
some sort, or ‘canon’. This problem is dealt with in chaps 8–11 of the Theologico-
political treatise. I shall not discuss it, Spinoza’s overall conclusion being simply
that tradition is unreliable and the question of canonicity irrelevant. Third, and
finally, interpretation should also have some sort of ‘authority’, not in the sense
that its authority would be the same as that of Scripture (that is, of God or the
Holy Ghost as being the true author of Scripture), but in the sense that there
should not be the slightest uncertainty about the meaning of the text; indeed, there
should be a norm or a judge of interpretation. In fact, this aspect of the problem
has proved to be the most intractable. According to Roman Catholic theology,
Scripture is so difficult that we need the guidance of the Church to understand
it, so the authority of all interpretation would be the Church. Reformed theology,
on the other hand, claims that the Bible is absolutely clear and that whatever
difficulties arise can be solved within the framework of the Bible itself, so the
authority of the interpretation of Scripture should be Scripture itself. Before dealing
with Spinoza’s solutions let us see how some of his contemporaries dealt with these
problems.
Orthodox Views
According to the Enchiridium religionis reformatae by Antonius Walaeus
(1573–1639), a Leiden theologian whose work is fairly representative of the Dutch
Reformed tradition, theology is knowledge (sapientia) of things divine.2 Scripture is
Divine (has ‘divinity’ or ‘authority’) because each and every part of it is written by
the Holy Ghost. It is also true and clear because the Holy Ghost is a spirit of truth.
If, therefore, any difficulty arises it should be solved ex sola Scriptura – from
Scripture alone. Scripture must be interpreted ‘by itself’, that is, all we want to
interpret Scripture is Scripture and nothing but Scripture. Here is how Walaeus
formulates the principle: ‘Holy Scripture is its own interpreter or rather the Holy
Ghost speaking in Scripture; for just as the sun shows its light to the eye without any
help Scripture indicates its true sense in clear passages whilst obscure passages
become clear by comparing them with those that are more clear.’3 For Walaeus that
means that we should interpret Scripture literally throughout unless that yields a
meaning which clearly contradicts another, very clear, passage in Scripture. Thus,
for example, there are places in Scripture in which God is said to ‘walk’ or to ‘sit’,
but a literal interpretation (which would imply that God has a body and is in space)
would contradict the numerous very clear passages according to which God is
incorporeal. So the clear passages provide the authority for the interpretation of
other passages.4 Finally, although Walaeus is not very explicit on this point, the use
94


The Impossibility of Theology
95
of ancillary disciplines, like philosophy and history, is generally encouraged, despite
the principle that Scripture should be understood ‘by itself’.
In fact, the use of late scholastic philosophy is highly characteristic of the
