theologians that came of age during the Remonstrant crisis (1610–20), such as
Gysbertus Voetius (1589–1676), Jacobus Revius (1586–1658) and Johannes
Maccovius (1588–1644).5 According to them the use of philosophy is not to clarify
the text – which after all is supposed to be absolutely clear – but to give its contents
a systematic and articulated form. According to Voetius, for example, many dogmas
are expressed in different ways and different words (verbis aequipollentibus), so the
use of logic and metaphysics is necessary.6 Besides, reason is a divine gift, whose
use can never be an obstacle to the right understanding of Scripture.7 Accordingly,
St Paul’s warning against ‘philosophy and vain deceit’ (Col. 2:8) is not directed
against philosophy in general but only in so far as it is vain and deceitful!8 Indeed
faith is knowledge and therefore must have the form of natural knowledge even if its
source and scope could not be more different. Still, philosophy is never the norm or
the judge of a given interpretation; that is Scripture and nothing but Scripture.9 So if
we find in Scripture something which on a literal interpretation would be contrary to
philosophical truth we should always prefer Scripture and be content with ‘learned
ignorance’, a decision no doubt facilitated by the idea that fallen man cannot know
very much anyway.10
The Voetian approach (to characterize it after its most prominent representative)
amounts to an uneasy compromise between scholastic rationalism and the rule of
sola Scriptura.11 For on the one hand it is claimed that nothing external to Scripture
is needed to know its true meaning, whereas on the other it is admitted that without
philosophy many things would remain obscure. Voetians claimed, however, that
‘sound’ philosophy is nothing but the result of common sense and experience – and
of course the good Lord never forbade us to use those.12 As a result philosophies
explicitly based on experience and common sense – Aristotelian philosophy in
particular – took place of honour. The fear of ‘enthusiasm’, on the other hand, which
explains much of the logic of Calvinism, was acutely revived by the confrontation
with Descartes, who rejected common sense, doubting even the existence of the
external world, and who, in the eyes of his adversaries at any rate, replaced logic and
common experience with subjectivism and hero worship.13 Accordingly, it is on the
precise issue of the nature of philosophy that the difference between ‘Voetian’ and
‘Cartesian’ Orthodoxy can be located.
Cartesian Orthodoxy
Cartesians did not consciously change the theological premises of the debate but
since they rejected ‘ordinary’ philosophy and broke with common sense they found
it necessary to revise a hermeneutic model that was already strained beyond its
capacity. Their strategy was to separate philosophy and theology, basically by
assigning to each a different object and a different method: nature to philosophy and
natural reason, and salvation to theology (interpretation of Scripture) and faith. This
was achieved in various ways by Abraham Heidanus (1597–1678), Christopher
Wittich (1625–87), Lambertus van Velthuyzen (1622–85) and Johannes de Raey


(1622–1702).14 For our purpose we may concentrate on Wittich, whose
Dissertationes duae (1653) contain the first public expression of a view repeated
over and over again until the end of the 17th century.15
According to Wittich, whenever the Bible speaks of nature it is secundum
opinionem vulgi (according to the opinions of the vulgar); not secundum accuratam
rei veritatem (according to the precise truth of the matter).16 That does not mean that
the Holy Ghost endorses the opinions of the vulgar; all it means is that it uses their
language.17 Many ordinary expressions are based on an erroneous view of the world
but by using them one does not commit oneself to that view. An astronomer can refer
to the sun as ‘rising’ even if he knows that the sun does not move; a physician can
speak of a ‘cruel’ disease without thinking of diseases as agents, and so on. And in
the same way Scripture refers to the sun as ‘moving’ without meaning to say that it
actually moves. In fact, Scripture is concerned, not with conveying a scientific view
of the world, but with salvation. And from that point of view physics and astronomy
are simply irrelevant. And finally Scripture addresses, not primarily specialists and
philosophers, but the common people, who generally do not understand a scientific
explanation of the world. Indeed, given its difficulty, it would prevent them from
accepting revelation.
Wittich takes some of his arguments from Scripture itself. Thus he quotes Isaiah
to the effect that the Bible is written stylo humano, ‘with a man’s pen’ (Is. 8:1).
According to Wittich, all, including the authors of the Statenvertaling (the Dutch
‘authorized version’), agree that this means that the Bible speaks the language of the
people rather than the learned. There is no disagreement either – vide Calvin’s
commentary on Gen. 1:16 – that if God had spoken more accurately he would have
made things very difficult indeed for most people.18 As a result, if in Jos. 10:13 (‘and
the sun stood still and the moon stayed, until the people had avenged themselves
upon their enemies’), the locus classicus of the debate on the relation between
theology and philosophy, movement is attributed to the sun, this is nothing but a
concession to ordinary language and ordinary thinking.19
One can imagine one objection at least to this view: if Scripture respects the
prejudices of the vulgar when speaking of nature, how can one be certain that it
speaks plain truth when dealing with other things?20 Wittich has two rather different
answers. First, given the fact that the mysteries of faith cannot be known without
revelation, common sense has no prejudices in this field that should be respected.21
Second, faith is a condition for salvation but from that point of view nature can be
ignored without danger.22 Again, Wittich turns to Scripture to prove that its aim is
thoroughly practical. It ‘is profitable for doctrine, for reproof, for correction and for
instruction in righteousness’ (2 Tim. 3:16–17).23
In a way Wittich interprets Scripture ‘by itself’; in any case he has no
explicit intention to turn philosophy into the norm or the judge of interpretation.
Instead of claiming that whatever Scripture says on nature cannot be true on the
ground that it is demonstrably false he argues that, if we read Scripture
accurately, natural philosophy is seen to be none of its business and therefore
that whatever it says on nature must not be treated as knowledge – it simply
is not part of the true meaning of Scripture. In principle at least it is on
the basis of a reading of Scripture that he defines the aim of Scripture from
which in turn he derives a hermeneutic model. In any case, philosophy and
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
96


The Impossibility of Theology
97
theology are separated because they deal with different types of truths on different
types of subjects.
‘Cartesian’ Heresy: Lodewijk Meyer
Among Spinoza’s friends the main figure to be dealt with is Lodewijk Meyer
(1638–81).24 In 1666 Meyer published Philosophia S. Scripturae Interpres, a book
that provoked almost more debate than all the works of Descartes and Spinoza
together.25 The alleged basis of Meyer’s theory – of which only the barest outline
can be given here – is that words do not refer to things, to which everybody has
access through experience, but to ideas, which are private.26 So we can never be
certain of the meaning of a text because we can never be certain what precise ideas
the author attached to his words. Accordingly, if the aim of interpretation is to
identify the author’s meaning, a cloud of uncertainty hangs over it. This is a general
claim, valid of all sorts of texts. It would be destructive of theology if it could in no
way be qualified. However, Meyer pursues, Scripture is the only text we know
having ‘authority’. That implies that the true meaning of Scripture is by definition a
number of truths.27 So if we have a method of knowing the truth we should use it by
all means to interpret Scripture, or rather to confer or deny certainty (‘authority’) to
a given interpretation. Now Descartes does teach such a method, linking truth and
certainty; it is that method therefore that allows us to identify the Bible’s true
meaning and be certain of any given interpretation.28 Accordingly, the philosophy of
Descartes should be the ‘norm’ or the ‘judge’ of any interpretation. Meyer admits
that parts of Scripture resist the use of this method and continue to admit of different
interpretations. However, the Holy Ghost must have foreseen and willed this. In
such cases therefore no particular interpretation can claim authority and that means
that there is a plurality of meanings.29
Eccentric though it is, Meyer’s theory seems straightforward. In the ‘epilogue’,
however, and in answer to an objection, Meyer restates it in a significantly
different way. One could object, indeed, that if we can interpret the Bible only in so
far as we already know the truth – if knowledge of truth is necessary to confer
authority on a particular interpretation – interpretation of Scripture becomes
irrelevant. Indeed, why should we read and interpret Scripture if we can know its
true meaning only to the extent that we already know the truth? Meyer’s reply is a
discussion of the notion of ‘signification’. Signification is a contingent relation
between a sign (a word) and what is signified by that sign (a concept). We must
know a sign before it can be used as a signifier and have a concept (idea) before
it can be signified. So we must have the concepts «man» and «tree» and know
the words ‘man’ and ‘tree’ before we can decide (or learn) that ‘man’ signifies
«man» and ‘tree’ «tree». According to Meyer, however, it is not necessary to have
an adequate concept: To use ‘man’ and ‘tree’ correctly ‘it is enough to have
seen [men and trees] once or twice or to be aware of them through some other
sense or to be otherwise acquainted with them’.30 In sum, we can know the
meaning of ‘tree’ without having the true and adequate idea «tree»; we can know the
meaning of ‘man’ without knowing anything precise or true about human nature.
Therefore, since a correct understanding of the word ‘tree’ is possible even in the


absence of an adequate idea «tree», the interpretation of a text on trees neither
presupposes nor entails any exact or true knowledge of trees. If interpretation is
based on inadequate ideas – as it usually is – it does not produce knowledge. But
even if the ideas on which it is based are adequate it still does not produce
knowledge because knowledge is the result of a reflection on ideas. We do not know
that God knows everything by frequently reading or hearing, and indeed
understanding the meaning of, the phrase ‘God is omniscient’ but by reflecting on
ideas such as «God», «perfection», «omniscience», and so on. Consequently,
interpretation never yields knowledge; all it can do is to stimulate reflection on the
ideas we already have.
Although they do not necessarily contradict each other, the epilogue and the main
text are in an essential way different. According to the main text, interpretation is
possible in one privileged case (Scripture) and on the basis of true and certain
knowledge (Cartesian philosophy). According to the epilogue, no interpretation ever
yields knowledge. So either interpretation is impossible unless it is based on
knowledge, or interpretation is possible but then it will never produce knowledge. If
we apply this to theology, Meyer’s conclusion would be twofold: (a) theology-as-
interpretation presupposes, not only that Scripture contains the truth and nothing but
the truth (which is traditional) but also that we have independent knowledge of the
truths contained in Scripture; (b) theology-as-knowledge cannot be based on
interpretation because interpretation never yields knowledge. In the epilogue this
conclusion is based, not primarily (as in the main text) on a theory of meaning, but
on a definition of knowledge: since knowledge is the result of reflection it can never
be the result of interpretation. The argument of the main text on the other hand is
concerned with meaning: we can interpret a text (identify its meaning) if and only
if we attribute authority to it; that is, if we believe that it contains the truth and
nothing but the truth (implying that texts without ‘authority’ cannot be interpreted at
all).
Now obviously that is not true, for there are many texts we can perfectly
understand without assuming that they contain the truth. And Meyer admits as much,
even for Scripture. Thus, and the example is his, the literal meaning of expressions
like ‘God’s arm’ or ‘God’s finger’ is clear enough but no theologian sound of mind
would claim that to be the true meaning of those expressions as they occur in
Scripture.31 Why? Obviously because theologians know (or believe) that God has no
body. But that means that if Scripture had no authority at all we would have no, or
in any case less, difficulty in interpreting it. Indeed, we could simply take the literal
meaning and decide that the author was mistaken about the true nature of God – just
as we might decide that, say, Descartes is mistaken about the essence of material
body but could still interpret him as claiming that the essence of material body is
extension. As a result, the interpretation of Scripture is problematic, not because
interpretation and meaning are problematic, but because we assume that Scripture
contains the truth and nothing but the truth, that is, because it has ‘authority’.32
Meyer’s true claim, accordingly, is not that interpretation is impossible except in one
particular case but that if the object of interpretation is a text with ‘authority’ it can
be interpreted only with the help of an instrument having as much ‘authority’ as the
text. His second claim, on the other hand, would be that if we proceed on the
assumption that interpretation is not problematic it is impossible to base any
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
98


The Impossibility of Theology
99
knowledge on it, given the fact that, by definition, knowledge is the result of a
reflection on ideas.
Obviously, Meyer’s book is a provocation, first and mainly, because he explicitly
rejects the Reformed principle of the absolute clarity of Scripture. Indeed, according
to him nothing is clear ‘in itself’: something is clear, or obscure, only with respect
to what a particular individual knows or believes about truth, language and so on.33
As a result, the principle of interpretation ‘by itself’ – interpreting ‘obscure’
passages by means of ‘clear’ ones – cannot be applied. Meyer’s book is also a
provocation because he gives the same weight to the authority of Scripture and the
authority of Cartesian method. No Cartesian would be willing to concede that,
presumably, but Meyer shows how difficult it is to avoid it. For if ‘certainty’ means
anything at all it is that, if a proposition p is ‘certain’ (known to be true), there is
nothing on the basis of which p could become doubtful or false. Accordingly, if we
certainly know that the sun is at rest in the middle of the universe we are forced to
conclude that, on the basis of a literal interpretation, Scripture would contain
something known to be false – and that means that we can save the authority of
Scripture only by making a distinction between whatever Scripture says about nature
and the rest. So the aim of Meyer’s demonstration seems to be to show that, although
Cartesians try to base their proposal on a reading of Scripture, they actually turn
philosophy into the norm or judge of interpretation. Finally, Meyer’s proposal is a
provocation because he does not restrict his argument to natural philosophy but
extends it to all those subjects the Cartesians had so far avoided:
A great hope smiles upon us: Now that René Descartes, the principal founder and
propagator of philosophy has illuminated the world of letters and left it his example,
philosophy will see its territory extended to all sides by people wishing to walk in his
footsteps. Indeed, we will see pages on God, the rational soul, the highest happiness of
man that will make authority in the interpretation of Holy Scripture and will prepare and
pave the way towards reuniting and reassembling in the sweetness of Charity the Church
of Christ, which so far has been torn apart and divided by eternal schisms.34
It is not simply the case that Cartesian method has not been used; its results cannot
be used because Cartesians have been too afraid to apply it. Moreover, Meyer is
thinking of Spinoza rather than Descartes and that is a significant difference. For
whereas Descartes and the Cartesians had fixed the limits of philosophy in a rather
narrow way, leaving plenty of room for revelation and faith, for Spinoza there are
no limits to what philosophy could ever know, given the identity of the real and the
conceivable. Indeed, the very fact that Meyer mentions God, the soul and human
happiness as subjects on which philosophy could not only say more than it had done
so far, but in saying more could be more helpful in interpreting Scripture, contains
a clear message.
Spinoza: a ‘History’ of Scripture
Two points that are generally made about Spinoza as an ‘interpreter of Scripture’
should be briefly discussed first. It is often claimed that Spinoza is original in
advocating a method of interpreting Scripture ‘by itself’, meaning that his method


of interpretation requires the abandonment of all prejudices. And it is also claimed
that Spinoza promoted a scientific study of Scripture, making full use of all sorts of
ancillary disciplines.35 Both claims, which are intimately related, are tendentious and
possibly false.
As regards the first we have already seen that ‘interpretation of Scripture by itself’
was also a trademark of orthodox theology. This should be enough to render
Spinoza’s claim suspect, especially because the author of the Theologico-political
treatise (and for that matter of the Ethics as well) more or less continually interprets
Scripture in the light of his own philosophy, which may or may not be a legitimate
procedure but in no case can be seen as interpretation ‘by itself’. Thus Spinoza’s
interpretation of the Fall which I discussed in chap. 1 is based, not on a study of the
text, but on his own philosophy.36 From chap. 4 of the Theologico-political treatise
one could cite an abundance of examples to illustrate the same point, including a
second interpretation of the Fall, which Spinoza does not further develop, allegedly
because he cannot be sure that his explanation is ‘in agreement with the author’s
intention’ (cum scriptoris mente), and because ‘there are many who do not grant that
this narrative is a parable’.37 But how can one, simply by basing oneself on the
evidence provided by Scripture, know that it is a parable? And if it is not in
agreement with the author’s intentions how can one claim that it is a correct
interpretation? Something similar could be said of Spinoza’s treatment of miracles
in chap. 6, where, as we have seen, he arrives at the general conclusion that reports
of miracles ‘were inserted by sacrilegious men’.38 Again, whether that is true or not
is a matter of opinion, but, true or false, it can hardly be based on an interpretation
of Scripture ‘by itself’.
The second claim, on the other hand, ignores the fact that Spinoza is not in favour
of interpretation at all: ‘If men were really sincere […] they would not be gripped
by this blind and passionate desire to interpret Scripture and introduce innovations
in religion.’39 Interpretation is the cause of sectarianism especially in combination
with superstition, ‘which teaches men to despise reason and nature and to admire
and venerate only that which is opposed to both’.40 The problem is aggravated by
theologians:
The chief concern of theologians has been to extort from Holy Scripture their own
arbitrarily invented ideas for which they claim divine authority […]. And if while so doing
they feel any misgivings, their fear is not that they may be mistaken in their understanding
of the Holy Ghost and may stray from the path of salvation but that others may convict
them of error, thus annihilating their personal prestige and bringing them into contempt.41
Of course Spinoza might think of ‘scientific’ interpretation as an antidote. But
according to chap. 14 the sin of the theologians is not that they adapt Scripture to
their own ideas – indeed, that is what everybody should do – but that they prevent
others from doing the same:
I will not level the charge of impiety against those sectaries simply because they adapt the
words of Scripture to their own beliefs. Just as Scripture was once adapted to the
understanding of the people of that time, in the same way anyone may now adapt it to his
own beliefs if he feels that that will enable him to obey God with heartier will in those
matters that pertain to justice and charity. My accusation against them is that they refuse
to grant the same freedom to others.42
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
100


The Impossibility of Theology
101
The problem is not that the method of the theologians is arbitrary but that it produces
a false authority. But if the problem is the authority claimed by theologians the real
problem is their academic status. Indeed, that is what distinguishes them from
ordinary believers. And that makes it rather unlikely that Spinoza is concerned with
promoting a ‘scientific’ study of the Bible, unless it would help him to unmask the
authority of the theologians.
Spinoza’s own method is based on a hermeneutic model borrowed from Bacon:
‘The method of interpreting Scripture is no different from the method of interpreting
Nature and is in fact in complete accord with it.’43 So the interpretation of Scripture
should follow the rules of the ‘interpretation of nature’, which is Bacon’s word for
‘natural science’. Now Bacon chose the term interpretatio naturae for natural
science to insist that we should display the same zeal and the same accuracy in the
study of nature as we do in the interpretation of Scripture and according to him that
means that we should not pontificate about nature but examine it thoroughly.44 But
is it helpful to reapply Bacon’s model to the study of Scripture?
Bacon distinguishes the interpretation of nature (interpretatio naturae) from the
anticipation of nature (anticipatio naturae).45 Whereas anticipation is based on
prejudice, interpretation is based on a careful review of all the facts, that is, on a
natural and experimental history, corresponding presumably to a careful reading of
Scripture.46 So if we reapply Bacon’s method to the interpretation of Scripture the
point seems to be no other than that the interpretation of Scripture must be based on
a careful reading of the text. That also seems to be the suggestion of Spinoza:
The method of interpreting nature consists essentially in composing a detailed history of
nature from which, as from sure data, we can deduce definitions of the things of nature.
Now in exactly the same way the task of Scriptural interpretation requires us to make a
reliable history of Scripture, and from this, as from sure data and principles, to deduce by
logical inference the mind [mentem] of the authors of Scripture.47
So historia would mean ‘a careful and attentive reading of the text’. In the next
sentence, however, ‘reading’ and ‘history’ are dissociated: ‘In this way – that is, by
allowing no other principles or data for the interpretation of Scripture and study of
its contents except those that can be gathered from Scripture itself [that is,
presumably, from reading it, Th.V] and from its history – steady progress can be
made….’48 So ‘history’ would not just be a ‘careful and attentive reading’ but an
attempt to ‘collect facts about the text’. Accordingly, it looks as if Spinoza manages
to make his point only by means of an equivocation on the word ‘history’ (reading
a text as well as collecting facts about that text), which in turn is made possible by
an equivocation on the word ‘interpretation’ (knowledge and interpretation properly
speaking).49
This strategy – for I assume that it is a conscious strategy – is even more obvious
as soon as Spinoza starts to explain for what reasons and on what subjects a history
is necessary:
If we read a book relating incredible or incomprehensible events or which is written in a
very obscure style and if we do not know the author or the time or the occasion of its
composition it will be vain to achieve a greater understanding of its true meaning.
Deprived of all those facts we cannot possibly know what was or could have been the
author’s meaning.50


So reading is not enough. The meaning of an obscure text can be established only on
the basis of a ‘history’, that is, obviously, information on the text (date of
composition, language, and so on) and its presumed author. Granted that that is
sensible advice it also means that the reason why Spinoza needs a ‘history’ cannot
be the same as why he is a supporter of interpretation ‘by itself’. Indeed, a ‘history’
would be necessary only to the extent that Scripture can not be interpreted ‘by itself’.
Spinoza admits – and that is another reason why historia is necessary – that not
everything in Scripture is incomprehensible given the fact that ‘the moral doctrines
of Scripture […] can be demonstrated from common notions’.51 Now since a history
is claimed to be necessary only to the extent that a text cannot be understood ‘by
itself’, one would presume that, as far as the moral doctrine of Scripture is
concerned, we do not need a history. According to Spinoza, however, that is wrong
because ‘it cannot be proved from common notions that Scripture teaches those
doctrines: That can be established only from Scripture itself’.52 So here historia does
mean study of the text: ‘if we want to testify, without any prejudice, to the divinity
of Scripture it must be made evident from Scripture alone that it teaches true moral
doctrine for it is on that basis alone that its divinity can be proved’.53 The truth of a
moral doctrine can be demonstrated independently but that Scripture contains that
doctrine can be ascertained only by reading it. So we must read and examine
Scripture before we can know to what extent it is ‘divine’, that is, to what extent it
contains a moral doctrine – a far-reaching claim because it implies that (unlike
Walaeus, for example) Spinoza dissociates ‘authority’ (truth and certainty) and
‘divinity’ (morality).
Finally, Spinoza argues that Scripture provides no definitions of things, which
therefore, ‘just as the definitions of things of nature are inferred from the various
operations of nature’, must be ‘elicited from the various Biblical narratives on each
thing as they occur in those writings’.54 It is here that the analogy with Bacon breaks
down. That nature does not provide definitions is obvious: indeed, nature is not a
discursive text. However, there is no reason why we should require definitions in the
domain of interpretation (as the recovery of meaning). Indeed, definitions are
necessary only to the extent that we aim for knowledge (that is, interpretation in the
Baconian sense). The problem is of course that from a traditional point of view
theology aims for both: meaning and knowledge. Accordingly, and again, the use of
Baconian terminology allows Spinoza to commute between theology-as-
interpretation and theology-as-knowledge by playing on the ambiguity of
‘interpretation’ (knowledge and identification of meaning) and ‘history’ (reading
and fact finding).
In the paragraphs that follow Spinoza indicates on what particular subjects
historia should provide information: (1) ‘It should inform us of the nature and
properties of the language in which the Bible was written and which its authors were
accustomed to speak.’55 (2) ‘The pronouncements made in each book should be
assembled and listed under headings, so that we can have to hand all the texts that
treat of the same subject.’56 (3) ‘It should set forth the circumstances relevant to all
the extant books of the prophets, giving the life, character and pursuits of the author
of every book, detailing who he was, etc.’57
Now before we go on let us see how this relates to 17th-century theology. In fact,
from that point of view, Spinoza’s proposals do not contain anything very
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
102


The Impossibility of Theology
103
revolutionary. Any Reformed theologian was supposed to be able to read the Bible
in the language in which it was written.58 To make lists of the various occurrences
of a word or expression was common practice – indeed, if anything, it is
presupposed in the Orthodox method of interpreting Scripture ‘by itself’.59
Furthermore, archeological, geographical and historical studies on Scripture and on
the way in which it was handed down were generally encouraged even if very few
people, if any, were ready to use the results of those studies to undermine the
authority and canonicity of Scripture.60 So from the point of view of theology-as-
interpretation Spinoza does not propose much that is strictly new. What is new is
that he applies to theology-as-knowledge the Baconian model of the interpretation
of nature, as if to say that, in so far as theology is knowledge at all, it must be
ordinary scientific knowledge and follow Baconian rules – more or less as Meyer
had used Cartesian rules. So Spinoza is playing two different games. He plays the
game of interpretation according to the rules of his adversaries but he plays the game
of knowledge according to the rules of Bacon. Let us first concentrate on the first
game, for it is in that field that Spinoza’s merits seem to be least contested.61
Theology-as-Interpretation
According to Spinoza, interpretation can go ahead once we have a history of
Scripture: ‘Now that we possess this historical account […] we can embark on the
task of investigating the meaning of the prophets and the Holy Ghost’ (ad mentem
prophetarum et Spiritus Sancti investigandam accingamus).62 Since Spinoza then
goes on to actually propose an interpretation, in which the moral aspects of Biblical
teaching are emphasized, one would say that the required ‘history’ is completed –
that the only reason why it is not given is its length and detail. However, not only
are the moral doctrines the only part of Scripture Spinoza claims can be understood
without a history (because they are essentially clear in themselves); a few pages
later it emerges that the history he wants is not available. Indeed, according to
Spinoza, its difficulties are insuperable, so much so that ‘those who are in possession
of a sure tradition of true explanation transmitted from the prophets themselves, as
the Pharisees claim, or those who have a pontiff whose interpretation of Scripture is
infallible, as the Roman Catholics boast, would have greater certainty’.63 Given the
fact that Spinoza immediately rejects those possibilities because we can be sure
neither of the tradition of the Pharisees nor of the authority of the Pope, that means
that as far as interpretation is concerned history has very little to offer.
Difficulties are caused by various factors, which Spinoza relates in consider-
able detail. First of all there is no certain knowledge of the language: ‘The men of
old who used the Hebrew language have left to posterity no information concerning
the basic principles and study of that language.’64 That language also raises intrinsic
problems: consonants are interchangeable, there are no vowels, the use of tenses is
unclear, and so on.65 And then, ‘we do not possess [certain books of Scripture] in
the language in which they were first written’.66 So the first part of the history of
Scripture is entirely lacking. Moreover, it is useless to make a comparative study of
parallel places because Scripture was written by different authors: ‘It is only by
chance that a comparison of passages can throw light on any particular passage,


since no prophet wrote with the deliberate purpose of explaining another’s words.’67
So the second part of history cannot be provided either. Finally, we do not know the
historical circumstances of each book:
We either have no knowledge at all or but doubtful knowledge of the authors […] of many
of the books. We do not even know on what occasion or at what time these books of
unknown authorship were written. Furthermore we do not know into whose hands all these
books fell, or in whose copies so many different readings were found nor yet again
whether there were not many other versions in other hands.68
So the information Spinoza wants as a basis for interpretation is totally lacking. And
that means that, in so far as Scripture does not contain moral doctrines, the
interpretation of Scripture is impossible: ‘The path [via] which it tells us is the true
and correct one has never been pursued nor trodden by men and so with the passage
of time has become exceedingly difficult and almost impassable.’69
That leaves us with the moral doctrines of Scripture, for which no history was
needed. These are not only true but also ‘clear’ – that is, they are not specifically in
need of interpretation – for no other reason than that they can be conceived:
I must again emphasize, with regard to all those difficulties that they can prevent us from
grasping the meaning of the prophets only in matters beyond normal comprehension,
which can merely be imagined; it is not true of matters open to intellectual perception,
whereof we can readily form a clear concept [clarum conceptum]. For things which of
their own nature are readily apprehended can never be so obscurely worded that they are
not easily understood.70
Little science is needed to understand the precept ‘Thou shalt not kill’. This is not
something ‘merely imagined’, as prophecy produced by the imagination alone, but
a precept that can be understood even by those who do not know it as an eternal
truth: ‘For the teachings of true piety are expressed in quite ordinary language, and
being directed to the generality of mankind they are straightforward and easy to
understand.’71 Accordingly the borderline between what can and cannot be
understood without a history is ultimately of an epistemological nature: things
‘intellectually perceived’ are ‘clear’, whereas things ‘merely imagined’ are
‘obscure’. Scripture’s moral doctrines are clear and do not require interpretation for
that reason. All the rest is fundamentally obscure. All we can do is ‘discover what
they [the prophets] saw or heard and not what they intended to signify; indeed, this
we can only guess at, not infer with certainty on the basis of Scripture’.72 So the
conclusion must be either that ‘theology’ is impossible (because most of the relevant
data are completely lacking) or, if we restrict it to an assessment of the moral
doctrines of Scripture, that it is a matter of common sense and straightforward
reading. Still, whereas on the whole whatever is true in Scripture is also clear, the
fact that something is known to be true must not be a reason to ascribe it to Scripture:
What God is, in what way he sees and provides for all things and similar matters, Scripture
does not teach formally and as an eternal doctrine. The prophets were not in agreement on
these matters and therefore on topics of this kind we should make no assertion that claims
to be the doctrine of the Holy Ghost, even if the natural light of reason is quite decisive.73
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
104


The Impossibility of Theology
105
In sum, the only doctrine that can certainly be ascribed to Scripture is its moral
doctrine. And since that can clearly be understood, theology as a specific discipline
is not necessary. Since, on the other hand, whatever could make the interpretation of
Scripture possible is not available, theology-as-interpretation is impossible. The
claim  that theology-as-knowledge is impossible is further elaborated by Spinoza in
his discussion of possible objections.
Objections and Replies
The first objection, presented as a view directly opposed to Spinoza’s own,
is that ‘the natural light of reason does not have the power to interpret Scripture
and that a supernatural light is absolutely essential for this task’.74 This view is
dismissed in a few words, not only because even a ‘supernatural’ interpretation
is thoroughly human (and therefore natural), but also for two other reasons:
(a) the fact that we cannot interpret Scripture by natural means (that is, by
Spinoza’s own method) is not sufficient reason to resort to ‘supernatural’ means;
(b) the original audiences of prophets did not enjoy any supernatural light
either. Hence Spinoza’s ironic conclusion that ‘those who look to supernatural light
to understand the meaning of the prophets and the apostles are sadly wanting in
natural light and I can hardly think that such men possess a divine supernatural
gift’.75
Maimonides (1135–1204) on the other hand, who is quoted as the source of a
second objection, would believe that ‘every passage of Scripture admits of various
and even contrary meanings and that we cannot be certain of the true meaning of any
passage unless we know that, as we interpret it, there is nothing in that passage that
is either not in agreement or contrary to reason’. Indeed, ‘if in its literal sense it is
found to be contrary to reason, then however clear that passage may appear, he
maintains that it must be interpreted in a different way’.76 Spinoza illustrates this
with a page-long quotation from the Guide of the Perplexed, which, somewhat
abridged, runs as follows:
The texts that teach that the world was created are not more numerous than those that teach
that God is corporeal.77 There are ways, not barren to us nor even difficult of access, by
which we can explain the texts that deal with the question of the world’s creation. Our
explanation could have followed the same lines as when we denied the corporeality of God
and perhaps this might have been much easier to achieve […]. Yet there are two other
reasons that prevent me from so doing and from believing that the world is eternal. First
[…] the eternity of the world has not been proved […]. Second, the belief that God is
incorporeal is not contrary to the basic tenets of the Law, whereas the belief that the world
is eternal, in the way that Aristotle held, destroys the very foundations of the Law.78
So, to be perfectly clear, on the issue of the creation of the world, Maimonides takes
Scripture literally, not only because the philosophers’ claim that the world is eternal
is insufficiently demonstrated, but also because it would undermine the authority of
the law not to assume that there has been creation. Indeed, in the same chapter
Maimonides admits that the assumption of the eternity of the world would ‘give the
lie to every miracle and reduce to inanity all hopes and threats the Law has held


out’.79 This means that Spinoza must reformulate Maimonides’ proposal as a
counterfactual conditional:
If he had been convinced on rational grounds that the world is eternal, he would not have
hesitated to distort and explain away Scripture until it appeared to teach the same doctrine.
Indeed, he would have been quite convinced that Scripture, in spite of its plain denials at
every point, intended to teach this same doctrine of the eternity of the world.80
In itself this is a fair presentation of the views of Maimonides, who held that, given
the fact that truth is one, we should be ready to resort to non-literal interpretation
whenever a passage in Scripture can be shown to be contrary to a demonstrated
truth.81 However, Spinoza suppresses Maimonides’ precise description of the way
such a passage should be reinterpreted. In such a case Maimonides claims it should
be interpreted figuratively: ‘Nor are the gates of figurative interpretation shut in our
faces […] we could interpret them as figurative […] we should be very well able to
give a figurative interpretation […] as we have given a figurative interpretation of
those other texts.’82 So, presumably, figurative interpretation as such is not the issue.
Indeed, as we have seen, Spinoza himself believes that, for example, the story of the
Fall is ‘a parable’ and frequently indulges in what can only be called figurative
interpretation. Then what is the issue? And is ‘Maimonides’ the historical
Maimonides? Spinoza’s rejoinder may create some uneasiness on both counts.83
As against ‘Maimonides’, Spinoza proposes three arguments, which I state
succinctly before discussing them in any detail. First, if ‘Maimonides’ were right,
one could not be certain of any meaning at all for ‘as long as we are not convinced
of the truth of a statement we cannot know whether or not it is in conformity with
reason and consequently neither can we know whether the literal meaning is true or
false’.84 Second, ‘nearly all the contents of Scripture are such as cannot be deduced
from principles known by the natural light.’85 Third, the common people ‘would
have to rely on the authority and testimony of philosophers’.86
With respect to the historical Maimonides, that makes little sense. The first
objection disregards the fact that Maimonides’ proposal is meant for a limited
number of texts, namely, those which, if interpreted literally, would be contrary to
reason. Other texts that are not clearly contrary to reason (on the creation of the
world, for example) should be interpreted literally, especially if there is a moral
implication. So perhaps Spinoza’s objection should be read as an accusation of
inconsequence and inconsistency; perhaps Maimonides should have extended his
method to all of Scripture, including those parts of Scripture on the creation of the
world. Something similar could be said of the second objection, given the fact that,
actually, Maimonides is prepared to accept prophecy as literally true whenever it
deals with problems that cannot be solved by speculation. Thus the creation of the
world ‘should be accepted without proof because of prophecy, which explains things
to which it is not in the power of speculation to accede’.87 So the true accusation
could be that, if Maimonides is serious about his proposal, he should also reject the
literal meaning of statements that are ‘above reason’. And the third objection plainly
ignores the fact that Maimonides was not writing for ordinary people at all but for
an intellectual elite.88 But let us have a closer look.
The first argument seems to run as follows: ‘The Bible contains any number of
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
106


The Impossibility of Theology
107
sentences (p, q, r, s …), each of which is true but could be ambiguous. As long
therefore as we do not certainly know whether p or ¬p there is no way to become
certain that p actually means «p».’ The argument is correct if one assumes that the
only way to know a belief to be true is to demonstrate it to be true – which is not
Maimonides’ view, as we saw, for he believes that for a statement to be known to be
true it is enough for it to be in Scripture and not to be contrary to reason.89
Accordingly, Spinoza’s point would be that ‘Maimonides’ is not entitled to the
simultaneous use of two criteria of truth, given the fact that he is prepared to accept
the authority of Scripture only in so far as it does not lead him to accept something
as true which on rational grounds he should believe to be false. In any case, the issue
is the authority of Scripture in relation to the ‘authority’ of reason. If we assume (like
‘Maimonides’) that Scripture contains the truth and nothing but the truth, the only
way to identify its meaning would be either to apply the method of reason (but then
we should apply it not in a limited number of cases but throughout), or to take
Scripture literally (and then we should do that even in those cases where its meaning
is contrary to reason and philosophy). But Spinoza’s argument could also be that of
Meyer, namely, that if we interpret a text we believed to be true we need philosophy
as a norm of interpretation throughout, especially if we have a philosophy that
covers all of reality.
In his second argument Spinoza claims that much of the contents of the Bible
‘cannot be deduced from general principles, as we have already shown’.90 This is
somewhat ambiguous. For, apart from the fact that the historical Maimonides was
perfectly aware of that, as we have seen, what Spinoza has in fact shown a few pages
earlier is that the essential doctrines of Scripture (its moral teachings) can be
deduced from ‘common notions’ (even if on the basis of common notions we cannot
prove that Scripture contains those doctrines). The only way to make sense of the
argument, therefore, is on the basis of Spinoza’s assumption that something is
known to be true only if it is demonstrated to be true; so if ‘Maimonides’ accepts the
authority of reason he should not assume the truth of those parts of Scripture that
surpass human understanding, or in other words ‘Maimonides’ should not believe in
the authority of Scripture if he also believes in the ‘authority’ of reason.
The third argument seems to be of a somewhat different order: ‘the common
people […] would have to rely on the authority and testimony of philosophers [and]
assume that philosophers are infallible in their interpretation of Scripture’.91 Now in
itself the idea that philosophers are ‘infallible’, or in any case know the truth in a
definitive way, is not completely foreign to Spinoza. So that presumably is not the
point. That seems to be, again, the authority of Scripture. For if it has authority – if
it does contain the truth and nothing but the truth – then philosophers do have a
privileged position, for they have a method of knowing the truth and of being certain
of it, which moreover is not accessible to common people. So the point would be
that, if we start by giving ‘authority’ to a text – in the sense of believing that it
contains the truth and nothing but the truth – it is inevitable, not only that its
interpreters also have authority (for unless we are dealing with moral rules the
majority of the people are unable to know the truth), but also that they derive their
authority from the use of philosophy.
What creates confusion is Spinoza’s simultaneous use of two contradictory
assumptions: the fact that a claim occurs in Scripture is enough to be certain of its


truth; and the only way to become certain of a claim is to demonstrate it to be true.
Now whereas the first claim can safely be attributed to Maimonides, the second is
clearly Spinoza’s own. Still, one cannot say that ‘Maimonides’ rejects it; indeed, the
fact that something is demonstrated to be true (in philosophy) gives him the
‘authority’ to propose a non-literal interpretation of those passages in Scripture that
would be contrary to it if they were interpreted literally. So Spinoza’s precise point
presumably is that it is impossible to work with two different criteria of truth and
certainty – that is, with two types of ‘authority’. Either we accept the ‘authority’ of
reason (and then we must examine every statement before believing it to be true), or
we accept the authority of Scripture (and then we should interpret it literally even if
the result is contrary to reason). ‘Authority’ is indivisible, Spinoza seems to say. If
one attributes authority to Scripture only in so far as it is not contrary to reason and
philosophy one does not attribute any authority to Scripture at all. But ‘authority’ is
also a belief, namely, the belief that Scripture contains the truth and nothing but the
truth. And given the fact that truth can be known by reason and philosophy, that
would mean that the true ‘authority’ of an interpretation derives from its using
philosophy as a norm or judge. As a result, Spinoza’s point is more or less the same
as Meyer’s, namely that one cannot negotiate between the ‘authorities’ of reason and
Scripture; and that if one attributes authority to Scripture one is bound to use reason
and philosophy for its interpretation.
Now the idea that that we can accept the authority of reason and the authority of
Scripture (albeit in different areas) is the trademark, not only of the historical
Maimonides, but of a long succession of Christian philosophers and theologians,
especially, for the modern period, Descartes and Hobbes.92 And that means that
‘Maimonides’ could be almost anybody except Meyer.93 And, indeed, in chap. 13,
Spinoza ascribes the position of ‘Maimonides’ to ‘most theologians’ (communes
theologi), all of whom would argue that ‘those passages [in Scripture] which their
natural light has convinced them are not in agreement with divine nature should be
interpreted in a metaphorical way, while whatever is beyond their understanding
must be taken literally’.94 And again Spinoza’s conclusion is that ‘if every passage
of the former kind in Scripture was meant to be understood and interpreted
metaphorically, Scripture must have been written not for the common people and the
uneducated masses but for the learned alone and for philosophers in particular’.95
Still that very argument may provide a further clue to the identity of ‘Maimonides’.
Apparently ‘Maimonides’ believes that Scripture primarily addresses the vulgar.
And that means that he does not belong to a theological tradition which holds that
Scripture can be fully and authoritatively understood only by professional
theologians. Clearly, ‘Maimonides’ is neither a Roman Catholic nor a Voetian
Calvinist. But he could be a Cartesian Calvinist (like Wittich) if only because the
basic postulate of Cartesian theology was that Scripture is written for the vulgar.96
That Spinoza does see the Cartesians as the more scientific successors of the
historical Maimonides is suggested in any case by his discussion of Jos. 10:11–13 in
chap. 2. Again the issue is interpretation: ‘All commentators have displayed an
extraordinary eagerness to convince themselves that the prophets knew everything
attainable to the human intellect; and although certain passages in Scripture make it
absolutely clear that there were some things the prophets did not know, rather than
admit that there was anything the prophets did not know they prefer to declare that
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
108


The Impossibility of Theology
109
they do not understand those passages or alternatively strive to twist the words of
Scripture to mean what they plainly do not mean.’97 Spinoza rejects both alternatives
for, ‘if whatever is absolutely clear can be accounted obscure and incomprehensible
or otherwise interpreted at will, it will be vain to try and prove anything from
Scripture’.98 So if we come across something clear we should accept that as the
meaning of Scripture even if it is manifestly false. Thus, for example, Joshua
undoubtedly believed that the sun moves. However,
there are many who, refusing to admit that there can be any mutability in the heavens
explain this passage so that it means something quite different. Others who have adopted
a more scientific attitude and understand that the earth moves and the sun is motionless
[…] make every effort to extort that meaning in the teeth of the Scriptural text.99
With the first (who ‘refuse to admit that there can be any mutability in the heavens’)
presumably are meant Maimonides and Gersonides  (Levi ben Gerson, 1288–1344),
according to whom miracles cannot occur in the spheres above the moon.100 But the
‘others’ are undoubtedly Cartesians, given the fact that the discussion between
Cartesians and Voetians concentrated precisely on the question of the movement of
the earth in its relation to the interpretation of Scripture.101 Spinoza, however, finds
this discussion irrelevant because Joshua ‘believed that the sun revolves around the
earth with a diurnal motion’.102 Joshua was a soldier, not a scholar, so any attempt
to ascribe to him the opinions of a scholar is misguided. As a result, ‘Maimonides’
could be a Cartesian, someone who without denying the authority of Scripture tries
to negotiate between reason and Scripture, whilst at the same time claiming that
Scripture is written for everybody.
However, Spinoza’s argument goes beyond spelling out the consequences of the
authority of Scripture for the interpretation of Scripture; indeed, Spinoza’s point is
also that, if Scripture has no authority, no interpretation of Scripture can have any
authority either:
The interpretation of religion is vested above all in each individual. And this again affords
further proof that our method of interpretation is the best. For since the supreme authority
for the interpretation of Scripture is vested in each individual, the rule that governs
interpretation must be nothing other than the natural light that is common to all, and not
any supernatural light nor any external authority. Nor must this rule be so difficult as not
to be available to any but skilled philosophers; it must be suited to the natural and
universal ability and capacity of mankind.103
Everybody has the right to interpret Scripture. That right cannot be restricted –
neither by theologians nor by the magistrate:
The supreme authority to interpret laws and the supreme judgment on affairs of state is
vested in magistrates for this reason only that these belong to the sphere of public right.
Thus, for the same reason, the supreme authority to explain religion and to make
judgments concerning it is vested in each individual because it belongs to the sphere of
individual right.104
If the sovereign is the only interpreter of ‘God’s will’ and if in a modern society the
right of the sovereign is restricted to behaviour, it is a logical step to give every


citizen the purely individual right to ‘explain’ religion, that is, to read Scripture and
draw his own conclusions – but that implies that theologians have no authority at all,
no matter how much they have studied Scripture.
Again: the Authority of Scripture
All theological methods we have seen so far (Spinoza’s own ‘scientific’ method, the
‘supernatural’ method, the ‘Maimonidean’ method) fail for fundamental reasons. A
fourth possibility remains to be considered, namely, that Scripture is essentially
clear and that all we have to do is submit to its ‘authority’. Spinoza examines that
option in his discussion of Alpakhar.105 Alpakhar makes three claims: ‘Reason
should be ancillary to Scripture and completely subservient to it’; ‘Nothing in
Scripture requires a metaphorical explanation merely on the grounds that its literal
meaning is contrary to reason but only if it is contrary to Scripture itself’; ‘Whatever
Scripture teaches in dogmatic form and quite expressly affirms must be accepted as
absolutely true simply on its own authority.’106
On the whole ‘Alpakhar’ resembles the Voetian Orthodox: if he uses philosophy
at all it is to articulate whatever is revealed in the essentially clear text of Scripture.
This does not mean that ‘Alpakhar’ rejects all interpretation; indeed, if Scripture
‘appears to assume what is contrary to its express doctrine’ we must interpret it in a
different way.107 So the criterion for accepting something as the true meaning of
Scripture would not simply be its occurrence in Scripture but also its compatibility
with the ‘express doctrine’ of Scripture. The question would be whether in this way
one avoids the problems of ‘Maimonides’. According to Spinoza one does not. He
praises Alpakhar for interpreting the Bible ‘by itself’ but blames him for refusing to
use his natural judgment:
It is true that, as long as we are simply concerned with the meaning of the text and the
prophet’s intention, Scripture should be explained through Scripture; but having extracted
the true meaning we must necessarily resort to judgement and reason before we can assent
thereto […]. If reason, in spite of her protests, is nevertheless to be made completely
subservient to Scripture must this submission be effected with reason’s concurrence or
without it, blindly? If the latter, then surely we are behaving like fools without judgement.
If the former, then it is only at reason’s behest that we accept Scripture.108
So the problem with ‘Alpakhar’ seems to be that he cannot avoid the use of reason
despite the fact that the only ‘authority’ he acknowledges is that of Scripture. Indeed
the authority of Scripture – the belief that it contains the truth and nothing but the
truth – is either completely irrational (and then it is against human nature) or it is
based on reasons and arguments (and then it is against the authority of Scripture).109
Again, the underlying argument is of the ‘all-or-nothing’ kind: one cannot accept the
findings of reason sometimes without having to accept them always. In any case the
authority of Scripture is an absurd notion, either because it is against our nature or
because it is illogical.
Spinoza’s second argument is that Alpakhar’s method of clarifying obscure
passages by means of clear passages, which is the Orthodox method of interpreting
Scripture ‘by itself’, is ‘rash’ and ‘premature’ (temere dicta), apparently because it
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
110


The Impossibility of Theology
111
rests on the assumption that each and every sentence of Scripture is true.110 So we
must not assume that Scripture contains no falsehood, nor must we believe that it
‘never expressly affirms or denies anything that contradicts what it elsewhere
affirms or denies’.111 Scripture is neither invariably true nor invariably consistent. In
fact, a ‘history’ – and in this case ‘history’ clearly means a reading of the text –
shows that neither the one nor the other can be the case, for there are many instances
in which Scripture is in fact wrong and many more in which it clearly contradicts
itself. Now the first point is of course an ignoratio elenchi, Alpakhar’s point being
that, if something is found in Scripture, it should be held to be true for no other
reason than that it is in Scripture. The second point, however, neatly summarizes
what it means to interpret Scripture ‘by itself’. It turns out to be impossible because
Scripture is not one book but a collection of books; so we could apply Alpakhar’s
method only in cases where we are absolutely certain to be dealing with the same
author. Furthermore, the method of illuminating obscure passages by means of clear
passages is inapplicable anyway. To say of God that he is a fire (Deut. 4:24) is as
‘clear’ as to say that he has no visible qualities (Deut. 4:12). Accordingly, the
problem of which of the two can be used as the authority to interpret the other
passage metaphorically cannot be solved from Scripture alone:
Samuel directly denies that God ever repents (1 Sam. 15:29); on the other hand, Jeremiah
asserts that God repents of the good and evil that he may have decreed (Jer. 18:8–10).
Well, then, are not these teachings directly opposed to each other? So which of the two is
he going to explain metaphorically?112
If Biblical sentences p and q are both equally clear but cannot both be true, nothing
can induce us to prefer p to q but the fact that p would have ‘more authority’ than q.
But since both are in Scripture the authority of p should be as great as the authority
of q. Accordingly, the only reason we could have for attributing more authority to p
than to q is by definition external to Scripture. Therefore the use of this method
entails the rejection of the authority of Scripture.113 So, again, the underlying issue
is the authority of Scripture in its relation to the interpretation of Scripture. What
Spinoza tries to show is that, even if we give up the authority of reason completely,
we cannot interpret Scripture. A consistent interpretation is impossible for either our
judgment constantly intervenes at the expense of Scripture’s authority or we end up
with conflicting ideas. The general result of Spinoza’s discussion is that the very fact
of attributing authority to Scripture turns its interpretation into an impossible task.
However, is not the main lesson of Spinoza’s Treatise that philosophy and
theology are separated? And does not Spinoza profess his belief in the possibility of
interpreting Scripture ‘by itself’ and in the authority of Scripture? Indeed, the title of
chap. 15 seems to be clear enough: ‘neither is theology ancillary to reason nor reason
to theology; the reason why we are convinced of the authority of Holy Scripture’.114
So we have to re-examine the question. The text of chap. 15 starts with some
confusion:
Those who cannot separate philosophy from theology disagree whether Scripture should
be subservient to reason or, by contrast, reason subservient to Scripture; that is, whether
the meaning [sensus] of Scripture should be adapted to [accommodari] reason or reason to
Scripture. The latter is claimed by the sceptics, who deny the certainty of reason, but the


former by the dogmatists. That both are completely mistaken is however certain from what
has already been said.115
The text makes sense only if we assume that all (‘dogmatists’ as well as ‘sceptics’)
believe in the authority of Scripture. So, according to both ‘dogmatists’ and
‘sceptics’, some things can be learned from Scripture. However, ‘sceptics’ attribute
all authority to Scripture (so nothing can be learned from reason) whereas
‘dogmatists’ believe in that of reason as well (so some things at least can be learned
from reason). So ‘dogmatists’ would be Cartesians; ‘sceptics’ would be Voetians.
Now it is Spinoza’s claim that neither dogmatists nor sceptics manage to separate
philosophy and theology. Why? Presumably because both believe that some things
at least can be known to be true simply by being in Scripture. Accordingly, Spinoza’s
point would be that the only way meaningfully to separate philosophy from theology
is to decide that Scripture, and as a result theology, has nothing to do with truth, or
that ‘the domain of reason is truth and wisdom; the domain of theology, piety and
obedience’.116 Philosophy and theology are radically separated because the one has
to do with truth and the other with motives for ‘moral’ behaviour. Indeed, true
knowledge is a sufficient but by no means a necessary condition for ‘moral’
behaviour. The role of theology therefore is none other than to provide beliefs that
can work as motives of ‘moral’ behaviour without bothering about their truth.
But that raises another problem. For how does that relate to Spinoza’s own
professed belief in the authority of Scripture? That subject is introduced in the
context of what Spinoza calls the principle of theology: ‘the power of reason, as we
have already demonstrated, does not extend so far as to enable us to conclude that
men can achieve blessedness simply through obedience without understanding,
whereas this alone is the message of theology, which commands only obedience and
neither seeks nor is able to oppose reason’.117 So theology does seem to be entitled
to some truth – indeed, to a truth that would be inaccessible to reason – and that
would mean that theology is entitled to some ‘authority’. This is a puzzling
statement. For, first of all, it is by no means clear where exactly Spinoza has shown
that ‘reason does not extend so far as to conclude that men can achieve blessedness
through obedience without understanding’.118 Second, his conclusion against
‘Maimonides’ was precisely that the notion of ‘some authority’ is contradictory:
that neither reason nor Scripture can be said to have any authority unless it is total.
And, third, there is nothing unintelligible in the fact that people become ‘blessed’
(beati) by obedience, as long as it is to a sovereign who manages to preserve peace.
This is even further complicated in a number of passages from, or connected with,
chap. 15:
Since reason cannot demonstrate the truth or falsity of this fundamental principle of
theology, that men are saved [salvantur] simply by obedience, we may also be asked why
it is that we believe it. If we accept this principle without reason, blindly, then we too are
acting foolishly without judgement.119 If on the other hand we assert that this fundamental
principle can be proved by reason, then theology becomes part of philosophy and
inseparable from it. I reply that I maintain absolutely that this fundamental dogma of
theology cannot be investigated by the natural light of reason, or at least that nobody has
been successful in proving it and that therefore it is essential that there should be
revelation.120
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
112


The Impossibility of Theology
113
So at the end of the day Spinoza seems to accept, albeit on one limited point, the
authority of Scripture. According to Spinoza, however, accepting the theological
principle does not entirely have to be without reasons: ‘we can use judgement before
we accept with moral certainty at least whatever has been revealed’.121 Just as in the
domain of action, we should be satisfied with moral certainty:
It would be folly to refuse to accept, merely on the grounds that it cannot be proved with
mathematical certainty that which is abundantly confirmed by the testimony of the
prophets, that which is a source of so much comfort to those that are less gifted with
intelligence, and of considerable advantage to the state, and which we can believe without
incurring any peril or hurt. Could we live our lives wisely if we were to accept as true
nothing that could conceivably be called into doubt on any principle of scepticism? Are
not most of our actions in any case fraught with uncertainty and hazard?122
But these pronouncements make things even worse. For, as we have seen in chap. 3,
nothing much is actually confirmed by the testimony of the prophets; indeed,
whatever ‘authority’ they have was seen to derive from other sources. Second, as we
have also seen in chap. 3, ‘moral certainty’ has nothing to do with probability.
Finally, the fact that this belief is a ‘great comfort to those less gifted with
intelligence’ and that ‘it is to a great advantage for the state’ may be true but is at
best a motive for finding that belief socially useful – not for finding it true.
The only solution seems to me to dissociate the viewpoint of the philosopher,
interested in eternal truths, from that of all others, who must be content with
belief and faith. That at any rate seems to be the point of a note belonging to
chap. 15:
[that simple obedience is a way to salvation]. That is, it is not reason but revelation that
can teach us that it is enough for blessedness or salvation for us to accept the divine
decrees as laws and that there is no need to conceive them as eternal truths. This is made
clear by what we have demonstrated in chap. 4.123
Now what is demonstrated in chap. 4 (‘Of a Divine Law’) is that we cannot at the
same time think of ‘God’s decrees’ as commands (which can be obeyed but not
understood) and as eternal truths (which are understood but cannot be obeyed).124 If
we know why we are ‘saved’ by a particular type of behaviour, no obedience is
necessary to make us act accordingly; if on the other hand we do not know why a
particular action is in our interest we can choose it only by obedience to a law, that
is, out of respect for a lawgiver. In any case, to the extent that we are guided by
reason we do not obey and, inversely, to the extent that we obey we do not know.
This means that, if the aim of theology is obedience, its sphere is by definition not
that of truth and understanding. Accordingly theology would be nothing but ‘faith’,
that is, a motive for moral behaviour, the rationale of which, however, is in itself not
understood by those who have to rely on theology and faith:
By theology I here exactly mean revelation in so far as it manifests Scripture’s objective
as we have stated it, that is, the way of achieving obedience or the dogmas of true piety
and faith. In other words, by theology I mean the Word of God properly so-called, which
does not consist in a set number of books. Theology thus understood, if you consider its
precepts and moral teaching, will be found to agree with reason; and if you look at its


purpose and end, it will be found to be in no respect opposed to reason and is therefore
valid for all men.125
Theology is revelation ‘in so far as it manifests Scripture’s objective’, that is, in so
far as it actually is ‘the word of God’ – that is, a moral lesson. ‘Theology’ is not
speaking (discoursing, reasoning) on God but ‘words spoken by God’: not a text or
a collection of texts sanctioned by tradition but moral inspiration. In sum, theology
is faith (moral motivation) and that means that its relation to truth and philosophy is
irrelevant: ‘Between faith, that is [sive], theology, and philosophy there is no relation
or affinity.’126 So Spinoza’s separation of philosophy and theology is of a completely
different nature from that of the Cartesians. It is not between two types of truths –
for if we would admit two types of truths we would end up with the same problems
as ‘Maimonides’ – but between truth and moral motivation.
Now would it make sense according to Spinoza to read and interpret Scripture?
Possibly, and in so far as it is part of faith (that is, in so far as it constitutes a motive
for moral behaviour), certainly. But that means that as a criterion of interpretation
we should replace ‘truth’ with ‘piety’. And for that to be possible we should first
establish that Scripture is indeed a ‘pious’ text, that is, that it can in fact incite people
to behave morally. It can, of course, for not only does it contain valid moral teaching
– that is, teaching which, if it is reduced to its hard core, proves to be compatible
with the sovereign law of any country – it also uses a ‘pious’ language, which can
be understood regardless of one’s particular prejudices or particular linguistic skills:
‘The teachings of true piety are expressed in quite ordinary language and being
directed to the generality of the people they are therefore straightforward and easy
to understand.’127 No Greek or Hebrew is necessary: ‘Whatsoever is necessary for
salvation […] can be readily grasped in any language because it is couched in
ordinary and familiar terms and it is this understanding, not the testimony of Biblical
commentators, that gains acceptance with the common people.’128 In fact the
ultimate judge of an interpretation is not its conformity with an external standard
(truth) but what Spinoza calls the Holy Spirit, that is, a feeling of moral satisfaction:
The testimony of the Holy Spirit is concerned only with good works – which therefore
Paul, too, in the Epistle to the Galatians ch. 5 v. 22 calls ‘the fruits of the Holy Ghost’.
Indeed, the Holy Spirit is nothing but the peace of mind resulting from good actions.129
Scripture is sacred or divine – it has ‘divinity’ – not in the sense that it contains the
truth and nothing but the truth but in the sense that it moves its readers to devotion
and piety: ‘A thing is called sacred or divine when its purpose is to foster piety and
religion.’130 Accordingly, Scripture can be sacred or divine only to a certain extent
and for some of its readers: ‘Nothing is sacred or profane or impure in an absolute
sense apart from a mind but only in relation to a mind.’131
Conclusion
The discussion of the interpretation of Scripture mainly shows the complexity of
Spinoza’s predicament, which is based on the idea that all forms of ‘authority’ not
explicitly political should either be subordinated to the sovereign or be reformulated
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
114


The Impossibility of Theology
115
in terms of truth and belief. Complications arise for three different reasons: the
authority of Scripture generates bastard forms of authority; the instrument normally
used in examining and criticizing  beliefs (reason) is not available to the faithful; and
the sovereign does not have the ‘right to teach’, so is not in a position to correct ideas
and beliefs. Since on the other hand Spinoza sees revealed belief as an important
source of moral motivation (provided it is corrected and rectified), he must find a
difficult means between a mindless acceptance of Scripture (which given the
difficulty of its interpretation would produce fanaticism and dogmatism) and its total
rejection. Part of the solution (‘moral certainty’) was already discussed in the last
chapter in connection mainly with chaps 1 and 2 of Spinoza’s Treatise. In chap. 7,
on the other hand, Spinoza’s concern is mainly with a form of authority derived from
Scripture, namely, that of theology. His solution builds on the conclusions reached
with respect to the ‘authority’ of Scripture in the sense that he tries to show that,
whenever we assume that Scripture contains the truth, that assumption interferes
with the possibility of interpreting it. Inversely, if we concentrate merely on an
interpretation of Scripture, that proves to be impossible for various reasons but
mainly because the history that should make interpretation possible is totally
lacking. Accordingly, theology in all its forms (as interpretation and as knowledge)
is radically impossible. Still, this should not mean that people must no longer read
Scripture. They must, basically, as long as it is useful for their moral motivation and
as long as they do it in private.

Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
120


Chapter 5
‘Government by God’
The political theories analysed in chap. 2 culminate with the defence of an autocratic
regime which subordinates everything to a sovereign, whose authority is not
restricted in any way by a higher ‘Divine’ law. Indeed, the only laws he is bound to
‘obey’, which he could not possibly disobey, are psychological laws (governing the
relations between human beings) and natural laws (having to do with the climate in
a certain region, the fertility of a certain area, and so on). But there are two types of
government that seem to be at odds with that scheme: democratic government and
theocratic government. Both are anomalous, for more or less the same reason. If all
are sovereign (as in a democracy) all are equally entitled to interpret the will of God,
so there would be no difference with the state of nature; if God is sovereign nobody
in particular interprets God’s will (indeed, God would be his own interpreter), so
there would be no difference with the state of nature either. Still, according to
Spinoza, it is possible for people to govern themselves without falling back into the
state of nature if they are governed by a law which they believe was given by God
and which does not need ‘interpretation’. Accordingly, that law must satisfy specific
criteria and so must the institutions created by it. Moreover it must be possible to
keep pure the religion that forms the basis for the people’s respect of, and obedience
to, the law. So in the end this form of ‘theocratic’ government is presumably no
more than a theoretical possibility, given the nature of the Christian religion, which
does not have the form of a law (its fundamental precept being ‘Love’), is universal
and seems to call for a lot of highly divergent ‘interpretation’. As in the case of the
interpretation of Scripture, I shall closely follow the text.
Theocratic Government
According to Calvinist theology, political authority is made necessary by man’s
corrupt and fallen nature.1 Rulers are instruments of God’s providence and must be
obeyed under all circumstances: ‘They have a mandate from God, have been
invested with divine authority and are wholly God’s representatives, in a manner,
acting as his vicegerents.’2 The source of this doctrine is chap. 13 of the Epistle to
the Romans. The office of the magistrate is sacred for ‘there is no power but of God’
(Rom. 13:1), rulers being ‘not a terror to good works but to the evil’ (Rom. 13:3).3
So ‘whosoever resisteth the power resisteth the ordinance of God’ (Rom. 13:2).
Magistrates carry out the judgement of God, which for that matter is also the reason
why they alone are allowed to kill human beings.4 According to Calvin, this applies
even to unjust magistrates: ‘We are not only subject to the authority of princes who
perform their office towards us uprightly and faithfully as they ought, but also to the
authority of all who, by whatever means, have got control of affairs, even though
121


they perform not a prince’s office.’5 Indeed, a wicked ruler reflects a judgment of
God as much as earthquakes, drought and pestilence do.
Calvin is of course particularly disturbed by Anabaptists and similar sects or
generally, as they were called, ‘enthusiasts’ – ‘fanatics who delight in unbridled
license’ – who in the name of the Christian faith reject all and every authority,
alleging that ‘it is a thing unworthy of us and set far beneath our excellence to be
occupied with those vile and worldly cares which have to do with business foreign
to a Christian man’.6 Quoting a spate of Biblical texts, Calvin points out that on the
contrary the Church needs protection and that nothing makes the magistrate super-
fluous.7 Inversely magistrates should ‘remember that they are vicars of God’ and
‘watch with all care, earnestness and diligence, to represent in themselves to men
some image of divine providence, protection, goodness, benevolence and justice’.8
In the Netherlands this was worked out in a doctrine of parallelism, according to
which a preacher can be as little in charge of government as a magistrate can be a
minister of the Church: a minister provides help and guidance (ministerium) whereas
the magistrate governs by law and decree (dominium). Although the magistrate
should protect the visible Church and fight superstition (Roman Catholicism),
heresy (Remonstrantism, Socinianism) and impiety (atheism) he should leave the
government of the Church, and a fortiori matters of doctrine and discipline, to
ministers of the Church.9 However, even if the Church and the state are parallel
structures, each with their own responsibility, the Church has the right, if not the
duty, to remind the magistrate of the rule of God, especially if the magistrate is a
member of the Church. And that is of course what frequently happened. Orthodox
ministers publicly criticized the magistrate over what they saw as unholy practices,
such as the worldly use of ecclesiastical benefits, the creation of public pawnshops,
and so on. So the ideal was definitely theocratic in the sense, not that ministers
should participate in government, but that the magistrate should obey the will of
God. Indeed, he was expected to avoid scandal, to observe God’s commandments
and to reform public life in a truly Christian way – all to the satisfaction,
presumably, of the ecclesiastical establishment.10
Hobbes on Theocratic Government
According to Hobbes, the principles of a Christian commonwealth should be taken
from Scripture, that is, ‘the prophetic word of God’, as the principles of an ordinary
commonwealth are taken from reason, that is, ‘the natural word of God’.11 Both
being ‘the word of God’, there can be no opposition between the two, even if the
mysteries of faith are above reason:
We are not to renounce our senses and experience; nor that which is the undoubted word
of God, our natural reason. For they are the talents which he hath put into our hands to
negotiate, till the coming again of our blessed Saviour; and therefore not to be folded up
in the napkin of an implicit faith, but employed in the purchase of justice, peace and true
