religion. For though there are many things in God’s word above reason; that is to say,
which cannot by natural reason be either demonstrated or confuted; yet there is nothing
contrary to it; but when it seemeth so, the fault is either in our unskillful interpretation or
erroneous ratiocination.12
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
122


If eventually the results of our interpretation are contrary to reason, the fault is
entirely and necessarily ours. So whatever may be the outcome of an examination of
Scripture the points established on the basis of reason should remain perfectly
valid.13 And that means that, even if ‘theocratic government’ turns out to be
recommended by Scripture, it must, in so far as it is government, fit the framework
established by reason.
According to Hobbes we may speak of ‘laws of God’ as specifically different
from positive laws: ‘subjects owe simple obedience in all things wherein their
obedience is not repugnant to the laws of God’.14 But that does not automatically
mean that God is ‘king’ in the proper sense of the word. The true meaning of the
word of the Psalmist, according to which ‘God is King’, is that ‘by denying the
Existence or Providence of God, men may shake off their Ease, but not their Yoke’.15
Even an atheist, someone who denies the will and providence of God, remains
subject to God’s laws, that is, to the laws of nature. But to call ‘this Power of God,
which extendeth itself not onely to man but also to Beasts and Plants and Bodies
inanimate, by the name of Kingdome, is but a metaphoricall use of the word’.16 To
govern in the proper sense of the word is to govern in virtue of lawful authority:
For he onely is properly said to Raigne, that governs his Subjects, by his Word, and by
promise of Rewards to those that obey it, and by threatening them with Punishment that
obey it not. Subjects therefore in the Kingdome of God, are not Bodies Inanimate, nor
creatures Irrationall; because they understand no precepts as his: Nor Atheists; nor they
that believe not that God has any care of the actions of mankind; because they
acknowledge no Word for his, nor have hope of his rewards, or fear of his threatenings.
They therefore that believe there is a God that governeth the world, and hath given
Praecepts, and propounded Rewards and Punishments to mankind, are Gods Subjects; all
the rest, are to be understood as Enemies.17
According to Hobbes, there are three different ways in which God’s law can be
known: natural reason, revelation and prophecy. Since revelation, which for Hobbes
means immediate revelation, can oblige nobody but its immediate recipient, there
are, as far as political obligation is concerned, no more than two relevant ways in
which God’s word can be known: reason and prophecy.18 As a result, there is also
room for no more than two types of Divine Kingdom: A ‘Kingdome of God by
Nature’, which is government of God as it is acknowledged by those who know and
obey the dictates of reason, and a prophetic Kingdom of God, ‘wherein having
chosen out one peculiar nation (the Jewes) for his Subjects, he governed them, and
none but them, not onely by naturall Reason, but by Positive Lawes, which he gave
them by the mouths of his holy Prophets’.19 So, whereas all other nations are ruled
by God only in the sense that their constitution agrees with ‘God’s natural word’ (the
laws of reason) the Jews were literally governed by ‘God’s revealed word’.
In Pt III of Leviathan, Hobbes’s concern is more specifically with a Christian
commonwealth, that is, with a theocratic government based on the word of God as
revealed in the New Testament.20 This creates a complex agenda, which involves
questions concerning the authority and the interpretation of Scripture, the difference
between the Old and the New Testament, the nature of Christian teaching, and so on
– all of which would also prove to be of great concern to the author of the
Theologico-political treatise. In the end Hobbes aims to show (a) that the Old
‘Government by God’
123


Testament does not make it necessary to put ecclesiastical power and civil power
into different hands; (b) that the God of the New Testament is not a sovereign and
therefore that the idea that there could be a specifically Christian theocracy is
contradictory; (c) that Christ’s Kingship will not be realized before the fullness of
time so that no temporal or spiritual authority can be derived either from him or from
his Apostles. So not only would it be impossible to create a Christian theocracy; the
solution favoured by Scripture for the relation between the civil and ecclesiastical
power is to put everything into the hands of the worldly authorities.
In any case, according to Hobbes, the expression ‘kingdom of God’ as used in
Scripture means ‘a Kingdome properly so named, constituted by the votes of the
people of Israel in a peculiar manner, wherein they chose God for their King by
Covenant made with him, upon Gods promising them the possession of the land of
Canaan; and but seldom metaphorically, and then it is taken for Dominion over
Sinne (and onely in the New Testament)’.21 But sovereignty presupposes a covenant.
Accordingly, if there is a kingdom of God there must also be a covenant with
God.
The question is whether that is possible. It is, apparently. God spoke to Abraham,
as he had done to Adam, and ‘though the name of King be not yet given to God’, it
is legitimate to speak of Abraham’s descendants as forming a kingdom of God, for
by his covenant ‘Abraham obligeth himself and his posterity in a peculiar manner to
be subject to God’s positive Law.’22 This covenant was renewed by Moses, as is
shown in Exodus: ‘If you will obey my voice indeed and keep my Covenant, then
yee shall be a peculiar people to me, for all the Earth is mine; and yee shall be unto
me a Sacerdotall Kingdome, and an holy Nation’ (Ex. 19:5).23 According to Hobbes
that means that the Jews as a people were God’s peculium, that is, his own.24 By
adding that ‘all the earth is mine’ God would declare that whereas the other nations
‘are all mine by reason of my Power, […] you shall be mine by your own consent
and covenant’.25 In other words, as far as the Jews are concerned God is their
sovereign by institution whereas he is king of the other nations in the sense in which
he is king of the earth. The other key expression is ‘Sacerdotal Kingdom’ or
‘Regnum Sacerdotale’. What is meant, according to Hobbes, is not a kingdom of
priests but a royal priesthood (meaning that the highest worldly authority is by
definition also the highest ecclesiastical authority) as the paraphrase in Peter’s First
Epistle shows: ‘Ye are a chosen generation, a royal priesthood, a holy nation, a
peculiar people’ (1 Peter 2:9).26 This would show that ‘the Civill and Ecclesiasticall
Power were both joined together in one and the same Person, the High Priest; and
ought to be so, in whosoever governeth by Divine Right; that is, by Authority
immediate from God’.27 And that was also the case when Israel was ruled by kings.
For, ‘whereas before all Authority, both in Religion and Policy, was in the High
Priest, so now it was in the King’.28 So if there is a king the high priest is by
definition his minister. In any case, ‘the Kingdome of God is a civill Kingdome;
which consisted, first in the obligation of the people of Israel to those Laws which
Moses should bring onto them from Mount Sinaï; and which afterwards the High
Priest for the time being should deliver to them from before the Cherubins in the
Sanctum Sanctorum; and which Kingdome having been cast off in the election of
Saul, the Prophets foretold should be restored by Christ; and the restauration
whereoff we daily pray for when we say in the Lord’s Prayer “Thy Kingdome
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
124


come”.’29 So the properly theocratic government that was abolished by Saul will be
restored with the second coming.
Could there be after all a Christian theocracy? The answer is ‘No’. Christ came as
a redeemer; he worked and continues to work as a pastor and teacher; and it is only
‘after his coming again [that] shall begin his glorious Reign over his elect, which is
to last eternally’.30 The Church incorporates only the pastoral office of Christ.31
Since therefore whatever authority the Church lawfully has cannot derive from
Christ, it must come from the sovereign. And that is also the teaching of Christ
himself, nothing of which ‘tendeth to the diminution of the Civill Right of the Jewes,
or of Caesar’.32 Accordingly, a Church properly speaking, which would be ‘capable
to command, judge, absolve, condemn or do any other act’, is nothing but ‘a
company of men professing Christian religion, united in the person of one
Soveraign’.33
On the whole the position of a Christian king is not essentially different from that
of other kings, especially as regards the right to teach: ‘This Right of the Heathen
Kings [the right to teach] cannot bee thought taken from them by their conversion to
the Faith of Christ, who never ordained that Kings for beleeving in him should be
deposed, that is, subjected to any but himself or (which is all one) be deprived of the
power necessary for the conservation of Peace amongst their Subjects and for their
defence against foraign Enemies.’34 A Christian sovereign is the supreme pastor of
his people: ‘Seeing that in every Christian Commonwealth the Civill Soveraign is
the Supreme Pastor to whose charge the whole flock of his Subjects is committed
and consequently […] it is from the Civill Soveraign that all other pastors derive
their right of Teaching, Preaching and other functions pertaining to that Office and
that they are but his Ministers.’35 In the end, therefore, the notion of a Christian
theocracy in the proper sense of the word is vacuous. A ‘Christian theocracy’ is
hardly distinguishable from a kingdom of God by nature, except that all its citizens
are Christians.
Spinoza on Theocratic Government
What Spinoza and Hobbes have in common is the idea that the only historical
theocracy was the first state of Israel; that a Christian theocracy is impossible; and
that any ordinary commonwealth is by definition ‘a kingdom of God’. But their
analysis is in some respects different and so are the conclusions they draw from it.
Finally, as compared with Hobbes, Spinoza’s presentation is often confusing and
perhaps even confused, possibly because, unlike Hobbes, he cannot counter the
theocratic argument with a firm and unequivocal defence of absolutism, but
undoubtedly also because he faces a different political situation.36 So much is clear:
his reflections on the Law of Moses must have started long before he was
excommunicated. In any case, he gives two somewhat conflicting accounts of the
government of the Hebrews (one in chap. 5 and the other in chaps 17 and 18), whilst
on the other hand his political conclusions lack consistency and do not really match
his analysis.
According to Spinoza, after the flight from Egypt the Jews ‘regained their natural
right over everything that lay within their power and every man could decide afresh
‘Government by God’
125


whether to retain it or surrender it and transfer it to another’.37 In other words, they
returned to a state of nature and were free, individually as well as collectively, to do
as they pleased. But they ‘hearkened to Moses […] and resolved to transfer their
right not to any mortal man but to God alone’.38 So on the advice of Moses they
proceeded to submit themselves to God as to a sovereign, that is, without appointing
a single earthly ‘interpreter of his will’: one must presume, indeed, that the right ‘to
interpret the will of God’ was kept by all together.
The role of Moses, on the other hand, was not that of a sovereign but of a spiritual
leader, who preaches the will of God without having the authority to impose it as a
law, let alone to punish those who disobey God’s will. Finally, the wish to be
governed by God was expressed in regular fashion, ‘by express covenant and oath
[…] freely, not by forcible coercion or fear of threats’.39 The result was a theocracy
in the proper sense of the word, that is, a commonwealth ruled and governed by God
on the basis of a pact concluded in freedom – so God was sovereign ‘by institution’.
According to Spinoza, however, God’s sovereignty ‘was a matter of opinion rather
than fact for in reality the Hebrews retained their sovereign right completely’.40
They ‘did not transfer their right to any other man but as in a democracy surrendered
their right on equal terms, crying with one voice: “Whatever God shall speak we
shall do it”, without making any explicit mention of a mediator’.41 With a mediator
there would have been a monarchy; without there is a democracy despite the fact that
God is king. So the theocratic nature of the government would be no more than a
subjective notion, as subjective indeed as that of a God–Lawgiver, dependent as it is
on the subject’s lack of knowledge.42 In any case, given the fact that this type of
theocracy would be nothing but popular democracy, there is no fundamental
incompatibility between democratic and theocratic government; on the contrary, it
looks as if they are basically identical.
Spinoza’s reconstruction of this episode has mythical traits; indeed, what little
Biblical evidence there is on this episode hardly substantiates it. Thus, according to
Exodus, Moses was actually supreme judge: ‘And it came to pass on the morrow that
Moses sat to judge the people’ (Ex. 18:13). It is from Moses that the people learn the
law of God: ‘I judge between one and another and I do make them know the statutes
of God and his laws’ (Ex. 18:16). So Moses did what is normally done by a
sovereign: He ‘interpreted’ the will of God, giving it the form of a positive law in
the name of which justice is done. Moreover, all contacts with God were mediated
by Moses: the offer of a covenant was made to Moses (Ex. 19:5) and it was Moses
who communicated the answer of the people to God (Ex. 19:8–9). Accordingly,
there may be some doubt as to whether Moses was really no more than a spiritual
leader, especially because, in the alternative version of chap. 5, Spinoza skips the
‘democratic’ phase entirely: ‘Moses easily succeeded in keeping sovereignty
because he surpassed all others in divine power, which he convinced the people he
possessed, providing many proofs thereof.’43 So, according to this version, Moses
would be sovereign from the beginning.44 But according to chap. 17, he became
sovereign at a later stage only, after the democratic experiment failed. Accordingly,
Spinoza’s additional point in chap. 17 presumably concerns democratic government
and then it is no doubt significant that the episode of popular democracy was of very
short duration.
For when the Jews appeared before God to hear his will they became terrified –
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
126


‘they removed and stood afar off’ (Ex. 20:18) – and thought that their last hour had
struck.45 So when it came to actually translating their obedience into a positive law,
when it came to actually taking down in the form of a law the will of God, they
proved as unable ‘to govern themselves’ as they always had been. Accordingly, they
decided to transfer their ‘right to interpret the will of God’ – that is, their rights of
sovereignty – to Moses, saying to him: ‘Speak thou with us and we will hear: but let
not God speak with us, lest we should die’ (Ex. 20:19).46
According to Spinoza, that makes an essential difference: ‘What they promised
was not, as before, to obey all that God should speak to them but what God should
speak to Moses.’47 Moses became their ‘interpreter of the will of God’, that is, their
sovereign king: ‘Moses was left as the sole lawgiver and interpreter of God’s laws
and thus also the supreme judge, whom no one could judge and who alone acted on
God’s behalf among the Hebrews, that is, he held the supreme kingship, having
alone the right to consult God, to give God’s answers to the people and to compel
them to obey.’48 Although he did not cease to be a prophet, he from now on also had
the authority to interpret ‘the will of God’ as a sovereign, that is, ‘to make and repeal
laws, to make decisions on war and peace, to send envoys, to appoint judges, to
choose a successor’.49 So the fact that the monarch is a prophet is not enough for us
to be able to speak of a theocracy – indeed, Moses was an absolute monarch even if
his being a prophet did cause an important difference:
Ordinarily a monarchy is ruled in accordance with a decree of God which is hidden even
from the monarch, but the Hebrew state would be, or should have been, ruled in a definite
way by God’s decree as it was revealed to the monarch alone. This difference does not
diminish the monarch’s dominion and right over all his subjects; on the contrary, it
increases it. As for the people, in both cases they are equally subject and ignorant of God’s
decree for in both cases they are dependent on what the monarch says, understanding from
him alone what is right and what is wrong.50
An ordinary monarch rules by his own arbitrary will (the true causes of which escape
him as well as others). Moses’ rule, however, was believed to be God’s revealed
will. And that gave him more rather than less authority. Nor was Moses’ authority
diminished by his being appointed by the people. For these ‘were bound to accept as
chosen by God whichever successor Moses would choose’.51 Otherwise, the people
would be sovereign, which in Spinoza’s view they clearly were not.
After Moses’ death things changed again and another period of theocratic rule
started: ‘The right to interpret the laws and to promulgate God’s answers was vested
in one man and the right to govern the state in accordance with the laws thus
expounded and answers thus made known in another.’52 The result was ‘neither a
democracy, nor an aristocracy, nor a monarchy but a theocracy’.53 Why? Spinoza
mentions the following specific reasons. First, a temple – ‘a dwelling to serve as the
palace of God, the state’s supreme sovereign’54 – would be built and maintained at
the expense of the people. Second, Aaron was ‘supreme interpreter of God’s laws,
giving the people the answers of the divine oracle and entreating God on the
people’s behalf’.55 Third, the land was divided among the tribes (except Levi’s).
Fourth, Joshua obtained the ‘right to consult God’, although not like Moses alone in
his tent or the tabernacle, but through the high priest. Finally, ‘all men between the
ages of twenty and sixty were ordered to bear arms and to form armies recruited only
‘Government by God’
127


from the people, which swore allegiance not to their commander-in-chief nor to the
high priest but to their religion and their God’.56
Now some of these features are in an obvious sense theocratic, like the central
place of the temple (as the symbolic palace of the God–Lawgiver), the role of the
priests (as interpreters of the law) and the oath of the soldiers (to show their
allegiance to God rather than to a mortal man). What links them is the fact that all
were subject to a law known as ‘the will of God’, the written version of which was
kept in the tabernacle as the visible representation of the God–Sovereign. And since
nobody had the right to change that law, there was no earthly lawgiver. But if there
is no earthly lawgiver – if there is nobody to interpret the will of God – there is
government immediately by God, that is, by means of an impersonal law (‘the will
of God’) which does not require any further ‘interpretation’. Finally, that there was
a law at all is the difference from the first, democratic, phase, which collapsed
because the people were unable to give themselves a law. So what Spinoza actually
describes is a form of constitutional government: there is a fundamental law, which
forms the basis of all particular ‘decrees’, which are seen as ‘interpretations’ of that
law. It is ‘theocratic’ only to the extent that the law is ‘revealed’ and that obedience
is based on ‘faith’ and ‘devotion to God’.
Another reason why Spinoza speaks of ‘theocratic’ government is that the state
was characterized by a delicate system of checks and balances which prevented any
person or group (captain, princes, priests, people) to usurp the law. According to
Spinoza, such a division of power is indicated in Num. 27:21: ‘And [Joshua] shall
stand before Eleazar the priest, who shall counsel for him after the judgement of the
Lord: At his word they shall go out and at his word they shall come in, both he and
all the children of Israel with him, even all the congregation.’57 Now, again, that is
rather weak evidence; indeed, Eleazar seems to be described as a governor (albeit in
the name of God) and Joshua as his minister and that is also the way Hobbes
reads this passage.58 Spinoza, by contrast, emphasizes Joshua’s comparative
independence. Thus he takes exception to current translations of Num. 27:19–23,
which suggest that Joshua was a subordinate of Moses and of the high priest,
whereas the true meaning would be, according to him, that Joshua was established
as a ‘prince’ (princeps).59 Spinoza also gives Joshua ‘the right to consult God’, that
is, to take political decisions. Moreover, he could claim obedience (he had political
authority) and was entitled to declare war. But unlike ordinary sovereigns he could
exercise all those rights only in conjunction with others – the high priest and the
leaders (‘princes’) of the tribes – and, presumably, under the law. Moreover,
Joshua’s office was neither hereditary nor permanent. After his death there was a
commander-in-chief only when it was necessary and then he was ‘chosen
immediately by God’, which I take to mean that the commander took office without
the intervention of a positive law, on the basis of personal charisma and ‘natural
selection’. So if Joshua was not a minister, he was not a sovereign either.
That still does not mean that the high priest was sovereign or enjoyed sovereign
authority. For, although his office was ‘to consult God’ and ‘interpret his will’, he
was allowed to do so ‘only when requested by the commander-in-chief or the
supreme council or similar authorities’.60 Moreover, ‘God’s words as given by the
priest were not decrees, as when given by Moses, but only answers; only when
accepted by Joshua and the councils did they have force of commands and
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
128


decrees.’61 Finally, the high priest had no armed forces, no land, no civil rights at all.
So, whatever he was, he was not a sovereign.
The priests as a collective body, on the other hand, were completely dependent on
the other tribes.62 Their position was that of legal advisers, civil servants or judges,
but that gave them much power, partly because they had no other rights: ‘The
princes found their scope for transgression severely curtailed by the fact that the
entire right to interpret the laws was assigned to the Levites (Deut. 21:5) who had no
share either in the administration of the state or in its territory and who saw their
entire welfare and prestige dependent on a true interpretation of the Law.’63 But
whereas the priests succeeded each other by hereditary right, it is by no means clear
how worldly leaders were chosen:
I cannot find anything definite in Scripture […] I conjecture that, since each tribe was
divided into families whose heads were chosen from their more senior members, the senior
of these succeeded by right to the office of prince […]. But for our purpose it matters very
little if this point remains undecided; it is enough to have shown that after Moses’ death
no one exercised all the functions of a sovereign.64
One became a leader not as a result of a formal procedure: ‘The prince had no
superiority over others by nobility of descent or right of birth; the government of the
state was in his hands only by reason of his age and qualities.’65 Accordingly, for a
government to be ‘theocratic’ its precise form seems to be unimportant as long as
there is division of power and authority:
The management of the affairs of state was not entirely in the hands of one man, one
council, or the people; some affairs were managed by one tribe and others by the rest, with
equal right in both cases. Thus it clearly follows that after Moses’ death the state was left
neither a monarchy nor an aristocracy nor a democracy but as we have said a theocracy.66
At the end of the day, however, the party really in control was the people, if only
because their knowledge of the law enabled them to prevent the worldly leaders as
well as the priests from usurping the law. Accordingly, they were, as Spinoza claims,
the true guardians of the law:
The entire people was required to assemble at an appointed place every seventh year to
learn the laws from the priest and in addition everyone was expected to read and re-read
the book of the Law on his own, constantly and with the utmost concentration. See Deut.
31:9, etc., and 6:7.67 Thus, if only in their own interests, the princes had to take great care
to govern entirely in accordance with laws laid down and familiar to all, if they wished to
enjoy the highest esteem of the people, who would then revere them as ministers of God’s
kingdom and as God’s vice-regents. If they acted otherwise they must have inevitably
encountered the bitterest hatred – such as religious hatred is wont to be – on behalf of their
subjects.68
So this theocracy, too, like the first, has strong ‘democratic’ features. Indeed, when
referring to this episode later, Spinoza describes it as one in which ‘the people held
the reins of government’.69 And the informal leadership which characterizes this
period is also in line with what Spinoza in at least one passage of the Political
treatise claims to be characteristic of democratic government: ‘The chief difference
‘Government by God’
129


between this [aristocracy] and a democracy is that in an aristocracy the right of
governing depends on election only whereas in a democracy for the most part it
depends either on some innate right or on a right acquired by fortune’ (a jure
quodam innato vel fortuna adepto).70 So what Spinoza actually describes as
‘theocratic government’ is a second experiment in democracy, which unlike its
predecessor succeeds, temporarily at any rate, because there is a written law; and
because there is a real division of power and authority, which makes it impossible to
usurp the law. On the other hand, the people’s motive for respecting the law is
religious and that also makes them eminently fit to act as ‘guardians of the law’
without giving them the chance to usurp the law. So the very fact that the motive of
obedience is religious ultimately protects the commonwealth against mob-rule – if it
is the mob that rules, it is in the name of a law known and revered by all.
Apart from these factors, Spinoza’s mentions, albeit less explicitly, a third reason
for which the government of Israel should be called a ‘theocracy’. That is, its being
a confederation of independent republics (the ‘tribes’), which are united by a
common law. This aspect is of no concern to Hobbes and is also absent from
Spinoza’s alternative account in chap. 5; indeed, it is hard to see why it should be
specifically ‘theocratic’. But it may belong to a different agenda:
When all the tribes had divided among themselves the territories […] they must have been
regarded as confederated states rather than as fellow-citizens. With respect to God and
religion they must indeed have been regarded as fellow-citizens but in respect of the rights
of one tribe as against another they were only members of a confederation, in much the
same position (disregarding the common temple) as the High Confederated States of the
Netherlands.71 For the division of the shares of property held in common simply implies
that each member now owns his share alone, the others having surrendered their right to
that particular share.72
So the situation resulting from the division of the land can in all but one respect (‘the
common temple’) be compared with that of the United Provinces. That suggests that
Dutch recent history illuminates the history of Israel. So, like the Seven Provinces,
the tribes of Israel would be nominally independent republics, each with their own
territory and their own prince; together they would form a confederation; and they
would need an army and a captain-in-chief only in times of national crisis. The only
difference is, apparently, that the United Provinces lack ‘a common temple’, that is,
presumably, a common religion and/or a common law.73 So Spinoza’s real point
might be that the Dutch (the inhabitants of all provinces together) are not fellow-
citizens and that the provinces should be regarded as ‘confederated states’. In any
case the Seven Provinces would be a ‘Second Israel’ only in so far as they are a
confederation, not in so far as there would be a ‘God of the Dutch’. Still, something
can be learned from their comparison, apparently:
Now when it was learnt that it fell to the tribe of Judah to be the first to attack its enemy
this tribe alone made an agreement with the tribe of Simeon to join forces in attacking their
common enemies. The other tribes were not included in this agreement (Judges, 1:1–3).
[…] But the tribe of Benjamin, which had wronged the other tribes and had so violated the
bond of peace that none of the confederates could lodge safely among them, was attacked
as an enemy.74
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
130


A confederation does not exclude other alliances: as sovereign states the
confederates are free to do whatever they believe to be in their interest as long as
they do not damage the interests of the others.75 Moreover, the confederation is a
‘bond of peace’ and should be maintained only in the interest of peace: the law
governing the relations between states as well as persons is that ‘nobody makes a
contract or is bound to abide by an agreement except through hope of some good or
apprehension of some evil’.76 Finally, if one of the confederates undermines the
peace, the others have the right to correct and if need be to attack her.77
Again, if we re-situate this in the Dutch context and read the history of Israel as a
metaphor of Dutch history, it suggests that, as long as Holland does not actually
damage the interests of the others, she should be free to do whatever she wishes to
do, such as having her own ecclesiastical settlement (which was contested by the
Church and by the other provinces) or concluding treaties with foreign nations.78
Moreover, the Union should be maintained only as long as it is helpful in achieving
peace; if it undermines it – for example, because it allows people from, say, Utrecht
to meddle with the affairs of Holland – it might be better to break it. Finally, the
territorial consolidation of the Union (confirmed by the Peace of Westphalia in
1648) makes a Stadholder (one of whose offices was that of captain of the Union’s
army) superfluous: ‘When all the tribes had divided among themselves those
territories […] there ceased to be any reason for a common commander; for as a
result of the allocation the different tribes must have been regarded as confederated
states rather than as fellow-citizens.’79 So, presumably, the meaning of Spinoza’s
analysis of theocratic government would be a veiled defence of the sovereignty of
Holland, a subject of great interest to Spinoza’s friends, as we have seen.80
That being said, it is far from clear why Spinoza should interpret the ‘tribal’
(confederate) structure of Israel as specifically ‘theocratic’. The reason may be that
a confederation is based on freedom and on respect for a law which, since it concerns
different states, cannot impose an obligation on any of the participants, and that to
the extent that it creates the conditions for peace and prosperity it ‘obeys the will of
God’. Indeed, from Spinoza’s point of view, there is no obvious reason to apply to
the relations between states a different analysis than to those between human beings,
both being individuals in the sense of the Ethics.81 That would mean that three types
of theocratic government are conceivable, although not all of them actually possible:
democratic in the absolute sense (as popular government), democratic in a more
relaxed sense (as republican government) and ‘democratic’ in the very loose sense
(as a confederation between sovereign states). But to determine the precise reason
why a confederation should be called a ‘theocracy’ we must examine the way in
which theocratic government came to an end.
The Decline of Theocratic Government
According to Spinoza, the intrinsic possibility of theocratic government – of ‘a
covenant with God’ – is not limited to the Jews. They were a ‘chosen’ people only
in the sense that their social organization and their ‘good fortune’ allowed them ‘to
achieve supremacy and retain it for so many years’.82 Their ‘election’ must be
understood in terms of a special relationship, not to a mythical God–Lawgiver, but
‘Government by God’
131


to God–Nature.83 The claim that God would have chosen them for himself (Deut.
10:15) is only a manner of speaking, adapted ‘to the understanding of those who […]
did not know true blessedness’.84 Even if Scripture tells us nothing certain about the
relation of God to other nations, it is clear that ‘they had their own state and special
laws by God’s external guidance’.85 In any case, neither the success nor the decline
of Israel can be ascribed to ‘racial’ factors: ‘Nature creates individuals, not nations,
and it is only difference of language, laws, established customs that divides
individuals into nations.’86 As a result we should probably see the notion of the
election of the Jews, and therefore of a divine ‘covenant,’ as an ideological ploy,
devised by Moses to provide the people with a strong motive for obedience.
Inversely, one cannot explain the ultimate decline of theocratic government as a
specific ‘punishment of God’; it must be explained as a result, either of a free
decision, or of a flaw in the constitution. Whereas Hobbes clearly decides for the
first, Spinoza is inclined to think that it is the second, even if he does not entirely
rule out the first.
According to Hobbes, theocratic government came to an end when the Jews
demanded an ordinary king: ‘By this they signified that they would no more bee
governed by the commands that should bee laid upon them by the Priest, in the name
of God; but by one that should command them in the same manner that all other
nations were commanded; and consequently in deposing the High Priest of Royall
authority, they deposed that peculiar Government of God.’87 The covenant was
dissolved and the nation reverted to ordinary kingship: ‘They cast off Gods yoke,
and called for a King, after the manner of their neighbour nations.’88 But because the
Law of Moses was never abolished the power of the kings never was very strong:
When they demanded a King, after the manner of the nations; yet it was not with a design
to depart from the worship of God their King […]. So that they alwaies kept in store a
pretext, either of Justice, or Religion, to discharge themselves of their obedience,
whensoever they had hope to prevaile […] And generally through the whole History of the
Kings, as well of Judah as of Israel, there were Prophets that alwaies controlled the Kings,
for transgressing the Religion; and sometimes also for Errours of State.89
The king was not a true sovereign, his authority being limited by a law which he
could not change and by priests and prophets whom he could not depose. The result
was that the state declined and became an easy target for foreign invaders.
Spinoza’s reading seems to agree with Hobbes’s; indeed, the wording and
sequence of the relevant passages in the Theologico-political treatise closely
correspond to those in Leviathan. However, according to Spinoza, the ultimate cause
of the destruction of the theocratic state was the organization of religion. The Law
of Moses had entrusted all ecclesiastical power to one particular tribe, that of Levi,
because they had not participated in the worship of the Calf (Deut. 10:8). According
to Spinoza, this humiliated the other tribes and made them see the law ‘not as it
should be, namely, as a safeguard of the people but as penalties and punishments’.90
So this is what happened:
The people were keen to watch over the Levites, who were no doubt just human, and, as
often happens, to accuse them of all the misdeeds of one. Hence there were continual
murmurings, culminating in a sense of resentment […]. Little wonder, then, that in times
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
132


of peace, when there were no more striking miracles and no men of unquestionable
authority appeared on the scene, the people’s morale began to fail through discontent and
greed and eventually they looked for change, forsaking a worship which, although worship
of God, nevertheless involved their humiliation and was also the object of suspicion. Little
wonder, too, that their princes, who are always seeking a way to obtain all authority for
themselves, made every concession to the people and introduced new forms of worship,
with a view to securing the people’s favour and alienating them from the High Priest.91
Although it is not absolutely clear what period Spinoza is referring to – the word
princeps suggests that it is that of the ‘Judges’ – the way he further presents things
shows that, like Hobbes, he makes theocratic government definitively cease with the
advent of the kings: ‘They [the Hebrews] came to open rupture with divine rule and
sought a mortal king, making the seat of government a court rather than a temple.’92
So, definitely, the theocratic period ended with the advent of the kings. The ultimate
cause, however, would be the position of the Levites, which provided the worldly
leaders (the judges, the kings) with an opportunity to weaken religious sentiment.
This would never have happened in what Spinoza claims was the original
settlement, namely, that the firstborn of each tribe be priests. Again the evidence is
weak, if not simply failing. Spinoza refers to this passage in Numbers: ‘For all the
firstborn of the children of Israel are mine, both man and beast: on the day that I
smote every firstborn in the land of Egypt I sanctified them for myself. And I have
taken the Levites for all the firstborn of the children of Israel’ (Num. 8:17–18).93 But
the fact that in this passage reference is made not only to humans but also to beasts
means that the priesthood of the Levites replaced human and animal sacrifices.94
Moreover, the alleged privileged position of the Levites is not compatible with
Spinoza’s earlier claim that their position was one of complete subordination and
lack of civil rights.95 In any case the end of theocratic government would be the
result of a flaw in the constitution, which gave the Levites that position: ‘God was
angry with them, not only from the foundation of their city, but right from the time
that their laws were ordained.’96
However, there is an even more fundamental problem. For why should the advent
of the kings make an end to theocratic government? According to Hobbes, the period
immediately preceding the kings was theocratic because the country was ruled by
priests in the name of God – so the fact that from now on there was a king means
that there is no longer a theocracy. For Spinoza, however, the properly theocratic
nature of the period preceding that of the kings was characterized by three factors:
the law (which was not abolished); the checks and balances imposed by the law
(which nominally at least were still in place); and the fact that the tribes formed a
confederation. So the only relevant change is that by acknowledging the central
authority of a king the Jews gave up their confederation.
Perhaps we should, again, interpret these passages against the background of
Dutch history. Although one could disagree over whether the Dutch Republic was a
federal state or a confederation, there is no disagreement that the Union was in fact
dominated by Holland.97 Of course the other provinces, supported by the Church and
by the Stadholder, resented this.98 In their view, what Holland wanted was a societas
leonina: a lion’s association, the lion being of course the ‘Lion of Holland’.99 Now
if, as I assume, the only reason why Spinoza emphasizes the confederate structure of
Israel is to make a point about Dutch politics, his analysis of Israel’s decline could
‘Government by God’
133


be read as a complex warning. First is a warning to Holland: her superior wealth and
power (comparable to that of the Levites) could easily lead to a disintegration of the
Union, which in turn could lead to the establishment of central rule (which was the
last thing Holland wanted). But Spinoza’s analysis could also be read as a warning
to the Orangists that, the Church being what she is, no Stadholder would ever enjoy
the authority of an absolute monarch. And, finally, it could be read as a warning
to the Church that allying herself with a specific party undermines the position
of faith – that her stance could lead to the ‘substitution of a court to the temple’. Let
us see whether the lessons explicitly drawn by Spinoza make these points any
clearer.
Political Lessons
Although, for reasons we have yet to discuss, Spinoza is as convinced as Hobbes that
the theocratic period in Jewish history was a unique experiment, he also draws some
general conclusions. They form a miscellaneous collection of notes assembled in
chap. 18, which have little if any connection with the analysis of chap. 17. I shall
discuss them briefly, reserving further comments for the conclusion of this chapter.
Spinoza’s first remark is that it is not incompatible with ‘divine kingship’ to
appoint an earthly sovereign ‘for after the Hebrews had transferred their rights to
God, they gave the supreme sovereignty to Moses’.100 In the most trivial way that is
of course true, given the fact that every sovereign is said to be ‘an interpreter of the
will of God’. Still it is strange to come across this claim here, given the fact that a
few pages earlier Spinoza still insisted on the fact that by transferring their rights to
Moses the Jews made an end to theocratic government and that by appointing a king
they definitively buried the theocratic system. This can hardly be explained with
reference to contemporary politics unless we assume that Spinoza has no objections
to monarchic rule.
Spinoza’s second remark is that ‘though the priests were the interpreters of the
laws, it was not for them to judge citizens or to excommunicate anyone: this right
belonged only to the judges and princes chosen from the people’.101 The point fits
into Spinoza’s general programme. In the way it is formulated here, however, it
looks like an argumentum ad hominem against the ministers, Calvinist and perhaps
Jewish, the odd word being ‘excommunication’. This is a subject on which Hobbes
is very elaborate but which does not play any conspicuous role in the Theologico-
political treatise. It may be a reflection on Spinoza’s own excommunication from
the Synagogue.102 In any case, unless we interpret ‘excommunication’ as intolerance,
the notion was not very important, civilly or socially, in the Calvino-Dutch context,
given the fact that the Church was a confessional church: one could in the end be
excommunicated (people were frequently ‘excommunicated’ in the sense that they
had no access to the Holy Table), but the fact that one did not belong to the
dominating Church did not make one a pariah.
The various remarks that follow are presented by Spinoza as being founded on a
consideration of ‘the fortunes and histories of the Hebrews’.103 Their relation to the
analysis of theocratic government is, again, extremely loose. First, Spinoza points
out that, as soon as the priests gained power, they introduced innovations and
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
134


superstitions; they changed the law and became theologians; they flattered the
people in order to usurp secular power and so on, all of which contributed to the
ultimate downfall of the state.104 Again, the point is valid from Spinoza’s general
point of view (he will revert to it in chap. 19) but it is inconsistent with his earlier
assessment that it was the kings and the princes who, in order to stir up the people
against the priests, introduced superstitions and eventually caused the decline of
theocratic government.
Spinoza then remarks that the prophets irritated people; that they were
unsuccessful in their attempt to improve moral standards; that they virtually caused
civil wars.105 Again the point fits into Spinoza’s programme, being directed probably
against ‘prophetic’ preaching.106 But it is difficult to see how it relates to the analysis
of theocratic government; indeed, the prophets’ main role was in the period after the
advent of the kings, that is, after theocratic government came to an end.
Finally, ‘as long as the people held the reins of government there was only
one civil war’, whereas under the kings there were many, often with disastrous
consequences.107 So in terms of peace the ‘theocratic’ period preceding the kings –
that of the judges, but note that the period is described as ‘democratic’ – was more
successful: there were long periods of peace and only a few prophets were necessary
to remind the people of the law.108
There seems to be little consistency in these ‘conclusions’, which can be read as
a defence sometimes of royal, sometimes of popular democratic and sometimes of
theocratic (republican) government. So much is clear: government by a king unable
to change the law and confronted by an independent political class is disastrous, a
point on which Spinoza clearly agrees with Hobbes. Although, therefore, it would
have been better if there never had been any kings, once they were there it would
have been better for them to enjoy absolute authority. On the other hand, the reason
why the kings failed was also, and perhaps mainly, that they were the product of a
revolution: ‘After the people, who were little accustomed to kings, changed the
original form of their state to monarchy, there was practically no end to civil
wars.’109 So the main lesson might simply be one of conservatism. That that may in
fact be the case is also suggested, but again indecisively, by another series of
‘general conclusions’, which read as concrete political advice.
First, religious officers should not have the right to issue decrees, or to meddle
with matters of state: ‘stability is far better assured if these officials are restricted to
giving answers only when requested and at other times to teaching and practising
only what is acknowledged as customary and traditional’.110 So in no case should the
priests – read ‘ministers’ – be allowed to preach revolution, let alone participate in
government. The point is familiar. Note, however, that the Calvinist doctrine of
parallelism made it impossible for ministers to participate in government.
Second, Spinoza finds it dangerous to refer purely speculative matters to divine
right (ius divinum) and to legislate concerning beliefs.111 This is of course one of
Spinoza’s main concerns in the Theologico-political treatise as a whole, but the
argument is only indirectly concerned with Jewish history. For although Spinoza
cites the Pharisees as an example, this is only to illustrate his more general point that
legislation on opinion leads to mob-rule: ‘in such circumstances the anger of the
mob is usually the greatest tyrant of all’.112 So the spectre seen behind the priests is
the power of the people, which can be stirred up against the magistrate, not because
‘Government by God’
135


he acts against the law (for, as we have seen, Spinoza sees a legitimate role for the
people as guardians of the law) but either because he is of a different religious
opinion or because he does not do enough to combat dissenting opinions.
Spinoza’s third conclusion is that ‘it is essential for both commonwealth and
religion that the sovereign power should be given the right to decide what is right
and wrong’.113 Again this is a general point, only loosely connected with the analysis
of theocratic government, which indeed is characterized by the absence of an earthly
sovereign, right and wrong being determined by the law.
Spinoza’s last and presumably most important conclusion is that change is always
for the worse. On the one hand, ‘it is fatal for a people unaccustomed to the rule of
kings and already possessing established laws to set up a monarchy’.114 On the other,
‘there is no less danger involved in removing a monarch even if his tyranny is
apparent to all [because] the people, accustomed to royal rule and constrained
by that alone, will despise and mock a lesser authority and therefore on removing a
king will find it necessary to appoint another in his place, as did the prophets of
old’.115 In sum, ‘every state must necessarily preserve its own form and cannot be
changed without incurring the danger of its complete ruin’.116 So the most general
lesson seems to be one of cautious conservatism. If we refer it to Dutch history the
point may simply be that things should be left as they are. In any case, what is
commonly called ‘the Dutch Revolt’ should be interpreted as a conservative
revolution:
As for the States of Holland, as far as we know they never had kings but counts, to whom
the right of sovereignty was never transferred. As the States of Holland themselves make
plain in documents published at the time of the Earl of Leicester, they have always
reserved to themselves the authority to remind the said counts of their duty117 […]. From
this it follows that sovereign right was always vested in the States and it was this
sovereignty that the last count [Philip II] attempted to usurp. Therefore it is by no means
true that the States revolted against him, when in fact they recovered their original
sovereignty which had almost been lost.118
By subscribing to the Act of Abjuration (1581) the States of Holland simply
reclaimed a right that had always been theirs.119 Indeed, even as ‘Count of Holland’,
the Spanish king was no sovereign. So the aim of the so-called ‘revolt’ was to
retrieve the sovereignty of the States of the Province, clearly a defence of the
sovereignty of Holland and a sign of Spinoza’s allegiance to the States’ party.120
Given the fact that, if anything, Spinoza seems to prefer the theocratic period of the
judges to that of the kings – even to the point of conceding that it could have lasted
much longer if the position of the priests (as a caste or as a tribe) had been corrected
– this raises the more fundamental problem of how ‘theocratic’ a government can be
in modern times.
Power of the People
As we have seen, ‘the right of the sovereign [summae potestates] is nothing but
simple natural right, limited by the power, not of every individual, but of the
multitude’.121 This means that no government is absolute. Even under the most
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
136


despotic tyrant, the real power is with the people because it is on their minds (their
fears, hopes, and so on), and on their being ‘of one mind’ ultimately, that the fate of
the tyrant and therefore of the commonwealth depends. The very weakness of
despotic regimes is that the despot tends to forget this.122 Indeed, the irony of
government is that the more tyrannical it is the more power (meaning ‘right’) the
multitude in fact has: ‘If the strongest dominion were held by those who are most
feared, then it would assuredly be held by a tyrant’s subjects, for they are most
feared by their tyrants.’123 Governments are like human beings: ‘the greater cause
someone has for fear the less power and consequently the less right he possesses’.124
Inversely, no government but democratic government is absolutely absolute: ‘If
there be any absolute government it is in fact that which is held by the entire
multitude.’125 The reason is that, whereas in other commonwealths the power of the
sovereign is limited by the people, in a democracy the people are themselves
sovereign, so the power of the sovereign is limited only by the forces of history and
nature.126 Accordingly, from the point of view of the absoluteness of sovereignty,
democratic government would be ideal. Is it also possible? According to Spinoza it
is:
Without any infringement of natural right a community can be formed and a contract
always be preserved in its entirety in absolute good faith on these terms, that everyone
transfers all the power that he possesses to the community, which will therefore alone
retain the sovereign natural right over everything, that is, the supreme rule which
everyone will have to obey either of free choice or through fear of the ultimate penalty.
Such a community’s right is called a democracy, which can therefore be defined as a
united body of men which corporately possess sovereign right over everything within their
power.127
So ‘democracy’ would be a distinct possibility: it is not against natural right, that is,
it is not absolutely impossible. However, the way Spinoza ‘defines’ it (as ‘a united
body of men who all together [collegialiter] possess sovereign right over everything
within their power’) is somewhat disturbing. It is different at any rate from the
Political treatise, which defines it in the more usual way as government by all.128 In
fact the definition in the Theologico-political treatise applies to what the Political
treatise claims to be the stage preceding the choice of government, namely, that of
establishing ‘collective right’ as such. Positive law becomes possible after people
‘combine to defend the land they inhabit and cultivate, to protect themselves, to
repel all violence and to live according to the general judgement of all’.129 The
principle is formulated in words that strongly recall the Theologico-political
treatise: ‘If two come together and unite their strength they have jointly more power
and consequently more right over nature than each of them separately; and the more
there are that have so joined in alliance, the more right they all collectively possess
(emphasis added).’130 But it is only after collective right is established that one can
decide on the form of government:
Where men have general rights and are all guided as it were by one mind it is certain that
every individual has the less right the more the rest collectively exceed him in power; that
is, he has no right over nature but that which the common law allows him. But whatever
he is ordered by the general consent he is bound to execute or may rightfully be compelled
‘Government by God’
137


thereto. This right, which is determined by the power of the multitude, is generally called
government [imperium]. And in general he governs to whom by common consent are
entrusted the affairs of the state […]. If government belongs to a council consisting of the
general multitude then it is called democracy; if that council consists of people who are
elected then it is an aristocracy and if it rests with one man it is called monarchy.131
So government is always the exercise of a collective right; inversely, collective right
is always exercised regardless of the form of government. Accordingly, if
‘democracy’ were no more than ‘collective right’ (as in the Theologico-political
treatise) any commonwealth would be a ‘democracy’, even the most tyrannically
governed, although naturally the more tyrannically it is governed the weaker its
‘collective right’ will be.
The key to this confusion is presumably that, according to Spinoza, the authority
of any government ultimately depends on the people’s will. So, given the fact that
all authority is exercised ‘by common consent’, all government would be essentially
‘democratic’. That that is presumably the way we should read it is suggested in any
case by the Theologico-political treatise in a passage immediately following the
definition of democracy just quoted:
Hence it follows that the sovereign power is bound by no law, and all must obey it in all
matters; for this is what all must have covenanted tacitly or expressly when they
transferred to it all their power of self-defence, that is, all their right. If they intended that
there should be anything reserved to themselves they should have taken the precaution at
the same time to make secure provision to uphold it. Since they did not do so and could
not have done so without the division and the consequent destruction of the state they
thereby submitted themselves absolutely to the will of the sovereign power.132
The fact that a commonwealth is governed by a king or by patricians does not make
it necessarily undemocratic, given that the king and the patricians govern ‘by
common consent’. If the people would prefer something else they should by all
means say so, but since they never did they are bound to obey the ‘powers that be’.
In any case an arrangement that would have allowed them to keep some authority
for themselves – for example by giving the government a limited mandate – would
have led to the disintegration of the commonwealth because in that case the
sovereign would have no authority at all.
Presumably the definition of ‘democracy’ must be read in the same way as
Spinoza’s ‘definitions’ of revelation and prophecy, namely, as an attempt to
convince a democrat (that is, someone who believes that ‘the people’ should have
legislative power) that a situation which is not overtly ‘democratic’ could still be a
democracy on a deeper level.133 That, at any rate, is how Spinoza presents things in
the Political treatise when speaking of aristocratic government. According to that
work, aristocratic government should be ‘absolute’ in principle (the supreme council
should alone have legislative power) but not absolute in practice ‘because the
multitude are a cause of fear to the rulers and therefore successfully retain for
themselves some liberty’.134 This can be remedied ‘if the institutions are such
that it most nearly approaches the absolute, that is, that the multitude are as little
as possible a cause of fear and retain no liberty but such as must necessarily
be assigned to them by the law of the state as such and is therefore not so much
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
138


a right of the multitude as of the whole state, asserted and maintained by the
aristocrats as their own’.135 Accordingly, if the people are given some liberty by law
– provided the law is issued by the sovereign, that is, by the supreme council of
patricians – they no longer undermine the authority of the government by being a
cause of fear.
Presumably there is a polemical subtext, possibly directed against those among
Spinoza’s friends who were in favour of democratic government, like François van
den Enden (1602–74), Spinoza’s teacher of Latin.136 It would mean, however, that
Spinoza is not in favour of popular government in any of its forms and indeed that
one of the points of the Theologico-political treatise is to show precisely that. So
much is clear; Spinoza wants the Theologico-political treatise to be seen as a
discussion of democracy: ‘I have elected to discuss [the democratic state] before all
others because it seemed the most natural form of state, approaching most closely to
that freedom which nature grants to every man.’137 Since, on the other hand, the only
government Spinoza does discuss is theocratic government, that would mean that
even the republican variant of ‘democratic’ government is possible only under
‘theocratic’ conditions. In any case, although democratic government would be
ideal, it is a nostalgic ideal. Or is it?
According to Spinoza, the purpose of the state is ‘to avoid the follies of the
appetite and keep men within the bounds of reason’.138 Supposedly, this must be
understood in a collective sense: the purpose of the state is not to turn all individual
citizens into rationally thinking and acting beings (which is impossible) but to create
an environment in which most people act ‘rationally’.139 That is not an impossibility
in itself, given the fact that rational behaviour may have irrational motives.140 So,
generally speaking, the state would achieve its aim by providing people with a
motive to behave in what we call a rational way, either by mobilizing sufficient
power to frighten them or by rewarding them with security and ease.141 In any case
coercion alone never works: ‘Men are influenced not so much by fear as by hope of
some good that they urgently desire; for in this way each one will be eager to do his
duty.’142 The ideal state would be one in which the subjects believe that they are free,
even if all their thoughts and actions are controlled and directed: ‘Men are so to be
led that they may think that they are not led but living after their own mind and
according to their own decision.’143 This may be achieved by laws and institutions
(which is the approach in the Political treatise) but also by religion (which is the
way indicated by the Theologico-political treatise). Indeed, other things being
equal, religion might be the best and perhaps the only way to achieve this. It was at
any rate in the case of Moses, who was clever enough to introduce religion: in order
‘to make his people do their duty not by fear but from devotion’.144 Given the fact
that it is not realistic to expect that all people can ever reach the level of perfectly
rational and perfectly free beings, a religious motive seems to be indispensable.145
Indeed, people can govern themselves only if they voluntarily sacrifice some of
their freedom, which, if they are not perfectly rational, they are ready to do
only if they have a strong religious motive. Accordingly, some form of religion
would always be necessary. This makes it particularly urgent to look into Spinoza’s
reasons for claiming that theocratic government is impossible in modern times;
indeed, that would mean that democratic and republican government are also
impossible.
‘Government by God’
139


The Impossibility of Theocracy
‘Although,’ according to Spinoza, ‘the Hebrew state might have lasted indefinitely’
– that is, although in itself theocratic government is a perfectly viable form of
government – ‘it is not possible to imitate it now, nor would it be advisable.’146
Spinoza has two arguments. The first is that God has ‘revealed through his Apostles
that his covenant is no longer written in ink or engraved on tablets of stone but
inscribed by God’s spirit in men’s hearts’.147 The second argument is that this type
of commonwealth (‘theocracy’) is suitable only for people ‘who intend to live for
themselves alone with no external ties’.148
The second of these reasons is easiest to understand. The Jews lived in isolation
and were surrounded by enemies, so had no difficulty in preserving their religion,
whereas citizens of an open society are frequently challenged in their opinions.
Accordingly, since theocratic government depends on religious unanimity (people
must adore the same God and the same law), it is not likely to survive in the modern
world, especially, one would say, in the urban culture of Holland.149
The first argument, however, is more properly ‘theologico-political’, that is, based
on ‘revelation’. It is not that a theocracy is absolutely impossible, that no God could
be sovereign, but that the Christian God has revealed to the Apostles that he is not
available for that office. The ‘covenant’ of the Christian God is such that it cannot
be the basis of a commonwealth. Spinoza’s text is taken from the Second Epistle to
the Corinthians:
Ye are our epistle written in our hearts, known and read of all men: Forasmuch as ye are
manifestly declared to be the epistle of Christ ministered by us, written not with ink, but
with the Spirit of the living God: not in tables of stone but in fleshly tables of the heart.
(2 Cor. 3:1–3)
Earlier, Spinoza had cited the same text to condemn worship of the letter: ‘If in
accordance with the saying of the Apostle in 2 Cor. 3:3 they have within themselves
the Epistle of God, written not with ink but with the Spirit of God, not on tablets of
stone but on the fleshly tablets of the heart, let them cease to worship the letter.’150
So any attempt to identify religion or faith with a written ‘canon’ would be against
God’s will. Indeed, as we have seen, every text that provides moral inspiration may
be called ‘divine’.151
Spinoza may have something like that in mind here, although the political
implications of a rejection of the canonicity of Scripture are not obvious. Indeed,
other considerations are more likely. First, God’s will does not have the form of a
law (it is written neither in ink nor on tablets of stone); second, his will has no rela-
tion to one nation in particular (it is written in the heart of every man). The will of
the Christian God is that all people love each other. But apart from the fact that love
cannot be the object of a law – one cannot be ordered to love someone, any more
than one can be ordered to be wise152 – it is not the law of any nation in particular
even if it were a law, given the fact that it is universal: ‘Christ […] was sent not to
preserve the state and to institute laws but only to teach a universal law.’153 A third
possibility could be that the Christian religion is theological and therefore
sectarian.154 So, given the fact that there is not one single Christian theology, a
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
140


Christian theocracy would lack the necessary cohesion. The only solution would be
to turn the Christian religion into a strictly private morality which is compatible with
any government: ‘Everyone, wherever he may be, is able to worship God with true
religion and mind his own business, which is the duty of a private man.’155
It all means that theocratic government is no longer possible. But if theocratic
government is no longer possible, democratic government will no longer be possible
either, even in its republican variant. For democratic government requires, not just
obedience, but ‘devotion’. So, unless one could find a way of giving pietas patriae
a different basis, revealed belief seems to be inevitable. And if the foundation
provided by the Christian religion is unsuitable it is hard to see what could replace
it. Definitely, democracy is a nostalgic ideal.
Conclusion
Just as the main point in the first half of the Theologico-political treatise was a
chapter on the interpretation of Scripture, meant to show that theology is impossible,
the second half culminates in a chapter on Jewish history, meant to show that
theocracy is impossible. Neither in the field of knowledge nor in that of government
is it possible to refer in a meaningful way to divine authority. And that means, not
only that theocratic government is impossible, but also that democratic government
is impossible.
Although this may be surprising for most of us who believe in democracy as a
rational ideal, my conclusion places Spinoza in a long tradition, which holds that
democratic and republican governments are possible only on the basis of moral
virtue. Since, according to Spinoza, what we usually call the moral code can present
itself either as an eternal truth or as a ‘divine’ law, that would mean that democratic
government requires citizens that either are perfectly rational or share a belief in a
God–Lawgiver. Since the fall of the Republic of Israel, the second alternative has
become as impossible as the first. A moral foundation of politics is impossible;
indeed, it is just the reverse, namely, that without political backing morality is
impossible. Spinoza’s problem, and that of his modern readers, is compounded by
the fact that he moves on two different levels. He does not simply propose a political
theory but is also dealing with a particular and highly threatening situation. It is
often assumed that, since on the practical front Spinoza’s sympathies were with the
States’ party, which was anti-clerical as well as anti-monarchist, the author of the
Theologico-political treatise would also be concerned with defending a republican
or even a democratic theory of government – such as was developed by François van
den Enden or by Pieter de la Court (1618–85).156
There can be no doubt that Spinoza sympathized with the States’ party. Not only
was the Theologico-political treatise condemned by the Court of Holland almost
immediately after William III seized power in 1672; it is also known that, when in
the same year Johan de Witt and his brother Cornelis were lynched in The Hague,
Spinoza’s more prudent friends had to prevent him from publishing a pamphlet
condemning that event.157 And of course, given the fact that it was not in Spinoza’s
interest to side with the opposite party – the Orangist and Church party – his loyalty
to the States’ party is only what one should expect.
‘Government by God’
141


That being said, I find it impossible to read the Theologico-political treatise as a
sustained defence of republican, let alone democratic, government. First of all, the
only ‘democratic’ government actually discussed in the Treatise is the republican
government of Israel as it developed between the death of Moses and the advent of
the kings, which as I have shown Spinoza finds viable only under theocratic
conditions. Moreover, the political conclusions Spinoza draws from his analysis do
not amount to an unequivocal defence of republican government. In fact, the only
clear lesson emerging from chap. 18 is that there should be no sudden change –
which may be enough as support for the States’ party but is not enough for us to be
able to ascribe to Spinoza a republican theory. Finally, the fact that Spinoza uses
‘democracy’ for any form of ‘collective right’ suggests that he rejects democracy as
a form of government.
The fact of the matter is that Spinoza interpreted the political system of Holland,
not as a republic, but as an aristocracy. Whether that is an apt description is a
different matter. The most apt description so far is that it was a monarchy without a
monarch – a description which is also Spinoza’s:
If anyone replies that the State of Holland did not last long without a Count or a Stadholder
the answer should be that the people of Holland believed that to obtain their freedom it was
enough to renounce the count and to sever the body politic of its head. They did not think
of reforming the body but left all its members as they had been, so the County of Holland
remained, as a body without a head and as a government without a name. No wonder
therefore that most of the subjects have never known to whom sovereignty belongs. On
top of that those who did govern were too few to govern the multitude and suppress their
adversaries. As a result these could plot against them without being punished and in the
end managed to overthrow them. Accordingly the cause of the fall of this republic is not
that too much time was spent on futile deliberations but the imperfect form of government
and the small number of governors.158
The text is taken from the chapter on aristocratic government. It is clearly written
after the coup of William III, who in 1672 seized power with the support of the
people and the Church, a fact Spinoza evidently deplores.159 His diagnosis is that the
system collapsed, not because of the inefficiency of its government (a factor alleged
sometimes, then as well as now), but because of the common fault of all aristocratic
governments (namely, that it became an oligarchy) and the fact that no serious
attempt was made to reform the institutions. After the people of Holland sent
home the last count – that is, the King of Spain, Philip II, who by inheritance
became Count of Holland – a representative body, the ‘States’, whose only role
under the old constitution had been to advise the count and hear his decisions,
obtained legislative power, not by a formal decision or a treaty but simply because
there was nothing else.160 Since, on the other hand, nothing was done to reform the
institutions, the Stadholderate and the Church in particular, the people never
obtained a clear idea who the sovereign was: whether it was the Provincial States or
the Stadholder or any of the supraprovincial bodies such as the States General and
the State Council.
In any case there can be no doubt that Spinoza interpreted the government
of Holland as ‘aristocratic’. Holland is frequently cited as an example in the
chapters on aristocracy in the Political treatise.161 The only difference from other
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
142


aristocracies, such as Venice and Genoa, is that it does not take its name from a
single city but from a province, but that is also an advantage, for it means that the
inhabitants enjoy more freedom – presumably because the government consists of
representatives from different towns.162 What is more, Spinoza finds aristocratic
government the best there is, provided something is done to prevent its deteriora-
tion into oligarchic government.163 Admittedly, what Spinoza does describe as
‘aristocratic government’ has much in common with a representative democracy, but
even if the entire population sat on the general council it would still be an aristocracy
‘as long as that right is not hereditary and as long as others are not admitted on the
basis of a common law’.164 Indeed, for it to be an aristocracy, admission to the
council must take place on the basis of cooptation.165 Inversely, although those who
are excluded from the council enjoy as much freedom as is necessary to pacify them,
they have no legislative power at all.166
Apparently, what Spinoza fears most from democratic government is its lack of
form and direction, which could be compensated only by the presence of a precise
‘divine’ law which does not need any further ‘interpretation’ – except to apply it in
situations not foreseen by the law. Accordingly, an aristocratic government that
gives enough to the people to pacify them, and that is large enough not to become
an oligarchy, is the best there is. Indeed, it is likely that Spinoza’s praise of
Amsterdam, often cited as proof of his fundamentally democratic feelings, is
inspired by the fact that it was one of the few Dutch towns whose government was
purely aristocratic.167

law) each province had its own legal tradition, but also that the Treaty of the Union of
Utrecht (1579), which came closest to being a ‘Constitution’, left many things
undecided; cf. Robert Fruin, Geschiedenis der staatsinstellingen tot den val der

For both interpretations support can be found in historical documents and in any case
the Seven Provinces sometimes actually were governed as a federal state (under
Leicester, for example) and sometimes formed a confederation (for example, between
1650 and 1672). The argument for claiming that it was a confederation of states is that
the personal union (achieved by marriage and inheritance) between the various
provinces was never replaced by a monolithic structure. Philip II was not ‘King of the
Netherlands’ but Count of Holland, Duke of Gelderland and so on. The ‘federal state’
interpretation can be based on the Union of Utrecht (1579), by which the seven
provinces promised to do nothing without each other. See Israel, Dutch Republic,
pp. 179–230.

With the sudden death of William II in 1650 had started the first Stadholderless
period which was to last until the coup of his posthumous son William III (by
his marriage to Mary Stuart King of England) in 1672. The Church loathed the
tolerant policy of Holland and hoped that a Stadholder would be less lenient towards
impiety.

Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
150


Chapter 6
The Freedom to Philosophize:
Descartes and Spinoza
To demonstrate that the freedom to philosophize, that is, the freedom to know the
truth, is compatible with peace and piety and that without it both peace and piety are
made impossible is to show, not only that Spinoza’s own philosophy is innocuous
but also that, in the interest of peace and piety, particular truths must be known, such
as that everything is part of nature, that there is no God–Lawgiver, that the only
object of obedience can be a sovereign, and so on. But a philosopher must allow
himself to know them; that is, to achieve, by rational means, truth and certainty in
domains that are traditionally held to be inaccessible to reason and philosophy.
Since, on the other hand, Cartesian philosophy as it was understood in the Low
Countries protected revealed theology from the intrusion of philosophy by giving
each their own domain, it is likely that Spinoza aims at lifting the epistemological
obstacles Cartesians had erected to prevent philosophy from interfering with
theology and faith.
Cartesianism: Theoretical and Practical Knowledge
The basic axiom of Descartes’ method is traditional. It is that the only way to explain
something is to reduce it to something already understood. The question is what
counts as ‘understood’. For Descartes that is neither an experiential pattern nor a
generally held view.1 Things are explained with reference either to a mathematical
model (as in The World) or to a piece of machinery, designed to account for a given
set of phenomena (as in the Treatise of Man). Knowledge based on experience,
finally, is limited to practical problems, that is, technical problems (mechanics) and
problems of health (medicine) and happiness.
Without a metaphysical framework these various parts of Descartes’ heritage
easily fall apart; indeed, one may presume that one of the aims of Descartes’
metaphysics is precisely to prevent that from happening. In the Low Countries,
however, where metaphysical speculation never was very popular, nothing could
prevent the division of Cartesian philosophy into three distinct trends, connected by
little more than a feeling of personal loyalty to Descartes and to other Cartesians –
and sometimes not even that.2 The first trend is found in professional philosophers
and theologians. They generally emphasized the peculiar nature of philosophic
enquiry, so as to avoid its contamination by practical problems, especially those of
theology but also medicine, moral philosophy and political theory. A second trend
is manifest in the medical profession. Physicians used the machine model as a
heuristic tool, leaving it to anatomy to find out in precisely what way nature made a
151


particular function possible.3 Finally, a third trend, mainly non-academic, which was
based on Descartes’ Treatise of the Passions, aimed at developing theories of action,
spreading into the domains of politics, morality, the arts, and so on.
Undoubtedly, Spinoza reacted to Cartesian philosophy as much as he reacted to
Descartes himself. More specifically he must have been looking for a new unity that
would leave intact the link between knowledge of nature and practical concerns,
which in his view definitely included those traditionally associated with religion.
This makes it likely that Spinoza’s sympathies were with the second and especially
the third trends, rather than with the first. Let us first see, therefore, what were the
ideas belonging to this first trend.
Its most interesting representative is the Leiden (from 1666 Amsterdam) professor
of philosophy Joannes de Raey (1622–1702).4 In a disputation of 1666 ‘on freedom
and slavery in general but also in philosophy’ (De libertate et servitute universim
atque etiam in philosophando), written possibly in reaction to Meyer’s Philosophia
S. Scripturae Interpres, which was published the same year, he carefully outlines the
