‘freedom to philosophize’.5 According to De Raey, we are subject to three kinds of
slavery: servitus ad intellectum, that is, our difficulty in grasping things of a purely
intellectual nature; servitus in voluntate, that is, our incapacity to adopt the precepts
of reason; and servitus in judicio, that is, our readiness to surrender to prejudice and
emotion.6 Freedom, on the other hand, is the effect of concentrating on clear and
distinct ideas, which are identified through a process of systematic doubt. As a
result, eclectic philosophers, who believe themselves to be ‘free’ because they
‘freely’ examine every available opinion to retain those only that are the most likely,
are no more free than common people. Their prison may be larger but it remains a
prison because they are always engaged in comparing ideas that are not clear and
distinct. So ‘eclectic philosophy is not the best but the worst’.7
By making clear and distinct ideas the condition for free judgment and freedom
generally, De Raey also marks his position with respect to Meyer and Spinoza.8
According to De Raey, indeed ‘clearness and distinctness’ is an intrinsic property of
a limited number of ideas. The aim of doubt is not to render clear and distinct ideas
that are obscure and confused but to separate clear and distinct ideas from all others.
Accordingly, there can be no question of applying the criterion of clear and distinct
ideas to theology or for that matter to any other discipline but philosophy. The line
that divides philosophy from other disciplines is precisely that its object is a set of
clear and distinct ideas (‘extension’ and ‘local motion’). So the notion of philosophy
developed by De Raey resembles that of modern natural science, more particularly
in so far as it is concerned with developing mathematical models.9
De Raey elaborated his ideas into an original theory of meaning and language.
Like virtually all 17th-century philosophers, including Meyer, he believes that a
word does not directly refer to a thing but to the idea of a thing.10 Since there are two
classes of ideas – the clear and distinct ideas of the intellect and the obscure and
confused ideas of the senses – there are also two languages: the language of
philosophy (that is, basically, the language of mathematics) and ordinary language.
The first refers to the clear and distinct ideas of extension and motion, which, being
innate, are proper to the mind; the second to the obscure and confused ideas of the
senses, which the mind acquires by being united with the body. Correspondingly,
there are also two methods: the method of philosophy, which is to contemplate clear
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
152


and distinct ideas (isolated from any others by means of systematic doubt) and the
Ramist art of persuasion, which is used in daily life. One is certain, of course, but all
that can be achieved by the other is probability.11 So in De Raey’s view philosophy
should give up its traditional task of achieving a harmonious synthesis of knowledge
and experience; on the contrary, the aim of philosophy is to separate them.12 And
that means that it is impossible to use philosophy either to solve practical problems
(which are formulated in ordinary language) or to ‘philosophically’ interpret
Scripture (which is written, not in the language of mathematics, but in that of
everyday life). The more philosophy is true, the less it can do for theology.
Superficially, De Raey’s idea that philosophy has no contribution to make to the
interpretation of Scripture has a Spinozist ring, but in fact the difference could
hardly be greater. The reason why De Raey separates philosophy and theology is
that they deal with two different types of truths (on different subjects which are
known in different ways) whereas, as we have seen, Spinoza separates them because
truth is irrelevant to theology. Inversely, Spinoza has a thoroughly practical
conception of philosophy: the aim of philosophy is to achieve lasting happiness
(which is possible only if it allows itself to criticize theology and faith), whereas
Descartes aims for theories that are true and certain even if that forces him to restrict
his efforts to a specific area. So there could be no doubt about the distance that
separates this version of Cartesian philosophy from Spinoza’s. But does Cartesian
philosophy contain any inherent checks to prevent it from developing into
Spinozism, that is, from attempting to achieve the same truth and certainty in the
domain of practice?
Descartes himself would undoubtedly point to his metaphysics, which makes the
certainty of science dependent on knowledge of God and of the soul. Probably, he
would also remark that, from the beginning, he had set apart the laws and customs
of his country – including the religion in which he was brought up – and therefore
that neither religion nor politics and morality were ever in danger of being criticized
by philosophy.13 Cartesians like De Raey, on the other hand, would point to the
intrinsic limits of reason, which in their view are determined by the availability of
clear and distinct ideas. In sum, the Cartesian tradition offers the following
arguments against philosophy from developing into a ‘radical’ system: philosophy
is a pure science; scientific certainty presupposes knowledge of God, and religious
beliefs can be set apart. Their common basis, however, is the theory of ideas and
judgment: (a) philosophy can be a pure science because clear and distinct ideas are
intrinsically distinct from obscure and confused ideas; (b) metaphysics is necessary
because even the most clear and distinct ideas can be doubted; (c) obscure and
confused ideas can be affirmed to be true (and as a consequence set apart before
being examined philosophically) because one of the components of judgment is the
will. Given Spinoza’s programme, he should be expected therefore to direct his
attention primarily to that theory. Let us see whether that is borne out by the texts.
Spinoza on Descartes
That Cartesian philosophy acted as a catalyst in Spinoza’s early development is
likely even if we do not know when and how. What we do know is that Spinoza
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
153


explicitly dissociated himself from Descartes as early as 1661 in a letter to Henry
Oldenburg:
Their [Descartes’ and Bacon’s] first and by far the greatest error is that they are far
removed from knowing the first cause and origin of all things. The second, that they do
not know the true nature of the human mind. The third that they never explain the true
cause of error.14
The first and allegedly most important point is that neither Bacon nor Descartes
know anything solid about ‘the first cause and origin of all things’. So Spinoza
fundamentally criticizes Descartes’ metaphysical project: the way Descartes speaks
of God cannot count as a foundation of true and certain knowledge. Spinoza’s
second point is that Descartes does not know the nature of the human mind.
Referring Oldenburg to the first half of his letter, Spinoza gives three specific
arguments: ‘First, in nature there cannot be two substances, except if they have a
totally different essence; second, a substance cannot be produced but exists in virtue
of its own essence; third, all substance must be infinite, that is, as perfect as possible
in its own kind.’15 So what Spinoza rejects is the idea that the individual human mind
(or soul) is a created finite substance. Finally, Spinoza dismisses Descartes’
explanation of error (which on dubious grounds he identifies with Bacon’s) because
the will is nothing but an ens rationis: the will is as little distinguished from this or
that ‘volition’ as whiteness is from a white body or humanity from a human being
and therefore totally unable to act as a real cause.
These points are familiar to any reader of the Ethics. Still one wonders
why Descartes’ theory of error is specifically singled out for criticism. The
reason seems to be that, according to Spinoza, a false judgment is necessarily
determined by external causes: ‘since the will is nothing but an ens rationis and
cannot be said to be the cause of this or that volition; since particular volitions
cannot be called free but are necessarily such as they are determined to be by their
causes (given the fact that to exist one needs a cause); and finally since, according
to Descartes, even errors (ipsissimi errores) are particular volitions, it follows
necessarily that errors, that is, particular volitions, are not free but are determined by
external causes instead of the will’.16 So the model Spinoza applies to false
judgments seems to be the Cartesian model of the passions: as little as we are free
to change or stop a passion simply by willing it (given the fact that a passion is the
perception of a bodily process on which we have no immediate influence), as little
would we be free to avoid a false judgment – indeed, the only way to eliminate
falsehood is by generating true ideas just as the only way to eradicate evil passions
is by giving ourselves ‘passions’ that are incompatible with the ones we would be
glad to get rid of.
Two years later, in the preface to Spinoza’s adaptation of Descartes’ Principles
(1663), Lodewijk Meyer, who edited the volume, mentions some further differences
to emphasize the fact that, although Spinoza had tried to be faithful to Descartes’
text, he does not agree with much of what he says.17 This is more particularly true of
Descartes’ theory of the will:
Indeed, he [Spinoza] does not believe that [the will] is distinguished from the intellect and
even less that it enjoys such freedom. In fact, whenever Descartes claims these things he
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
154


does not demonstrate but simply supposes that the human mind is a substance which in an
absolute sense thinks – as becomes clear in Pt 4 of the Discourse and in the Second
Meditation. Our author on the other hand, although he admits that there is a thinking
substance in nature (in rerum natura), denies that it constitutes the essence of the human
mind; indeed he claims that, as little as extension has any limits, thought has any limits
and therefore that as little as the human body exists in an absolute sense (for it is no more
than extension determined by motion and rest according to the laws of extended nature)
the human mind or soul exists in an absolute sense (for it is no more than a thought
determined by certain ideas according to the laws of thinking nature – a thought,
moreover, of which we must conclude that it is necessarily there whenever a body starts
to exist). And on the basis of that definition he [Spinoza] believes that it is not hard to
demonstrate that the will is not distinguished from the intellect (let alone that it could ever
enjoy the freedom Descartes ascribes to it); that the faculty of affirming and denying is
completely imaginary; that to affirm or deny is nothing but the idea; that other faculties
like the intellect, desire, etc., are fictions or in any case must be reckoned among notions
men have formed because they think of things in an abstract way, like humanity,
stoneness, and similar things.18
Although it is questionable whether outside the circle of the initiated many
contemporary readers fully grasped the meaning of these statements, it is perfectly
clear for us that, when writing this, Spinoza was already in the possession of much
of the Ethics. Indeed, there is a thinking substance but it is not a human mind; the
mind is a thought (cogitatio) which, like all thoughts, is determined by other
thoughts; what we call ‘thinking’ has, on the level of finite beings at any rate,
nothing ‘absolute’, which I take to mean that it is not a true activity or in other words
that a finite being is not really the subject of its acts.
These points are already more or less known from the letter to Oldenburg.
Another point is new and intriguing: according to Meyer, Spinoza does not believe
that anything is beyond human understanding (hoc aut illud captum humanum
superare):
Indeed he [Spinoza] judges that those things, and others even more sublime and subtle, not
only can be conceived clearly and distinctly but can also be explained quite easily
[commodissime], if only we would set ourselves to the search for truth along a different
road [alia via] from the one opened and prepared by Descartes; and therefore that the
foundations of the sciences worked out by Descartes – and the things built on them – do
not suffice to resolve and answer the most thorny questions of metaphysics; indeed, that
other foundations are needed if we wish to bring our intellect to that most elevated
knowledge.19
Now obviously the Cartesian ‘road’ is the Cartesian ‘method’ as the order in which
things can and should be known: doubt, metaphysics, physics and finally the applied
sciences (mechanics, medicine and morals). So Meyer’s point would be that, if we
did not start with doubt and metaphysics, the things Descartes considers to be
‘beyond the reach of the human intellect’ – indeed, even the ‘most thorny questions
of metaphysics’ – would become perfectly knowable and intelligible. In sum we
could solve all problems about God if metaphysics were placed, not at the beginning
of the search of truth, to lay the foundations of science, but at its end, after the
completion of physics, so the fact that Spinoza’s Metaphysical thoughts are placed
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
155


after his rendering of Descartes’ Principles could be more significant than is usually
thought.
Spinoza’s criticism of Descartes as it is presented in these texts is dominated by
three notions: ‘substance’, ‘mind’ and ‘method.’ The link that connects them is,
again, the Cartesian theory of ideas and judgment. For according to Descartes we
identify substances by means of the clear and distinct ideas of their attributes, the
aim of method is to isolate clear and distinct ideas as the basis of science and the
freedom of the human mind is strictly located in judgment. It is to Descartes’ theory
of ideas and judgment, therefore, that we should turn now.
Descartes on Ideas and Judgment
In the Second Replies Descartes defines idea as ‘the form of any given thought,
immediate perception of which makes me aware of that thought’ (formam illam per
cujus immediatam perceptionem ipsius cogitationis conscius sum).20 ‘Thought’
(cogitatio), on the other hand, would be ‘everything that is within us in such a way
that we are immediately aware of it’.21 Hence ‘all the operations of the will, the
intellect, the imagination and the senses are thoughts’.22 Since by definition we are
aware of our thoughts we can also refer to them as ideas: ‘When I want something
or am afraid of something, I simultaneously perceive that I want or am afraid; and
that is why I count volition and fear among my ideas.’23 However, there is a
distinction between ‘ideas’ in the strict sense and ‘ideas’ in a broader sense:
Some of my thoughts are as it were images of things and it is only in those cases that the
term ‘idea’ is strictly appropriate – for example, when I think of a man, or a chimera, or
the sky, or an angel, or God. Other thoughts have various additional forms: Thus when I
will, or am afraid, or affirm, or deny, there is always a particular thing which I take as the
object of my thought but my thought includes something more than the likeness of that
thing.24
An ‘idea’ in the strict sense is ‘as it were’ the image of an object, regardless of the
nature of that object, which could be real or imaginary, material or immaterial.
‘Thought’ in general however (‘idea’ in the broad sense) could relate in many
different ways to an object, taking on ‘additional forms’ like fear, desire, and so on.
In any case, an idea as explicitly belongs to the mind as a thought: ‘In so far as these
images are in the corporeal imagination, that is, in so far as they are depicted in some
part of the brain, I do not call them “ideas” at all; I call them “ideas” only in so far
as they determine [informant] the mind itself, when it is directed to that part of the
brain.’25 That is also their significance in the context of the Third Meditation: ‘Even
though the objects of my sensory experience and imagination might have no
existence outside me, nonetheless the modes of thinking which I refer to as cases of
memory perception and imagination, in so far as they are simply modes of thinking,
do exist within me.’26 The fact that ideas belong to the mind alone means that they
are not affected by systematic doubt and that I can reflect on them even if I suppose
that I have no body.
But the fact that I can have ideas without ‘knowing’ about the rest also makes it
necessary to differentiate between ideas (provided by the understanding) and the act
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
156


by which they are asserted to be true.27 Indeed, ‘the will is required so that, once
something is perceived in some manner, our assent may then be given’.28 So
whenever we judge something to be true there is a ‘volition’ or act of the will.
Accordingly, given the fact that the will is free and that we can also suspend
our judgment, any judgment is literally a free act – ‘free’ because the will is
free.29
This theory makes it possible for Descartes to solve several problems. First, he
can show that error must not be imputed to God.30 If it is my responsibility to affirm
or deny an idea or set of ideas as true or false, God is not responsible for my false
judgments.31 Second, he can justify practical judgments, based on obscure and
confused ideas, by claiming that, even if they are theoretically false, they are
practically useful.32 Third, he can explain religious beliefs (relating to ideas that are
not clear and distinct) as acts of obedience, facilitated by Divine grace.33 Finally,
Descartes can extend his method of doubt to all ideas, because we are free to doubt
even clear and distinct ideas – hence the necessity of metaphysics.34 However, the
theory also creates several problems, being built on two distinctions that are not
absolutely evident: (a) between ideas that are clear and distinct and ideas that are
not; and (b) between an ideational (static) and a volitional (dynamic and free) aspect
of judgment. Given the fact that these distinctions may also have been the starting
point of Spinoza’s investigation of the problem, I deal with them first.
What does it mean for an idea to be clear and distinct? According to Descartes’
Discourse, something is clear and distinct if it cannot be doubted.35 But what is
actually doubted is judgments; indeed, ideas are not affected by doubt, as we have
seen. It is ideas, on the other hand, that can be either ‘clear and distinct’ or ‘obscure
and confused’; indeed, it is hard to see in what way a judgment could be either ‘clear
and distinct’ or ‘obscure and confused’ unless these qualifications refer to the ideas
that are the object of that judgment. According to the Principles, finally, an idea is
clear if it is ‘present and accessible to an attentive mind’ and distinct if it is ‘sharply
separated from all other perceptions’.36 But a sensation of, say, ‘red’ or ‘hunger’ or
‘pain’ can be as ‘present and accessible’ to one’s mind as, say, the idea of God or
the idea of a triangle, and that would mean that a sensation of colour or a feeling of
pain could be as clear as the idea of a triangle or the idea of God. And, in fact,
Descartes does admit that the perception of an intense pain is ‘very clear’, although
he denies that it is always distinct, given the fact that ‘people commonly confuse this
perception with an obscure judgment concerning the nature of something which
they think exists in the painful spot and which they suppose to resemble the
sensation of pain’.37 If my foot hurts, the sensation of pain may not be very distinct
because it is compounded with the obscure judgment that the pain is in my foot or
that whatever is going on in my foot is ‘pain-like’, but the sensation of pain as such
would still remain ‘very clear’. So it would be the lack of distinctness rather than
clearness that constitutes the trouble: ‘In our childhood the mind was so immersed
in the body that, although there was much that it perceived clearly, it never perceived
anything distinctly.’38 It is much easier for an idea to be clear than to be distinct, but
all it takes for a clear idea to become distinct is to be separated from other ideas and
from judgments. Accordingly, someone who manages to avoid the ‘obscure
judgment’ that the pain is in his foot would have a sensation of pain which is clear
as well as distinct.39 In principle, therefore, any idea could be clear as well as distinct
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
157


and, from the point of view of someone like De Raey, that would mean that
‘clearness and distinctness’ cannot serve as a criterion of demarcation.
It also seems questionable whether our judgments always require a free act of the
will or ‘volition’. Thus I do not suppose that I am free to doubt that I exist, or that I
am free to doubt that God exists if and when I reflect on the idea of God. Descartes
solves the problem by making a distinction between freedom and indifference. We
are indifferent if we have no reason for either affirming or denying what is given:
‘The indifference I feel when there is no reason pushing me in one direction rather
than another is the lowest grade of freedom.’40 Freedom does not necessarily mean
freedom of choice:
In order to be free, there is no need for me to be inclined both ways; on the contrary, the
more I incline in one direction – either because I clearly understand that reasons of truth
and goodness point that way, or because of a divinely produced disposition of my inmost
thoughts – the freer is my choice.41
So freedom would not be arbitrariness. Indeed, the clearer the ideas, the freer our
judgment: ‘the spontaneity and freedom of my belief [is] all the greater in proportion
to my lack of indifference’.42 But, also, the greater the force that pushes me in one
direction, the freer the act: ‘Neither divine grace nor natural knowledge ever
diminishes freedom; on the contrary, they increase and strengthen it.’43 So, if we
assume that practical judgments are based on ideas that are not clear and distinct, it
would mean that ordinarily an act is not free. A thought has more chance to be ‘free’
than an act – an idea that would prove very attractive to Spinoza.
The Cartesian theory of ideas and judgment is highly complex and probably
inconsistent.44 So much is clear; with the possible exception of the ideas of God and
of the self, which if properly examined entail an existential judgment, Descartes
generally deals with ideas as if they are images.45 This has two important
consequences. First, in order to judge the relation of an image to something else, we
need more than that image. Either we must compare it with whatever it is an image
of or, if that is not possible, we must rely on ‘authority’ – and that is precisely what
is done by Descartes, who relies on God for the claim that we can trust our ideas in
so far as they are clear and distinct. Second, images are passive, so to turn them into
judgments an act of the will is required.
Spinoza on Ideas and Judgment
Spinoza defines an ‘idea’ as a ‘concept of the mind which it forms for being a
thinking thing’ (propterea quod est res cogitans).46 In an explanatory note
(explicatio) he explains why he uses the word ‘concept’ (conceptus) instead of the
Cartesian ‘perception’ (perceptio): concept ‘seems to express an act of the mind’
(actio mentis) whereas ‘perception’ suggests that ‘the mind is passive with regard to
its object’ (mentem ab objecto pati).47 So ‘ideas’ would be concepts, formed by the
mind ‘because’ or ‘in so far as’ (propterea quod) it is a thinking thing. ‘To think’,
on the other hand, would be the process of actually ‘conceiving’ something; that is,
of actually forming a concept. This suggests that, as long as the mind is passively
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
158


recording sensations and impressions, it does not really ‘think’. Indeed, images
would not be ‘ideas’ in the proper sense of the word, but no more than passive
perceptions ‘of the traces found in the brain from the motion of the spirits aroused
in the senses by objects’.48 Like Descartes, accordingly, Spinoza would
acknowledge a distinction between an active and a passive element in thinking but,
unlike Descartes, he would situate it, not between ideas and volitions, but between
images and ideas. Let us see whether that interpretation finds support in the texts.
According to Spinoza’s Metaphysical Thoughts, ‘to think’ is to judge, that is, to
affirm or deny.49 That suggests a Cartesian analysis of ‘judgment’ – the act of
thinking or judging (affirming, denying) would be distinguishable at any rate from
the perception of an idea. According to the Ethics, however, a judgment is not a
volition but is contained in, as Spinoza says, the idea in so far as it is an idea: ‘There
is no volition in the mind, that is, no affirmation or negation, apart from that implied
by the idea in so far as it is an idea.’50 So ‘in so far as an idea is an idea’ there would
be no need to affirm or deny it, that is, to do anything at all to turn it into a judgment
– but, again, only ‘in so far as an idea is an idea’. The argument put forward in the
demonstration of this proposition is that all it takes to demonstrate a mathematical
theorem, say, that the sum of the angles of a triangle is equal to the sum of two right
angles, is to have the idea of a triangle, so when we go from the idea to the theorem
the will never intervenes: the theorem simply makes explicit something that was
already contained in ‘the idea in so far as it is an idea’.51
From a Cartesian point of view, that is a petitio principii. For we can know a
judgment to be true either because the ideas to which it relates are clear and distinct
or because we deduce it from another judgment that is known to be true (because it
relates to clear and distinct ideas). So if we see the theorem on triangles as a
judgment true and known to be true, either its demonstration is a judgment relating
to the clear and distinct idea of a triangle, or the idea of a triangle from which we
deduce it is not really an ‘idea’ (in the Cartesian sense) but itself a judgment true and
known to be true (that is, a judgment relating to clear and distinct ideas). The first
alternative being pointless, the second must be Spinoza’s real argument.
Accordingly, in so far as the idea of a triangle is an idea it would not be the clear and
distinct mental picture of a triangle but a judgment true and known to be true: in
forming the idea of a triangle we actually form a judgment true and known to be true.
Obviously, if we think of ideas in the Cartesian sense that does not make much
sense, the point about ‘ideas’ being precisely that to constitute a judgment they
require a volition – because they are ‘pictures’. So what Spinoza means by ‘idea’ is
not a picture but, presumably, a definition, not simply one that would allow us to
identify triangles among other geometrical objects but one showing that triangles
can exist and that their properties can be explained in a particular way (which is
actually a judgment). And that is, of course, correct: to be used in a demonstration
at all, the idea of a triangle is not a picture but some sort of proposition. In sum,
Spinoza’s demonstration is valid only if ‘an idea in so far as it is an idea’ is of the
nature of a proposition.
On the other hand, the fact that we can refer to an idea in so far as it is an idea as
a judgment suggests that an idea ‘in so far as it is not an idea’ is a ‘picture’ or an
‘image’. And, indeed, Spinoza does separate images from the actual thinking
process:
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
159


We must investigate whether there is any affirmation or negation in the mind but the one
implied by the idea in so far as it is an idea […] so that the process of thinking is not
reduced to images [ne cogitatio in picturas incidat]. For by ideas I understand, not the
images that are formed at the back of the eye, but the concepts of thinking.52
So the ideas which the mind forms in so far it is a thinking thing are not pictures,
not only in the sense of Descartes that they are not corporeal, but also in the sense
that they do not picture anything at all. They show in a propositional form the
possibility of a thing or a state of affairs. By forming the idea of a triangle or a circle
we do not primarily form pictures of (‘imagine’) triangles and circles but show the
possibility of triangles and circles as real beings. Their being a picture is the least
interesting aspect of ideas: images are nothing but ‘dumb pictures on a board’
(picturae mutae in tabula).53
Finally, by demonstrating his point in the way he does – that is, by claiming that
all we need to demonstrate a proposition is ‘an idea in so far as it is an idea’ –
Spinoza implicitly claims that ‘in so far as an idea is an idea’ it is impossible for it
to be false. Indeed, if ‘in so far as it is an idea’ the idea of a triangle could be false,
we would not be entitled to deduce from it a proposition true and known to be true.
So the very act of forming the idea of an object, that is, the very act of conceiving
an object, would entail a true and certain judgment on that object as a real or a
possible being as well as a true and certain explanation of its properties. In sum, it
is impossible for an idea ‘in so far as it is an idea’ to be false.
We are now in a position to explain why Spinoza ascribes Descartes’ theory of
error to his not making a distinction between ‘ideas’ and ‘images’.54 For in so far as
ideas are images – that is, ‘in so far as they are not ideas’ – they can be part of a
judgment only in virtue of some act of the mind: ‘I should like you to note that in
themselves the imaginations of the mind contain no error, that is, that the mind does
not err from the fact that it imagines.’55 Images are called ‘images’, not because they
accurately picture their objects (which they do not), but because they are not
concepts: ‘To retain the customary words the affections of the human body whose
ideas [that is, of those affections] present external bodies as present to us, we shall
call images of things even if they do not reproduce the figures of things.’56
Consequently, the reason why Spinoza rejects Descartes’ theory is presumably not
his distinction between an active and a passive element in ‘thinking’ but two other
things: the notion that a judgment is free and the notion that in judgment no other
activity is involved than that of affirming or denying a given content. Indeed, as
Spinoza’s own example of the sum of the angles of a triangle shows, it is just the
reverse: a judgment is the act by which the various implications of a concept are
evolved – and these are true and known to be true if the concept is duly formed, that
is, if an object was duly conceived.
This is confirmed by the Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect. According
to that work, to prevent error it is essential to keep in mind the distinction between
the imagination and the intellect.57 The imagination is ‘that by which the mind is
passive’.58 The definition of the intellect, on the other hand, is dominated by the
word ‘to form’ (formare). The intellect forms positive ideas before it forms negative
ideas; it forms clear and distinct ideas, and so on.59 So the intellect is the mind in so
far as it is active, its activity being to ‘form’ ideas, that is, to conceive possible
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
160


beings, whereas the imagination is the mind in so far as it is passive, that is, in so far
as it simply perceives whatever goes on in the body. Admittedly, ideas of the
imagination are also ‘formed’ but, according to Spinoza, ‘against our will’ (nobis
invitis).60 The fact, therefore, that there are complex ideas of the imagination does
not speak against the passivity of the imagination as a mental faculty; indeed,
fictitious ideas ‘have their origin in the imagination, that is, in fortuitous and, so to
speak, loose sensations, which do not spring from the power of the mind but from
external causes, in so far as the body, whether waking or sleeping, undergoes various
motions’.61 The imagination is the mind in so far as it perceives the body, that is, in
so far as that body is affected by other bodies: ‘the imagination is affected only by
bodies’.62 Its ideas are formed by the laws of association, which in turn reflect the
physical structure of the brain.63 As a result, thinking in images and by association
is not an activity of the mind but a perception (awareness) of what goes on in the
body.
‘To conceive’, on the other hand, that is, to form an idea, is not to imagine
anything: ‘If we do not make a distinction between the imagination and the intellect
we believe that whatever we imagine easily is more clear and therefore better
understood, whereas all we actually do is to have an image of something.’64 A clear
and distinct image definitely is not a clear and distinct concept:
For example, if we say that men instantaneously change into animals, that is a very general
proposition; indeed, so general that there is no concept, no idea; or in other words no
connection [cohaerentia] in the mind between a subject and a predicate. If there were, [the
mind] would immediately see the means and causes by which that would happen.65
The proposition that men can change into animals is ‘general’ in the sense that it is
‘vague’: it does not specify when and how and under what conditions humans could
change into animals but simply states that it happened. The very attempt to conceive
the process of humans changing into animals (that is, to think the chain of causes
leading to that event) is enough to realize that it cannot happen and therefore that the
proposition expressing it is false. Although it can be imagined clearly and distinctly
(by a poet or a prophet) it cannot be conceived. So this ‘general’ proposition is false,
not primarily because we cannot form a clear and distinct picture of it (indeed we
can) or because so far it has never happened (which would be inconclusive) but
because we cannot conceive it as happening in a uniform and intelligible way. So
‘the form of a true thought must be placed in that very thought without relation to
other things, nor does it recognise the object as its cause but must depend on the very
power and nature of the intellect’.66 A thought is true for what it is as a conceptual
process, not for what it is as an image of something outside the mind.
This explains why Spinoza, although nominally subscribing to the corres-
pondence definition of truth, is comparatively relaxed about it.67 For not only can an
idea be true if nothing in the world actually corresponds to it; it may not be true if
something does: ‘If an artisan has duly conceived a machine his thought is true even
if there has never been nor will be such a machine, whereas if someone says, for
example, that Peter exists but does not know that Peter exists that thought is false as
far as he is concerned or, if you like, not true even if in fact Peter exists.’68 To the
extent that a machine is actually conceived, its idea is true and known to be true even
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
161


if momentarily we cannot create the physical conditions required for its realization
– but we could use it to explain, for example, the workings of animal bodies.
However, to the extent that we have no good reasons for assuming that Peter exists
the belief that Peter exists is not an idea even if in fact Peter exists; indeed, it is
questionable whether, on this view of ‘idea’, actual existence could ever be an object
of it.69 So the truth of an idea is measured in terms, not of its being a picture of
reality, but of its being intrinsically intelligible. Accordingly, my initial claim that
there should be a distinction between ‘idea in so far as it is an idea’ and ‘idea in so
far as it is not an idea’, identical to that between ‘idea–concept’ and ‘idea–image’,
as well as the related claim that ‘an idea in so far as it is an idea’ (‘idea–concept’)
cannot be false, is borne out by the Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect.
Metaphysics and Certainty
According to Spinoza, an idea – and now that we have made a distinction between
‘idea–image’ and ‘idea–concept’ we may assume that here ‘idea’ means
‘idea–concept’ – is ‘objectively’ (objective) whatever its ideatum is in actual fact
(realiter).70 An idea is an ‘objective’ essence or being (essentia objectiva), that is, an
essence or being in so far as it is an object of thought or in so far as it is conceived.
This does not mean that the thing as it is and the act by which it is conceived
coincide: ‘It is one thing to be a circle and quite another to be the idea of a circle.’71
So the process of conceiving an object can itself be conceived: ‘An idea can be the
object of another objective being [that is, of an idea] and, being something real and
intelligible, this second objective being [idea], too, and so forth and so further.’72 We
can form the idea of an idea by forming the concept ‘idea’; we can form the idea of
the idea of an idea by forming the concept ‘reflexive idea’, and so on. This makes
room for a discussion on ‘method’, that is, on the way in which we form ideas, that
is, true ideas.73 Still, method does not result in a set of criteria used to see whether a
given idea is true, not only because that is not necessary, but because we do not have
an idea of truth independently of a true idea: ‘If you ask me what truth is apart from
a true idea, you might as well ask what whiteness is apart from a white body.’74
There is no formal criterion of truth – as there is in Descartes – even if the fact of
having a true idea allows us to eliminate any idea contrary to it as false. It is in that
sense, and in that sense alone, that truth is an indication of itself and of the false
(verum index sui et falsi).75 The idea of ‘substance’ allows us to reject as false the
idea that the human mind is a substance: that is, to denounce the idea of the human
mind as a thinking substance as a false image. The idea of ‘perfection’ allows us to
reject as false the idea of a God–Lawgiver. Accordingly, the only meaningful
question we could raise with respect to method is to know in what way we should
form ideas, that is, how we should conceive things – which we learn if we try to
conceive a given thing and its properties.
Again, from a Cartesian point of view most of this is profoundly absurd. If ideas
are ‘images’ (whether of material or of immaterial beings) there is always room for
asking whether what is represented by an idea is a ‘real’ object or, inversely, whether
the idea is a true picture of that object. If, however, an idea of an object is a way to
account for the properties of that object, the question of how we can be certain of the
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
162


truth of that idea does not even arise: ‘Certainty is nothing but an objective being
[that is, an idea, Th.V] as such or rather the way in which we are aware of [sentimus]
its formal being is certainty.’76 An idea is true if it does account for a thing and its
properties and it is known to be true if we realize that. Accordingly, certainty is not
a form of indirect knowledge (to the effect, for example, that this or that idea must
be true because we know that God cannot deceive us) but the awareness (feeling,
perception) of the formal being of an idea, that is, the awareness of the act of
conceiving something. To know, therefore, that my idea of a thing is true – that is,
to be certain that I have actually conceived a thing – is to realize that I actually
conceived that thing, that is, that I actually found a way to account for its properties.
It is all we have but it is also all we need.
This deals a serious blow to Cartesian metaphysics. For it means that in so far as
an idea is an idea – in so far as we actually conceive something – there is no reason,
and indeed no possibility, to subject it to doubt. And that means that there is no
reason and perhaps no possibility to engage in an attempt to provide a metaphysical
basis for science. According to Spinoza, doubt is caused ‘by another idea that is not
so clear and distinct that it could be the basis of a certain conclusion on whatever is
doubted’.77 The only reason why we doubt a given idea is a second idea, which being
contrary to, or incompatible with, the first makes us doubt the first without being
itself certain enough to form the basis for a conclusion (otherwise it would make the
first idea, not doubtful, but known to be false). So ‘doubt is nothing but a suspension
of the soul about an affirmation or a negation, which it would affirm or deny if there
were not something which, as long as it remains unknown, prevents knowledge of
that thing from being perfect’.78
Again, we should remember that ideas are acts of conceiving, not images. So what
Spinoza means is that, to the extent that we do not successfully conceive something,
it is possible to conceive it in a different way. Accordingly, the possibility of doubt
depends on the possibility of conceiving something differently from the way we do,
which possibility in turn depends on the imperfection of the idea (concept) with
which we started. In other words, to doubt something is simply the consequence of
having formed no more than an inadequate idea. Radical doubt, therefore, as a
deliberate attempt to eradicate from the mind all judgments, whether true or false,
without examining any of them in particular is not only impracticable but logically
impossible: ‘There is no doubt in the soul through the thing itself concerning which
one doubts.’79 It is not things or types of things that are doubtful but ideas of things
in so far as they do not successfully account for a thing and its properties.
Accordingly, Cartesian metaphysics as an attempt to build the foundations of
science on the ruins of doubt is as impossible as it is unnecessary. We can try to
conceive a particular thing or a particular class of things and conclude that, as yet,
we do not manage to form an adequate idea of that thing (that is, to successfully
account for the properties of that thing), which in turn may lead us to doubt our idea
of that thing. But we cannot doubt ideas we have not even started to form.
Accordingly, Descartes’ approach in the First Meditation, which consists in
doubting classes of ideas and beliefs on the ground that they have a ‘doubtful’ origin
(for example, the senses), rather than particular ideas (which according to Descartes
would be a work without end) does not result in any doubt at all but in an arbitrary
decision to look for metaphysical justification even where it is not wanted.80
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
163


Even so, the fate of Cartesian metaphysics more particularly depends on the
argument of the malicious demon, which presumably cannot be disposed of in the
same manner. Indeed, the force of the ‘malin génie’ argument is that we cannot
exclude its possibility – that it can be conceived and therefore that it could exist. In
the Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect, Spinoza deals with this problem
after explaining the nature of doubt:
And from that follows that we cannot call into doubt true ideas simply because there could
be some deceiving God, who belies us even in things most certain – unless we have no
clear and distinct idea at all. That is, if, when concentrating on the knowledge we have of
the origin of all things, we find nothing to convince us that he is not a deceiver – and that
by the same type of knowledge by which, when concentrating on the idea of a triangle, we
find that its angles are equal to two right ones. But if we do have the same knowledge of
God as we have of a triangle then all doubt is removed.81
So ultimately the argument of the God–deceiver would be refuted by concentrating
on the clear and distinct idea of God (‘the origin of all things’); inversely, the idea
of a God–deceiver would be possible only on the basis of an obscure and confused
idea of ‘God’. But that would mean that Spinoza’s position is the same as that of
Descartes, who also refutes the hypothesis of there being a malicious demon by
concentrating on the true idea of God and discovering that it is impossible for God
to be a deceiver.82 According to Spinoza, however, the argument does not mean that
we cannot form any true ideas before forming a true idea of God: indeed, we can
form the true idea of a triangle without having a true idea of God. So to form a true
idea of a particular thing there would be no need to form a true idea of God and that
means that we can form true and certain ideas without knowing that God cannot
deceive us. The only question that could bother us would be whether we can form a
true idea of ‘the origin of all things’ without knowing that God cannot deceive us:
that is, whether we can actually refute the idea of a deceiving God (by forming the
true idea of God) without knowing that there is no deceiving God. Spinoza’s answer
is that we can:
Just as we can arrive at such knowledge of a triangle even if we do not certainly know
whether a supreme deceiver [summus deceptor] deceives us we can also arrive at such
knowledge of God even if we do not certainly know whether there might be a supreme
deceiver. And once we have that that, as I said, is enough to remove all doubt concerning
clear and distinct ideas. Indeed, someone who proceeds correctly in his investigations and
investigates first the things that should be investigated first, and then linking them with
other things in an uninterrupted chain, and who knows how problems must be formulated
before going about to solve them, will have nothing but the most certain, that is, the most
clear and distinct ideas. For doubt is nothing but a suspension of the soul.83
So, just as we can refute the argument against motion simply by moving we can
refute the argument that we cannot form true and certain ideas (because there might
be a powerful deceiver) simply by forming true and certain ideas. And that suggests
that the whole question of a deceiving God is simply irrelevant. But perhaps we
should differentiate between the traditional idea of God, which given the fact that it
attributes to God an unlimited and arbitrary power makes room for his being a
deceiver, and Spinoza’s own idea of ‘God’ (the idea of ‘the origin of all things’) as
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
164


nature, which being fully intelligible and completely adequate presumably does not
give rise to any form of doubt. So the question would be whether we can form an
adequate idea of nature without knowing that there is no God–deceiver. According
to Spinoza we can, in the same way as we can form the idea of a triangle, that is, by
forming it and seeing that it is possible to form it. So if we examine things in the
right order and, one would say, start with ideas like that of the triangle or similar
simple things, there is no reason to be disturbed by the ‘idea’ (which is not actually
an idea) of a deceiving God: ‘Doubt always arises from investigating things without
order.’84
This is an interesting argument, not only because it allows us to suggest a sense
for Meyer’s remarks in his preface to the geometrical version of Descartes’
Principia but also because it suggests an order of knowledge which is really
different, not only from that of Descartes, but also from that of the Ethics. Indeed,
the argument suggests that, as long as we start with ‘small’ subjects and problems
and gradually work towards a more comprehensive view, without trying to lay a
metaphysical foundation first, we may be more successful than Descartes. After all,
‘the more the mind knows the better it understands its own powers and the order of
nature’.85 But that would also mean that Pt I of the Ethics is not meant as a
foundation of science but as an essay in – to quote Locke – ‘clearing the Ground a
little, and removing some of the Rubbish, that lies in the way to Knowledge’:86 a
dialectical exercise on the notion of God (meant to eliminate a notion of God that
stands in the way of knowledge) rather than a way of developing a concept of God.
Other versions of the same argument seem to confirm that.
The first occurs in the introduction (‘prolegomenon’) to the ‘geometrical’ version
of Descartes’ Principles, where Spinoza discusses Cartesian doubt. After explaining
Descartes’ reasons for doubt and the way in which those doubts are resolved,
Spinoza goes on to discuss what he calls a final difficulty: ‘Given the fact that the
existence of God is not known by itself it seems that we can never be certain of
anything and that we will never know that God exists; for one cannot conclude
anything from uncertain premises (and we have said that everything is uncertain as
long as we do not know our origin).’87 The argument, which Spinoza takes from the
Second Objections (written by Mersenne), is a variant of the argument known as ‘the
Cartesian circle’.88 It is based on the idea that the certainty of a conclusion cannot
surpass that of the premises on which it is based. So, if we must prove the existence
of God (if God’s existence is not self-evident), the arguments we use must be better
known and more certain than the existence of God and in no case for their truth and
certainty depend on knowledge of God. Descartes’ own answer, as rendered by
Spinoza, is that it is impossible to doubt a clear and distinct idea in the presence of
that idea but that if all we have is the memory of that idea the hypothesis of an evil
demon destroys our certainty in so far as it is based on that memory: ‘As a result,
although the fact that God exists can be known, not by itself but only by something
else, we shall still be able to arrive at the certain knowledge of God’s existence as
long as we carefully attend to the premises from which we conclude that
existence.’89
According to Spinoza, some people do not find this a satisfactory solution, so he
proposes one of his own.90 He agrees with Descartes that a clear and distinct idea
cannot be doubted in the presence of that idea. He also agrees that the notion of a
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
165


deceiving God could be a motive for calling into question the certainty of
mathematics (meaning presumably, not this or that particular theorem of which we
have a clear and distinct idea, but mathematics in general). His solution is that we
should form an idea of God such that it becomes as impossible to believe that God
can deceive us as to think that the angles of a triangle are not equal to two right ones
once we have formed a clear and distinct idea of a triangle. He concludes rather
surprisingly: ‘Given these arguments I answer: “the reason why we cannot be certain
of anything is not that we do not know the existence of God (for of this matter I have
not even spoken) but that we do not have a clear and distinct idea of him.”’91 So as
far as the certainty of knowledge is concerned, the notion of God’s existence would
be irrelevant, the only relevant notion being that of God’s nature, of which we
should form a clear and distinct idea. Again, there is a possible rejoinder:
If someone wishes to argue with me his argument should be this: ‘We cannot be certain of
anything before we have a clear and distinct idea of God; however, we cannot have a clear
and distinct idea of God as long as we do not know whether the author of our nature
deceives us. Ergo, we cannot be certain of anything as long as we do not know whether
the author of our nature deceives us, etc. To which I reply by conceding the major and by
denying the minor. For we do have a clear and distinct idea of a triangle although we do
not know whether the author of our nature deceives us; and provided we have a similar
idea of God […] we will be unable to doubt either his existence or any truth of
mathematics.92
If we take Spinoza’s argument in its totality it is in its first stage confusing, not only
because the essential questions – whether or not God’s existence is self-evident;
whether or not it can be demonstrated – are not addressed at all, but also because,
after all, his solution seems to be more or less the same as that of Descartes. Above
all, he ignores the fact that the reason why the question of God’s existence was
raised in the first place was an argument of Mersenne about the possibility for the
atheist to know something with certainty, a possibility that could be called into
question by Descartes’ procedure in the Meditations. However, according to
Mersenne, ‘an atheist is clearly and distinctly aware that the three angles of a triangle
are equal to two right angles’.93 By claiming therefore that God’s existence is
irrelevant in this context Spinoza suggests that it is possible for an atheist to have
true and certain knowledge as long as he has a clear and distinct idea of ‘the origin
of all things’, a suggestion that certainly makes sense in the context of Spinoza’s
own philosophy. The question would remain whether we can form a clear and
distinct idea of ‘the origin of all things’ without knowing that God cannot deceive
us.
In the second stage of the argument, when addressing the rejoinder, Spinoza
assures us that he accepts the major of the counter argument, so apparently he agrees
with the claim that we ‘cannot be certain of anything before we have a clear and
distinct idea of God’. But he rejects the minor (‘we cannot have a clear and distinct
idea of God as long as we do not know whether the author of our nature deceives
us’), so his claim would be that we can have a clear and distinct idea of God even if
we do not know whether or not God is a deceiver because ‘we also have a clear and
distinct idea of a triangle even if we do not know whether the author of our nature
deceives us’. But of course that looks like begging the question, which was after all
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
166


whether we can be certain of any idea (including that of a triangle) without knowing
that God cannot deceive us.
The only way to make sense of this is to assume that to form the idea of the origin
of all things we should proceed in the same way as when we form the idea of a
triangle, which becomes certain simply by being formed. So, just as we should try
to form the idea of a triangle (and realize that that is the only way to account for the
properties of triangles), we should try to form the idea of the origin of all things (and
realize that that is the only way to account for the existence and properties of all
things). Still that does not account for Spinoza’s agreement with the major, which
holds that ‘we cannot be certain of anything before we have a clear and distinct idea
of God’. So, apparently, a clear and distinct idea of the ‘origin of all things’ is
necessary if we are to have any certain idea at all.
Finally, another, more elaborate and complex but in a way also more interesting,
version of the argument occurs in the Theologico-political treatise:
Since God’s existence is not self-evident, it must necessarily be inferred from notions so
firm and unshaken that there can neither be nor be conceived any power that could change
them. At any rate, once we have inferred from them God’s existence, they must present
themselves to us in that way if we wish to conclude God’s existence from them in a way
free from all doubt. For if we could conceive that these notions themselves can be
impugned by any power, of whatever kind it be, then we should doubt their truth and
consequently the conclusion following therefrom, namely, God’s existence; and so we
could never be certain of anything. Further we know that something agrees with or
contravenes Nature only when we can prove that it agrees with or contravenes those
principles. Therefore, if we could conceive that in Nature something could be produced by
some power, of whatever kind it be, to contravene nature, it would contravene those first
notions. So it must be rejected as absurd, or else […] these first notions and consequently
God and all our beliefs of every kind must be called into doubt.94
Although the primary object of the argument is miracles, its meaning and impact are
general. In any case its main premise is exactly the same as in the other versions:
God’s existence must be proved, for which we need notions (notionibus) that must
not be less certain than God’s existence, otherwise the existence of God becomes
uncertain. But from then on the wording is slightly but significantly different.
Indeed, the truth of those notions (the notions used to prove the existence of God)
must be such ‘that no power can exist or be conceived by which they could be
changed’, the surprising words being ‘power’ and ‘change’ (immutari), which
presumably we must interpret as ‘power to undo’. So the question here concerns not
so much the certainty of the premises as what power could make those premises
uncertain; indeed, no power must be able to undo the truth and certainty of the
notions by which we prove the existence of God. Suppose, accordingly, that to prove
the existence of God we need principles p, q, r; then we must not allow for the
possibility of something (‘a power’) that could undo or render uncertain p, q or r.
Indeed, that possibility would make uncertain, not only p, q and r but also whatever
we prove by their means, that is, God. Now the fact that in this context we are
dealing, not with abstract hypotheses, but with ‘miracles’ makes it easier to form a
concrete picture of what Spinoza has in mind. His point, indeed, is that anything that
forms an exception to some given rule or principle undermines the validity of that
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
167


rule or principle. Accordingly, suppose we use, say, the principle of causality to
prove the existence of God; then we must not allow anything to be an exception to
that principle, for that would make uncertain, not only the principle of causality, but
also whatever we proved by means of that principle, including the existence of God.
Accordingly, by using the principle of causality to prove the existence of a God who
is himself an exception to that principle (as he is according to many people), we not
only undermine the validity of the principle of causality but also the certainty of the
existence of that God, so traditional proofs of the existence of a traditional God
would be self-defeating.
In the second half of the argument Spinoza switches from God to nature. It now
emerges that the principles mentioned earlier are also constitutive of nature and of
the science of nature. So something does or does not belong to nature (or to the
science of nature) only to the extent that it is or is not in accordance with those
principles. Since, on the other hand, those principles are the same as we use to prove
the existence of God, the idea that God could be an exception to those principles
undermines, not only our knowledge of God, but also our knowledge of nature.
Accordingly, the certainty of those principles as well as their utility for the science
of nature depend on their absolute universality: if on any level there were an
exception to those principles they would become invalid and we should conclude
that nothing can ever be known with certainty. Consequently, either we use those
principles and then the God we prove by their means must not be an exception to
them, or we do not use them (or accept that they are not universal) but then we know
nothing at all. This is further explained in a note on this very passage:
We doubt the existence of God, and consequently everything else, as long as we do not
have a clear and distinct idea of God but only a confused idea. No more than someone who
does not exactly know the nature of a triangle knows that its three angles are equal to two
right ones, someone who has a confused conception of God’s nature can see that it belongs
to the nature of God to exist. But in order to conceive God’s nature clearly and distinctly
we have to fix our attention on certain very simple notions ordinarily called ‘common’ and
link [concatenemus] them with those things which belong to God’s nature. Only then does
it become clear to us that God necessarily exists and is everywhere, and then it also
appears that whatever we have conceived involves the nature of God and finally that
whatever we have conceived adequately is true. But on this, see the introduction to the
book called Principles of philosophy geometrically demonstrated.95
So Spinoza admits that we should have a clear and distinct conception of God if we
are to achieve certainty at all levels, but the implication is not only that that idea of
God must be based on ‘common notions’, but also that, by forming an idea of God
in accordance with those notions, we confirm those very notions. Now those
common notions, presumably, are the same as the principles mentioned earlier.
What Spinoza means therefore is that to determine the nature of God we should use
the principles we also use in the science of nature because only in that way can the
idea of God become clear and distinct – which in turn is necessary because otherwise
both the existence of God and the science of nature would become uncertain.
However, those principles are also constitutive of nature. Accordingly, the use of
those principles with respect to God entails either that God is identical with nature
or that he is a part of nature. Inversely, as long as we suppose that God is not
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
168


identical with nature (or a part of nature) we undermine, not only the certainty of his
existence, but also the certainty of natural science.
Of course, the traditional reply would be to make a distinction between ‘above
nature’ and ‘against nature’. But Spinoza rejects that distinction:
Nor do I admit any difference between a work contrary to nature and a supernatural work
(that is, according to some, a work that does not contravene nature but nevertheless cannot
be produced or brought about by nature). For since a miracle occurs not externally to
nature but within nature, even though it be claimed to be supernatural, yet it must
necessarily interrupt nature’s order which otherwise we would conceive as fixed and
immutable by God’s decrees. So if there were to occur in nature anything that did not
follow from its laws, this would necessarily be opposed to the order which God maintains
eternally in nature through its universal laws. So this would be contrary to nature and
nature’s laws and consequently such a belief would cast doubt on everything and lead to
atheism.96
The belief that something could be ‘above nature’ has the same destructive effect as
the belief that something could be ‘against nature’, for both mean that we accept that
something inconceivable could exist. So, either we conceive God and on the basis of
that concept prove his existence but then the only viable solution is to make him
coincide with nature, or we allow God to be an exception to the principles of nature
and then all knowledge, whether of God or of nature, becomes radically impossible.
This shows that Spinoza’s argument is a clever reformulation of Descartes’
hypothesis of a powerful deceiver, except that this time the deceiver is not a
mysterious demon but the God of tradition: as long as we suppose that there could
be a God having the power to undo the principles of nature, the knowledge of nature
and indeed all knowledge is radically uncertain.
If we ask, again, to what extent certainty requires a metaphysical basis, the answer
must be extremely complex. For on the one hand we can use common notions only
fruitfully if we assume that they are universal (and that means that to the extent that
we use them at all we must not assume anything that forms an exception to them)
whereas, on the other hand, the very notion of a God being ‘above’ those principles
undermines the notion of science and for that reason should be refuted (assuming of
course that the notion of a God ‘above nature’ is prior in time to the notion of
nature). However, that notion of God can be refuted only by the consistent
application of those principles. Accordingly, the use of principles like causality is
self-validating: the more we use them, the more we understand that reality is in
accordance with them, the alternative being complete scepticism. This suggests two
things: anything could be the object of a concept (nothing is excluded from scientific
investigation a priori); and ideas are true, not primarily because they are ‘clear and
distinct’, but only in so far as they account for the known properties of a thing.
‘Clear and Distinct’
If ideas are acts, the expression ‘clear and distinct’ used in connection with ideas
becomes adverbial – except of course in so far as ideas are not ideas but images.97
This seems of small consequence but it means that it is in virtue not of the quality of
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
169


its contents that an idea can be called ‘clear and distinct’ but of the way in which it
explains the properties of the ideatum. That would mean not only that ‘clear and
distinct’ is no longer a criterion of truth (for ideas of the imagination can be as clear
and distinct as anything) but also that it can no longer be used as a criterion of
demarcation. So there would be no object which we should not try to conceive
‘clearly and distinctly’, including our own passions and the so-called mysteries of
the faith.98
In the Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect, we may distinguish – at first
sight at least – two different uses, which provisionally I call static and dynamic.
Spinoza uses ‘clear and distinct’ in a static sense when speaking of the ideas of
simple things: ‘The idea of the most simple thing must be clear and distinct for it
must make itself known, not partially, but totally (or indeed not at all).’99 Ideas of
simple things are privileged because ‘whatever they contain in the manner of
affirmation equals [adaequat] whatever is conceived in them, without extending
beyond it’.100 So whatever is affirmed to be the case with respect to something
simple is necessarily also contained in the idea we have formed of that thing.
However, even a simple thing is not simply perceived but understood (intelligi).101
So ‘simple things’ would not be simple sensations (like ‘red’, ‘warm’, and so on)
but, presumably, simple concepts (like ‘point’, ‘line’, ‘local motion’, and so on).
These are true and known to be true because, given their simplicity, if we conceive
them at all, we conceive them adequately, that is, we perfectly and completely
account for all their properties.102 By contrast, the material used and produced by the
imagination is not simple at all but complex and confused: ‘Fiction cannot be
simple; indeed, it comes from compounding various confused ideas which relate to
different things and actions occurring in nature.’103 As a result, false and fictitious
ideas can be avoided by analysis: ‘If a thing composed of many [parts] is divided by
thought into all its most simple [parts] and if it carefully attends to each and any of
them separately, all confusion will disappear.’104 So the use of ‘clear and distinct’ in
this context is not as ‘static’ as it first seemed; in fact, it is the first stage of concept
formation.
A more dynamic use of ‘clear and distinct’ belongs to the second part of the
method, which is concerned with the method of forming (complex) ideas and of
connecting them together so as to form an ‘objective’ reproduction of nature.105
Ideas are produced clearly and distinctly if they are formed, ‘not by the fortuitous
motions of the body’, but ‘by a pure mind’.106 The result is a definition or something
deduced from an ‘affirmative essence’, that is, again, from a clear and distinct
definition: ‘we must never infer anything from abstractions […] but the best
conclusion will have to be drawn from a particular affirmative essence, that is, from
a true and legitimate definition […] so the right way of discovery is to form thoughts
on the basis of some given definition’.107 Definitions fill the gap between universal
axioms (axioms, presumably, like that ‘everything has a cause’) and the world of
particulars: ‘From universal axioms alone the intellect cannot descend to singulars
because axioms have an infinite extension and do not determine the intellect to the
contemplation of one singular thing rather than another.’108 Accordingly, the
necessity to form definitions directs the intellect to a set of properties for which it
must account in a way consistent with ‘universal axioms’. This can be done by
providing a dynamic account of the way in which the properties of a thing could be
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
170


produced. Thus the ‘clear and distinct’ idea (‘idea in so far as it is an idea’) of a
circle is not a clear and distinct representation of a circle, which would count as an
‘image’ rather than an ‘idea’, but the concept of a circle as it is formed clearly and
distinctly, that is, by thinking the motion of a line, one of whose extremities remains
fixed in a single point.109 So we conceive (‘define’) a mathematical figure, not by
making our image of it as clear and distinct as possible, but by devising a dynamic
structure such that it is able to produce the properties commonly ascribed to a circle:
once we have identified a process by which all properties of any circle are produced
we have understood the nature of a circle and formed an adequate concept of it.
This is not limited to mathematical figures and other entia rationis.110 The clear
and distinct idea (‘definition’) of a real object should also allow us to understand
how its properties are produced; or, inversely, we have understood a thing if we see
a way of producing its properties, as in Cartesian medicine we understand, say,
respiration if we imagine a machine producing the same regular motion we observe
in animals. So, again, what Spinoza demands is that we form a concept such that the
properties of the thing to be ‘defined’ are accounted for. But there is a complication.
Things can be either ‘created’ or ‘uncreated’, so there are also two types of
‘definitions’. Definitions of uncreated things must be such that any external cause is
eliminated; they must make it evident that the object exists; and they must not
contain adjectives turned into substantives.111 Definitions of created things must
include a specification of their proximate cause (causa proxima).112 Let us briefly
review these requirements for, if ‘definitions’ are nothing but clear and distinct ideas
of complex things, their analysis may clarify what it is for an idea to be ‘clear and
distinct’.
The first requirement of definitions of uncreated things simply follows from the
notion of ‘uncreated’. If a thing is uncreated it has no external cause; it is causa sui.
Once we have understood that notion, we see that it is possible for an infinite being
(a being with an infinite ‘reality’) to exist; that is, to actually cause its own existence.
The second requirement follows from the first, for if the idea of causa sui is an idea
at all, that is, if causa sui can be actually conceived, causa sui exists necessarily.113
The third requirement is, I suppose, purely polemical in so far as it excludes
‘definitions’ of ‘God’ as the Good, the True and so on – indeed, ‘good’ and ‘true’
are not perfections, the only ‘perfection’ being ‘reality’.114 So to conceive an infinite
being is to conclude that an infinite being necessarily exists and that whatever may
be attributed to that being follows from its necessary existence. Accordingly, the aim
of the definition of ‘infinite being’ is not so much to account for a set of given
properties as to lay down a necessary principle.
The one specific requirement of definitions of created things is that they must
specify the proximate cause of the thing defined. It is by no means clear what that
means.115 Proximate cause sometimes means ‘natural cause’. Thus Spinoza urges
Descartes to explain the union of body and mind per proximam suam causam; that
is, presumably, without having recourse to divine intervention.116 Similarly,
Scripture is said not to explain things per causas proximas.117 Sometimes ‘proximate
cause’ simply means ‘subject’. Thus the mind is the ‘proximate cause’ of its acts,
meaning presumably that substance (in the Spinozist sense) is not the subject of a
mental act even if it is the ultimate cause (given the fact that it is the ultimate cause
of everything).118 Whatever follows from human nature ‘must be understood by
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
171


means of human nature as its proximate cause’, meaning, presumably, that although
human nature is not a substance we can use that notion to explain much of what we
see in humans.119 But this does not shed any light on the use of that term in the
Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect, where ‘proximate cause’ is used mainly
to express the contrast between finite and infinite being; that is, between caused and
uncaused being:
A thing is conceived [concipitur] either by means of its essence alone or by means of its
proximate cause. For if a thing is in itself, or, as it is put ordinarily, if it is causa sui, then
it must be understood [intelligi] by its essence alone; but if it is not in itself but requires a
cause to exist, then it must be understood by its proximate cause. Indeed if we know an
effect, this is nothing but a more perfect knowledge of a cause. Which is also the reason
why, as far as the investigation of things is concerned, we must never conclude something
from abstract concepts and be on our guard not to mix things which are only in our intellect
with real things.120
An infinite thing is conceived by means of (a concept of) its essence, which, since it
is an infinite being, also explains its existence (existentia), whereas the existence of
a finite thing must be explained by something external to it, which is called its
proximate cause. That would suggest that the concept of ‘proximate cause’ is
included in the definition of a finite thing only to account for the existence of that
thing. But, apart from the fact that that would be very unusual (for it would mean
that we need a different definition for every single thing), it would also mean that no
finite thing can be fully defined (for the proximate cause of a thing has itself a
proximate cause, which in turn … and so on).121 Moreover, Spinoza is hardly
interested in what he calls changeable particulars: ‘It would be impossible for
human weakness to pursue the whole chain of changeable particular things.’ Their
observation yields ‘extrinsic properties, relations, or at best circumstances’.122
Finally, Spinoza stipulates that the ‘intimate essences’ of things (which are
presumably the true object of their definition) must be taken from ‘fixed and eternal
things and the laws inscribed in those things […] according to which all singular
things are made and ordered; for these changeable particulars depend from those
eternal things in such an intimate and essential manner that without them they can
neither be nor be conceived’.123 So to conceive finite things we should not bother
about their existence or about the proximate cause of their existence but about
‘eternal things and laws’, which in turn provide the intimate essences of things.
Whatever the Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect further says on this
subject is even more sketchy and less satisfactory. Eternal things are said to be
particulars that function as universals (universalia) because in relation to changeable
particulars they are ‘the kinds of their definitions [genera definitionum] and form
their proximate causes’.124 So universals are particulars used universally, or rather
universal ideas are ideas of particulars used universally. Furthermore, they are also
the genera definitionum, so to define a thing per genus et differentiam specificam we
need ‘eternal things’ to provide the genus to which the defined thing belongs.
Finally, they are the proximate causes of finite things, which if it must not mean the
proximate cause of their existence, would mean ‘the proximate cause of their
essence’. So presumably the relation between universals and particulars is the same
as that between ‘a substance conceived under a certain attribute’ and the various
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
172


things that can be seen as modi of that substance. Thus, for example, the genus of
the definition of a material body would be ‘extension’, whereas its specific
difference should be sought in a particular configuration of extension. ‘Extension’,
on the other hand, or rather the idea of extension, would not be an abstraction but a
particular idea used universally.
In any case, definitions (complex ideas of real or possible things) are explanatory
concepts, formed with ‘ideas of simple things’, which are used to account for the
observed properties of a thing. If those definitions are ‘concatenated and ordered
[…] our mind objectively represents nature as it is [formalitas naturæ]’.125 So by
conceiving the origin of all things we provide our ideas with unity and coherence
and therefore ultimate certainty; it is in that sense that the concept of God ‘is also
the cause of all our ideas and our mind’.126 To acquire true knowledge, therefore,
there is only one way to proceed, namely, by forming adequate ideas of particular
things, that is, by finding a way to account for their properties by constructing a
mechanical model. Such a model is intrinsically certain, simply because we see that
it does account for the properties we wish to explain. And if we do that in a
consistent way we will eventually understand that all reality can be understood in the
same way. So true method would start with physics, continue presumably with the
application of physics to psychological problems, and end with a completely
deterministic view of the universe. The role of metaphysics, on the other hand,
would not be to lay the foundations of science, but to remove whatever theological
obstacles are put in the way of science.
Conclusion
Much remains unclear in Spinoza’s epistemology. So much is clear: its main point
is to make the systematic and empirical investigation of nature possible and to turn
metaphysics into a dialectical exercise, directed against theology and Cartesian
ontology and metaphysics. Still, obviously, Spinoza’s relation to Descartes is
extremely complex, not only because in the highly polarized world of ideas
Cartesians represented a ‘progressive’ force, but also because the research method
proposed by Spinoza has much similarity with the one proposed by Descartes in the
fields of, for example, medicine and optics.127 Moreover, it does not look as if
Spinoza himself has a clear epistemological model, moving as it does uneasily
between a sharp distinction between the imagination and the intellect, as presented
in the Treatise on the Improvement of the Intellect, and a more inclusive view
according to which the intellect develops from the imagination, as developed in the
Ethics. Finally, Spinoza’s general framework does not really help him to solve the
problems raised by his epistemological stance; indeed, from that point of view he
would have been served better by a dualistic model of Platonic, Averroistic or even
