Cartesian origin.
It seems likely, but it is beyond the scope of this book to develop this hypothesis
any further, that at a certain point in his evolution Spinoza has tried to replace this
metaphysical dualism with a more dynamic dualism he could also find in Descartes,
namely, in his theory of the passions. A Cartesian ‘passion’ is the perception of a
bodily process (which in turn is seen as the ‘action’).128 From Spinoza’s point of
The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
173


view, this is an attractive model also for ideas in general, because it would allow him
to account, not only for ideas as such without committing himself to metaphysical
dualism, but also for the activity inherent to ideas, without ascribing any freedom to
the mind. For just as there is a distinction between passions which more or less
activate the subject, that is, contribute to the reality or perfection of the body, and
other passions (properly called ‘passions’) which do not, ‘ideas’ (in the broad sense)
could be divided into ideas of the intellect or concepts (‘ideas in so far as they are
ideas’), which activate the subject, and ideas of the imagination (‘ideas in so far as
they are not ideas’), by which it is simply subject to the body, that is, to the rest of
nature. What stands in Spinoza’s way, in fact, is his twofold notion of a ‘divine
intellect’, on the one hand as something completely different from the human
intellect (as which, presumably, is no more than the symbolic expression of the
intelligibility of nature), and on the other as the totality of all ideas (in the
psychological sense); that is, as some sort of world-soul in which all human minds
participate.

distinction between imagination and pure understanding, which undermines my
claim (Meditationes, VI, AT, VII, 72/CSM, II, 50). But it does not make Descartes
change his theory of judgment, which in my view depends on the notion of ideas as
images.

See above, p. 160. The transition from the first to the second requirement illustrates
what Spinoza means by combining ‘common notions’ with divine attributes
(‘uncreated’): the only way to conceive an uncreated being is to conceive something
that causes its own being. However, if something has so much reality that it has the
power to cause itself to exist, it also has the power to give itself all the attributes of
which an idea can be formed. Accordingly, something causing itself to exist necessarily
has an infinite number of attributes (which shows that a substance, which necessarily
causes itself to exist, has by definition as many attributes as can be thought and
therefore that the idea of a substance with only one attribute is incoherent if we think
of ‘substance’ as something infinitely perfect).

The Freedom to Philosophize: Descartes and Spinoza
179


Epilogue
Although the reading I propose of the Theologico-political treatise makes sense, I
believe, not only in itself, but also in relation to Spinoza’s general philosophy, I also
strongly believe that any interpreter of Spinoza is bound to be left with some
unsolved problems. This raises difficult issues concerning the unity and evolution of
Spinoza’s philosophy that I cannot address here. In this epilogue, though, I will
restate some of my points and put them into perspective and briefly comment on
some of the problems I did not solve.
I started out with three claims: (a) the primary aim of the Theologico-political
treatise is to vindicate Spinoza’s philosophy rather than defend toleration in general;
(b) there is a significant relationship between the Theologico-political treatise and
Hobbes’s Leviathan, in the sense that the one could be seen as a reaction to or a
commentary on the other; and (c) as part of his defence, Spinoza is concerned with
‘redefining’ concepts like ‘religion’, ‘piety’ and ‘faith’ in such a way that they
become compatible with what is, according to the criteria of the 17th century at any
rate, an atheist philosophy, that is, a philosophy which denies God’s legislative will.
All this involves a discussion of revealed religion in general and Christian religion
in particular, which is possible only if certain fundamental distinctions made by
Cartesian philosophy are disregarded.
I think further analysis confirms my point that the Theologico-political treatise is
concerned with vindicating Spinoza’s own philosophy rather than propagating
‘toleration’ in general. All he is arguing is that, from the point of view of a
sovereign, there is no essential difference between ‘revealed’ religion and an
allegedly ‘impious’ philosophy like his own; indeed, if there is any at all it figures
as an argument of the defence, given the fact that his philosophy entails the most
perfect obedience to the sovereign. Toleration, on the other hand, in the more or less
ecumenical sense adopted nowadays, is not a political aim. Indeed, the term is best
avoided and replaced by ‘peace’, if only because that would make it understandable
why in fact Spinoza finds a certain amount of intolerance and even repression
unavoidable. Moreover, whereas modern ideals of tolerance are generally inspired
by some form of scepticism, Spinoza’s plea is animated by a strong conception of
truth. If, on the other hand, he believes a variety of opinions should be ‘tolerated’
without truth being imposed, it is because there is a practical limit to what can be
achieved by political means. It is not because there is a chance that some of those
other opinions are true. So, to put it briefly, nothing more, nor less, should be read
into the subtitle of the Treatise than what it normally means: the freedom to
philosophize, that is, the freedom to know the truth – or, alternatively, the freedom
to develop and embrace Spinoza’s philosophy – is compatible with peace (does not
threaten the public order) and is an indispensable contribution to peace.
If, on the other hand, the aim of a civil society is peace, if peace is the highest
conceivable ‘moral’ value, the only ‘duty’ of the sovereign is to create the conditions
under which human beings, who are naturally antagonistic, can live peacefully
181


together. A sovereign would not be necessary if all people were rational, or if all
people were inspired by a common devotion to a God–Lawgiver. The first is
intrinsically impossible, given the fact that the emergence of reason depends on
factors that are not universally given. The second ideal, however, is projected on the
Old Testament period of the judges, between the death of Moses and the advent of
Saul. The comparison of that period with the first period after the flight from Egypt
shows that to be successful this system requires a written law, known and revered as
the ‘will of God’, which is detailed enough to give guidance and simple enough not
to require interpretation. However, given the fact that the Christian religion does not
have the form of a law and is a system of opinions for which truth and certainty are
claimed, that ideal has become nostalgic; to revive it would be to destroy the
commonwealth.
Like Hobbes, indeed, Spinoza believes that Christianity is naturally divisive, a
judgment which becomes understandable if one realizes that most of the civil wars
had a religious background and that modern Christianity is to a large extent a fruit
of the Enlightenment. Although Spinoza strongly believes that human nature makes
a sovereign generally indispensable (except under the exceptional circumstances
under the judges) he does not believe (unlike Hobbes) that the problems created by
the Christian religion can be solved by political means. Indeed, he seems to embrace
only half of Hobbes’s solution – ‘seems’, for he does condone, in the Political
treatise at any rate, some national ecclesiastical structure in the context of
aristocratic government. In any case, presumably, there will always remain an
important role for persuasion, that is, for philosophy, given the fact that all problems
cannot be solved by having recourse to political authority.
I think I also made my point with respect to the relation between Spinoza and
Hobbes. Undoubtedly, there were other influences, and in any case I do not suggest
that Spinoza mechanically copied Hobbes. Indeed, there are several points on which
he clearly disagrees with his great contemporary. Above all, he draws on a
metaphysical system which allows him to reduce right to power. A more accurate
comparison between the Theologico-political treatise and Leviathan would be
necessary, on the other hand, to assess Spinoza’s dependence on Hobbes’s
theological argument and to dispel once and for all the myth of Spinoza being a
pioneer in Biblical scholarship. Indeed, he had a brilliant predecessor in Hobbes,
whose clearness and dialectical force in handling theological problems compare
favourably with the clouds of dust sometimes raised by Spinoza.
The relation with Hobbes, though, raises another problem, namely, to what extent
problems of political philosophy were forced upon Spinoza. I am not aware of any
explicit political dimension either in the Short treatise or in the Treatise on the
Improvement of the Intellect, and although there certainly is a political dimension in
Pt IV of the Ethics it is of limited significance. Nor is it known whether it has always
been there; indeed, the fact that it eventually appeared there may be due to the work
on the Theologico-political treatise.1 In any case a concern with political problems
in the specific sense of the word seems to be foreign to Spinoza’s general
programme, given the fact that, according to the Treatise on the Improvement of the
Intellect, the aim of philosophy is to achieve lasting happiness under any
circumstances; that is, one would say, regardless of the particular political system in
place. The Political treatise, on the other hand, presupposes in a very specific way
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
182


Spinoza’s metaphysics, especially the idea that God is power. Moreover, it is likely
that the excommunication (1656) was based on Spinoza’s rejection of the Law of
Moses as a specifically religious law, which suggests that even in those years he
thought of religion as an essentially political phenomenon. But a political
interpretation of religion does not necessarily require a political theory. Elements of
such a theory are undoubtedly present in the Theologico-political treatise, but they
seem to be limited to chap. 16. Moreover, the relaxed way in which Spinoza refers
to all and any political systems as basically ‘democratic’, the indecisiveness of the
conclusions he draws from the history of the Jews and the general lack of
equilibrium between the theological and the political parts of the Treatise suggest
that Spinoza’s political consciousness was still in an initial stage.
My third claim concerned the way Spinoza deals with concepts like ‘piety’,
‘prophecy’, ‘religion’, ‘faith’ and ‘revelation’. Spinoza’s use of all these concepts, I
suggested, should be interpreted in a ‘dialectical’ fashion. On the whole I think I
showed that that makes sense, especially in the case of ‘piety’ and ‘faith’. I also
think it is an essential part of Spinoza’s defence of his philosophy; indeed, it is only
by reducing religion and piety to a specific type of behaviour and faith to whatever
motivates such behaviour that Spinoza can show the fundamental but only practical
equivalence of philosophy and faith. The fact that the object of philosophy could
also be called ‘God’ is of no theoretical importance – on the contrary, it expresses
no more than a practical equivalence. Indeed, the term ‘God’ does not refer to a
particular being but to any being whose contemplation is morally significant.
It is with his notion of ‘God’, though, that Spinoza crosses the line that separates
dialectical argument and linguistic innovation. To argue that, because ‘piety’ is
defined as ‘habitual moral behaviour’, true and certain philosophy is compatible
with piety and indeed constitutes a condition of piety is part of a dialectical
argument, given the fact that it is based on a ‘common’ definition. Indeed, it is by
adhering rigorously to the stipulated use of those words that Spinoza arrives at his
most striking conclusions. To argue, on the other hand, that we may call ‘nature’
‘God’ because anything whose idea produces moral behaviour may be called ‘God’
is a linguistic innovation meant to conceal a real difference. On a speculative level,
indeed, no compromise is possible between philosophy and theology, not even with
respect to apparently neutral questions which do not prejudge the nature of God, like
that of the existence of God. That, moreover, this impossibility of compromise is not
a matter of indifference is another proof that the notion of ‘tolerance’ is profoundly
misleading. Indeed, as I showed in my last chapter, even the smallest chance of there
being a ‘God’ in the classical sense of that word would destroy whatever certainty
we can have in science and philosophy. Accordingly, that notion can be ‘tolerated’
only to the precise extent that it produces ‘moral’ behaviour. Still, in so far as
traditional theologians reason at all, that is, in so far as they use ‘common notions’
(like ‘causality’), it is possible to engage in a dialectical dispute with them, the
outcome of which would be, ideally, that they admit that ‘God’ cannot be
distinguished from nature – as in the Ethics (especially Pt I) or the Metaphysical
thoughts.
It is also with Spinoza’s notion of ‘God’ that we enter the domain of the unsolved
problems. For in Pt V of the Ethics, as well as the Short treatise, Spinoza refers to
God as an object of ‘intellectual’ love. Since this notion hardly plays any role in the
Epilogue
183


Theologico-political treatise – except I suppose in one brief passage2 – it is not my
purpose to deal with it here. Still, I cannot entirely avoid it, not only because this
seems to introduce a different notion of ‘God’, but also because, in the Short treatise
at any rate, it seems to be connected with a different theory of action.
On the whole, the theory of action used in the Theologico-political treatise is
rationalistic: if we understand that peace is better than war we will inevitably try to
establish peace; if we understand that it is in our interest not to kill or to steal we will
automatically refrain from stealing and killing. Inversely, the only reason why
people do not invariably strive for peace is that they do not know the advantages of
it. Spinoza deduces this from what he calls the universal law of human behaviour,
according to which ‘everyone will choose of two goods that which he judges the
greater, and of two evils that which seems to him the lesser’.3 So if I can do either p
or q but believe that p is ‘better’ than q, I will necessarily act in accordance with p,
and vice versa. Of course, the problem can be made more complex by introducing a
time scale as well as different degrees of certainty about the result of the proposed
act. If p is seen as better than q, q could still be preferred to p, either because q is
more certain than p, or because the fruit of q is less remote than that of p, other things
being equal. Still, on the whole, Spinoza suggests that knowledge and belief are
enough and therefore that true knowledge and true belief are sufficient. If we believe
that p is better than q we will choose p; if we certainly know that p is better than q
we will certainly choose p. Accordingly, if p is objectively better than q, no more
would be required to make us act in accordance with p than the certain knowledge
that p is the best we can do.
According to the Short treatise, however, and presumably also according to Pt V
of the Ethics, true beliefs are not enough. They may be enough to perform individual
‘moral’ acts and are presumably enough to create and sustain a civil society, but they
are not enough to achieve what is called virtue: ‘Blessedness is not the reward of
virtue but virtue itself; nor do we enjoy it because we restrain our lusts; on the
contrary because we enjoy it we are able to restrain them.’4 And in the Short treatise
Spinoza makes virtue depend on the possibility of the soul to unite with its object –
hence presumably the term ‘love’.5 Although I am not sure that it is possible to
provide a satisfactory interpretation of those doctrines at all (‘satisfactory’ in the
sense, not only that one understands what they mean, which is difficult enough, but
also that one sees how they fit in with the rest of Spinoza’s philosophy), a few
remarks are in order.
First of all, ‘virtue’ does not seem to be ‘moral virtue’ in our sense, or what in
other parts of the Ethics and in the Theologico-political treatise Spinoza calls ‘piety’
– that is, the habit of performing ‘moral’ acts (these being the acts the sovereign
demands from us). Indeed, although one could have some doubt with respect to the
Short treatise, the theory proposed in Pt V of the Ethics does not seem to be a moral
theory in the ordinary sense (as a theory of moral actions) at all. It is ‘ethics’ in the
more traditional sense as a ‘doctrine of happiness’ and in the even more traditional
sense as a ‘theory on the control of the passions’. Spinoza’s professed aim in
proposing it is not peace but individual happiness, more particularly a happiness that
is completely autonomous and independent of a particular environment. Indeed,
although it is hardly conceivable that someone who grew up in the state of nature
could ever reach this stage of intellectual perfection, it is perfectly conceivable that
Spinoza’s Theologico-political Treatise
184


someone who would have the required intellectual perfection actually reacts in this
way to a situation comparable with the state of nature (for example, a civil war).
Secondly, with respect to God as an object of ‘intellectual love’, the question
should be, basically, whether the fact that nature (‘God’) can be ‘loved intellec-
tually’ is an extra reason for calling it ‘God’ – apart from its having a moral
relevance. If it were ordinary (passionate) love that would certainly be the case, but
in that case we would be in the domain, not of intellectual knowledge, but of the
imagination, and accordingly our love would be ‘devotion’ rather than ‘intellectual
love’.6 In any case, ‘intellectual love’ cannot be ordinary love, which is ‘joy
accompanied by the idea of an external cause’, given the fact that it has as its
precondition the true and certain knowledge that we are part of nature. So what we
love if we love ‘intellectually’ is not something external to ourselves but ourselves
precisely in so far as we are part of nature.7 What it does have in common with
ordinary love, though, is the fact that its object is a particular – indeed, that is
presumably the main reason why the knowledge by which intellectual love is
produced is called ‘intuitive’. So perhaps all we should read in it is that, in so far as
(true and certain) knowledge of ‘God’ (that is, knowledge of nature) is (true and
certain) knowledge of particular things, it produces in us the same effect as love,
namely, the will to be united with them, or rather, given the fact that we are not free
either to will or not to will that, that it produces in us ‘a satisfaction […] on account
of the presence of the thing loved’, which in turn would ‘strengthen or at least
encourage’ our love for that thing.8 In other words, all that is meant by ‘intellectual
love of God’ may be that, the more we truly and certainly know singular things
(which we can do according as we know more about nature in general), the more we
‘love’ them and the more we ‘love’ nature as a whole – more, at any rate, than when
we know in a general way that anything is part of nature and is as determined as any
other thing. That seems to be the idea behind one of the last scholia of the Ethics:
Although I have shown generally in Pt I that all things (and consequently also the human
mind) depend on God both for their essence and their existence, nevertheless that
demonstration, though legitimate and put beyond all chance of doubt, still does not affect
the mind as much as when this is inferred from the very essence of any singular thing
which we say depends on God.9
Not only are there two ‘ways’ or ‘methods’ one can use in the investigation of
nature, only one of which is truly effective; what is ‘intellectually loved’ is not so
much ‘God’ (nature as a whole) as a singular thing in so far as it depends on ‘God’
(that is, in so far as it is understood to be part of nature). However, there is nothing
particularly mystical in ‘loving’ something according as it is better known. A
thorough analysis of, say, a piece of music or a painting does not diminish our love
of that music or that painting. On the contrary, it generally makes us love that music
or that painting better and with a love that to all intents and purposes is intellectual:
we want to know more and more about it without for that matter having any desire
to possess it or to have it exclusively for ourselves. And in the same way one could
imagine that someone who knows nature and comes to see himself as part of nature
experiences the joys of a lover, albeit ‘intellectually’.
In sum, the fact that nature or a part of nature is loved intellectually is not an
additional reason for calling it ‘God’ or a part of ‘God’. Still, a closer study of the
Epilogue
185


relevant texts, preferably in connection with Spinoza’s theory of action, would be
necessary to arrive at a definite conclusion.
Notes
1
Pt I and II and a first version of Pt III and IV of the Ethics, at any rate, would have been
ready by June 1665; see Spinoza to Bouwmeester (June 1665), Ep. 28, G. IV, 163/C. I,
196. Cf. F. Akkerman, ‘Studies in the Posthumous Works of Spinoza’, PhD diss.,
Groningen University, 1980; Piet Steenbakkers, Spinoza’s Ethica from manuscript to
print, Assen: Van Gorcum, 1994 (PhD diss., Groningen University).
2
‘So he who loves above all the intellectual cognition of God, the most perfect Being, and
takes especial delight therein, is necessarily most perfect, and partakes most in the highest
blessedness […] Since the love of God is man’s highest happiness and blessedness and
the final end and aim of all human action, it follows that he alone observes the Divine Law
who makes it his object to love God not through fear of punishment nor through love of
some other thing such as sensual pleasure, fame, and so forth, but from the mere fact that
he knows God or knows that the knowledge and love of God is the supreme good’ (TTP,
iv, G. III, 60–61/S. 103–4).
3
TTP, xvi, G. III, 191/S. 239; see above, chap. 2, p. 48.
4
Eth. V, prop. 42, G. II, 307/C. I, 616.
5
KV, II, xxii, G. I, 100–102/M. 98–101/ C. I, 138–40.
6
See above, chap. 1, p. 28.
7

8
